### SUMMARY OF PRIVILEGES.

It is provided by Royal privilege <note>A "privilege" was an early form of copyright granted by the sovereign to protect a printer's investment.</note> that no one other than Christopher Plantin <note>The most influential printer of the Northern Renaissance, based in Antwerp.</note> may print the book titled *History of Flowers and of Certain Fragrant Plants Used in Garlands, by Rembert Dodoens, Physician of Mechelen*, or sell copies printed elsewhere for the next six years, under penalty of confiscation of the books, as is more fully set forth in the letters issued at Brussels on December 2, 1567.

->Signed below,<-
->De Langhe.<-

The same is provided by the Council of Brabant <note>The high court of the Duchy of Brabant, which held jurisdiction over Antwerp and Brussels.</note> for the same number of years and under the same penalties as in the superior Privilege; as is more fully set forth in the letters issued at Brussels on December 3 of the same year.

->Signed below,<-
->De Langhe.<-

<summary>This page contains the legal copyright protections (privileges) granted to the printer Christopher Plantin for Rembert Dodoens' botanical treatise, issued by both the Royal authority and the Council of Brabant in December 1567.</summary>
<keywords>copyright privilege, Christopher Plantin, Rembert Dodoens, Brussels, Brabant, botanical history, 16th-century printing</keywords>

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND
# DISTINGUISHED MAN,
## LORD JOACHIM HOPPER,
->*Knight of the Golden Spur, Lord of Dalem,*<-
->*Councilor to the King, and Master of Petitions,*<-
->*his kinsman, Rembert Dodoens, Physician, sends*<-
->*heartiest greetings.*<- <note>original: "S. P. D." (Salutem Plurimam Dicit), a standard Latin epistolary greeting.</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'D' depicting a horse walking to the left within a square frame decorated with floral and foliate patterns.</image-desc>

Concerning FLOWERS, and fragrant and garland-making plants <note>Plants used for weaving wreaths or crowns, highly valued in Renaissance gardens.</note>, most illustrious HOPPER, many things would occur to us to say, if we intended to run through every topic of discussion and write a formal eulogy: but we have not resolved to do that, thinking it sufficient to have shown in few words, in this preface to you, their utility and how much they contribute to the <term>Medical Materials</term> <note>original: "materia medica," the body of collected knowledge about the therapeutic properties of any substance used for healing.</note>. For concerning the discovery and origin of flowers and herbs: concerning the pleasantness which they bring to gardens, orchards, and all—

<summary>This page features the formal dedication of Rembert Dodoens' botanical treatise to his relative Joachim Hopper, a high-ranking official under King Philip II. Dodoens emphasizes the medicinal utility of flowers over mere aesthetic praise.</summary>
<keywords>Rembert Dodoens, Joachim Hopper, botany, medicinal plants, Renaissance gardens, Materia Medica, dedication</keywords>

# E P I S T L E

<note>original: "Epistola"</note>

...is added from these in almost all places: or what need is there to speak of that pleasure of the mind or recreation which is conceived from the sight, scent, or handling of flowers? Indeed, concerning the origin and the discoverers of flowers and herbs, many fabulous tales are found among the Poets and other authors, both Greek and Latin <note>original: "tam Graecos, quàm Latinos"</note>, which would be long and exceedingly tedious to recount here. It is, however, very well known that gardens receive their greatest ornament, their greatest beauty, and their highest grace from these. For not from any topiary works or trellises <note>term: "perticalibus" refers to pole-supported structures or lattices used for climbing plants</note>, however artfully woven or adorned with colors, nor from shaded bowers or other garden works, does so much decoration come to them as from flowers and scented or garland-herbs <note>term: "coronariis" refers to plants specifically grown to be woven into crowns or wreaths</note>; without which, indeed, all garden works are destitute of ornament, grace, and pleasantness. Moreover, the pleasure and recreation of the mind derived from these can be nothing but most honorable. For they remind man of that which is beautiful and virtuous. Indeed, by their beauty, the elegance of their color, the excellence of their form, and the sweetness and fragrance of their scent, flowers suggest...

<summary>This page discusses the aesthetic and psychological value of flowers in gardens, arguing that natural blooms provide more beauty and moral "recreation" than artificial structures like trellises or topiary.</summary>
<keywords>gardening philosophy, aesthetics, floriculture, Greek and Latin poets, topiary, mental recreation, floral fragrance</keywords>

# DEDICATION

<margin>A 3</margin>

to noble minds, the remembrance of what is honorable and beautiful, and of all the virtues. For it would be a shameful thing, as a certain wise man says, for one who contemplates beautiful things, handles beautiful things, and dwells in beautiful places, to possess a mind that is not beautiful, or even base. Furthermore, the utility that comes from flowers to the <term>materia medica</term> <note>original: "materiæ medicæ"; the study of the therapeutic properties of natural substances</note> and the pharmaceutical arts is neither small nor barren, but rather fruitful, varied, and manifold in every way. They are beneficial by their scent alone in no small measure; and then, by their other properties, flowers are of the greatest use. Indeed, by their fragrance, they revive and refresh the spirits; they restore collapsed strength; they not infrequently rouse a sluggish mind; and at times, they gently induce a lingering sleep. Among those things which are called <term>odorables</term> <note>original Greek: "ὀσφραντὰ" (osphranta); things perceived by the sense of smell</note>, they easily hold the primary place, being readily available and familiar through the habit of a friendly nature. For other fragrant things which are brought from the Indies, or the farthest East or West, are not so familiar; they are, meanwhile, more expensive, and not immediately or promptly available to everyone. Flowers, however, are supplied by the garden, and occasionally even the fields provide them of their own accord. Truly, by their other properties—namely, of warming, re-

->E P I S T O L A<-
<note>original: "Epistola" - The Epistle or Dedicatory Letter</note>

of cooling, restraining, strengthening, concocting <note>The process of "ripening" or preparing humors for expulsion</note>, digesting, dispersing, dissolving, opening, thinning, softening, and any others of a similar nature; flowers are so beneficial that no part of the body can be poorly affected for which a remedy cannot be applied from flowers. Likewise, there are few compositions and mixed medications that do not admit some flowers into their recipe. For headaches and most other diseases of the same part, flowers provide a cure. For the lungs or for those suffering from pain in the side <note>likely referring to pleurisy</note>, flowers serve as a protection. They strengthen the stomach; they restrain a loose bowel; and they loosen and open one that is constricted. Flowers are mixed into medications for the liver and the spleen. If the pain of stones, the colon, or the bladder urges, flowers are ready at hand. They soothe the pains of the uterus; they stop or summon the monthly menses. They are mixed with great utility into antidotes and other compound medicines. They are received into poultices <term>cataplasmata: soft, moist applications of herbs</term>, emollient plasters <term>malagmata: topical treatments to soften inflammatory swellings</term>, plasters, ointments, fomentations <note>warm, medicinal washes</note>, and baths. Enemas are rare without flowers. There are many compositions of oils made from flowers. Flowers are preserved. From flowers are made sweetmeats <term>tragemata: sugar-coated treats or medicinal lozenges</term> and syrups <term>serapia: medicinal syrups, often from the Arabic 'sharab'</term>; a liquid is elicited from them. To wine, honey, and vinegar, flowers [add] their powers

### DEDICATION.

<sig>A 4</sig>

impart. Thus, Nature seems to have bestowed no property upon any plant species which she has not also shared with its flowers, and then even more abundantly conferred upon them. And it is indeed far from the truth that the substance of flowers is either meager or sterile, or that it contributes nothing to medicine, as certain unlearned sycophants and slanderers loudly claim. These men believe that knowledge not only of flowers but of all herbs and simple medicines <term>simplicia: medications made from a single natural ingredient, usually a plant</term>—and even of compound ones—is unnecessary for a physician. In fact, they condemn such knowledge with rash daring as if it were superfluous, casting it aside and judging it to belong only to pharmacists <term>Pharmacopœi: from the Greek 'pharmakopoios', meaning those who prepare drugs; apothecaries</term> or to women. 

By this opinion alone, they clearly show themselves to be unworthy of the title and name of "physician." For the knowledge of plants <note>original: "stirpium"</note>, herbs, and other simple medicines, as well as many compound ones, is so intertwined with the other parts of the medical art that no one can truly possess a knowledge of Medicine who is ignorant and unskilled in them. This is something both I have written elsewhere, and Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium</note> himself has left recorded in clear words. For if every craftsman has the instruments of his art known and [kept] in num-

# LETTER

numbered and fixed, how can a physician, being ignorant of these things, be excused for the incompetence of his art? For among the instruments which the art of healing uses in the treatments of diseases are the plants themselves and their roots, bark, pith, leaves, flowers, fruit, seeds, juices, tears <note>original: "lacrymæ" — refers to the resins, gums, or saps that naturally exude from a plant's bark</note>, and other such things. It is inevitable that physicians of this sort must often hesitate and wander about like lost men, falling into the gravest and most ruinous errors. 

Indeed, we have heard from trustworthy sources that this happened not long ago in a certain famous city to a certain physician, who nevertheless prided himself on a magnificent title. He had prescribed to a patient two drachms <note>A drachm was a unit of apothecary weight; two drachms of a potent resin would be a massive, potentially fatal overdose</note> of Diagridium <term>Diagridium: a preparation of scammony (a harsh resinous juice from the root of *Convolvulus scammonia*) used as a very powerful laxative</term>. However, when the Pharmacist <term>Pharmacopœus: an apothecary or person who prepares medicines</term> warned him that the quantity was far too great, and asked him whether he had intended to write Diatragachanth <term>Diatragachantes: a mild, soothing medicinal powder made from gum tragacanth, often used for chest complaints</term> instead of Diagridium, the physician replied by asking whether Diagridium and Diatragachanth were not the same thing. 

So stupid and ignorant of all medicinal materials—and even of the art itself—was this magnificent word-doctor <term>λογιάτρος: (logiatros) a "doctor of words"; a derisive term for a physician who has theoretical learning or eloquence but lacks practical skill and knowledge of materials</term>. He did not know how great a difference exists between Diatragachanth, a composition for the windpipe <note>original: "arteriacam" — referring to the *arteria aspera* or trachea; these medicines were meant to soothe the throat and lungs</note>, and Diagridium, a most potent medication for purging bile through the bo-
<meta>catchword: bowels (uum)</meta>

<summary>The author argues that botanical knowledge is essential to medicine, illustrating the point with a story about a prestigious but ignorant physician who nearly killed a patient by confusing a powerful purgative with a mild cough remedy.</summary>

->### DEDICATION ###<-

subdrawing <note>original: "subducens" — in this context, the physician is withdrawing himself from the responsibility or the danger of his mistake</note> himself from the danger, he was plainly ignorant of it. Another man recently wished to extract water from an herb which is called *Thyrsus* in Latin (for so indeed he had it written down in his notes). This man was both very arrogant and took a great deal of pleasure in his own pride; he was convinced that he surpassed all living physicians in judgment and learning, despite the fact that he had only begun to learn the medical art late in life, having been a physical trainer <term>pædotriba: a teacher of gymnastics or a wrestling coach; here used as an insult to imply the man had no formal academic medical education</term> before becoming a physician. 

He ought to have remembered that a *Thyrsus* is not a specific species of herb, but properly refers to a staff or torch wrapped in ivy <note>The "thyrsus" was the ritual staff carried by Bacchus (Dionysus) and his followers</note>. Metaphorically, however, the name *Thyrsos* is used to describe the upper stalks of any herb upon which the flowers grow. 

There was also once a certain physician who, having found the name *Origanum Onitis* <term>Origanum Onitis: a species of wild marjoram or oregano used in ancient pharmacology</term> in a certain recipe within the works of Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD) was the most influential medical authority of the ancient world</note>, soon prepared a composition for a sick patient which required this *Onitis*. When the Pharmacist <term>Pharmacopœus: an apothecary or person who prepares medicines</term> did not know what kind of herb *Onitis* was and asked for clarification, the physician had no answer to give. When the pharmacist suggested whether he might perhaps mean *Ononis* <term>Ononis: commonly called "Restharrow," a tough-rooted plant very different from oregano</term>, the physician quickly nodded and permitted *Ononis* to be substituted for *Origanum Onitis*; an herb which...

<keywords>medical education, botany, Galen, Thyrsus, Origanum Onitis, Ononis, pharmacy errors, pædotriba</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of physicians who lack botanical knowledge, following an anecdote about a man who confused a plant name with its anatomical structure (Thyrsus) and another who misunderstood a specific type of oregano (Origanum Onitis).</meta>

->## DEDICATORY EPISTLE ##<-

...differing greatly from *Origanum Onitis* <note>original: "Origano Onitide" — a variety of oregano or marjoram mentioned by ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Galen</note>. And into such errors physicians are led by ignorance of plants <term>stirpium: the stocks, stems, or the plants themselves</term> and medical materials <term>materia medica: the study of the origin, properties, and use of drugs</term>, as well as the neglect of that part of the art which alone deserves to be called by the name of **Medicine**: namely, the reasoning and method of curing diseases. 

For it cannot happen that a physician who is learned, well-versed, and practiced in the works of the art would fall into errors of this kind. Indeed, he will hold exactly to the dose <term>dosim: the specific quantity of a medicine to be administered</term> and the proper weight of each individual ingredient. He will not think to put into use things that are unknown, vain, and which do not exist in the nature of things, or which occurred to him while dreaming or raving. 

In the compositions of the ancients, if one thing should be lacking, he will substitute a replacement responding not merely in name and title, but in powers and efficacy <note>This refers to the pharmaceutical practice of finding a "succedaneum"—a substitute that performs the same medical function even if it is a different species</note>, adding it in the place of the missing one. 

From these examples, it can be fully and abundantly clear how necessary the knowledge of plants and medical materials is to a physician; and how this argument we have treated is nothing foreign or alien to our profession or to the exercise of the art in which we are engaged; since among plants, flowers do not hold the last place;

<summary>The author argues that medical errors stem from an ignorance of botany and pharmacology, asserting that a true physician must understand the functional properties of plants to prescribe and substitute them safely.</summary>

<keywords>medical methodology, botany, materia medica, pharmaceutical substitution, Origanum Onitis, dosage, medical education</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the introductory remarks on the medical utility of flowers and begins the formal dedication to Joachim Hopperus, transitioning from technical justification to personal and political tribute.</meta>

...and are useful for various human purposes in many ways. And concerning the utility of flowers, this is enough.

-># DEDICATORY EPISTLE #<-

NOW I turn to you, most distinguished and honorable Hopper <note>Joachim Hopperus (1523–1576) was a prominent Frisian jurist and a trusted counselor to King Philip II of Spain; he was also a relative of the author, Rembert Dodoens</note>, and I offer and dedicate this history of ours regarding flowers and certain fragrant coronary herbs <term>coronariis: plants used specifically for weaving into wreaths, garlands, or "coronals"</term> to you. This shall serve not only as a kind of pledge to you of our long-standing friendship, or a memorial <note>original Greek: "μνημόσυνον" — a souvenir or token of remembrance</note> of an absent kinsman and friend, but chiefly and most especially for the sake of offering my congratulations. 

For since the Lord Philip, Catholic King of Spain, etc. <note>Philip II of Spain, who ruled the Netherlands during this period</note>, has bound and obliged you to himself with great offices and rewards, and has increased your standing with no small honors—and other leaders and magnates follow you with the highest favors—I thought it altogether necessary to congratulate you on these things through the dedication of this History of ours. I have done this on account of that friendship which has flourished between us since our early youth, lest I should seem to have failed in the duty of a friend and kinsman. 

For friends are accustomed not only to rejoice and exult in the prosperous and happy affairs of their friends, but also to express and show that joy in words and deeds. Therefore, this History...

<summary>In this dedication, the author presents his botanical work to his friend and kinsman Joachim Hopperus, a high-ranking official under King Philip II of Spain, celebrating Hopperus's political success and their lifelong bond.</summary>
<keywords>Joachim Hopperus, Philip II of Spain, dedication, friendship, coronary herbs, botanical history, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page completes the word "Historia" (History) from the previous page and concludes Rembert Dodoens's formal dedication to his friend and kinsman, Joachim Hopperus.</meta>

->### DEDICATORY EPISTLE ###<-

...this history of ours, congratulating you on the favor and gifts of the greatest and most powerful King <note>Philip II of Spain</note>, and furthermore on the friendship of other noble and great leaders, while praying for all things auspicious and happy for you. It would remain for me to recommend this work further, were I not already certain that it would be welcome and pleasant to you. Therefore, I shall not add even a single word more; wishing only that it may be useful to all scholars and as well-received by them as it is by you.

Farewell, most distinguished Master Hopper. On the Nones <note>original Latin: "Nonis" — the 5th day of January in the Roman calendar</note> of January, in the year 1568. At Mechelen <note>A city in the Spanish Netherlands (modern-day Belgium) where Dodoens lived and worked</note>.

REMBERT DODOENS’S HISTORY OF FLOWERS AND GARLAND-MAKING PLANTS <note>original: "coronariarum" — plants traditionally used to weave wreaths or garlands</note> AND CERTAIN FRAGRANT HERBS.

### CHAPTER 1. ON THE BLACK OR PURPLE VIOLET.

<margin>
Common black violet or March violet <note>original: "Viola nigra vulg: siue martia"</note>
</margin>
The **black** *or purple Violet <note>today known as the Sweet Violet or Viola odorata</note> immediately brings forth from the root many wide, veined leaves, which are lightly scalloped <term>crenate|having a edge with rounded teeth</term> around their border; they are rounder, thinner, and darker—especially on the upper side—than the leaves of ivy. From the midst of these leaves, thin stalks emerge, and upon each of them, individual flowers bloom,*

<summary>This page begins Dodoens's botanical treatise with the first chapter on the Sweet Violet, describing its physical characteristics such as its veined, scalloped leaves and individual flowering stalks.</summary>
<keywords>Sweet Violet, Viola odorata, botany, leaf morphology, 16th-century herbals, Rembert Dodoens, March Violet</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Sweet Violet (Viola odorata) from page 13. On the previous page, Dodoens began detailing the plant's leaves and stalks; here, he moves on to the appearance of the flowers, their growing conditions, and their historical and mythological names.</meta>

# ON THE BLACK OR <note>This heading continues the title from the previous page: "On the Black or Purple Violet"</note>

...individual, beautiful, fragrant, of a purple color shifting into dark blue, occasionally but rarely white, composed of five small petals, of which the lowest is the largest; then there are hanging little cups <term>calyces|the outer protective part of a flower that holds the petals</term> which, upon ripening, split into three parts: the seed is small and oblong in its roundness: it is anchored by thin and fibrous roots. It remains green in winter as well as summer, being most patient of the cold. In shady places, near thickets, walls, and the edges of fields and gardens, it springs up frequently and in great numbers in rich soil: the flowers usually emerge in March, or when later, in the month of April.

<gloss>the double violet among us</gloss>
There is a certain type of this genus that multiplies the petals of its flowers <note>referring to "double-flowered" varieties common in gardens</note>, appearing in both purple and white; both are garden varieties.

There is also a certain wild kind, with smaller leaves and paler flowers, which are less fragrant or not at all: it grows in shady places, and along paths and the edges of fields, but in dry and barren soil.

The Greeks call the Violet *ion* <note>original Greek: ἴον</note>, and the purple *ion* <note>original: ἴον πορφυροῦν</note>, that is, the Purple Violet. Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and the 'father of botany'</note> indeed calls it the black *ion* or *melanion* <note>original: ἴον μέλαν ἢ μελάνιον</note>, that is, the black Violet, from the dark purple color of the flowers. Pliny <note>the Roman naturalist</note> reports that this is the only one distinguished from other Violets by the Greek name, and is called *ion*, and that from these the *Ianthine* <note>violet-colored</note> garment is named. The apothecary shops <term>officinae|the workshops or pharmacies of the era</term> preserve the Latin name; yet they call the herb *Violaria*, and the "mother of violets." The Germans call the flower **blue violet or March violet** <note>original German: "blau veiel oder Mertzenviolen"</note>: the French similarly call it **Violet of March** <note>original French: "Violette de Mars"</note>, that is, the March Violet: the Belgians call them **Violets** <note>original Dutch/Flemish: "Violetten"</note>.

Nicander <note>a Greek poet and physician</note> believes, as recorded in the *Geoponica* <note>a collection of ancient agricultural lore</note> according to Hermolaus <note>Hermolao Barbaro, a Renaissance scholar who commented on ancient texts</note>, that it was called *ion* by the Greeks because certain Ionian Nymphs first gave that flower to Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods</note> as a gift. Others assert it was called *ion* because when Jupiter turned the girl he loved, Io, into a cow, the earth produced this flower for her grazing; having been created for her sake, it took its name from her. And from there also...

<meta>catchword: Latinis (for the Latins)</meta>

<summary>This page details the physical characteristics, growing habits, and linguistic history of the Sweet Violet. Dodoens provides various regional names and relates the classical myths that explain why the Greeks named the flower after the nymph Io.</summary>
<keywords>Sweet Violet, Viola odorata, botany, Theophrastus, Pliny the Elder, Io, Greek mythology, plant nomenclature, 16th-century herbals</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical and historical profile of the Violet. The previous page ended with a reference to Pliny the Elder and the Greek nomenclature for the plant (ion). Here, the author explores Latin etymologies, classical literary references to the plant's identity, its use in pigment-making according to Vitruvius, and its various medicinal applications for cooling the body.</meta>

# PURPLE VIOLET

Among the Latins, the Violet is thought to be called *Viola*, as if from *vitula* <note>original: "quasi vitula," meaning 'little calf'</note>, with the letter 't' removed.

Servius reports that the Violet was also called *vaccinium* <note>A term in classical Latin that could refer to the whortleberry/blueberry or a dark hyacinth; here the author discusses the confusion between these species</note> by the Latins on the same occasion, citing a passage from Virgil's *Bucolics*:

> *White privets fall, but black whortleberries are gathered.* <note>original: "Alba Ligustra cadunt, Vaccinia nigra leguntur"</note>

<margin>The yellow marigold paints the soft whortleberries</margin>
However, Virgil in his 10th Eclogue shows that the Whortleberry differs from the Violet:

> *And black are the Violets, and black the Whortleberries.* <note>original: "Et nigræ Violæ sunt, & Vaccinia nigra"</note>

Vitruvius also, in the seventh book of his *Architecture*, distinguishes the Violet from the Whortleberry. For he records that the color of Attic Ochre <term>Silis Attici|a prized yellow pigment used in ancient painting</term> is made from the Violet, whereas an elegant purple is produced from the Whortleberry. Dyers, he says, when they wish to imitate Attic Ochre, throw dried Violets into a vessel with water and boil them over a fire; then, when it has reached the right consistency, they pour it onto a linen cloth, and squeezing it with their hands, they collect the violet-colored water into a mortar. By pouring Eretrian earth <note>a type of medicinal and pigment-grade white clay from the Greek island of Euboea</note> into this and grinding it, they produce the color of Attic Ochre. By the same method, tempering Whortleberry and mixing in milk, they make an elegant purple. *How these came to be called Whortleberries will be explained in another and more appropriate place.*

We return now to Violets, whose flowers and leaves possess a cold and moist temperament <note>According to the humoral theory of medicine, these properties were used to counteract "hot" and "dry" illnesses like fevers or inflammation</note>. Indeed, the flowers are useful for all internal inflammations, especially of the sides <note>referring to pleurisy</note> and the lungs: they soothe and smooth the roughness and irritation of the chest, the windpipe <term>asperæ arteriæ|literally "rough artery," the historical term for the trachea</term>, and the throat. They quench the excessive heat of the liver, kidneys, and bladder; they mitigate the burning of raging fevers; they dull the sharpness of bile; and they keep away and soothe thirst.

The syrup made from them—called *drosaton* <note>original Greek: δροσάτον, meaning 'dewy'</note> or, as Actuarius <note>Joannes Actuarius, a 13th-century Byzantine court physician</note> calls it, *serapion* <note>original: σεράπιον, from which the word 'syrup' is derived</note>—if it is boiled down from a liquid in which fresh Violets have been soaked many times, softens the bowels and draws out bile through the internal passages when taken in a dose of three or four ounces.

An oil is also made from Violets which is powerful for its cooling and moisturizing ability; when rubbed on the temples, it gently encourages sleep.

<summary>This page discusses the classical etymology of the violet and its confusion with the 'vaccinium' in Latin literature, while detailing its use as a dye and its medicinal role in cooling internal inflammations and inducing sleep.</summary>
<keywords>Viola, Vaccinium, Vitruvius, Virgil, Attic Ochre, Eretrian earth, drosaton, serapion, humoral medicine, botanical pigments</keywords>

# ON THE PURPLE VIOLET

[It] promotes [sleep] <note>The text begins mid-word with "ciliat," likely completing "conciliat" (procures/promotes), referring to sleep mentioned on the previous page</note>, which a hot or dry imbalance hinders: with egg yolk it softens the pains of the seat <note>the rectum or anus</note> and hemorrhoids: it is also most usefully added to cooling poultices that lull pain.

Let the oil in which the Violets are steeped be either from unripe Olives—which the Greeks call *omotribes* or *omphakinon* <note>original: "ὠμοτριβὲς ἢ ὀμφάκινον," referring to oil pressed from unripe olives, valued for its astringent properties</note>—or from sweet Almonds, as Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a 9th-century physician</note> says. The violets themselves should be fresh and moist. For once they are dry and their moisture is lost, they cool less and seem to have acquired something of a hot quality.

MORE RECENT authors believe that dry violets are usefully mixed into medicines thought to strengthen the heart.

The LEAVES of Violets, when eaten as a vegetable, cool, moisten, and soften the bowels: applied externally, they mitigate all hot inflammations, whether on their own or applied with pearl barley <note>original: "polenta," meaning a barley meal or poultice</note>. They are also applied to a burning stomach and to the eyes, according to Galen. Dioscorides writes that they should even be applied to a prolapsed rectum. <insert>Hangovers</insert>, Pliny says, and heaviness of the head are dispelled by wearing [violet] wreaths or by smelling them; and they dispel quinsy <term>angina|a severe sore throat or tonsillitis</term> when drunk with water.

<insert>That part of them which is purple heals the falling sickness <term>comitialibus|epilepsy</term>; especially for children when drunk in water.</insert>

The SEED acts against scorpions <note>as an antidote for their stings</note>.

<margin><image-desc>A hand-drawn manicule in red ink pointing to the text.</image-desc> This passage in Pliny is suspect.</margin>
<insert>DIOSCORIDES writes that the purple flower—not of the violet, but of the Attic Aster—drunk with water, helps quinsy and the falling sickness in children.</insert> From his account, one may suspect—and not without reason—that these remedies were wrongly attributed to the Violet in Pliny's work.

# ON THE THREE-COLORED VIOLET.

*...shining with a threefold color, namely purple, yellow, and white or blue: by whose elegance and variety of colors they are most pleasing, since they possess little or no scent. On the top two small petals of the flower, and sometimes on the lower one, and occasionally even on the outermost part of the middle ones, the purple of the* Purple Violet <note>original: "Violæ nigræ" — literally 'black violet,' a common Renaissance term for the dark purple sweet violet</note> *shines back. On the middle ones for the most part, and not infrequently on the lower one, a white or blue color sits. Yellow not rarely occupies the lower petal or a large part of it, but always the center of the flower, with some small black rays running through it together. The seed is contained in small, rounded-oblong vessels* <term>seed capsules|the dry fruit that splits open to release seeds</term>, *the size of a bitter vetch* <note>original: "ochri"</note>, *which follow the falling flowers and gape open of their own accord once the seed is mature. It rests upon fibrous roots. It comes up in many places in the fields: it grows also in gardens, and indeed frequently of its own accord, more beautiful and elegant than the wild variety. In spring it springs forth together with the* Purple Violet *or a little after; it blooms then through the whole of summer and the greatest part of* Autumn, *and occasionally even in winter.*

**IACEA** *is what it is called by many: yet there is also another iacea which is nicknamed the black variety. By others it is called* Trinity herb <note>original: "herba Trinitatis"</note>, *on account of the triple color of the flower. To some it is the* nail-herb <note>original: "herba clauellata," possibly referring to the shape of its parts or its use in treating "clavi" (calluses)</note>. *The French call the flowers* pansies <note>original: "pensees" — literally 'thoughts'</note>, *which name is also known to the people of* Brabant <note>A historical region in the Low Countries</note> *and the neighboring* Belgians. *The Germans call it* convulsion herb <note>original: "Freyscham kraut" — a traditional name for plants used to treat "frights" or childhood seizures</note> *and* Trinity flowers <note>original: "Dreyefelticheyt blumen"</note>. *It seems to agree with the* phlox <note>original: "φλογί" (phlogi)</note> *of Theophrastus* <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and the 'father of botany'</note>, *which is also called* flame or little flame <note>original: "φλόξ ἢ φλόγιον" (phlox ē phlogion)</note>, *and is a purely wild flower without scent. Emerging in spring together with the* White Violet *or a little after, and lasting for a very long time: which is the nature of this three-colored* Violet, *which as we have written, is wild and scentless by its nature, and blooms for a long duration.* Flame *or* little flame, *however, Gaza* <note>Theodorus Gaza, a 15th-century translator who helped rediscover Greek botanical texts</note> *interprets as the* Flame violet <note>original: "Violam flammeam"</note>.

**THE QUALITY** *of the herb is cold in an obscure way, but more clearly moist, with a slow and sticky juice.*

<sig>E</sig>

# ON THE STOCK OR WHITE VIOLET.

...partaking of a juice like that of the **Mallow** <note>original: "Maluae"</note>; for that reason, it also possesses emollient <term>softening|substances used to soothe or soften the skin or internal tissues</term> properties, though less so than the Mallow. It is beneficial, as more recent authors say, for those suffering from fevers, especially children and infants, whose convulsions and seizures of the falling sickness <note>original: "comitialis morbi" — a historical term for epilepsy</note> it is believed to help. It is likewise recommended for inflammations of the lungs and chest, and for the mange <note>original: "scabiem"</note> and itching of the body; it is also said to knit together ulcers.

---

->ON THE STOCK OR WHITE<-
->VIOLET. CHAPTER III.<-

Under the name **STOCK** <note>original: "Leucoii" — from the Greek 'leukos' (white) and 'ion' (violet)</note>, several types of Violets are included, which differ chiefly in the color of their flowers, but also somewhat in their leaves. These can be distinguished into two kinds. One kind, which has whiter and softer leaves, might be called the "White Stock" because of the color of its leaves rather than its flowers. The other is the "Yellow Stock," named for the yellow color of its flower. Furthermore, there are two types of the White Stock: namely, the greater and the lesser.

In the **GREATER** variety, the stalk is two feet or higher, round, and divided into several "wings" or branches. The leaves are oblong, white, soft, and covered in a kind of down <term>lanugo|fine, soft hair found on the leaves or stems of plants</term>, similar in some way to willow leaves but softer. The fragrant flowers, composed of four small petals, emerge along the stalks on thin stems at the upper part. They are various in color: sometimes reddish, or white, or a scarlet-purple, or very close to a pale ash-color, and occasionally variegated. Then follow the pods <term>siliquae|long, narrow seed vessels characteristic of the mustard family</term>, which are oblong and narrow, containing broad, round seeds. It is supported by fibrous but strong roots. It blooms in early Spring and nearly the whole of summer, in the second year after sowing, and for several years following, provided it is protected from the cold during the winter months with diligence and care.

The **LESSER** kind differs from the former not in its form but in its size...

->ON THE STOCK OR WHITE<-
->VIOLET. CHAPTER III.<-

...differs only in this: it has similar stems and leaves, but smaller; the flowers are also not of a single color, but rather white, or reddish, or purple-red, like the larger variety. The seed pods and seeds are also similar. This kind blooms not in the Spring, but in June, July, or August, and in the same year it was sown; it perishes in winter.

There is indeed also a Stock <note>original: "Leucoion"</note> with multiple petals <note>This refers to a "double-flowered" variety, which was highly prized by early modern gardeners</note>, but it is very rare; it is of the larger variety and begins to bloom in early Spring, requiring greater care in the winter months so it is not harmed by the cold. These are generally sown in gardens.

Besides these, there is also a certain wild white Stock, low-growing and small, which grows in maritime places in Zeeland <note>A coastal province of the Netherlands</note>; and also near Montpellier, as Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), a renowned Swiss naturalist and polymath</note> writes.

Furthermore, there is also a certain bulbous Stock <note>Likely referring to the Snowdrop or Snowflake, which early botanists often grouped with stocks because of their white color</note>, which will be discussed in the following chapters.

Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist</note> also depicts a certain shrubby Violet with broad leaves; a fragrant flower like that of the **Larkspur** <note>original: "Consolidae regiae"</note>; a stem two cubits <term>cubit|An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches, based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</term> high; brought from Mount Baldo and sent to him by Francesco Calzolari, a pharmacist of Verona. He names it the "Arborescent Violet" <note>original: "Violam arborescentem"</note>.

It is called *leukoion* <note>original: "λευκοϊον"</note> in Greek, indeed *Viola alba* <note>White Violet</note> in Latin, and *Keyri* by the Arabs. Our people call the larger kind **Stock Gillyflowers** <note>original: "Stock Violieren"</note>; and the smaller kind, **Gentlemen's Gillyflowers** <note>original: "heere Violieren"</note>. Both may be appropriately called "white"—on account of the whiteness <note>Referring to the hoary, silver-white fuzzy texture of the leaves</note> of the leaves, as we have said—to distinguish them from the yellow variety, which is the most excellent.

In their medicinal properties <note>original: "facultate"</note>, these Stocks do not seem unlike the Yellow Stock; yet they are weaker in their powers.

# YELLOW WALLFLOWER
<note>original: "LEVCOION LVTEVM." Early modern botanists used the Greek-derived name *Leucoion* (literally "white violet") to refer to several different fragrant, four-petaled flowers, including what we now call Wallflowers and Stocks.</note>

->[Full-page woodcut illustration of the Yellow Wallflower]<-

<note>This detailed illustration depicts the entire anatomy of the Yellow Wallflower (*Cheiranthus cheiri*). The central stem divides into several branches covered in long, narrow, lance-shaped leaves. At the tips of the stalks are clusters of four-petaled flowers. Among these clusters are the plant's developing seed pods, known as <term>siliques|Long, slender seed vessels characteristic of the mustard family (Brassicaceae), which split open when mature to release seeds</term>. The gnarled, woody root system at the base highlights the plant's perennial nature and its ability to thrive in poor or rocky soil.</note>

<summary>This page consists of a detailed botanical woodcut of the Yellow Wallflower (Leucoion luteum). It serves as a visual guide to the plant’s morphology, identifying its flowers, seed-bearing siliques, and root structure.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Yellow Wallflower (Leucoion luteum) following the illustration on the previous page. ## ON THE YELLOW WALLFLOWER
<sig>B 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: such</meta>

[the leaves are] smaller than those [of the previously described plants]: the flowers are yellow and extremely fragrant; the seed pods are slender and oblong, containing broad seeds. The entire shrub is woody and easily withstands the severity of winter. It blooms in April and May, and if the winter has been mild, even in March or earlier. It grows most freely on very old walls, among ruins, and in other rocky places; it is also planted in gardens next to walls and partitions.

**There is also** a certain variety of this Wallflower with a double flower, which has no seed pods. This is planted from cuttings and cultivated in gardens.

The Greeks also call this *leukoion* <note>original Greek: λευκόϊον</note> and *quince-yellow leukoion* <note>original Greek: λευκόϊον μήλινον</note>. The Latins call it the White Violet <note>original: "Violam albam"</note> and Yellow Wallflower <note>original: "Leucoion luteum"</note>. To the Arabs, it is *Keyri*; to our people, "stone wallflowers" <note>original Dutch: "steen Violieren"</note>. You might call them wall-dwelling or rock-dwelling Violets, or "yellow wallflowers" <note>original Dutch: "geel Violierē"</note>. To the Germans, they are "yellow violets" <note>original German: "geel Violaten"</note>. To the French, "yellow gillyflowers" <note>original French: "Giroffles iaulnes"</note>.

**Now the whole** Wallflower shrub, as Galen says, possesses a cleansing quality and consists of thin parts, though the flowers possess this more; and among these, the dried ones even more than the fresh.

**Dioscorides** writes that yellow wallflowers are of primary use in medicine; he notes that their dried flowers, boiled in <term>decoctions|Medicinal teas made by boiling hardy plant materials like roots or dried flowers to extract their active properties</term>, are effective for inflammations of the uterus and for inducing menstruation. Prepared with a <term>cerate|A stiff medicinal ointment made of wax, oil, and medicinal extracts</term>, they heal cracks in the seat [the anus], and with honey, they treat mouth sores. The seed, drunk in wine in a weight of two <term>drachms|An ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.4 grams or about a teaspoon of powder</term>, or applied [as a suppository] with honey, brings forth the menses, the <term>afterbirth|The placenta and membranes expelled after birth</term>, and the fetus. The roots applied with vinegar restrain the spleen and benefit those suffering from gout.

**Hippocrates**, in his book *On the Nature of Women*, prescribes Wallflower seed ground and drunk with wine to expel the afterbirth and drive blood from the uterus; and he records that the root of the yellow wallflower should be taken in the same way for the same purposes.

**The Dame’s Violet** has leaves that are long, broad, and somewhat dark right from the root, with notched edges. The stalks are a <term>cubit|An ancient measurement roughly the length of a forearm, about 18 inches</term> [long]...

# ON THE BROAD-LEAVED VIOLET.
<note>This header refers to the new chapter starting mid-page, though the text begins by concluding the previous section on the Dame's Violet.</note>

...such stems or taller ones, surrounded by similar but smaller leaves; flowers burst forth at the ends of the branch-like shoots, similar to *Wallflowers* <note>original Latin: Leucoiis; a general term for stock or wallflowers</note>, in color either white or reddish, or a pale purple; the small seed is produced in oblong and round pods, oblong in shape and of a reddish-black color; the roots underneath are fibrous and strong. It grows in gardens. It flowers especially in the months of May and June during the second year after it is sown; not infrequently its flowering continues through the entire summer.

The FRENCH call them *Violettes des dames* and *Girofflees des dames*, that is, *Matronly Violets* <note>known today as Dame’s Violet or Dame’s Rocket</note>; our people, using a corrupted name, call them **Damas bloemen**. The Germans call them **Winter violen**, that is, *Winter Violets*.

Moreover, the leaves of these Violets are sharp and hot to the taste; *they seem to be a species of Rocket <term>Rocket: Eruca sativa, a plant in the mustard family known for its peppery bite</term>, being very close to it in flavor and medicinal property.*

### ON THE BROAD-LEAVED VIOLET. CHAPTER VI.
<note>This chapter describes Lunaria annua, commonly called "Honesty" or "Money Plant" due to its translucent, coin-shaped seed pods.</note>

The *Broad-leaved Violet* puts forth green, broad leaves, notched around the edges, similar to the herb called *Garlic Mustard* <note>Alliaria</note> but larger. The stems are round and branched. The flowers are purple-blue, the color of a *Wallflower* <note>Leucoium</note>; these are followed by broad, flat, and nearly round pods <term>siliques: the elongated seed capsules characteristic of the crucifer family</term> made of very thin membranes, as if constructed from thin plates. Within these are *flat, broad, dark-colored seeds, shaped somewhat like a half-moon* <note>original Greek: διχοτόμου; meaning 'cut in half' or 'bisected'</note>, with a third membrane in the middle. The roots underneath are thick, white, and spread in various directions, somewhat similar to the roots of the female *Peony*, but smaller and thinner, and porous on the inside. The Belgians plant this Violet in gardens alongside others. The flowers appear in April, in the second year after sowing.

**BULBONAC** or *Bolbonac* is its common, unrefined name <note>a medieval name for Honesty, likely derived from the Latin 'bulbus'</note>; we call it the Broad-leaved Violet, or as many prefer, the Foreign Violet

...such stems or taller ones, surrounded by similar but smaller leaves; flowers burst forth at the ends of the branch-like shoots, similar to *Wallflowers* <note>original Latin: "Leucoiis"; a historical grouping for various fragrant, four-petaled flowers like stock or wallflowers</note>, in color either white or reddish, or a pale purple; the small seed is produced in oblong and round pods, oblong in shape and of a reddish-black color; the roots underneath are fibrous and strong. It grows in gardens. It flowers especially in the months of May and June during the second year after it is sown; not infrequently its flowering continues through the entire summer.

The FRENCH call them *Dame's Violets* <note>original: "Violettes des dames"</note> and *Dame's Gillyflowers* <note>original: "Girofflees des dames"</note>, that is, *Matronly Violets* <note>Known today as Dame’s Violet or Dame’s Rocket, a plant often found in old cottage gardens.</note>; our people <note>Referring to the Flemish or Dutch speakers of the author's home region.</note>, using a corrupted name, call them **Damask Flowers** <note>original: "Damas bloemen"</note>. The *Broad-leaved Violet* puts forth green, broad leaves, notched around the edges...

-> ### OF THE BROAD-LEAVED VIOLET. <-

...for we have preferred to name it thus. Those in Brabant <note>A historical region in the Low Countries, spanning parts of modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands.</note> call them **Penninckbloemen** <note>literally "Penny-flowers"</note> from the shape of the seed pods, which somewhat resemble a denarius <note>A silver coin; hence the modern name "Money Plant" or "Silver Dollar."</note>, and **Paeschbloemen** <note>literally "Easter-flowers"</note> because they almost always bloom around the solemnity of Easter. *Most modern writers call it Lunaria <note>Moon-wort</note>, others call it Greek Lunaria:* either from the shape of its seed, or from the silvery brilliance of the central membrane of the pods, which shines once the outer skins fall away with the seed during extreme dryness.

<margin>Crateuas's Thlaspi.</margin>
*Among the ancients, however, it seems to be the "second Thlaspi" <note>original: "Thlaspi alterum"</note>, specifically the one described by Crateuas <note>A Greek physician and pharmacologist from the 1st century BCE.</note>, which is called by some "Persian Mustard" <note>original: "Sinapi Persicum"</note>. For as Dioscorides <note>The famous Greek physician and author of De Materia Medica.</note> says, Crateuas records a certain Thlaspi that is* "broad-leaved and with large roots" <note>original Greek: "πλατύφυλλον κὶ μεγαλόρριζον"</note>, *which is exactly the nature of this Violet, which we have named "broad-leaved."*

Another certain Violet is similar to this one in its broad leaves and flat, round pods, which relies on a perennial root and does not wither in winter. It has woody stems a cubit high <note>About 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>: many broad leaves, flat and wide pods, both slightly smaller than the former plant: white flowers like the Dame's Violet. It is found in gardens; it blooms in summer.

FURTHERMORE, in property and power, the seed of the Broad-leaved Violet is indeed hot and dry, sharp to the taste, similar in flavor and strength to the seed of Thlaspi <note>Likely referring to Field Penny-cress (Thlaspi arvense).</note>: the roots also share some sharpness, though not much: they are eaten in salads like those of certain other plants.

Then, in the second year, it produces stems that are round, straight, and branched,

-> ### MARIAN VIOLET <-
<note>original: "Viola Mariana." Today this plant is widely known as Canterbury Bells (*Campanula medium*).</note>

[This page is a full-page botanical illustration]

The illustration depicts the **Marian Violet** in complete detail, showing the plant from root to bloom:

*   **The Root:** At the base is a thick, segmented taproot with several smaller branching roots, indicating a sturdy, well-anchored plant.
*   **The Foliage:** Several long, lance-shaped leaves grow from the base. Their edges are serrated (toothed like a saw), a common feature of this species.

The artist has used delicate line-hatching—a technique of drawing closely spaced parallel lines—to create a sense of three-dimensional depth and texture, particularly on the rough surface of the stem and the soft folds of the blossoms.

# ON THE MARIAN VIOLET.
<meta>catchword: visible</meta>

...somewhat hairy as well, around which are similar but smaller leaves. The flowers emerge at the tips of the branches; they are oblong, hollow, and round, divided at the edges by five shallow notches. Their color is for the most part a bluish-purple, though occasionally they are whitish, and they contain two or three white stamens; before these flowers open, they are five-angled in shape. After the flowers, a seed-head <note>original: "capitulum"</note> is produced, wider at the top and also possessing five angles. It is wrapped in five compartments that look like rough, hairy little hoods <note>original: "caligulis"</note> hanging downwards. Within this is a tiny, reddish seed, almost the color of a chestnut fruit when it has been removed from its prickly husk <note>original: "echino"</note>. The roots underneath are long, thick, and white, with a flavor that is not unpleasant. It grows, as they say, in forests and mountains, as well as in certain shady tracts—as Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist</note> reports—in many places across Italy and Germany. Among the Belgians <note>Referring to the Low Countries/Netherlands</note>, it is cultivated in gardens. Its flowering period lasts through the months of June, July, and August; meanwhile, the seed reaches maturity, for it does not bloom all at once but bit by bit.

Our people call this plant the **Marian Violet**, and commonly *Mariettes*. <margin>Medion.</margin> Most recent authors maintain that it is the *Medion*. However, Dioscorides <note>The primary Greek authority on medical botany in the 1st century AD</note> records that the *Medion* has leaves like *Seris* <note>Wild chicory or endive</note> (though some manuscripts say *Iris*), a stem three cubits <note>Approximately 4.5 feet</note> high, and on it a large, round, purplish flower. He describes its fruit as small, similar to *Cnicus* <note>Safflower</note>, and its root as nine inches <note>original: "dodrantalem"</note> long, as thick as a walking stick, with a bitter taste. The Marian Violet does not correspond particularly well with this description: for although its flower is large and round, the leaf is not properly that of an Iris nor a Seris, and the fruit or seed is not at all like the seed of Safflower. For these reasons, it appears not to be the *Medion*, but rather the true **Wild Turnip** of Dioscorides; called *gongyle agria* <note>original: "γογγύλη ἄγρια"</note> by the Greeks, with whose description it better agrees. For it is a shrubby and branched plant, whose lobes or seed-heads are covered by several hanging compartments; when these are removed, the inner lobe or head is visible <meta>The text ends mid-sentence to match the catchword "spicuum," part of "conspicuum."</meta>

# ON THE MARIAN VIOLET.
<meta>catchword: OF</meta>

<margin>
A corrupt passage in Dioscorides.
</margin>

...it is clear: what sort of plant the Wild Turnip <note>The author uses the name *Rapum sylvestre*, which literally means "Wild Turnip" but often referred to what we now call Rampion Bellflower (*Campanula rapunculus*).</note> is, is sufficiently evident from its description in Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist whose 1st-century text was the standard for 1,500 years.</note>. Although this description is corrupted by the addition of certain superfluous details, it nevertheless encompasses the most specific traits of the Wild Turnip. The Wild Turnip is described by Dioscorides in these words: *The wild turnip <note>original Greek: ἄγρια γογγύλη</note> grows in fields, a shrub a cubit <note>About 18 inches or 45 cm</note> in height, many-branched, smooth at the tip; having smooth leaves, a finger’s width or even larger; its fruit is in cup-like pods; when the seed-vessels are opened, there is another head-shaped pod inside, containing small black seeds, which are white inside when crushed.* *That is to say, the Wild Turnip grows in fields, a shrub of cubit height, smooth at the highest part: having smooth leaves, a finger's width or larger: fruit in cup-like pods, whose coverings when they are opened, another head-like pod lies beneath, in which are black seeds, but when crushed are white inside.* In this description, those things which are related concerning the smoothness of the tops of the stalks and leaves, and the blackness of the seed with its white inner marrow, do not fit the description of the Wild Turnip—especially this one—but belong elsewhere. For the stalks and leaves of this Wild Turnip are not smooth, but somewhat hairy; the seed also is not black, but rather reddish, and close in color to chestnuts, as we have said. It seems, however, that the details about the smoothness of the leaves and upper parts belong to the *Bellflower* <note>original: "Campanulæ"</note>, which is discussed in the following chapters, and which perhaps was also considered a species of Wild Turnip; whereas the seed described here belongs to the cultivated Turnip. Thus, the descriptions of several plants have been confused with one another.

AS FOR what pertains to its properties, the root of the **Marian Violet** possesses a cooling and somewhat astringent power: it is suitable for eating just like other wild turnips, and is not unpleasantly added to salads <note>original: "acetarijs," referring to vinegar-based salads or herb mixes</note>.
<note>The final paragraph has been underlined in red ink by a later hand.</note>

### ->ON OTHER WILD TURNIPS. CHAPTER VIII.<-

There are indeed many other kinds of *Wild Turnip* <note>original: *Rapi sylvestris*</note>, with *cup-shaped flowers* and *roots* not much different from the *Marian Violet* <note>The Marian Violet refers to the Canterbury Bell (*Campanula medium*).</note>.

<margin>2</margin>
One type, which is distinct from the *Marian Violet*, has stalks a cubit <note>About 18 inches or 45 cm</note> or higher, which are angular, rough, and hairy. The leaves are somewhat rough, broad, ending in a point, and notched around the edges; in both form and color they are somewhat similar to the leaves of a nettle. The flowers emerge along the stalks, smaller than the flowers of the *Marian Violet*, and are similarly divided into five notches around the edge, but these are deeper. They are somewhat hairy inside, and in color they are either white, a pale purple, or leaning toward blue. The Germans call this genus **Throat-herb** <note>original German: *Halſzkraut*</note>; our people <note>Likely referring to the Dutch/Flemish, as the author is writing from the Low Countries</note> call it **Throat-weed** <note>original: *Halſcruyt*</note>; that is, in Latin, <term>Throatwort</term> <note>original: *Ceruicaria*, named for its supposed ability to heal the neck (*cervix*)</note>, and <term>Greater Throatwort</term> <note>original: *Ceruicaria maior*</note>, in Greek <margin>Greater Throatwort.</margin> <term>Throat-herb</term> <note>original: *τραχήλιον* (trachelion)</note>; many call it <term>uvular-herb</term> <note>original: *vuularia*</note>, and Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a founding father of German botany</note> calls it <term>Bellflower</term> <note>original: *Campanula*</note>.

<margin>3</margin>
The third kind has shorter stalks and smaller, whiter, less rough leaves that are oblong and more similar to the leaves of *Sage*. The flowers are smaller than those of the *Throatwort*, resembling in color the pale purple of the *Dark Violet* <note>original: *Viola nigra*</note>; the roots are also similar. Most people call this kind <term>Lesser Throatwort</term> <note>original: *Ceruicaria minorem*</note>. These two grow in *Germany* and *Belgium* in many dry places along the edges of meadows, and frequently in brambly areas; they are transplanted into gardens for the sake of their flowers. They bloom in the months of *June*, *July*, and *August*.

<margin>4</margin>
There is also a fourth kind similar to these, which produces shorter stalks. The leaves are broad and very close to the first *Throatwort*, yet less rough and less notched around the edges. The flowers hang downward and are usually of a pale purple color. In place of [a single] root, there are many thin, white little roots and fibers creeping crosswise and obliquely...

# ->ON WILD <term>TURNIPS</term>.<-
<note>original: *DE SYLVESTRIBVS RAPIS*. In this 16th-century context, "Wild Turnips" refers not to modern brassicas, but to various bellflowers and rampions whose roots were thought to resemble small turnips.</note>

*Roots similar to <term>Rampion</term> are sent down. Thus this species easily propagates itself and spreads widely. It is found along the edges of fields in <note>original: *Zelandia*. A coastal province of the Netherlands.</note> Zeeland and other maritime locations; it blooms at the same time as the others: it can be called Sea Throatwort.*

THE FIFTH kind, commonly named <term>Rampion</term>, produces thin stalks: oblong, narrow leaves: flowers smaller than the Throatwort, mostly blue in color: white roots, the length of a finger, suitable for eating. It grows frequently in fields in many places, blooming for most of the summer: in the winter months the roots are gathered and served in salads. They commonly name this species, as we said, Rampion <note>original: *Rapunculum*, literally "little turnip."</note>, as if it were a small turnip: the Germans call it **Rapuntzeln**; our people **Raponce**; the French **Raiponce**.

OF ALL these, the flowers before they open are five-angled; but when fully opened they are round and resemble the shape of an inverted basket, with white stamens inside; and around the circumference they have five notches and just as many protruding angles: however, the small heads in which the seed is held are angular, wider at the top, but bare, and covered by no appendages or small cases or <term>seed-vessels</term> <note>original: *pericardijs*. In early botany, this referred to the seed-containing structure, now called a pericarp.</note>, as are the Canterbury Bells <note>original: *Violae Marianae*. Likely *Campanula medium*.</note>; which for that reason—namely, because it has covered heads or lobes—fits the description of the Wild Turnip more exactly than any of these, though all of these are nonetheless Wild Turnips.

BESIDES these, however, there is also a certain sixth kind, differing somewhat in form from the six species mentioned above: this one has leaves that are wide at first, then narrower along the stalks: the stalks are <note>original: *dodrantales*. ## ON THE BASKET VIOLET.
<note>original: *DE VIOLA CALATHIANA*. Despite the name "violet," this plant is the Marsh Gentian (*Gentiana pneumonanthe*), named "calathiana" because of its basket-like shape.</note>

### CHAPTER IX.

The **Basket Violet** emerges with thin stems nine inches long <note>original: *dodrantalibus*. A *dodrans* was a Roman measurement of about 9 inches, or three-quarters of a foot.</note>: the leaves are oblong, narrow, and placed in opposite pairs. The flowers emerge both at the top of the small stalks and from the axils [creases] of the leaves; they are oblong, basket-shaped <note>original: *calathoides*</note>, gaping open at the top, of a deep saturated blue-purple, with white stamens in the middle. They are somewhat similar in shape to the flowers of the <term>Wild Turnip</term> <note>original: *Rapi syluestris*</note>, likewise five-angled when not yet fully open, and divided into five parts around the edge, but they are less wide, narrower, and smaller. The tiny seed is contained in small oblong heads. The roots underneath are thin and oblong, occasionally split into two or three parts, or divided into more, with a bitter taste. It is found sometimes in meadows, but more frequently in other uncultivated places, the same ones where Heath...

->**ON THE BELLFLOWER.**<-

Among our people, it has no common name; however, there are some who call it "little blue lilies" <note>original Dutch: *blauw Leliekens*</note>. In the countryside of Anagni <note>Anagni is a town in Italy</note>, they commonly call it *Pettimorsa*, according to the testimony of Matthioli. It seems to be the plant Pliny called the Basket Violet <note>original Latin: *Calathiana Viola*; named for its resemblance to a *calathus*, a flared wicker basket</note>, of which he writes in Book 21, Chapter 6, in these few words:
> Entirely without scent, and with a very small leaf, the Basket Violet is a gift of Autumn, while the others belong to Spring.

Ruellius, however, describing the Basket Violet from Pliny’s accounts, uses more numerous and less abbreviated words:
> The Basket Violet, he says, is a gift of Autumn, while the other Violets are of Spring; it has a small leaf, not unlike the white Violet, is entirely without scent, and shaped like a basket. Coming forth only in Autumn, it is of such beautiful color that it challenges the very blue which it imitates; it shines forth in the meadows after the summer; they call it the Autumnal Violet.

It seems to be the same plant named *Pneumonanthe* <note>from the Greek for "lung-flower"</note> by Valerius Cordus, which he reports is called "lung flower" <note>original German: *Lungen blume*</note> in the German tongue.

Regarding its **quality**, this Violet is hot <note>In the humoral system of medicine, "hot" plants were thought to counteract "cold" illnesses</note>. Its properties are somewhat similar to Gentian, of which some consider it a species, though it is much weaker. It is considered effective by more recent authors against pestilential diseases, and for the bites and stings of venomous animals.

---

### ON THE BELLFLOWER. CHAPTER X.

There are two **BELLFLOWERS**: one larger garden variety, the other smaller and wild.

The **GARDEN VARIETY** produces many leaves from the roots close to the ground; these are oblong, narrow, and of a deep green color. Between them rise striated <note>ribbed or grooved</note> stems, two or three cubits <note>a cubit is roughly 18 inches; the plant is about 3 to 4.5 feet tall</note> or occasionally even taller. Around these stems are similar leaves, and flowers on the upper parts, with one flower on each stalk. They are larger and more open than those of the Rampion <note>original Latin: *Rapunculus*; a related bellflower with edible roots</note>, resembling little bells or handbells, and are usually of a color—

# ON THE BELLFLOWER.

...mostly blue, not rarely white, in which there are also some white stamens: the flowers are followed by small seed-capsules, quite similar in shape to the heads of the Rampion <note>original: *Rapunculus*; a plant with edible roots often grouped with bellflowers</note>, but full of small holes and perforated, in which the seed is very small: the roots are white, thin, and fibrous.

<margin>2</margin> **THE WILD VARIETY** <note>original: *Sylvestris*</note> is similar to the garden variety except for the first leaves, but it is overall smaller: the first leaves closest to the ground are broad and few, quite similar to the leaves of the *Black Violet* <note>original: *Viola nigra*</note>, yet not so large: the rest of the leaves are truly oblong and narrow; the flowers are similar and blue, often larger than the flowers of the Rampion: the roots are thin.

**BOTH** plants provide a juice similar to milk when the stems and leaves are cut <note>original: *vulneratis*, literally "wounded"</note>, though this is more abundant in the wild variety: the flowers of both, when not yet fully opened, are also five-angled, and when they open, they extend into five similar angles, just like the Wild Rapes; for which reason these are thought to be species of them, even though their roots are small and are not considered edible. The former is sown in gardens: the other grows everywhere in the Low Countries <note>original: *Belgio*</note> along the uncultivated margins of plowed fields and lands. They flower in the months of June, July, and August.

**COMMONLY** they are called Bellflowers <note>original: *Campanulas*</note>, and most people call the first one *Belvedere*: but to the Italians, *Belvedere* is the *Osyris* of the Greeks, with which the *Bellflower* has no resemblance. *Matthiolus* <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> considers this same garden bellflower to be *Phyteuma*: but to us, the *Bellflower* seems more similar to the *Iasione* <note>A plant mentioned by ancient authors, likely a species of Bindweed or a similar bell-shaped flower</note>. *Phyteuma* is truly a species of wild *Snapdragon* <note>original: *Antirrhini*</note>, about which we will speak below in its own place. For indeed *Theophrastus* <note>The ancient Greek "Father of Botany"</note> says that the *Iasione* <note>original Greek: *ἰασιώνης*</note> flower consists of a single petal <note>original: *folio*, here meaning the corolla or petal structure</note>, yet shows the appearance of many which are nonetheless not separated, and that the upper part of the flower is angular. Such certainly is the flower of this *Bellflower*, and similarly of all the Wild Rapes (except for the sixth kind), which nonetheless for that reason [they]

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of the Bellflower (Campanula) family. # ON THE BELLFLOWER.

These things cannot be confirmed with certainty, unless the *Iasione* <note>A plant name used by ancients, possibly a bindweed or a bellflower</note> of Theophrastus and Pliny is indeed the same plant. Pliny says that *Iasione* has an abundant milk <note>original: *lac*; referring to the milky sap or latex</note>, which only the Bellflowers especially pour out. Someone might perhaps doubt this and think the Bellflower is not the *Iasione*, because Pliny writes that the *Iasione* is a wild vegetable <note>original: *olus... syluestre*</note>, creeping on the ground, with a white flower. 

But if every edible herb is considered a vegetable, then the Bellflower is a vegetable, since there is no doubt it can be eaten. Furthermore, the roots frequently creep along the ground, and for that reason, a single stalk multiplies very quickly and becomes quite large. We have already written that the flower of the garden variety is not rarely white; and who would deny that such a flower could be found in the wild as well? Indeed, it is well known that both the colors of flowers and the shapes of the stalks themselves are often changed in some way according to the variety of locations and regions. 

These are the words of Theophrastus regarding the *Iasione* in his first book, <margin>Iasione.</margin> where he discusses the differences between flowers: "Some," he says, "grow with a single petal <note>original: *folij*; here meaning petal</note>, showing only the appearance of many, like the flower of the *Iasione*; for in this flower no petal is separated, nor does the lower part lack a container, while the upper part is angular." Pliny, in Book 22, Chapter 22, writes of the *Iasione* in this manner: "*Iasione* is considered a wild vegetable, creeping on the ground, with much milk; it bears a white flower; they call it *Concilium*."

<margin>3</margin>
**BESIDES** these two bellflowers, there is also a third, which is low and small. It has delicate stems, about nine inches long <term>dodrantales; a "dodrans" is a Roman measurement of about nine inches or three-quarters of a foot</term>, divided into many branches, mostly lying on the ground. The leaves around the stalks are small and gently notched <note>original: *crenata*</note> around the edges. The flowers are quite small, of a reddish-purple color <note>original: *puniceo purpurei*</note>, and beautiful. They are very much like open little bells or *campanulae*, with a white stamen in the center. These flowers open during the day; however, around sunset, they close up and contract into a five-angled shape, which they also resemble before they have opened, just like other Bellflowers. The roots are very thin. It grows in grain fields among the crops in fertile and productive soil. It blooms in June and July. It is called "Anonymous" <note>original: *Anonymos*; referring to a species not yet given a formal name by the author's sources</note> by...

<sig>C 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: our (nostris)</meta>

### ON THE FOXGLOVE. CHAPTER XI.

There are many species of Foxglove <note>original: Digitalis; the name is derived from the Latin "digitus" or finger, referring to the shape of the bloom</note>, differing not only in the color of the flowers but also in the shape of the leaves.

THE MOST COMMON *variety has a purple flower; it has long, broad leaves that are notched around the edges and pale green, somewhat similar to Mullein <note>original: Verbascum; a plant with thick, velvety leaves</note>, yet smaller and less fuzzy. The fresh stalk is round, and from the middle upward it is heavy with many flowers, arranged not alternately or in a circle, but from one side of the stalk, one above another—or as the Greeks say, "one upon another" <note>original: ἐπ' ἀλλήλων (ep' allēlōn)</note>—hanging downward. They are oblong in shape, hollow inside, and almost like thimbles <note>original: dactylothecis; literally "finger-cases"</note>; in color they are a reddish-purple, with certain white spots running through the interior. Round little pods follow these, containing a tiny seed. The roots consist of many thin fibers. It grows wild in many rocky and shaded places of Germany and Belgium, in mountain valleys, and near where iron and coal <note>original: Lithanthracon; literally "stone-coal"</note> mines are located. It is also transplanted into gardens.*

<margin>2</margin> THE SECOND *Foxglove is similar to the first in its leaves, though they are longer and broader; the flowers of this variety also match the first in shape, but are white in color.*

<margin>3</margin> THE THIRD *variety has narrower leaves that are veined, smooth, and a dark, deep green, yet lighter on the underside and somewhat serrated around the edges. This one has yellow flowers, in shape like the pre-*
<meta>vious ones</meta>

...almost similar to the others. These two types are very rare and are seen only in the gardens of a few people.

4 **THE FOURTH** variety of Foxglove puts forth leaves similar to the third, also dark green, turning blackish, and similarly whiter on the underside, but smaller and narrower; it has small flowers hanging downwards, but they are smaller and of a yellowish or pale color. This variety is quite common and well-known among the Belgians <note>referring to the people of the Low Countries</note>.

**THE GERMANS CALL** this plant in the common tongue *Fingherhut* and *Fingherkraut* <note>literally "Thimble" and "Finger-herb"</note>; the Belgians call it *Vingherhoet* <note>"Thimble"</note>; the French call it *Digitale*, or, as some say, *Gants nostre dame* <note>"Our Lady's Gloves"</note>. Fuchsius <note>Leonhart Fuchs, a famous 16th-century botanist</note> appropriately named it *Digitalis* in Latin.
<margin>wick-plant <note>original: θρυαλλίς (thryallis)</note></margin>
Some call it *Thryallis* and consider it a species of *Mullein* <note>original: Verbascum</note>, which is called *lychnitis* <note>original: λυχνῖτις, or "lamp-plant"</note>. But *Lychnitis* or *Thryallis* has fat, thick, and hairy leaves, suitable for wicks <note>term: ellychnia; wicks made from plant fibers</note> in lamps, which the Foxglove certainly does not have, as its leaves are not so thick or hairy that they could serve the purpose of a wick.

**CONCERNING THE PROPERTIES**, the leaves of the Foxglove, since they are bitter, possess a hot and dry quality, joined with some cleansing power; however, they are currently of no use, nor are they mixed into any medicines. The flower alone is pleasing and welcome for its beauty and form alone.

### ON THE ROSE CAMPION.
#### CAP. XII.

**THE ROSE CAMPION** <note>original: Lychnis coronaria; a popular garden flower with woolly foliage</note> bears oblong leaves, a thumb's width or a little wider, soft, woolly, and white-gray; they are indeed smaller than the leaves of *Mullein*, but whiter and more downy. The stalks are a cubit high <note>about 18 inches</note>, round, jointed, also white-gray and downy, and divided into several short branches. At the top of these are single flowers emerging from their cups; they are beautiful in appearance but odorless, larger than the flowers of *Dame’s Rocket* <note>original: Viola matronalis</note>, and of a red and, as it were, flaming color...

<meta>catchword: ...shining <note>original: fulgen-</note></meta>
