# ON THE ROSE CAMPION
### <note>original Latin: DE LYCHNIDE CORONARIA</note>

...shining, though sometimes and more rarely they are white or pale red. They usually consist of five petals, very rarely six and no more; unless the petals have multiplied (which happens sometimes to this plant as it does to other garden flowers). In the middle of the flowers are sharp and prickly points <note>referring to the pointed tips of the calyx or the stamens</note>. The roots underneath are thin. It is sown in gardens and is common in Italy, Germany, France, and Belgium. However, as Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> reports, it even grows wild and of its own accord among the people of Anagnia <note>Anagni, a town in central Italy</note>. The flowers bloom in the summer months of June, July, and August.

The Germans call this flower **Lady’s Rose** <note>original: *Frauwen roszlin*</note> / **Mary’s Rose** <note>original: *Mariē roszlin*</note> / **Morning Rose** <note>original: *Marghen roszlin*</note> and **Heavenly Rose** <note>original: *Himmel roszlin*</note>. In Latin, they are called *Ladies’ Rose*, *Mary’s Rose*, *Rose of the Sky*, and *Flower of Heaven*. The Belgians call it **Christ’s Eye** <note>original: *Christus ooghen*</note>; the French, *Pinks* <note>original: *Oeillets*</note> and *God’s Pinks* <note>original: *Oeillets Dieu*</note>. To Dioscorides, it is the *Garland Lychnis* <note>original Greek: λυχνὶς στεφανωματικὴ</note>, that is, the *Rose Campion* <note>original Latin: *Lychnis coronaria*</note> or the *Cultivated Lychnis*. Gaza <note>Theodorus Gaza, a translator of Aristotle and Theophrastus</note> translates *lychnis* as "little lamp" <note>original Latin: *lucernulam*</note>. It seems to be the same as *lychnitis* or *lygnitis* (spelled with a 'g'), which is a species of *Mullein* <note>original Latin: *Verbasci*</note>, also known as *thryallis* <note>original Greek: θρυαλλίς</note>. Indeed, it has soft, hairy leaves suited for making lamp wicks <note>term: ellychnia; the material used in oil lamps</note>, a quality which Dioscorides testifies belongs to the *Lychnitis*. It likewise has a mostly red flower, which Nicander <note>Ancient Greek poet and physician</note> suggests is the color of the *thryallis*, calling it red in his *Theriaca*:

> As many as the *Lychnis* below, and the reddish *thryallis*. <note>original Greek: ὅσσα τε λυχνὶς ἔνερθεν, ἐρευθήεις τε θρυαλλίς</note>

In this verse, the wild *Lychnis* should be understood, as Nicander's own commentator advises, lest anyone think from this that the *Rose Campion* <note>original: *Lychnida coronaria*</note> differs from the *thryallis*. Conrad Gesner <note>A Swiss naturalist known as the father of modern bibliography and zoology</note> believes this plant is called the **Flower of Jove** <note>original Latin: *Iouis florem*</note> or, in Greek, *Dios anthos* <note>original Greek: διὸς ἄνθον</note>. But Theophrastus <note>The "Father of Botany" and student of Aristotle</note> makes a 
<margin>Flower of Jove, book 6.</margin>
distinction between the *Lychnis* and the *Flower of Jove*. He says that the *Lychnis* and the *Flower of Jove* are summer plants. Similarly, Pliny (Book 21, Chapter 11) cites these words of Theophrastus, writing that the summer plants follow them—namely the *Lychnis* and the *Flower of Jove*. The *Flower of Jove* is a summer flower...

It specifically explains the plant's historical use as a source for lamp wicks due to its woolly foliage.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the nomenclature and classical identification of the Rose Campion (Lychnis coronaria) and begins the description of the Wild Lychnis (likely Silene or a similar genus). It continues the discussion of physical characteristics and medicinal uses derived from ancient authorities like Galen and Dioscorides.</meta>

...it is a summer-blooming plant, used for garlands, and lacks any scent; it has woody roots, according to Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> in his sixth book. There are also those who believe this *Lychnis* is the plant Pliny <note>a Roman naturalist and author</note> calls the **Greek Rose** <margin>Greek Rose</margin>. He reports that it grows only in damp places and never has more than five petals, having the size of a violet and no scent. Furthermore, many other names for the *Lychnis* are found, though they are categorized as false or substituted names. For it is also called *athanatos* <note>Greek: ἀθάνατος</note>, which means "immortal," because it retains its beauty for a long time. Likewise, it is called *ekkyklonion* <note>Greek: ἐκκυκλώνιον</note>, *ballorion* <note>Greek: βαλλώριον</note> or **Sweetmeat** <note>original Latin: *Bellaria*</note>, *geranopodion* <note>Greek: γερανόποδιον</note>, which means "crane's foot"; and *korymbion* <note>Greek: κορύμβιον</note>. Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel, a French physician and botanist</note> judges this to be the **Corymbe** <margin>Corymbe</margin>, which Columella <note>a Roman writer on agriculture</note> mentions in his tenth book with this six-foot verse:
*Now let the Corymbe come, though it is unfriendly to the eyes.*
For when it is woven into crowns, he says, it shines with a fiery appearance and dulls the sharpness of the eyes. This *Lychnis* is also called *taurion* <note>Greek: ταύριον, likely meaning "bull-like"</note>. However, the same Ruellius says there is another plant commonly called **Tora** <margin>Tora</margin> or *Taura*, which has a blue flower, a round leaf, a hairy root like a Polypody fern, and the appearance of a black worm; it is a poisonous herb. It is also named *skeptron* <note>Greek: σκῆπτρον, meaning "scepter"</note>, as well as *maloion* <note>Greek: μαλόϊον</note>, and in Latin, **Genicularis** <note>meaning "jointed," referring to the stem</note>.

As for its **properties**, Galen <note>an influential Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> says the seed of the Rose Campion is hot and dry in the second degree. When drunk with wine, it helps those bitten by a scorpion, according to Dioscorides <note>a Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*</note>.

## ON THE WILD LYCHNIS.
### CHAPTER 14.

The **Wild Lychnis** has jointed stems and soft leaves, much like the Rose Campion; however, it is less grey or woolly. Its leaves are also narrower, and its stems are more tender, producing more "wings" <note>side branches or shoots</note>. The flowers are also smaller, white or reddish in color; however, the cups <note>the calyxes holding the petals</note> are larger, containing a small, round, somewhat blackish seed. The roots are long and sometimes the thickness of a finger. Near the mea[dows]...

# **WILD LYCHNIS** <note>original Latin: *Lychnis sylvestris*</note>

->[Detailed woodcut illustration of the Wild Lychnis plant]<-

<note>This illustration depicts the plant known in the Renaissance as the Wild Lychnis. It shows a robust specimen with several leafy stems rising from a thick, horizontal rootstock. The leaves are lanceolate (spear-shaped) and grow in opposite pairs along the stem. At the top, the plant bears several flowers and distinctive ribbed, tubular buds. In the 16th century, these illustrations were vital for physicians and apothecaries to correctly identify plants in the wild for medicinal use.</note>

<summary>This page consists of a full-page botanical woodcut of the Wild Lychnis, providing a visual reference for identification alongside its Latin name.</summary>

<keywords>Wild Lychnis, Lychnis sylvestris, botanical illustration, woodcut, herbal, 16th-century botany, medicinal plants</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Lychnis family from page 46, specifically moving from the general introduction and illustrations to the specific varieties and medicinal properties of the "Wild" version.</meta>

*...the margins of paths and fields, and it grows elsewhere of its own accord;* it is also transplanted into gardens, where sometimes the small petals of the flowers multiply. *It blooms for the greater part of the summer, and even into autumn.*

OUR people call it Jenettekens <note>A common Middle Dutch/Flemish name for small pinks or carnations, often associated with St. John’s Day</note>; the Germans also call it Mary’s little rose <note>original: *Marien roszlin*</note> and the wild Mary’s little rose <note>original: *wilde Marien roszlin*</note>; likewise *Lijdweyck* and *widerstosz*; the French call them wild carnations <note>original: *Oeillets sauuages*</note>; the Greeks call it wild lychnis <note>original: λυχνὶς ἀγρία / *lychnis agria*</note>; and in Latin it is called *Lychnis sylvestris*.

HOWEVER, there are also other plants called and named "Wild Lychnis" that are not at all like this one. For the Wild Lychnis is also called *Antirchinon* <note>original: ἀντίρρινον, meaning "snapdragon-like"</note> according to Dioscorides <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist from the 1st century AD</note>; and it is similarly *called Lion’s Leaf <note>original: *Leontopetalon*</note> according to the witness of Apuleius.*

*TRULY there are also certain other names for this Lychnis, such as: Goat’s Knee <note>original: τραγόνατον / *tragonaton*</note>, the "sterile" plant <note>original: ἀτόκιον / *atokion*</note>, Hawk’s foot <note>original: ἱερακοπόδιον / *hierakopodion*</note>, the torch <note>original: λαμπάς / *lampas*</note>, and among the Romans, "wild endive" <note>original: *Intybum agreste*</note>, names which are found among the spurious and rejected ones.* Most of the more recent writers call this *Saponaria* <note>Soapwort, so called because its crushed leaves produce a lather</note>, though there is yet another *Saponaria* which we will discuss *below.* To some it also seems to be *Ocimastrum* <note>A plant resembling wild basil</note>; but *okimoeides* <note>original: ὠκιμοειδές</note> or *ocimastrum* has leaves similar to Basil <note>original: *Ocimum*</note>, and has short branches only nine inches <note><term>dodrantales</term>: a Roman unit of length approximately 9 inches</note> long, and a thin, insignificant root. This Lychnis, however, puts forth stems longer than a cubit <note><term>cubit</term>: a measure of about 18 inches</note>, with oblong leaves not at all like Basil, and long, thick roots—which show a clear contradiction and an evident difference between this plant and *Ocimastrum*.

*FURTHERMORE, the seed of the Wild Lychnis, drunk in the weight of two drachms <note><term>drachm</term>: a Greek weight of about 3.5 grams</note>, purges bile through the bowels and is suitable for those struck by a scorpion. They even say that scorpions become sluggish and are rendered ineffective at harming when this herb is placed near them; Dioscorides is the authority for this claim.*

THE COCKLE has oblong, narrow, pointed leaves, with a stalk taller than a cubit, round, and both are hairy...

# CORN COCKLE <note>original: *Nigellastrum*. In modern botany, this is known as *Agrostemma githago*. The name "Nigellastrum" suggests a resemblance to *Nigella* (Fennel Flower), though they are distinct species.</note>

->[A detailed botanical woodcut of the Corn Cockle plant. A single, sturdy vertical stem grows from a cluster of slender, spreading roots. The stem is adorned with long, narrow, pointed leaves that have slightly bristly edges; some leaves point upward while those at the base of the plant curve toward the ground. At the very top of the stem sits a large, open flower with five broad, rounded petals. Next to the flower is a long, pointed bud, prepared to bloom.]<-

<summary>This page features a botanical woodcut of the Corn Cockle (Nigellastrum), a tall flowering herb historically associated with the Lychnis family and frequently found growing in grain fields.</summary>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of Corn Cockle (Nigellastrum) from the previous page, detailing its names across different languages and its medicinal uses. It then introduces the Cornflower (Cyanus). The catchword "cantia" suggests the next page will begin with a word like "cantianis" or similar, continuing the description of the Cornflower.</meta>

# OF CORN COCKLE <note>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on *Nigellastrum*, known today as *Agrostemma githago*.</note>

...and hairy; with a dark purple flower leaning toward crimson, similar to the **wild Lychnis** <note>original: *Lychnidis fatuae*</note> but smaller; with black seed, bitter and somewhat rough, which is contained in oblong heads or calyxes like those of the wild Lychnis; with a slender root. It grows everywhere in grain fields <note>original: *segetibus*</note>; among which it flowers in May and June.

**GERMANS** call it *raden*, *ratten*, *kornrosz*, or *kornnegele*; **Belgians** call it *Corenroosen* and *negelbloemen*; and the **Italians** call it *githone* or *gitthone*. From this, many have been led by the similarity of the name to believe it is the true and legitimate **Gith** or **Melanthium** <note>Fennel Flower or *Nigella sativa*</note>. It is well known that these people have wandered from the truth and need not be refuted at length. Indeed, it differs from the true Melanthium not only in its leaves but also in other properties. It is nevertheless called *Gith* or *Melanthium* because of the blackness of its seeds, though not simply by that name, but with some addition to distinguish it from the legitimate Melanthium. Indeed, **Hippocrates** <note>The famous Greek "Father of Medicine," c. 460–370 BCE.</note> called it "Melanthium from the wheat" <note>original Greek: *μελάνθιον ἐκ τῶν πυρῶν*</note>. **Octavius Horatianus** <note>A 4th-century Roman medical writer.</note> names it "Gith which is born in the grain field"; and for the same reason, the learned men of this age call it **Nigellastrum**, **Githago**, and **False Melanthium** <note>original: *pseudomelanthium*</note>. **Ruellius** <note>Jean Ruel, a French physician and botanist, 1474–1537.</note> reports that the French call it *nielle* and the "micaucalum flower," and he judges it to be the "leafy flower" <note>original Greek: *ἄνθεμον φυλλῶδες*</note> of **Theophrastus**. However, the *Anthemon* of Theophrastus is the *Anthemis* <note>Chamomile</note> found in **Dioscorides**, and the "leafy flower" or leafy species of *Anthemis* has little in common with the Corn Cockle.

**FURTHERMORE**, the seed of the Corn Cockle is hot and dry in the second degree, and quite intensely so. Placed in a **pessary** <note>original: *pesso*; a medicinal plug used for gynecological treatment.</note> with honey, it provokes menstruation, as Hippocrates testifies in his book *On the Diseases of Women*. Octavius Horatianus suggests that the seed, roasted and ground, should be given in a drink to treat **jaundice** <note>original: *auriginem*</note>.

# CORNFLOWER: THE FLOWER OF THE GRAINS <note>original Latin: *CYANVS FLOS FRVMENTORVM*</note>

<note>This page is a dedicated botanical plate featuring a detailed woodcut illustration of the plant commonly known today as the Cornflower or Bachelor's Button (*Centaurea cyanus*).</note>

The woodcut depicts the entire plant in fine detail, starting from a fibrous root system at the base. The lower leaves are shown as deeply lobed, while the leaves higher up the stem are narrow and lance-shaped. The plant branches toward the top, ending in several flower heads at various stages of development. These include fully open composite flowers with fringed outer petals and several tightly closed buds. The base of the flower heads (the involucres) are rendered with a distinct scaly or fringed texture, a key identifying feature of this genus.

### ON THE CORNFLOWER.

...constricted; the heads at the top of the small stems are round, top-shaped, and narrowing at the tip, composed of many small scales layered upon one another like a small cone; from which the flowers burst forth, arranged in a beautiful circular series of many fringe-like petals, sky-blue in color; the seed is small, oblong in shape, smooth, shining, and slightly bearded at the top, similar to the seed of Greater Centaury <note>original: *Centauri maioris*</note>, but smaller and whiter, which is found within the heads or cups themselves, nestled among the soft hairs that grow there: the roots are slender, hairy with certain fibers. It grows in grain fields, together with wheat, rye, barley, and other crops: it is also sown in gardens, where by the artifice of cultivation <note>original: *mangonio*; referring to the techniques used by gardeners to produce variety</note> it often changes color—sometimes whitish, sometimes purple, or at other times a pale red, or even leaning toward a bright crimson. It begins to bloom in May along with the crops, and then continues to flower through June and July.

The ancients gave the name **Cyanus** <note>from the Greek *kyanos*, meaning dark blue</note> to the flower because of its deep blue color. Most more recent authors, following the common German name, call it the "flower of the grains." For the Germans call it **Kornblumen** and **blauw Kornblumen**: and the Belgians similarly call it **Corenbloemen**. The French say **Blaueole**, **Aubifoin**, or **bluet**. By the Italians it is named **fior Campese** <note>field flower</note>, and **battisecula** or **baptisecula**: as if it were **Blaptisecula** <note>original: *Blaptisecula*; a folk etymology combining the Greek *blaptein* (to hurt) and the Latin *secula* (sickle)</note>, because it hinders those reaping and cutting, as the sickle is blunted when it strikes it.

But besides this one, there is another, the Greater Cornflower <note>likely *Centaurea montana*</note>, with oblong leaves, wider than a thumb, soft, woolly, and whitish, yet smaller and darker than the leaves of Mullein <note>original: *Verbasci*</note>: with stems nine inches long <note>original: *dodrantalibus*</note>: the heads are also scaly like the other variety: the flowers are likewise similar and blue, but tending somewhat toward purple, and larger: with roots that cling with thick fibers; it is of a durable nature, sprouting again every year. According to Tragus <note>The Latinized name of Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), a pioneering German botanist</note>, it is found in woods and mountainous places: [it...]

# THE GREATER CORNFLOWER.
<note>original: *Cyanus Maior*; currently identified as the Mountain Cornflower or Perennial Cornflower (*Centaurea montana*).</note>

[This page consists of a full-page botanical woodcut illustration.]

This illustration depicts the **Greater Cornflower** in its entirety, providing a clear reference for identification. The plant is shown with a thick, fibrous root system at its base, from which several sturdy, upright stems emerge. The leaves are large and lance-shaped, appearing to clasp or run down the length of the stem. 

At the top of these stems are the flower heads:
*   Three flowers are shown in full bloom, displaying the radiating, fringe-like petals that distinguish this genus. 
*   Two flower heads are depicted as unopened buds, characterized by their "scaly" appearance, where small, overlapping bracts form a shape resembling a tiny pinecone. 

This woodcut is typical of early modern herbals, emphasizing the physical structure of the plant—from root to blossom—to assist the reader in distinguishing this "Greater" variety from its smaller, wilder relatives found in grain fields.

# ON THE ROYAL FLOWER.
<note>original: *DE FLORE REGIO*</note>

...grown in our gardens. It blooms in summer. The Germans call it **Wald korn-blumen** <note>literally "forest corn-flowers"</note>.

The **GREATER CORNFLOWER** <note>original: *CYANVS maior*</note> can be appropriately named. It appears to some to be a species of *Thryallis Verbasci* <note>a plant mentioned by ancient authors, possibly a type of Mullein</note>, but *Thryallis*, as we have written above, has a red flower.

**FURTHERMORE**, the medicinal properties of these flowers are not yet sufficiently understood, as they have no established use in medicine. However, it seems the former *Cyanus* <note>the common cornflower</note>, or grain flower, possesses some cooling power, and for this reason may be beneficial for inflammations of the eyes; a quality attributed to it by many.

### ON THE ROYAL FLOWER. CHAPTER XVI.
<note>The "Royal Flower" described here is the Larkspur (*Consolida regalis* or *Delphinium*), recognizable by its distinctive "spur."</note>

The **ROYAL** flower produces stems that grow higher than a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note>, round, and divided into many small branches; around which are thin leaves, divided by multiple segments, larger than the leaves of **Southernwood** <note>original: *Abrotonum*</note>, and of a blackish-green color. At the upper parts of these stems are beautiful flowers, arranged in rows and spikes. On the back of these flowers is a small part like a little horn, narrow and pointed, resembling the shape of the <term>spur</term> <note>original: *calcar*</note> used by the ancients. Their color is either white, purple, or blue. These are followed by oblong, round, blackish pods, containing angular and black seeds. It is both cultivated in gardens and sown every year.

<margin>2</margin>
A wild variety also **GROWS** in grain fields in richer soil. This wild type is similar to the cultivated one in its leaves, flowers, pods, and seeds, but it is shorter; it also has fewer flowers on each branch, and these are only blue in color.

**BOTH** varieties bloom in the summer months; the garden variety lasts a very long time.

It is **CALLED** by the Germans **Ritter sporn** <note>knight's spur</note>; by our people **Ridder sporen** <note>Dutch: knight's spurs</note>, which means the knight's spur; in Italian **Sperone de caualliere** <note>knight's spur</note>; and by the French **pied d’alouette**, which means...

# THE ROYAL FLOWER
<note>original: *FLOS REGIVS*</note>

...grown in our gardens. It blooms in summer. The Germans call it **Forest corn-flowers** <note>original: *Wald korn-blumen*</note>.

The **GREATER CORNFLOWER** <note>original: *CYANVS maior*</note> can be appropriately named. It appears to some to be a species of **Mullein Thryallis** <note>original: *Thryallis Verbasci*; a plant mentioned by ancient authors, possibly a type of Mullein</note>, but the **Thryallis**, as we have written above, has a red flower.

**FURTHERMORE**, the medicinal properties of these flowers are not yet sufficiently understood, as they have no established use in medicine. However, it seems the former **Cornflower** <note>original: *Cyanus*</note>, or grain flower, possesses some cooling power, and for this reason may be beneficial for inflammations of the eyes; a quality attributed to it by many.

### ON THE ROYAL FLOWER. CHAPTER XVI.
<note>The "Royal Flower" described here is the Larkspur (*Consolida regalis* or *Delphinium*), easily identified by the "spur" on its flower mentioned in the text.</note>

->[Illustration: A detailed woodcut of a Larkspur plant showing its upright stem, finely divided feathery leaves, and flowers characterized by a distinct rear spur. Small seed pods and a taproot system are also visible.]<-

The **ROYAL** flower produces stems that grow higher than a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note>, round, and divided into many small branches; around which are thin leaves, divided by multiple segments, larger than the leaves of **Southernwood** <note>original: *Abrotonum*</note>, and of a blackish-green color. At the upper parts of these stems are beautiful flowers, arranged in rows and spikes. On the back of these flowers is a small part like a little horn, narrow and pointed, resembling the shape of the <term>spur</term> <note>original: *calcar*</note> used by the ancients. Their color is either white, purple, or blue. These are followed by oblong, round, blackish pods, containing small, black, and rough seeds. The root is single, slender, and woody, with a few small fibers attached.

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 16, "On the Royal Flower," focusing on the naming and historical identification of the Larkspur (Consolida regalis) with the ancient Delphinium described by Dioscorides.</meta>

### ON THE ROYAL FLOWER.
<sig>D 4</sig>

**Lark's Foot** <note>original: *Pes Alaudæ*</note>: in Latin *knight’s flower* <note>original: *flos equestris*</note>, and *royal flower*: by some called **Royal Consolida** <note>original: *Consolida Regalis*</note>, who include this plant among those called "consolidas" <note>a group of healing herbs believed to 'consolidate' or knit wounds together; modern *Consolida* is the genus for Larkspur</note>. **Valerius Cordus** <note>a German physician and botanist known for his pharmacopoeia</note> names it *calcatrippa*; but **Jean Ruel** <note>original: *Ioannes Ruellius*, a French physician</note> considers *calcatrepa* or *chaucetrepa* <note>names referring to the 'caltrop' or spur shape of the flower</note> to be among the number of thorny plants. It also seems to be the **Delphinium**, the description of which occurs in **Dioscorides**, Book 3; with which it can agree, if the term "dolphin-like" <note>Greek: τὸ δελφινοειδὲς</note> is applied not to the leaves, but to the flowers. I remember once hearing that this reading was found in certain ancient manuscripts from **Gerard van Veltwyck**, the ambassador of the **Emperor Charles V** to the Emperor of the Turks <note>Sultan Suleiman the Magnificent</note>, who reported that he had seen a manuscript in Constantinople which held that the *Delphinium* had flowers, not leaves, in the likeness of dolphins.

If this reading is true, it clearly demonstrates that the **Royal Flower** is the **Delphinium**. For indeed the flowers, and especially before they are fully open, exhibit a certain appearance and likeness to those dolphins which ancient paintings and the ancestral insignia <note>coats of arms</note> of several noble families express with a curved and upturned figure; by which image the celestial **Dolphin** <note>the constellation Delphinus</note> is also represented. Nor does it matter if the chapter on *Delphinium* is considered spurious or illegitimate <note>i.e., not originally written by Dioscorides</note>; for even if it belongs to someone else and not Dioscorides, it is nonetheless the work of some ancient author, from whom it was added to the books of Dioscorides.

<margin>Delphinium.</margin>
The **DELPHINIUM** is described in these words: <note>The following is a translation of the Greek text provided in the source</note> *Delphinium sends up shoots of two spans <note>about 18 inches</note> or even larger from a single root, around which are split, thin, elongated leaves, dolphin-like, from which it was also named; the flower is similar to a white-violet <note>original: *leukoion*</note>, turning purple. Seed is in pods, resembling millet.* **Then, further down, there is a second Delphinium:** *Another Delphinium, and this too is similar to the one before it, but much more slender in its leaves and little shoots.* The meaning of these words is as follows: *Delphinium sends out shoots of two spans, or larger, from one root: around which are divided, thin, long leaves, Dolphin-like*...

<meta>catchword: species:</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the botanical nomenclature of the Larkspur, identifying it as the 'Delphinium' of antiquity based on the dolphin-like shape of its flowers and historical manuscripts from Constantinople.</summary>
<keywords>Larkspur, Delphinium, Consolida Regalis, Dioscorides, Valerius Cordus, Gerard van Veltwyck, heraldry, botanical history, ancient manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Larkspur (Consolida regalis), specifically debating its identification with the "Delphinium" of Dioscorides and the "wild Cumin" described by ancient authors.</meta>

# ON THE ROYAL FLOWER.
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

in appearance: from which it also received its name: a purple flower similar to the *Leucoio* <note>likely the Wallflower or Stock, *Matthiola incana*</note>: the seed in pods is very close to millet. A second *Delphinium*, also itself similar to the one above, but much more slender in its leaves and branches.

**OF THESE** two descriptions, the first, with the word *dolphin-like* <note>original Greek: δελφινοειδῆ</note> applied to the flower, should be changed and read in this manner: *Delphinium* sends out shoots from a single root of nine inches <note>original: *duorum dodrantum*, measuring three-quarters of a foot</note>, or larger: around which are split, thin, oblong leaves: a flower similar to the *Leucoio*, purple, in the shape of a *Dolphin*; from which it also received its name: the seed in pods is very close to millet.

**AND** with the description standing in this way, the cultivated Royal Flower will be similar to the first *Delphinium*: the wild one indeed to the second. There are truly certain other names for the *Delphinium*, but most of them are considered illegitimate <note>original: *nothis*, meaning "spurious" or "bastard" names</note>, such as *diachysis*, *diachytos*, *paralysis*, *kamaros*, *hyacinth*, *nerion*, *nereiaidion*, *sosandron*, *kronion* <note>these are various Greek names associated with plants like oleander or hyacinth in ancient herbals</note>: and among the Latins, *Bucinus* or *Bucinum*, and *Bucinus minor* <note>literally "trumpet" or "small trumpet," referring to the flower's spur</note>.

**BUT** to return to the Royal Flower, there are those who would have it be the second kind of *wild Cumin* of **Dioscorides**, which is the opinion of **Mattioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note>, and very much in harmony with the truth. For indeed, it has as much similarity with his description as anything can have. For it has thin leaves, similar enough to cultivated cumin, and from each individual flower it raises little horns on high: then it brings forth a seed like that of *Melanthium* <note>also known as Fennel-flower or *Nigella*</note>: <margin>The second kind of wild Cumin.</margin> such as **Dioscorides** recorded the second wild *Cumin* to be; describing it in these words: *There is also another kind of wild cumin, similar to the cultivated one, producing from each flower little horns held high, in which is a seed like melanthium.* <note>original Greek: ἔστι δὲ ἕτερον γένος ἀγρίου κυμίνου ἐμφερὲς τῷ ἡμέρῳ, ἐξ ἑκάστου δ᾽ ἄνθους ἀφίησι κεράτια μετέωρα, ἐν οἷς ἐστι σπέρμα ὅμοιον μελανθίῳ</note>

<summary>This page discusses the botanical classification of the Larkspur, arguing that it matches ancient descriptions of Delphinium and wild Cumin when the flower's shape is properly interpreted as dolphin-like or horned.</summary>
<keywords>Delphinium, Larkspur, Dioscorides, Mattioli, botanical nomenclature, wild Cumin, Melanthium, Royal Flower, plant morphology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion regarding the identification of "wild cumin" and "Delphinium" before transitioning into a new chapter on the Marigold (Calendula). **AND** it is possible that it is the same as the second kind of wild cumin, and likewise the Delphinium; especially given the additional description of the Delphinium, which may have been transferred from some other author into the manuscript of **Dioscorides**. Frequently, the same plants are called by different names by various writers.

**MOREOVER**, the seed of the second wild cumin, when drunk, is an excellent remedy for snake bites: it also helps those who suffer from a dripping of urine <term>strangury: a condition where urine is passed drop by drop with pain</term>, those with stones, and those who urinate blood clots: but for these, boiled celery seed must be drunk afterward, according to the author **Dioscorides**. Indeed, the seed of the Delphinium drunk in wine helps those struck by a scorpion like no other remedy. They say that scorpions are paralyzed <note>original: "resolui"</note> when the herb is placed near them, and become sluggish and torpid; but when it is removed, they recover themselves.

## ON THE MARIGOLD OR CALTHA.
### CHAPTER XVII.

The leaves of the **Marigold** <note>original: "Calendula," likely the Pot Marigold, *Calendula officinalis*</note> are pale green, oblong, somewhat broad, soft, and slightly downy: the stalks are leafy, striated <note>marked with linear ridges</note>, with a spongy pith inside, divided into many "wings" or branches: the flowers at the top are beautiful, round, and many-petaled <note>original: "multiplices"</note>, somewhat fragrant with a certain heavy scent, the color of pale saffron or the purest gold, in the center of which are many short, pressed-together yellow or blackish stamens; the seed from each flower is numerous and curved: the roots are fibrous. It grows in gardens and cultivated places: also of its own accord around Montpellier <note>a city in southern France famous for its botanical gardens</note>, according to the testimony of **Gesner** <note>Conrad Gesner, a prolific 16th-century Swiss naturalist</note>. It blooms from the month of April or May even into winter: in mild weather, its flower even bursts forth then.

A more recent age calls it the **Marigold** <note>original: "Calendulam"</note>; because it is seen to bloom almost every month on the **Calends** <note>the first day of the Roman month</note>. The Germans call them **Marigolds** <note>original: "Ringelblumen"</note>: the Belgians **Gold-flowers** <note>original: "Goudbloemen"</note>, from the color of the flower which rivals gold. The French call it **Soucie** and **Soulšie**. In Tuscany, says...

## ON THE MARIGOLD OR CALTHA.
### CHAPTER XVII.

The leaves of the **Marigold** <note>original: "Calendula," likely the Pot Marigold, *Calendula officinalis*</note> <note>The author uses "Caltha" here as a synonym for Marigold, though in modern botany *Caltha* refers to the Marsh Marigold.</note> are pale green, oblong, somewhat broad, soft, and slightly downy: the stalks are leafy, striated <term>striated: marked with parallel ridges or grooves</term>, with a spongy pith inside, divided into many "wings" or branches: the flowers at the top are beautiful, round, and many-petaled <note>original: "multiplices," referring to the "double" appearance of the flower head</note>, somewhat fragrant with a certain heavy scent, the color of pale saffron or the purest gold, in the center of which are many short, pressed-together yellow or blackish stamens; the seed from each flower is curved like a little hook or sickle.

<summary>This page introduces the Marigold (Calendula), providing a high-quality botanical illustration and beginning a formal description of its physical characteristics, including its ribbed stems and golden, many-petaled flowers.</summary>

<keywords>Marigold, Calendula, Caltha, botanical illustration, flower morphology, medicinal herbs, Renaissance botany</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Marigold (Calendula/Caltha) from Chapter XVII on page 58. # ON THE MARIGOLD OR CALTHA.

Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> calls it *Fior rancio* <note>Italian: "orange flower"</note>. There are those who call it the "Sun-follower" <note>original: "Solsequium"</note> because the flower closes toward evening; however, it is not the "Sun-turner" <note>original: "Heliotropium"</note>, much less the "Scorpion-tail" <note>original: "Scorpioides"</note>, as many have rashly believed. Rather, it is the *Caltha* of the ancients. For Virgil and Columella show that the *Caltha* is a flower of a yellow or golden color. Indeed, Virgil, in the second eclogue of his *Georgics* <note>The author likely means the *Eclogues*, though he cites the *Georgics*</note>, writes:

> "He paints the soft violets with yellow Marigold." <note>original: "*Mollia luteola pingit Vacinia Caltha*"</note>

And Columella in his tenth book on gardens, with these words:

> "The white stocks and the yellow eyes of the Marigold." <note>original: "*Candida Leucoia & flauentia lumina Calthæ*"</note>

Pliny (Book 21, Chapter 6) also says that the *Caltha* is heavy-scented. Such is the Marigold (*Calendula*) in its entirety, which, as we have said, shines with a certain heavy fragrance and the yellow color of gold.

FURTHERMORE, in its medicinal faculty, the flower of the Marigold is hot, nearly in the second degree <term>second degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating a moderate intensity of a "quality" (heat, cold, moisture, or dryness) on a scale of one to four</term>; especially when it is desiccated and dried. It is believed to strengthen the heart, resist poisons, and be effective against pestilential fevers, in whatever way it is consumed. Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a primary source for Renaissance botany</note> left it written that when drunk in wine it provokes the menses, and used as a fumigant <note>smoke or vapors</note> it helps expel the afterbirth.

HOWEVER, the leaves of the herb are hotter, for they have a certain burning pungency, though it does not exert itself immediately because of the moisture joined to it; for this reason, when eaten as a potherb, they move and soften the bowels. Fuchs writes that the juice soothes toothaches if the mouth is rinsed with it. Others apply a powder of the leaves with cotton <note>original: "xylino"</note> to the teeth for the same purpose. There are also those who mix the herb into salads.

<margin>Alpine Marigold.</margin>
There is truly another *Caltha* besides this one, named the *Alpine Marigold* <note>original: "Caltha Alpina"</note> by Gesner <note>Conrad Gesner (1516–1565), a Swiss naturalist</note>; it has leaves like a plantain, a sharp and aromatic root, and the golden flower of a Marigold. It grows abundantly in the high meadows of the Alps.

# ON THE AFRICAN FLOWER.

<meta>Red underlining on lines 21-26 and line 32. Catchword: runianum</meta>

->CHAPTER XVIII.<-

*The flower nicknamed "African" also seems to be counted among the Marigolds* <note>original: "Calthas"</note>. *There are two species of this plant: one smaller and low-growing, with small branches about a cubit* <term>cubit: an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</term> *long that mostly spread across the ground; the other is larger, tall and upright, rising to three cubits or more.*

The LEAVES on both species are numerous along the grooved stems; they are oblong and composed of many individual leaflets that are notched around the edges, *appearing very similar to the leaves of Tansy. When these are held up against the Sun or a light, they are seen to be perforated like a sieve. The flowers at the tips of the branch-shoots emerge from long calyxes* <term>calyx: the green outer part of a flower bud that supports the petals</term>; *they are large, round, and crowded with eight or ten leaflets. These are yellow on the underside, but on the upper part they are a glowing reddish-yellow, imitating a dark red velvety silk* <note>original: "villosum holosericum"</note>. *Between these petals, yellow stamens emerge in the middle. The seed is black, oblong, and thin in shape; in place of a taproot, it has fibers. Both species, including both the leaves and the flowers, are heavily scented. The flowers of both, though more frequently the larger kind, are often "doubled"* <note>Referring to "double-flowered" varieties with extra layers of petals</note>; *the leaflets of these are usually yellow on both sides.* Among the Belgians, Germans, and elsewhere, it is cultivated in gardens and sown every year. In Africa, it grows wild in many places, which is where it was first brought into these regions: specifically, when the Lord Charles V, Emperor of the Romans, gained his famous victory at Tunis <note>Charles V's conquest of Tunis in 1535. This historical event often serves as the "origin story" for this plant's arrival in Europe</note>. From this event, the flower received the nickname "African" or "Tunis flower." *The smaller variety produces flowers in June and then through the rest of the summer; the larger one finally puts forth its flowers in the month of August.*

The people of Brabant and Flanders commonly call them **Tunis flowers** <note>original: "Thunis bloemen"</note>. The Germans call them **Indian flowers** <note>original: "Indianisch blumen"</note> and **Indian pinks** <note>original: "Indianisch negelin"</note>, that is, *the Indian flower or clove-pink*. From this, the French also call them **Indian pinks** <note>original: "Oeillets d'Inde"</note>. Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus (1515–1544), a German physician and botanist</note> calls it *Peruvian Tansy* <note>original: "Tanacetum Perunianum"</note>...

# ON THE AFRICAN FLOWER.

<meta>catchword: by moths (tineis)</meta>

<margin>The Petilius flower.</margin>
*...[some] call it **ruvianum**: evidently from its resemblance to tansy and the American province of Peru, from which the Germans believe it was first brought into Europe. Gesner calls it the African Marigold* <note>original: "Caltham Aphricanam"</note> *and says that in the Punic language it is called **Pedua**. There are those who wish it to be the **Petilius** flower of Pliny. However, the Petilius is an autumnal flower, growing around briars, valued only for its color, which is that of a Wild Rose: it has five small leaves, a small variegated calyx* <term>calyx: the green outer part of a flower bud that supports the petals</term> *enclosing yellow seed. A marvelous thing in that flower is that the top is bent, and the leaves do not grow unless it is twisted back. Pliny, Book 21, Chapter 8.*

*ANDREAS Lacuna suspected this to be the herb **Othonna**, though this flower does not agree in every detail with that description: unless perhaps it grows more ruggedly in Africa than in Europe, with fewer leaves that are more similar to Rocket* <note>original: "Erucæ", likely referring to Eruca vesicaria</note> *and more clearly perforated. For in this region, it grows large and multi-leaved, with leaves more like Tansy than Rocket; these are not truly perforated, but only appear so when held up to the light.*

*AND indeed it happens not infrequently that plants transplanted from a burning, hot, and dry region and a sterile or uncultivated and sunny soil into a more humid and cultivated one, where the climate is milder, grow luxuriantly. For the change of soil and sky has great influence on the transformation of plants; especially when the clever manipulation of cultivation* <note>original: "culturæ mangonio," implying the "doctoring" or skillful art of gardening</note> *is added. However, we leave it to be considered whether such power could have almost entirely transformed this flower.*

<margin>Othonna.</margin>
*NOW, the **Othonna** is a certain herb of the Troglodytes* <note>a name used by ancient Greeks for various cave-dwelling peoples, here referring to those near the Red Sea</note>, *growing in that stretch of Arabia that faces toward Egypt: Dioscorides says,* 
> *it has leaves like Rocket, full of holes as if moth-eaten, brittle, and with few leaves. It bears a saffron-colored flower with broad petals, which is why some thought it to be a species of anemone:* 
<note>original Greek: "ἔχει δὲ... τὰ φύλλα εὐζώμῳ ἐμφερῆ, πολύτρητα ὥσπερ σητόκοπα, ψαφαρά, ὀλιγόφυλλα. ἄνθος δὲ φέρει κρόκινον, πλατύφυλλον, ὅθεν ἔδοξαν ἀνεμώνης αὐτό τινες εἶδος εἶναι:"</note>
*that is, it has leaves similar to Rocket; full of holes, as if by*

-># ON THE AFRICAN FLOWER.<-

<meta>catchword: by moths (tineis)</meta>

...eaten by moths, rough, few-leaved: it bears a saffron-colored, broad-leaved flower: wherefore some have thought it to be a species of Anemone.

<margin>Lycopersium</margin>
GALEN <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire whose works dominated Western medical science for centuries.</note>, in the fourth book of *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*, mentions a certain herb called **Lycopersium**, the juice of which a certain Centurion had brought from the Barbarian region near Egypt. It had a smell so heavy and so unpleasant that Galen himself did not even dare to taste it, but conjectured it to be lethal. However, that Centurion used it for urgent joint pains; and it was seen to possess the power of cooling even for those suffering from them. It is, Galen says, of a yellowish color: with a smell as heavy as **Hemlock** <note>original: "Cicuta"</note>, except that it carries a certain light grace like the scent of spices. He said the name of the herb from which this juice is expressed is Lycopersium. What other form it has, Galen did not add; and therefore it cannot be absolutely determined whether the African flower is Lycopersium. Nevertheless, it seems to be if not the same, then very similar. For the leaves, and especially the flowers, are endowed with a very heavy and unpleasant odor; and with a poisonous quality, very close to Hemlock. However, what kind its expressed and dried juice is, we confess we have not yet observed: yet it is likely that from flowers of no other than a yellow or yellowish color, it would also be foul-smelling.

Moreover, that the African flower is endowed with a poisonous and cooling power is shown by its unpleasant odor, which weighs down the head and is similar to Hemlock, just as in the juice of Lycopersium. Furthermore, this is established by several experiments. For I remember seeing in a boy who had begun to chew the flowers, that his lips and mouth were swollen; just as often happens to those who, playing or making music with pipes made of Hemlock <note>The hollow stems of the hemlock plant were sometimes used to make simple whistles or flutes, often with toxic results.</note>, have held them between their lips for some time. We once gave the flowers with their **calyces** <term>calyx: the green outer part of a flower that holds the petals</term>, mixed with fresh cheese, to a cat; which soon became greatly swollen, and died a little while later.

-># ON CARNATION FLOWERS.<-

Mice which had gnawed the seed are also reported to have been found dead. All of these facts demonstrate the poisonous and destructive power of this herb; and those who argue that this is a species of **Tansy** <note>original: "Tanacetum"</note> or **Mugwort** <note>original: "Arthemisia"</note>, or that it is a harmless plant, should not be listened to.

### ON CARNATION
### FLOWERS. CHAPTER XIX.

There are indeed many species of **Carnation** <term>Caryophylleis; the name comes from the Greek word for "nut-leaf," originally referring to cloves, which these flowers resemble in scent</term> flowers, with those called **Pinks** <note>original: "armerijs"</note> being counted among their number; however, leaving those aside, there are two principal kinds: one larger, the other smaller.

<margin><image-desc>red ink flourish</image-desc></margin>
The **LARGER** kind has smooth, round stems, a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or the length of a forearm</note> or more in height, and is jointed. Two leaves grow from each joint; they are oblong, hard, narrow, pointed at the tip, and of a bluish-gray color. The flowers are beautiful, emerging from long, round, toothed calyxes <note>The protective outer part of a flower bud</note>. Those that are simple usually have six petals; however, the double ones have many more, joined together with finely fringed edges. They have a very sweet scent, resembling the fragrant **Indian Cloves** <note>original: "Caryophyllos Indicos"</note>. Their color is sometimes a pale purple, other times a deep, saturated red; occasionally they are white or white-turning-purple, and sometimes variegated. In their center, two white stamen tips shine out. The seed in the oblong pods is small and black; the roots are fibrous. They are kept in gardens, but the most beautiful are almost always found in clay pots. They are more frequently planted from small cuttings taken with their leaves, and more rarely from seeds. For those grown from seed return to a wild nature, and the flowers become smaller, less fragrant, and "simple" (single-layered), even if they were previously "multiple" (double-layered). Sometimes [true] Cloves are inserted into the split cuttings when they are planted, so that the flowers might draw in that scent and more abundantly present that pleasant sweetness to the nose. The plant is long-lived,

# ON CARNATION FLOWERS <note>original: "DE CARYOPHYLLEIS FLORIBVS"</note> #

...and it lasts for many years, if during the winter months it is kept safe from the injury of the cold in wine cellars or other similar warm places. It blooms for a very long time, throughout the entire summer and autumn; sometimes even in winter in cellars, favored by the warmth of the place, even if the frost outside is severe.

Modern writers call it the **Carnation** <note>original: "Caryophylleum"</note> flower from the scent of **Cloves** <note>original: "Caryophyllorum"</note>. The Germans call it **Negelblumen** [Nail-flowers or Clove-flowers] or **Graszblumen** [Grass-flowers]; the Italians call it **Garofoli**; the French **Oeilletz** or **Ouil-letz**; the Belgians **Ginoffelbloemen** and **Groffels bloemẽ**. In Latin, it is called by many **Ocellus** [Little Eye], **Ocellus Damascenus** [Damascus Little Eye], **Ocellus Barbaricus** [Foreign Little Eye], and **Barbarica**. 

There are also those who call it **Betony** <term>Betonica; a name often confused in early botany between the medicinal wood betony and the ornamental pinks/carnations</term>, or **Vetonica** or **Vettonica**, with the added name "cultivated" or "garland-type" <note>original: "altilem aut coronariam"</note>. (**Mattioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> preferred to call it the "Garland Vettonica"). This is to distinguish it from the other Betony, which the Greeks call **kestron** <note>original: "κέστρον"</note> and **psychotrophon** <note>original: "ψυχότροφον"</note>. Many believe that the plant called **Tunica** is also this same plant; but **Bernard Gordon** <note>a 13th-century physician</note> recorded that the *Tunica* of **Dioscorides** <note>ancient Greek physician and botanist</note> was actually **Polemonium** <term>Jacob's Ladder</term>. <margin>Cantabrica</margin> **Turner** <note>William Turner (c. 1508–1568), the "Father of English Botany"</note> identifies the Carnation as the **Cantabrica**, which **Pliny** <note>Roman naturalist</note> writes in *Book 25, Chapter 8* was discovered in Spain by the Cantabrian people during the time of the Divine Augustus. He says it grows everywhere with a rush-like stalk a foot long, on which are small oblong flowers like little baskets; in these is a very tiny seed. <margin>A second Vettonica.</margin> **Leonhart Fuchs** <note>1501–1566, a German physician and author of a famous herbal</note>, however, maintained it was a second type of Betony or Vetonica, which is found described among the "spurious" works of **Dioscorides**; and indeed in these words: "Bettonica grows in hay-fields and mountainous places, both in clean and cultivated spots, around the crops. It has a root that is entirely red and fragrant. The leaves are green, and the middle of the leaves is purple. It has triangular shapes on an upright stalk, and upon these are purplish flowers." <note>translated from the Greek: Βεττονικὴ γεννᾶται... πορφυρίζοντα</note> That is to say, *Vettonica grows in meadows and mountainous places, likewise in clean and cultivated ones: it has a root that is entirely reddish and scented: leaves like leeks, and the middle...*

E
<meta>catchword: middle (original: "dium", the end of "medium")</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the cultivation and naming conventions of the Carnation, detailing its various European vernacular names and debating its identity among classical botanical authorities like Pliny and Dioscorides.</summary>

# ON CARNATION FLOWERS <note>original: "DE CARYOPHYLLEIS FLORIBVS"</note> #

...the center of these is scarlet, on a straight, triangular stalk, and upon these are purple flowers. **Ioannes Ruellius** <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides</note> believed the **Carnation** <note>original: "Caryophylleum"</note> flower was unknown to the ancients; this seems to be the most accurate judgment, for certainly this flower bears little resemblance to the other **Betony** <note>original: "Vettonicæ"</note> or the **Cantabrian pink** <note>original: "Cantabricæ"</note>. It is "wondrous to believe," he says, "that so famous and pleasant a flower was passed over in silence by the ancients, as it can be seen as no less than the **Rose** in its size, beauty, and variety."

**ANOTHER and smaller species of Carnation** <note>original: "ALTERA & minor Caryophylleorum species"</note> is very similar to the first in its slender stalks and leaves, though both are smaller. It also has flowers coming from long, small, and smooth husks <note>original: "calycibus"</note>, but they are smaller and more deeply divided and fringed around the edges; they are white in color, or turning from white to purple. It grows in gardens among the Belgians; however, **Gesner** <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), a monumental Swiss naturalist and polymath</note> reports that it is also found in damp meadows among the Germans. The flowers burst forth in summer.

Some call this the smaller **cultivated or garland Betony** <note>original: "VETTONICAM altilem siue coronariam minorem"</note>; the Germans call it **Mutwillen** <note>meaning "mischief" or "wantonness"</note> or **Hochmut** <note>meaning "pride" or "haughtiness"</note>; and for this reason it is called the "proud one" by many. Our people call them **Pluymkens** <note>literally "little plumes," referring to the feathered appearance of the petals</note> from the resemblance of the fringed and divided petals to feathers; they also call it **cleyn Ginofflen**, which is to say, the **smaller Carnation**.

The **USE** <note>original: "VSVS"</note> of Carnation flowers in medicine is indeed truly none, but in garlands it is almost excessive; and especially the cultivated flower, which **Ruellius** notes also improves vinegar with a pleasant flavor and color. **Fuchs** <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a founding father of German botany</note>, however, has left it written that not only is a conserve (as they commonly call it) made from the flowers, but the roots of the cultivated variety are recommended by more recent writers against the contagion of the **plague** <note>original: "pestis"</note>. The juice of the wild variety is believed to be useful for wearing down and expelling **stones** <term>Calculus; kidney or bladder stones</term>, as well as for those suffering from the **falling sickness** <term>Comitialis morbus; a historical name for epilepsy</term>.

# ON SWEET WILLIAM FLOWERS <note>original: "DE ARMERIIS FLORIBVS"</note> #

### CHAPTER 20. ###

**THERE** *are also several species of* **SWEET WILLIAM** <note>original: "Armeriorum"</note> *flowers, differing primarily in the size and especially the color of the leaves of the plant itself.*

<margin>1</margin>
**THE FIRST** *species has small round stalks, jointed like Carnation flowers, which are for the most part reddish around the lower joints and shorter than a cubit <note>an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</note> in height. The leaves are mostly oblong, but somewhat wider than those of the larger Carnation, and are a grassy green color. At the top of the stalks are tiny blossoms very similar to miniature Carnations, but smaller; many are gathered together into a kind of umbel <note>a flat-topped flower cluster where the stalks spread from a common center</note>. These emerge for the most part one by one, and almost always shine with a scarlet redness, though they are sometimes variegated with white dots around the middle; very rarely they are white. This little flower adorns the garden beds of the Belgians; among the Italians and elsewhere it grows in uncultivated and dry places. Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), a monumental Swiss naturalist and polymath</note> writes that he found it near the banks of Lake Greifensee in Switzerland <note>original: "Heluetia"</note>. It also grows in some wild places in Belgium, and along the edges and ditches of fields and meadows, but it is much smaller and thinner there than in gardens. It flowers early, often in the month of April or May, preceding the Carnation flowers; it then thrives through most of the summer.*

**THE FRENCH** *call them* **Armoires** *and* **Armoiries**. *Jean Ruel <note>original: "Ruellius"; a French physician and botanist (1474–1537)</note> calls them "Armerian flowers" for this reason. The* **GERMANS** *call them* **Thunder-pinks** <note>original: "Dondernegele"</note> *or* **Field-pinks** <note>original: "felt negele"</note>. *The* **BELGIANS** *call them* **keykens** <note>a Middle Dutch term for small clusters or "tufts"</note>, *as if you were to say "a little bundle," for our people call bundles of flowers* **keykens** *in their own language. It seems to be a certain wild species of Carnation flower, for which reason it is called* **Wild or Field Betony** <note>original: "Vettonica agrestis aut sylvestris"</note> *by some more recent authors. There are even those who wish it to be the "tunic" herb <note>original: "tunicam herbam"</note>, but this plant matches the other* **Betony** *or* **Jacob's Ladder** <note>original: "Polemonio"</note> *no more than the Carnation does.*

# ON SWEET WILLIAM FLOWERS <note>original: "DE ARMERIIS FLORIBVS"</note> #

### CHAPTER 20. ###

**THERE** *are also several species of* **SWEET WILLIAM** <note>original: "Armeriorum"</note> *flowers, differing primarily in the size and especially the color of the leaves of the plant itself.*

<margin>1</margin>
**THE FIRST** *species has small round stalks, jointed like Carnation flowers, which are for the most part reddish around the lower joints and shorter than a cubit <note>an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</note> in height. The leaves are mostly oblong, but somewhat wider than those of the larger Carnation, and are a grassy green color. At the top of the stalks are tiny blossoms very similar to miniature Carnations, but smaller; many are gathered together into a kind of umbel <note>a flat-topped flower cluster where the stalks spread from a common center</note>. These emerge for the most part one by one, and almost always shine with a scarlet redness, though they are sometimes variegated with white dots around the middle; very rarely they are white. This little flower adorns the garden beds of the Belgians <note>referring to the inhabitants of the Low Countries, roughly modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands</note>; among the Italians and elsewhere it grows in uncultivated and dry places. Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), a monumental Swiss naturalist and polymath</note> writes that he found it near the banks of Lake Greifensee in Switzerland <note>original: "Heluetia"</note>. It also grows in some wild places in Belgium, and along the edges and ditches of fields and meadows, but it is much smaller and thinner there than in gardens. It flowers early, often in the month of April or May, precociously.* <note>The text likely completes as "precociously" or "earlier than other species."</note>

# ON SWEET WILLIAM FLOWERS <note>original: "DE ARMERIIS FLORIBVS"</note> #

<margin>2</margin> **A second** species of Sweet William <note>original: "Armeriorum"</note> bears leaves around its jointed stems that are *much wider, larger, and more frequent, as well as whiter, not much different from the leaves of the Flower of Constantinople <note>The "Flower of Constantinople" usually refers to the Maltese Cross (Lychnis chalcedonica)</note>. Several flowers are crowded together in a single umbel <note>a flat-topped flower cluster</note>; they are beautiful, reddish, and decorated with tiny white dots, similar to the previously mentioned Sweet Williams. It grows on rocky hills and in rough, sandy, and sunny places among the Germans and other nations; it is sown in gardens by the Belgians <note>referring to the Low Countries</note>, where it flowers in summer in the second year along with other Carnations.*

**The Germans** call it *Steinnegele* <note>literally "Stone Pink" or "Rock Carnation"</note>; *some call it Scarlata from the red color of the flower which resembles scarlet <note>original: "coccum," referring to the kermes or cochineal dye</note>. This is a wild Carnation, just like the preceding one; both certainly require no care: they are hardy by their own nature and endure the hardships of winter without difficulty.*

<margin>3</margin> **Now** *there is also a third species of Sweet William flower with small, round, jointed stalks that are sticky to the touch <note>This "sticky" trait suggests a member of the Silene genus, often called Catchflies</note> and a foot in height. Two leaves grow from each joint; they are oblong, narrow, and pointed, becoming white in their greenness and almost bluish-gray. The flowers are similarly crowded together in an umbel, beautiful and similar to Sweet Williams, but smaller and of a dilute reddish-purple color. The seed is tiny and held in oblong vessels; the roots are thin and fibrous. It is sown in gardens and flowers from the month of May or June until Autumn, and in the same year that it was sown.*

**There are** *those who wish this to be some species of Limonium or Behen <note>original: "Been," a name historically applied to several different plants including Sea Lavenders or Catchflies</note>, as it is commonly called now, because of the similarity it has with the red-flowered Behen, which Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> classifies as a species of Limonium. Indeed, it is somewhat similar to that plant in its jointed stalks and leaves, but more similar in color. However, the stalks are shorter and more tender, and the leaves smaller; yet the color and smoothness of the leaves are nearly the same in both. But we judge this plant not to be Behen or Limonium, but rather a species of Sweet William flower, for which reason we have given it this third place among them.*

# THE THIRD SWEET WILLIAM FLOWER <note>original: "ARMERIVS FLOS TERTIVS"</note> #

->E 4<-

<margin>3</margin> **Now** *there is also a third species of Sweet William flower <note>original: "Armerius flos"</note> with small, round, jointed stalks that are sticky to the touch <note>This "sticky" trait suggests a member of the Silene genus, often called Catchflies; the stickiness helps protect the flowers from crawling insects</note> and a foot in height. Two leaves grow from each joint; they are oblong, narrow, and lack individual stems, instead clasping the main stalk directly <note>original: "sessile"</note>. These leaves are marked by prominent, visible veins. The plant rises from a thick, fibrous root system that spreads horizontally through the earth.* 

*At the summit of the stem, the flowers are gathered into three distinct clusters; additional smaller clusters emerge from the upper leaf axils <note>the angle between the leaf and the stem</note>. Each flower consists of five small petals. Like its relatives, this variety is a hardy plant of the summer, blooming alongside the other Carnations and Pinks in the garden.*

<header>ON THE FLOWER OF CONSTANTINOPLE</header>

4 **Besides** these, there is also another species of either Carnation <note>original: "Caryophylleorum"</note> or Sweet William <note>original: "Armeriorum"</note> which grows wild everywhere, including in the Low Countries <note>original: "apud Belgas"</note>. It has small stalks about nine inches <note>original: "dodrantales"</note> long. Two small, pointed leaves grow from each joint. At the top of the stalks are single flowers, fringed around the edge; they are quite similar in shape and size to the smaller Pinks <note>original: "Caryophylleis minoribus"</note>, but their color is a diluted cochineal red. It grows here and there in meadows and damp places. The flowers are most prominent during the months of May and June.

The Belgians call them **Craeyenbloemkens**, which means "Crow-flowers" <note>original Latin: "Cornicis flores"</note>.

Furthermore, the flowers of all these Sweet Williams have no use in either medicine or as food; they are "coronary" plants <term>coronary: from the Latin 'coronarius,' referring to flowers used specifically for weaving into ceremonial crowns, garlands, or wreaths</term> and serve to adorn gardens and floral displays.

### ON THE FLOWER OF CONSTANTINOPLE. CHAPTER XXI.

**THE FLOWER OF CONSTANTINOPLE** <note>This is the *Lychnis chalcedonica*, often called the Maltese Cross due to the shape of its petals</note> has several stems rising from the root, which are three feet <note>original: "bicubitales" or two cubits</note> high, thin, jointed, and hollow. Two oblong, somewhat broad, and pointed leaves grow from each joint; they are dark green and slightly rough and hairy, as are the stems. The flowers grow in broad umbels <term>umbel: a cluster of flowers where the stalks spread from a common center, forming a flat or curved surface like an umbrella</term>, with many crowded together. They are beautiful to look at but otherwise scentless, possessing the color of a Marigold <note>original: "Calendulæ"</note>, a purple lily, or the skin of a ripe orange <note>original: "mali aurantij"</note>, being somewhat fiery in hue. The seed is tiny and reddish, contained in oblong, pointed vessels. The roots are long and divided into many thin parts, with a somewhat sharp taste. To the Belgians, it is a foreign flower, and it is sown in gardens. It blooms in June and July in the second year after sowing, and continues for several years thereafter. Indeed, it has a perennial root <note>original: "radice viuaci"</note> and lasts a long time, as it is able to endure the winter and the cold.

2 There is also a second and smaller species of this plant, which is about a foot and a half <note>original: "cubitalis"</note> high, or

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the "Flower of Constantinople" (Lychnis chalcedonica), completing the details regarding its foliage and then describing its vibrant flower clusters, root system, and origins.</meta>

# FLOS CONSTANTINOPOLITANVS.
## THE FLOWER OF CONSTANTINOPLE

...dark green and covered with very short, fine hairs. At the very tops of the stems, the flowers are packed closely together, gathered into a dense, rounded head <note>original: "in capitulum congesti"</note>. They are of an exceptionally beautiful and vivid color, ranging from a bright vermilion to a deep scarlet. Each individual flower is composed of five petals, which are notched or split into two parts at the tip <term>bifid: divided into two lobes, which gives the flower the appearance of a Maltese Cross</term>; in the center of these petals, several small stamens are visible.

The root is white and provided with many hanging fibers; it is a perennial root that does not perish in the winter, but instead puts forth new shoots every year.

This plant varies in the color of its blossoms: for it is found not only in the common vermilion, but also in a much deeper scarlet, and occasionally even in white. It typically reaches its full bloom during the months of June and July, and its seeds reach maturity in August.

### Names
It takes its name from the fact that it was first brought to the West from the city of Constantinople <note>modern-day Istanbul</note>; for this reason, it is most commonly called the **Lychnis of Constantinople**. The Italians call it *Croce di Cavalieri* <note>meaning "Cross of the Knights"</note>, because the four-lobed appearance of the petals resembles the heraldic cross worn by the Knights of Malta.

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the "Flower of Constantinople" (Lychnis chalcedonica), specifically addressing its various names, its potential identification with ancient plants, and its lack of known medicinal properties, before transitioning to a new chapter on Soapwort.</meta>

# ON THE FLOWER OF CONSTANTINOPLE.

*...a little taller, with sparser flowers, which produces blossoms in its first year: according to the testimony of Gesner.* <note>Conrad Gesner (1516–1565) was a monumental Swiss naturalist and scholar.</note>

Our people commonly call this the **Flower of Constantinople**; the Germans, however, call it the **Flower of Jerusalem**; Aldrovandi <note>Ulisse Aldrovandi, a celebrated Italian naturalist</note> gives it the name **Cretan**. There are those who would have it be the root called *Lanaria* <note>meaning "Wool-plant"; original: Lanariam</note> by the Latins, which is known as *strouthion* <note>original: στρούθιον; often identified as a plant used for scouring</note> to the Greeks and is called *Condisi* by the Arabs, which was once used for cleaning wool by those who wash it. It is quite evident that these people have failed to grasp the truth, and this hardly needs refuting. For the stalk of this flower is neither thorny nor prickly, as Theophrastus and Pliny report the *Struthion* to be; furthermore, it does not have a large root, nor leaves like an Olive, as the *Struthion* does.

<margin>The *Pothos* flower.</margin>
However, there will perhaps be some who suspect this Flower of Constantinople to be the flower called **Pothos** <note>original: πόθον</note> by the ancients, which Gaza <note>Theodorus Gaza, a 15th-century scholar who translated Greek botanical works into Latin</note> calls "Desire" <note>original: desiderium</note> in Latin. We have no reason at present to oppose their opinion. For Theophrastus, Pliny, and Athenaeus place the *Pothos* among the summer flowers and report that it is of two kinds: one similar to a hyacinth, and the other *achroun* <note>original: ἀχροῦν</note>, meaning colorless or white. Now, the Flower of Constantinople blooms in the summer months and very closely resembles either the "inscribed" hyacinth—which they call the Purple Lily—in its color, or it is white. These facts show a great similarity between the Flower of Constantinople and the *Pothos*.

Furthermore, the Flower of Constantinople, beyond the grace it has earned in gardens and garlands, has no medicinal use that we know of; for that reason, its properties remain undiscovered.

### ON SOAPWORT. CHAPTER XXII.

The stems of **Soapwort**, as it is commonly called, are smooth, thin, round, and jointed, reaching a cubit <note>an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches</note> or more in height. The leaves are broad,

# ABOUT SOAPWORT <note>original: DE SAPONARIA</note>
<meta>catchword: ...ness</meta>

*veined, very similar to the leaves of the broad-leaved plantain <note>The Plantago major, a common weed with prominent parallel veins.</note>, but smaller nonetheless; they grow in pairs from each joint, and for the most part—especially those closer to the roots—they are turned downwards. The flowers are numerous at the top of the little stalks and around the upper joints; they are fragrant and colored sometimes a beautiful rosy red, sometimes a pale purple, or white. These emerge from rounded and oblong calyxes <note>The "cup" of the flower that holds the petals.</note> and are composed of five petals, in the middle of which are some tiny stamens. The roots are thick, long, and creep obliquely, with certain fibers adhering to them, similar to the roots of black hellebore <note>original: veratri nigri. Used here as a point of physical comparison for the root structure.</note>. Among the Belgians <note>Referring to the inhabitants of the Low Countries or Netherlands in the 16th century.</note> it is cultivated in gardens; elsewhere it grows wild near rivers and torrents in sunny places. In gardens, it lasts a long time and is persistent in life. It flowers in June and July.*

They commonly call it **SOAPWORT** <note>original: SAPONARIAM</note> because of the cleansing power for which the leaves are known; for they release a certain juice when rubbed together that cleanses in almost the same way as soap. Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist known for his translations of Dioscorides.</note>, however, describes another "soap-like" plant. Some call the herb *Tunica*; yet it is not a species of the Carnation <note>original: Vettonicæ coronariæ</note>, much less of Jacob's Ladder <note>original: Polemonij</note>, which we wrote were considered *Tunica* herbs in the chapter on Pinks. It could more readily be seen as *Alisma* <note>Water-plantain.</note> or *Damasonium*, except that its stem is crowded with branches rather than being simple, and is occasionally divided into "wings" [side-shoots]; furthermore, the roots are thicker than those of black hellebore. To be sure, it has leaves consistent with the description of *Alisma* and a rounded mass of flowers at the top of the stems. But the stem of *Alisma* is thin and simple, and its roots are likewise thin; these features show the difference between this Soapwort and *Alisma*.

<margin>Alisma.</margin>
For *Alisma* is **DESCRIBED** by Dioscorides <note>The 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical authority for 1,500 years.</note> in this manner: *Alisma—there are those who call it Alcea, some Damasonium, some Acyron, and others Lyron—has leaves similar to Plantain, yet narrower and turned back toward the earth. The stem is thin, simple, higher than a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches.</note>, and bears small heads, similar to a Thyrsus <note>A ritual staff or wand tipped with a pinecone, associated with Dionysus; here referring to the shape of the flower cluster.</note>.*

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics and habitat of Soapwort, explaining its name through its soap-like properties and distinguishing it from the ancient "Alisma" described by Dioscorides.</summary>
<keywords>Soapwort, Saponaria officinalis, botany, Dioscorides, Ruellius, plant identification, Renaissance medicine, Alisma, taxonomy</keywords>

# BASTARD DAMASONIUM <note>original: DAMASONIUM NOTHUM. In early botanical Latin, "nothum" (meaning false or bastard) was used to identify a plant that resembled a species described by ancient authorities like Dioscorides but possessed distinct differences.</note>

->[Illustration]<-

The plate depicts a single vertical stem emerging from a thick, knotty horizontal rhizome <term>rhizome: a creeping underground stem that sends out roots and shoots</term> with numerous fibrous roots extending downwards. Five broad, lanceolate <note>spear-shaped leaves that are wider in the middle and taper at the ends</note> leaves with clear parallel venation are arranged alternately along the stem, clasping it closely at their bases. The stem terminates in a single large flower which identifies this plant as a member of the orchid family, specifically resembling what is known today as the Lady's Slipper orchid. The blossom features a prominent, rounded pouch—the labellum <note>the "lip" of the orchid, modified into a pouch shape to aid in pollination</note>—and long, slender, slightly twisted petals and sepals.

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of Soapwort (Saponaria). # CONCERNING SOAPWORT <note>original: DE SAPONARIA</note>

*...[its features are as follows]: the flowers are delicate, white, and pale yellow; the roots are like those of Black Hellebore <note>original: Veratri nigri. In the ancient world, hellebore was a well-known medicinal plant, though highly toxic.</note>, thin, scented, sharp to the taste, and somewhat oily: it loves watery places.*

**Pliny** describes the same plant in almost the same words in Book 25, Chapter 10: but he establishes two kinds; one in watery places, which is the one described by **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical reference for over 1,500 years.</note>; the other growing in forests, which is darker and has larger leaves. He also says its leaf is jagged <note>original: laciniosum</note>, a detail which Dioscorides omitted; and that the stem has the head of a **Thyrsus** <note>original: Thyrsi. A thyrsus was a ceremonial staff or wand associated with the god Bacchus, usually topped with a pine cone or a bunch of leaves.</note>, whereas Dioscorides writes that the stem is thyrsus-like <note>original: θυρσοειδῆν (thyrsoeidēn)</note>; Pliny suggests what a thyrsus-like stem is in this way: namely, that it is thicker and wider at the upper part.

<margin>Thyrsus.</margin>
For a **THYRSUS**, or *thyrsos* <note>original: θύρσος</note>, is a rod, torch, or spear covered with foliage or ivy, the top of which is packed with many leaves or flowers, specifically in the likeness of a globe or ball. Indeed, **Melchior Guilandinus** <note>A 16th-century Prussian physician and botanist who served as the director of the botanical garden in Padua.</note> testifies in a letter to Langius that the Thyrsus is found depicted in this way on the very ancient marbles that exist everywhere in Rome; namely, carrying something spherical like a ball at the very peak. The descriptions of certain plants also show that such a thing is a Thyrsus. For Dioscorides writes that the **Acanthus** plant (which they call **Bear's Breech** <note>original: Brancam vrsinam</note>) has a thyrsus-like head <note>original: κεφαλὴν θυρσοειδῆν (kephalēn thyrsoeidēn)</note>; moreover, when the stem of this plant first comes forth, before it has fully matured, it is thicker at the top. **Thyme** <note>original: Thymus</note> is also called a *thyrsion* <note>original: θύρσιον, meaning "little thyrsus"</note>, which produces a globe-shaped little head on its stalks: for which reason it is also nicknamed "the headed" <note>original: κεφαλώτος (kephalōtos)</note> or "capitate." Likewise, **Broomrape** <note>original: Orobanche</note> is called *thyrsine* <note>original: θυρσίνη</note>, because its stem is thicker at the upper part: the stem of the **giant fennel** <note>original: ferulæ</note> is now also called a Thyrsus, which, although it gradually tapers, nevertheless brings forth at the very summit a broad and round umbrella <term>umbrella: in botany, an "umbel," a flower cluster where stalks spring from a common center like umbrella ribs</term> full of flowers at first, and then seeds, in the manner of a Thyrsus: for which reason the *narthex* <note>original: νάρθηξ</note> or giant fennel is occasionally called a Thyrsus: and from this, **Bacchus**, who is the "thyrsus-bearer" <note>original: θυρσοφόρος (thursophoros)</note>, is also sometimes called the "fennel-bearer" <note>original: ναρθηκοφόρος (narthekophoros)</note> from the thyrsi which...

<meta>catchword: in eius (in his/its)</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes the botanical terminology used by ancient authors to describe Soapwort, focusing specifically on the definition of a "thyrsus-like" stem and comparing the observations of Pliny and Dioscorides.</summary>
