# CONCERNING SOAPWORT <note>original: DE SAPONARIA</note>

*...were formerly shaken in his rites <note>Referring to the Bacchic mysteries or festivals of Dionysus.</note>. All of which show that a Thyrsus is a wand, staff, or spear; with a rounded tip made of leaves or flowers: and that a stem is called "thyrsus-like" <note>original: θυρσοειδῆν (thyrsoeidēn)</note> when the top part is spherical or wider. Nor does Macrobius <note>A Roman provincial of the early 5th century AD, author of the Saturnalia, a work of encyclopedic lore.</note> contradict us in Book 1 of his Saturnalia, where he writes that Father Liber <note>An ancient Italian deity identified with Bacchus/Dionysus, the god of wine.</note>, when he holds a Thyrsus, carries a hidden weapon. For from this it is gathered that the Thyrsus does not end in a sharpened point, but rather in a blunt and rounded tip: because by saying it is a "hidden weapon," he shows the tip is so covered that it cannot be recognized; which is indeed achieved by many leaves arranged around it. These are the words of Macrobius: "Among the Spartans, an image of Father Liber is worshipped, distinguished by a spear, not a Thyrsus: but when he holds a Thyrsus, what else is being carried but a hidden weapon, whose point is protected by clinging ivy?" And so much for the Thyrsus.*

<margin>Bastard Damasonium</margin>
SINCE, HOWEVER, mention of *Alisma* or *Damasonium* <note>Alisma (Water Plantain) and Damasonium were often confused in early botany. Here, the author is distinguishing them from a specific orchid.</note> has occurred, it seemed we should not overlook a certain foreign plant which some think is Damasonium, an illustration of which was shared with us by Ioannes Vreccomius of Brussels, a most diligent cultivator of stalks and herbs. This is depicted with a single stem; vein-filled leaves similar to those of Plantain; but with a large, four-petaled flower, pale red in color, from the middle of which an egg-shaped hollow part protrudes; with a thick, slanted root from which thin fibers hang. They say this is found in a certain village above Augsburg <note>original: Augustam. Likely Augusta Vindelicorum, modern Augsburg in Bavaria.</note>, and is commonly called *Papenschon* <note>A version of the German "Papenschuh" or "Priest's Shoe," referring to the Lady's Slipper orchid (Cypripedium calceolus).</note> and in Latin, *Damasonium*. But this matches Alisma or Damasonium not at all, or very little—certainly much less than Soapwort does.

MATTHIOLUS <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist whose commentaries on Dioscorides were standard texts.</note> also depicts a certain *Alisma*, but one which corresponds even less to the true Alisma than this one does: for its leaf is not veined like Plantain; nor

### CONCERNING COLUMBINE. CHAPTER XXIII.

COLUMBINE <note>original: Aquileia</note> bears leaves like those of the *Greater Celandine* <note>original: Chelidony maioris</note>, though somewhat rounder and cut around the edges, of a bluish-green color. When these are wounded, they yield no sap—certainly not a yellow one <note>Unlike the Celandine, which is famous for its bright orange-yellow medicinal sap.</note>. The stem is a cubit high <note>About 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>, thin, reddish, and slightly hairy; its branches produce individual flowers made of five hanging, hollow, horn-like spurs, and just as many small intermediate leaves standing upright between them. These are the "simple" flowers, though there are also "composite" ones (for the "double" varieties are formed from many such horn-like spurs joined together). Their color is sometimes blue, sometimes reddish-purple, sometimes white, or purplish-white; occasionally they are variegated, shining with a mixture of blue and white. From the center emerge stamens from which small tips <note>anthers</note> hang. After each flower, five pods rise up, containing small, black, and shining seeds. Beneath are thick, fibrous roots that last for several years. It is introduced into the gardens of *Belgium* and *France*; in *Germany* and elsewhere, it grows spontaneously on sunny hills, wooded mountains, and rich meadows. The flowers bloom in May and June.
MODERN AUTHORS call it *Aquileia* or *Aquilina* <note>The name Aquileia likely derives from the Latin 'aquila' (eagle), referring to the spur's resemblance to an eagle's claw.</note>. The Germans call it *Agley* and *Ageley*; our people <note>Referring to the inhabitants of the Low Countries/Netherlands.</note> call it *Akeleyen*. The French call it *Ancoiles* or *Ancolye*.
Regarding what medicinal powers it possesses, not enough has yet been discovered; for indeed it has no other common use than to adorn garlands and gardens. *Tragus* <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), a German physician and botanist who used the Latinized name Tragus.</note>, however, writes that the seed, in the weight of a drachm <note>A small unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams.</note> with an obol <note>Approximately 0.7 grams.</note> of saffron in wine, is used for obstruction of the liver and...

<meta>The text cuts off here with the catchword "morbo" (disease/illness), suggesting the next page continues the discussion of medicinal treatments for liver ailments.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on stem shapes and provides a detailed botanical description of the Columbine (Aquileia), including its morphology, various colors, and regional names across Europe. The author notes that while it is primarily an ornamental garden plant, some contemporary botanists like Tragus have begun documenting its medicinal uses for the liver.</summary>
<keywords>Columbine, Aquileia, Soapwort, Saponaria, Greater Celandine, Tragus, Hieronymus Bock, botany, medicinal plants, garden flowers, liver obstruction</keywords>

->CONCERNING COLUMBINE<-

...a thyrsus-like <note>original: θυρσοειδὴς (thyrsoeidēs); a botanical term for a stem shaped like a staff or wand, often associated with the staff of Bacchus.</note> stem; but producing only a single flower, which seems to be different from a thyrsus-like form.
FURTHERMORE, *Soapwort* <note>original: Saponaria; so named because its leaves produce a lather when rubbed in water.</note> is of a hot and dry temperament <note>Referring to the ancient system of "humors" and "qualities" where plants were classified by their effect on the body's balance.</note>, and is endowed with no small power of cleansing.

### CONCERNING COLUMBINE. CHAPTER XXIII.

COLUMBINE <note>original: Aquileia; the name likely derives from "aquila" (eagle), because the flower’s nectar spurs resemble an eagle's talons.</note> bears leaves like those of the *Greater Celandine* <note>original: Chelidony maioris</note>, though somewhat rounder and cut around the edges, of a bluish-green color. The stem is a cubit high <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of measurement, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>, thin, reddish, and slightly hairy; its branches produce individual flowers made of five hanging, hollow, horn-like spurs, and just as many small intermediate leaves standing upright between them. These are the "simple" flowers, though there are also "composite" ones (for the "double" varieties are formed from many such horn-like spurs joined together). Their color is sometimes blue, sometimes reddish-purple, sometimes white, or purplish-white; occasionally they are variegated, shining with a mixture of blue and white. From the center emerge stamens from which small tips <note>the pollen-bearing anthers</note> hang. After each flower, five pods rise up, containing small, black, and shining seeds. Beneath are thick, fibrous roots that last for several years. It is introduced into the gardens of *Belgium* and *France*; in *Germany* and elsewhere, it grows ...

<header>CONCERNING SNAPDRAGON.</header>

*...he writes that it is usefully given to those suffering from the royal disease <note>original: morbo regio; a historical term for jaundice, so called because it was supposedly treated with costly "royal" remedies like wine and baths.</note>; but the one who has taken it must then be diligently covered and made to sweat. Most people attribute to it the powers of Aegylops <note>original: Aegylopis; likely referring to a type of wild grass or a plant used for eye ulcers.</note>, others of Greater Centaury, but they do so recklessly and without any reason.*

---

### CONCERNING SNAPDRAGON. CHAPTER XXIV.

SNAPDRAGON <note>original: Antirrhino; the name comes from the Greek meaning "counter-nose" or "like a nose," referring to the snout-like shape of the flower.</note> has stems that are round, smooth, fragile, and branched. Its leaves are numerous, for the most part oblong and pointed, similar to Toadflax <note>original: Linariæ</note> but larger; occasionally they are shorter and wider, more resembling Pimpernel <note>original: Anagallidis</note>. The flowers are arranged in order along the upper part of the stems; they are oblong in shape and wider at the front. While they remain closed as they grow, they are in some way similar to a frog’s mouth; but when open, they resemble the gaping maw of a lion. Their color is a rosy red, or white, or a dilute purple fading from white, and not infrequently they are also somewhat yellowish—specifically those varieties whose leaves are broader and shorter. These are followed by a seed-head, which is oblong, narrower at the front, and full of holes, resembling the nostrils of a calf or the head of a dog. Inside these are small, blackish seeds. Fibers hang from the roots, which are not large. 

It is planted in gardens among the Belgians, Italians, Germans, and elsewhere. In the month of May, those plants which have survived the winter begin to flower, and continue through the rest of the summer; however, those sown in the spring of the same year flower later, near the end of summer. In Belgium, it does not withstand the winter without damage unless the season is very mild or the plants have been moved into wine cellars for protection.

Among the GREEKS it is called <note>original: ἀντίρρινον ἢ ἀντίρριζον (antirrhinon or antirrhizon)</note>—if the manuscript of Theophrastus <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and the "father of botany."</note> is not faulty—and also <note>original: ἀνάρρινον (anarrhinon)</note>. Among the Latins, it is likewise called *Antirrhinum*. According to the author Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire.</note>, it is also named <note>original: βουκράνιον (boukranion); literally "ox-skull," referring to the shape of the seed pod.</note> and <note>original: λυχνὶς ἄγρια (lychnis agria)</note>, or *Wild Lychnis*. Apuleius also calls it <note>original: ἀμέθυσον (amethyson); "not drunken," perhaps suggesting it was a charm against intoxication</note>, <note>original: πιθήκιον (pithēkion); "little ape"</note>, <note>original: κυνοκεφάλιον (kynokephalion); "dog's head"</note>, *Dog's Brain*, *Female Herb*, *Lesser Beauty*, and *Pleasant Opalis*...

<summary>This page describes the morphology, varieties, and cultivation of the Snapdragon (Antirrhinum), highlighting its distinctive "lion-like" flowers and "calf-nose" seed pods. It also lists various historical names for the plant from Greek and Latin authorities.</summary>

# ON THE SNAPDRAGON <note>original: DE ANTIRRHINO</note>

...is said to resemble. It is also believed to be the plant called *Leo* [Lion], which *Columella* <note>a Roman writer on agriculture, 1st century AD</note> mentions among flowers in *Book 10*, writing thus:

> *The narcissus's locks, and the savage*
> *Mouth of the gaping wild lion.*
->*Likewise also below:*<-
> *The Lion also yawns.*

*Gesner* <note>Conrad Gesner (1516–1565), a Swiss naturalist</note> however, thought this *Leo* was the *Columbine*, which for that reason he named *Leontostomium* [Lion's-mouth]: but it seems to us that this name fits the *Snapdragon* more than the *Columbine*. For the Snapdragon's gaping flower resembles the lion's open maw more readily than the latter's does.

There is indeed also another *Leo*, which is called <term>ospreon</term> <note>original: ὄσπρεον; a pulse or legume</note>, and the "Lion of legumes," known by another name as *Orobanche* <note>Broomrape, a parasitic plant</note>, concerning which we have dealt in the *History of Grains*.

There is also another plant called *Amethystos*, which *Plutarch* mentions, namely <term>krambe</term> <note>original: κράμβη; cabbage</note>: of which we will treat in another place, namely in the *History of Pot-herbs*.

The Belgians call the *Snapdragon* **Ouant**.

Furthermore, the seed of the Snapdragon is useless in medicine, as *Galen* <note>a prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> says. The herb itself is similar in its properties to *Bubonium* <note>Starwort</note>, but much weaker.

They say, says *Dioscorides* <note>author of the primary Roman-era pharmacological manual</note>, that when worn as an amulet it protects against sorcery; and it procures favor if someone is anointed with it along with oil of lilies, or *Cyprine* <note>oil made from the henna plant</note>.

*Apuleius* reports that a decoction of the root in water, used as a wash, quickly relieves <term>epiphoras</term> <note>excessive watering or inflammation</note> of the eyes.

Besides the garden *Snapdragon* described in the previous chapter, another is found, entirely wild in its nature, which is both smaller and lower to the ground than the cultivated one, and somewhat—though only moderately—hairy. This one also has slender little stems,

# ON THE WILD SNAPDRAGON.
<meta>catchword: nora [referring to the continuation of the word "minora" on the next page].</meta>

...branched: the leaves are oblong and narrow: but the flowers do not come out at the top of the stem, but next to the leaflets from their axils <note>The "sinuses" or angles where the leaf meets the stem</note>, similar to the Snapdragon, but smaller, of a reddish-purple color, and yellow in the middle. The seed heads are likewise smaller and shorter, full of holes, almost resembling a human skull <note>The seed pods of many snapdragon species, particularly *Misopates orontium*, bear a striking resemblance to tiny skulls when dried</note>, in which the seed is tiny: the roots underneath are thin. It grows in many places in Belgium and Germany, around borders, and in the fields themselves, next to roads, in gardens, and elsewhere. And in one place it is found taller and more vigorous; in another shorter and more delicate. It flowers in July and August, and dies in winter: for it is a summer herb.

**OUR PEOPLE** call it *cleyn Orant* <note>Middle Dutch for "small snapdragon" or "lesser calf's-snout"</note>: it is undoubtedly a wild species of Snapdragon. There are those who wish it to be the **Orontium**, led by the similarity of the name, with a decoction of which **Archigenes** <note>A prominent Greek physician of the 1st/2nd century AD</note>, according to **Galen** <note>The most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note> in *On the Affected Places* [κατὰ τόπους] *Book 9*, advises those suffering from jaundice <note>original: "auriginosos", meaning those with a golden or jaundiced hue</note> to be washed. But whether it is truly that plant cannot be affirmed, as its description is found nowhere, nor its name, except in this place. For this reason, **Janus Cornarius** <note>1500–1558, a German humanist and translator of Greek medical texts</note> suspected the word *Orontium* was corrupted, and that it should read *Origanum* [Oregano]; which, on the authority of **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*</note>, is beneficial for jaundiced people when used in a bath.

<margin>Phyteuma.</margin>
The Wild Snapdragon has **MORE** similarity with the **Phyteuma**, which is described by **Dioscorides** in these words, in *Book 4*: <note>original Greek: φύτευμα φύλλα ἔχει ὅμοια στρουθίῳ, μικρότερα δέ· καρπὸν πολὺν τετρημένον· ῥίζαν μικρὰν, λεπτὴν, ἐπιπόλαιον.</note> *Phyteuma has leaves similar to the Struthium <note>Likely Soapwort (*Saponaria officinalis*)</note>, but smaller; much perforated fruit; and a small, thin root, clinging to the surface of the earth.* This description fits the Wild Snapdragon most aptly and properly. For as far as the leaves are concerned, it certainly has those of a smaller *Struthium*: since, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist and author</note> says, *Struthium* has a leaf like a small Olive tree; for indeed its leaves are narrower and smaller than the olive...

<summary>This page describes the "Wild Snapdragon" (Misopates orontium), detailing its physical appearance, its resemblance to a human skull, and its habitat in Europe. It also debates the plant's historical identity, comparing it to the "Orontium" of Galen and the "Phyteuma" of Dioscorides.</summary>
<keywords>Wild Snapdragon, Phyteuma, Galen, Dioscorides, medicinal herbs, jaundice, plant anatomy, botanical history, Misopates orontium</keywords>

# ON THE WILD SNAPDRAGON (CONTINUED).

<margin>fruit <note>original Greek: καρπός [karpos]</note></margin>
...smaller. It also has abundant fruit or <gloss>fruit <note>original Greek: καρπόν [karpon]</note></gloss>, and it is perforated; this is because that entire little head in which the seed is contained is taken for the fruit, which not infrequently—just like the head of the Snapdragon itself—is called <term>fruit <note>Greek: *karpos*</note></term>. Regarding the roots, there can be no doubt, as they are exactly the kind attributed to the **Phyteuma**. For these reasons, it appears that this wild Snapdragon is the true **Phyteuma** <note>A plant mentioned by ancient authorities, whose identity was often debated by Renaissance botanists</note>. **Matthioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist</note>, however, does not depict this Snapdragon (which he formerly presented as the legitimate species) as the **Phyteuma**, but instead illustrates a Bellflower; we have indicated the reasons for both the rejection of Matthioli’s opinion and our own judgment regarding this above in Chapter 10.

**Furthermore**, this wild Snapdragon is indeed of no practical use; however, if it is the true **Phyteuma**, it may be beneficial for love matters. For as **Dioscorides** <note>The primary Greek authority on herbal medicine in antiquity</note> records, **Phyteuma** is said to be useful for love potions.

->ON TOADFLAX. CHAPTER 26.<-

**Certain species** *also appear to be types of Snapdragon, which are commonly called Toadflax <note>original: "Linarias", a name derived from "Linum" (flax), due to the similarity of their leaves</note>.*

<margin>1</margin>
**OF THESE**, *one has stems about three feet long <note>original: "bicubitales," or two cubits</note>, which are numerous, round, and thin: it has many leaves, narrow and oblong, similar to the leaves of flax: yellow flowers grow along the stems near the top, which in the front part are indeed similar to the flowers of the Snapdragon, but in the back part they end in a tail or a certain little horn, resembling the Larkspur <note>original: "floris Regij," literally "Royal flower," commonly referring to Consolida or Delphinium</note>; these are followed by a round seed head in which the seed is black: the root is long, thin, and white.*

<margin>2</margin>
**A second** *species is similar to this one in its stem, leaves, and flowers: yet the stems are generally longer; the leaves are narrower; and the flowers are small, much smaller than the other variety, of a bluish-purple color, sometimes even whitish or yellowish; it has similarly tiny seed heads and thin, fibrous roots.*

->DE AMARANTO PVRPVREO.<- <note>This header, "On Purple Amaranth," appears at the top of the page, though the text immediately following it concludes the previous section on Toadflax.</note>

The first kind of *Toadflax* <note>original Latin: *Linaria*</note> grows everywhere along the edges of fields and in other places; the second grows in similar regions but is found more rarely. They bloom in July and August.

**Both species** are called **Toadflax** by our people, and **Urinal-herb** by some. The Germans call it **Flax-herb, Bottle-herb, Urine-herb, Our Lady’s flax, Wood flax,** or **Toad flax** <note>original German: *Lynkraut / flaschkraut / harnkraut / unser frauwẽ flaschz / waldt flaschz / krotten flaschz*</note>. The Belgians call it **Wild flax** <note>original Belgian: *wilt vlas*</note>; the French, **Wild flax** <note>original French: *Lin sauuage*</note>. Many believe this plant to be the **Osyris** <note>a plant mentioned by ancient authors like Pliny</note>; however, *Osyris* is a different plant from which they make **brooms** <note>original Greek: *κορήματα* (korēmata)</note> in Italy today, just as they did in the time of **Galen** <note>a prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note>; it is commonly called **Belvedere**.

**Toadflax** is warm and somewhat dry in temperament and possesses the power to open [the body's passages]; for, as the <term>root-cutters</term> <note>original Latin: *Rhizotomi*; historical term for herbalists who gathered and prepared medicinal plants</note> of our time believe, a decoction of it promotes urination. Many add that it benefits those suffering from the **Royal Disease** <note>original Latin: *Regio morbo*; a traditional name for jaundice, likely referring to the golden color of the skin or the expensive remedies required to treat it</note>, a property that was attributed to this plant because it was confused with *Osyris*.

## ON THE PURPLE AMARANTH.
### CHAPTER XXVII.

The **Purple Amaranth** rises on a stem about a cubit <note>approximately 18 inches</note> high or more. The stem is grooved, often reddish near the root, smooth, and divided into wing-like branches. Around the stem are leaves that are oblong, broad, pointed, soft, and smooth; they are green in color, or sometimes a reddish-green. In place of flowers, spikes emerge that are very pleasing to the eye, though odorless. They shine with a brilliant purple, resembling deep-dyed crimson that glimmers like plush velvet—indeed, in the beauty of their color, they almost surpass it. When rubbed, they produce a juice of nearly the same color. Once picked, they retain their beauty for a very long time, not withering even for several years; this is how the plant earned its name <note>the name Amaranth comes from the Greek *amarantos*, meaning "unfading"</note>. Small, black, and very shiny seeds sit within the ripened spikes. The roots are short and fibrous.

<sig>F 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Mirum</meta>

# CONCERNING THE PURPLE AMARANTH. <note>original Latin: *DE AMARANTO PVRPVREO*</note>

*It is a marvelous thing about this plant, Pliny says, that it enjoys being plucked and grows back more joyfully. The palm of excellence belongs to the Alexandrian variety, which is preserved once plucked. And it is wonderous that, after all other flowers have failed, it revives when moistened with water and makes for winter garlands.*

*Pliny calls it "Unfading" <note>Greek: ἀμάραντον (*amaranton*)</note> because it does not wither; our age calls it Purple Amaranth, to distinguish it from Gold-flower <note>Latin: *Elichrysum*</note>, which is likewise called "unfading," about which we will speak below in its own place. In German it is called Velvet-flower <note>original: *Samatblumen*</note>, Thousand-beauties <note>original: *dausentschoon*</note>, and Floramo. In our own dialect [Flemish], Velvet-flowers <note>original: *Flouweelbloemen*</note>. In Italian, Velvet flower <note>original: *Fior velluto*</note>. In French, Surpasses-velvet <note>original: *Passe velours*</note>, which Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel, a 16th-century French physician and botanist</note> renders as *Passivellutum*, a name the French use for a shaggy scarlet silk velvet, with which this plant competes for supremacy of color.*

*There is indeed another species of this plant, with a much taller <gloss>2</gloss> and thicker stem, four or five cubits <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches, making the plant 6 to 7.5 feet tall.</note> or even higher; with similarly larger leaves; its spikes are longer, but of a very dull red. Both Amaranths are sown in gardens, but the former more frequently. For the other Amaranth possesses little or no charm at all.*

The spikes APPEAR in the month of August and last into Autumn. Both are tasteless as potherbs <note>leafy vegetables intended for the cooking pot</note>, and are species of Blite <note>A group of plants in the amaranth family, often used like spinach</note>, about which we will speak in the history of Potherbs, where its specific properties will be given.

Most RECENT authors attribute to the Purple Amaranth, and especially the first kind, the power of binding <term>astringency: the ability to shrink or constrict body tissues</term> along with coldness and dryness, and the strength to stop every flow of blood; yet no quality shows these powers to be in it, except perhaps for the color alone, by which the spikes are red. For it is the opinion of some that all red things restrain and suppress blood flowing from anywhere, because certain things that suppress blood happen to be of such a color. But this opinion is as vain as it is unsupported by any probable reasons. For from colors, nothing certain can be gath-

<meta>catchword: ered [Latin: ligere]</meta>

<summary>This page describes the Purple Amaranth, noting its ability to "revive" with water and its various names across Europe, while debunking the contemporary medical belief that its red color implies an ability to stop bleeding.</summary>
<keywords>Purple Amaranth, Pliny the Elder, botanical naming, velvet flower, Ruellius, plant morphology, medicinal skepticism, signatures of color, astringency</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the medicinal properties of the Purple Amaranth before transitioning into a new chapter dedicated to the Rose. The previous page discussed the physical varieties of Amaranth; here, the author refutes the idea that a plant's color (red) automatically dictates its medicinal use (blood-related) before beginning an extensive botanical and cultural history of roses.</meta>

...one may gather: as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–216 AD), an influential Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> clearly shows in the first and fourth books of *On the Faculties of Simple Medicines*. For there are many red things that are also hot and opening, such as the red root of Madder <note>original: *Rubia*</note>, which draws blood through the urine and the menstrual flow. For this reason, those who judge by color alone reason incorrectly when they claim that Purple Amaranth suppresses blood and aids those with abdominal or dysenteric troubles.

## CONCERNING ROSES. CHAPTER XXVIII.

Although the stock of **Roses** is shrubby and thorny, it is nevertheless more fitting and decent to join it with flowers than to associate it with thorny shrubs: for the **Rose** easily holds the primary place among all flowers, and its use in all garlands is nearly excessive; and, as Henri Estienne <note>A famous 16th-century French printer and classical scholar.</note> rendered in a most elegant Latin poem from Anacreon of Teos <note>A Greek lyric poet (c. 582 – c. 485 BC) famous for his drinking songs and hymns to love.</note>, the very ancient Greek poet:

->**Rose**, the honor and glory of flowers.<-
->**Rose**, the care and love of Spring.<-
->**Rose**, the delight of the gods.<-
->The boy of Cythera <note>Referring to Cupid, the son of Venus (Cytherea).</note> entwines<-
->His head with **rosy** garlands,<-
->Joining the dances of the Graces.<-

There are, however, very many kinds of **Roses**, differing either by the size of the bush itself, its roughness or smoothness; or by the number of petals <note>The original Latin *folia* (leaves) was commonly used for petals in this period.</note> in the flower, whether many or few, or by their color and scent. Some are indeed tall and lofty, others short and low: some have five petals, others twelve, twenty, or more. Theophrastus <note>Ancient Greek philosopher (c. 371 – c. 287 BC), often called the "Father of Botany."</note> even mentions a certain "hundred-petaled" rose <note>Greek: ἑκατοντάφυλλαν (*hekatontaphyllan*)</note> that grows around Philippi <note>An ancient city in Macedonia.</note>: which the inhabitants say they bring from Mount Pangaeus: and Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of the *Natural History*.</note> mentions the same in Campania <note>A region in southern Italy.</note>. Likewise, some are red in color, while others are white: also fragrant...

### ON ROSES.

...[and] very many others, some being without any scent. Of all these, some are **cultivated** and **garden** varieties; others truly are **wild**.

The **BRANCHES**, or rather the stalks, are hard and woody in all varieties, bristling with biting thorns; indeed, the wild varieties are even thornier. The leaves are rough and serrated <note>Saw-toothed along the edges.</note> around their perimeter, with five or seven leaflets growing together from a single midrib. The flowers emerge from the tips of the small stems, initially enclosed in a grass-green calyx <note>The protective outer layer of a flower bud.</note>, which swells and tapers into green buds. As these gradually open, the flower unfolds. In the center of its calyx are hair-like **stamens** with yellow-tipped anthers. The **seed** is produced in oblong seed-heads <note>The "rose hip."</note> situated beneath the calyces and flowers; these turn red as they mature and contain hard seeds wrapped in a fuzzy down. The **roots** are hard, woody, and spread out in various directions.

<margin>1</margin>
In the assessment of the cultivated varieties, one surpasses the rest in the height of its stalks or branches. With its thick and woody stems, it climbs to ten or twelve feet, or even higher. Its flowers are white, fragrant, and possess many petals, of which the inner ones are smaller, while the outer ones are larger and broader.

<margin>2</margin>
The next in size has shorter stalks, but they are no less rough. These roses are sweet-scented but have a diluted and pale red color; the Italians call them *Incarnate* <note>original: *Incarnatas*. Literally "flesh-colored," referring to a pale pink or blush hue.</note>.

<margin>3</margin>
The third is similar to the second in its branches and leaves, but somewhat shorter. Its flower, however, is the most fragrant and beautiful, shining with a deep and somewhat scarlet redness. It is the most excellent of all roses and the most useful for almost every kind of **medicine** <term>medicamenta: refers to apothecary preparations like rose water, oils, or syrups</term>.

<margin>4</margin>
The fourth is shorter, with stalks about two cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, approximately 18 inches (45 cm).</note> or more in height. The flower is elegantly red, though less so than the third variety. Sometimes it is simple, having only five petals, but at other times it has many; it may even be "double" <note>Flowers with extra layers of petals, often a result of cultivation.</note>. Its scent is pleasing and very delightful.

<meta>Catchword: FIFTH</meta>

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical description of the rose's anatomy, followed by a classification of four specific cultivated varieties, ranging from the tall white rose to the medicinal red rose.</summary>

->## WILD ROSE<-
<note>original: *ROSA SYLVESTRIS*. In this period, this name typically referred to the Dog Rose (*Rosa canina*) or other native wild roses found in the European countryside.</note>

*This botanical woodcut illustrates the Wild Rose plant in its entirety, showcasing its growth from its woody, spreading roots to the delicate flowers at the tips of its stems. The plant’s characteristically thick, hardy branches are covered in the sharp, hooked thorns described in the text, designed for both protection and climbing. The leaves are shown as pinnately compound <note>Leaves arranged on either side of a central stalk.</note> with serrated edges.*

*The artist has depicted the plant’s reproductive cycle in various stages: tight, pointed buds; blossoms beginning to open; and five-petaled flowers in full bloom, revealing their central stamens. On the right-hand branch, there is a prominent mossy growth known as a bedeguar gall <note>original: *bedeguar*. Often called a "Robin's pincushion," these hairy red galls are caused by the larvae of a gall wasp and were historically used in medicinal preparations to treat various ailments.</note>. Below the flowers, the oblong seed-heads, or "hips," are visible, which turn red and contain the seeds when mature.*

<meta>Following the botanical illustration on page 95, this page continues the systematic description of rose varieties, moving from garden hybrids to the various wild species found in Europe.</meta>

->## ON ROSES<-

<margin>5</margin> The **fifth** garden variety of Rose resembles the wild Dog Rose in its branches and stems. It produces many small roses clustered together; they are mostly <term>simple</term> <note>Flowers with a single row of petals, usually five, as opposed to "double" flowers with many layers.</note> but occasionally, though rarely, they are double. They have a most fragrant and sweet scent, very much like musk, which is why they are called "Musk Roses" <note>original: *Rosae Moschettae*</note> by the Tuscans <note>People from Tuscany, Italy.</note>.

<margin>6</margin> Among the **WILD** varieties, there is one commonly called the Dog Rose <note>original: *Canina Rosa*; this is the most common wild rose in Europe, known for its hard, hooked thorns.</note>, which is very familiar in Belgium. With its thorny stems and rough leaves, it is similar to the cultivated roses, though each part is smaller. The flower is simple, not exceeding five petals, and is whitish or a pale purplish-white. Sometimes a certain hairy little ball grows on its stems, appearing somewhat like the prickly husk of a chestnut but soft; this they call the "Rose Sponge" <note>original: *Spongiola Rosae*. These are mossy-looking galls produced by the larvae of a tiny wasp.</note>.

<margin>7</margin> Similar to this is another species, though somewhat smaller, whose stems are covered in a reddish bark; the flowers are a pale red and are not entirely without scent. It is called the "Cinnamon Rose" <note>original: *Cinamomina Rosa*</note> by some.

<margin>8</margin> Among the wild varieties, some also bear **yellow-colored** Roses.

<margin>9</margin> There is also a small, very low-growing variety with extremely thorny stems, tiny leaves, and very small, white, sweetly scented roses.

<margin>10</margin> Now, a certain wild rose is also found with **fragrant leaves**; this one often surpasses all other wild varieties in height. It has hard, thick, and woody stems; the leaves are shiny and a beautiful green with a very pleasant scent <note>This refers to the Sweet Briar or Eglantine (*Rosa rubiginosa*), famous for leaves that smell like green apples when bruised.</note>. The roses, however, are quite small with five petals, most often whitish and rarely purplish, with little or no scent. The fruit is oblong and reddish in color, similar in shape to a small olive <note>original: *Oliua*</note> or the seed-heads of other roses, but smaller than those of the garden varieties. Inside the fruit are woolly fibers, and hidden among them is the seed, which is small and hard. Around the thin stems of this plant, one also finds round, soft, and hairy—

<meta>catchword: and hairy—</meta>

<keywords>Musk Rose, Dog Rose, Cinnamon Rose, Sweet Briar, rose hips, botanical classification, Tuscany, Belgium, thorns</keywords>

# ON ROSES

...and a hairy little sponge <note>original: *hirsuta spongiola*; these are "rose bedeguar galls" caused by gall wasps, which were historically used in medicine.</note>, exactly like those that grow around the thorns of the Dog Rose. And our age has discovered just as many types of Roses as these.

**THERE ARE** indeed many and very numerous kinds mentioned in **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman naturalist whose *Natural History* was the standard reference for botany in the Renaissance.</note> (Book 21, Chapter 4), called by their own names; however, not all of them grow on a bramble—that is, a thorny shrub—for even flowers from Mallows <note>original: *Maluis*; Pliny grouped plants by appearance rather than modern genetic relationships.</note> are counted in the list of Roses. It is likely that some of these correspond with our types, though not all of them. He says that our people have made the most famous varieties the Praenestine <note>From Palestrina, Italy.</note> and the Campanian <note>From the Campania region of Italy.</note>. Others have added the Milesian rose <note>From Miletus in Asia Minor.</note>, which has a most fiery color and no more than twelve petals <note>original: *folia*; in early botanical Latin, this word was often used for petals rather than green leaves.</note>; next to it is the Trachinian, which is less red; then the cheaper Alabandic, with whitish petals. The cheapest variety, however, which has many but very tiny petals, is the little thorny one <note>original: *spineola*</note>.

Then, a little further down: There is also one called the "Greek" by our people, and "Lychnis" <note>original: *Lychnis*; a name used for various bright flowers, likely a type of Campion or a bright red rose.</note> by the Greeks; it grows only in damp places, never has more than five petals, is the size of a violet, and has no scent. There is another called the "Little Greek" <note>original: *Græcula*</note>, with its petals twisted into tufts; it does not open unless forced by hand and always looks like it is just beginning to sprout, having very wide petals. Another grows from a mallow-like stem, having leaves like garden vegetables; they call it *Mosceuton* <note>From the Greek for "shrub-like."</note>. Between these in size is the Autumn rose, which they call *Coroneola* <note>The "garland rose," named for its use in weaving crowns or wreaths.</note>. All are without scent, except for the *Coroneola* and the one born on the bramble.

**THE GENTLER** and cultivated Roses are sown and planted in gardens among the Belgians, Germans, French, and elsewhere. Wild roses grow of their own accord in many places; among these, the first <margin>6</margin> <note>variety #6: The Dog Rose.</note> appears everywhere near ditches and trenches, at the edges of fields <margin>8</margin> <note>variety #8: The Yellow Rose.</note> and meadows; it grows in damp places. The yellow rose is found in Africa and Egypt, which many now keep in gardens, as they do the <margin>9</margin> Cinnamon Rose. The smallest, most thorny, and roughest kind <margin>10</margin> <note>variety #10: Likely the Burnet Rose (*Rosa spinosissima*).</note> is born in coastal regions; it is common in the territory of the Morini <note>A coastal tribe of the ancient Netherlands and Northern France.</note>, near the port of Itius <note>original: *Icium portum*; a Roman port likely located at modern-day Boulogne or Wissant.</note>, on the sandy embankments of the sea. But the scented wild—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rose varieties, moving from the specific types described by Pliny to the practical methods of cultivation and the environmental factors that affect the quality and scent of the flowers. # ON ROSES

...[the rose in] your [regions] is the garden Rose, and nowhere in France or Belgium, as far as we know, does it grow wild.

**FOR CULTIVATION**, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), whose *Natural History* was the primary source for botanical knowledge in the Renaissance.</note> says, the Rose does not desire rich, clayey, or soggy soil; it is content with sparse earth and particularly loves a rubbled <note>original: *ruderatum*; soil mixed with debris or crushed stone, which provides good drainage.</note> field. All roses are more fragrant in dry places than in humid ones. The temperament of the sky also matters; for in some years, they come forth less scented. **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–c. 287 BC), known as the "Father of Botany," was a student of Aristotle.</note> affirms that the excellence of scent and color also depends on the nature of the location; and he notes that the most fragrant Roses are those that grow at **Cyrene** <note>An ancient Greek and Roman city in modern-day Libya.</note>; and consequently, the most pleasant ointment is made there.

**THE ROSE GROWS** indeed from seed; but because it develops slowly, it is instead planted from a cut stem. It benefits from every pruning and burning, which makes it bring forth its flower better; for if left alone, it overgrows and turns wild. It is useful to transplant it often: for in this way, they affirm a more beautiful Rose is produced; **Theophrastus** and **Pliny** are the authorities for this.

**THE METHOD FOR SOWING** Roses is shown by **Palladius** <note>Rutilius Taurus Aemilianus Palladius, a 4th-century AD writer on agriculture.</note>. He says that if you are lacking plants and wish to have a large rose garden from a few twigs, you should cut off little "forks" four fingers long that are budding with their own "knees" <note>original: *geniculis*; these are the nodes or joints on a stem where new growth emerges.</note>, and lay them down in the manner of a graft, aiding them with manure and watering. When they have completed a year of age, transplant them separated by the space of a foot, and thus fill the soil that you have designated for this kind.

**ROSES BLOOM**; some earlier, others later, but all in May or the beginning of June. The early ones are the Cinnamon Roses <note>original: *Cinamominæ*; likely *Rosa majalis*, known for its spicy scent.</note> and certain other wild types; the later ones are the cultivated varieties. The Musk Roses <note>original: *Moschettæ*; likely *Rosa moschata*.</note> bloom even a second time in the month of August. **Theophrastus** testifies that if the autumn is long-lasting, Roses can even break out into bloom once more.

**THE ROSE** is called *rhodon* <note>original Greek: ῥόδον</note> by the Greeks. The bush itself of the Roses is called *rhodonia* <note>original Greek: ῥοδωνία</note>, which among the Latins retains the same name as the flower.

<keywords>Pliny the Elder, Theophrastus, Palladius, rose cultivation, soil quality, pruning, Cinnamon Rose, Musk Rose, Greek terminology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the rose, specifically focusing on its nomenclature and the terminology for its various parts (stamens, petals, sepals, and stems). # ON ROSES

...retains <note>This sentence fragment continues from the previous page's discussion on the rose's properties.</note>. *It is called a rose <note>original: ῥόδον (rhodon)</note>, as Plutarch <note>Plutarch (c. 46–120 AD), a Greek philosopher and biographer.</note> says, because it releases a great stream <note>original: ῥεῦμα (rheuma)</note> of scent; that is, because it emits a large outpouring of fragrance.*

The CENTER of the rose flower, namely the yellow stamens and tips, is called the *flower of the roses* <note>original: ἄνθος τῶν ῥόδων (anthos tōn rhodon)</note> and the flower of the Rose; in pharmacies, it is known as the **Anthera** <note>The pollen-bearing part of the stamen, still used in botanical English today.</note>.

The white parts of the PETALS of the flower itself, where they are inserted into the calyxes, are called **claws** <note>original: ὄνυχες (onyches) in Greek, *ungues* in Latin. This refers to the narrow, pale base of a rose petal.</note>.

The **CALYX** is the name for that which embraces and contains the stamens and the small petals of the flower.

The **ALABASTRI** <note>The sepals that form the protective outer layer of a flower bud.</note> are truly the fringed parts of the calyx, embracing the cone of the flower before it opens. There are five of these: two are bearded, two are beardless, and the fifth is partly bearded and partly not at all; many people call them the "shucks" <note>original: *cortices* (barks or rinds)</note> of the Roses.

**Heads and little heads** <note>original: Κεφαλαὶ καὶ κεφάλια (Kephalai kai kephalia)</note> are the names for the seed-vessels in which the seed is produced, which are situated beneath the flowers and the calyxes.

<margin>Viburnum</margin>
The SHOOTS <note>original: *virgas* (wands or rods)</note> of the rose stalk are named **Viburna** by **Strabus Gallus** <note>Walafrid Strabo (c. 808–849 AD), a Carolingian scholar whose poem *Hortulus* (The Little Garden) is a foundational work of medieval botany.</note> in his *Little Garden*, writing as follows:

> *Now unless a longer path hindered me in my weariness,*
> *And the stony order of a new poem were being worn down,*
> *I ought to have surrounded the Viburna of the Rose with the metal*
> *Of Pactolus <note>A river in ancient Lydia known for its gold-bearing sands; a metaphor for great wealth.</note>, and with the snowy gems of the Arabs.*

However, **Virgil** <note>The great Roman poet (70–19 BC).</note> and **Aurelius Nemesianus** <note>A Roman poet of the late 3rd century AD.</note> suggest that the *viburnum* <note>Likely the Wayfaring tree (*Viburnum lantana*), a flexible shrub used in basketry, rather than a rose shoot.</note> is a particular shrub of its own kind—small, low-lying, flexible, and submissive. The former, in his *first Eclogue*, praising the city of **Rome**, says:

> *But this city has raised its head as high among other cities,*
> *As the Cypresses are wont to do among the flexible Viburna.*

**Nemesianus**, in his *second Eclogue*, writes these verses:

> *We also, because of you, Donace, shall be gifted to the city,*
> *If only it is permitted for the Hazel to grow green among the cone-bearing Cypresses,*
> *Or among the Pines among the Viburna.*

# ON ROSES

1 **Among** the number of cultivated Roses, the first is commonly called the White Rose; the Belgians <note>The author uses *Belgae* to refer to Dutch speakers of the Low Countries.</note> call them **White Roses** <note>original Dutch: *Witte Roosen*</note>; the Germans, **White Roses** <note>original German: *weißz Rosen*</note>; and the French call them the **White Rose** <note>original French: *blanche Rose*</note>. This may be Pliny’s <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman naturalist whose *Natural History* was the standard botanical reference for centuries.</note> "Thorny Rose" <note>original: *Spineola Rosa*</note>, provided it is not the "Campanian Rose."

2 **The second**, which Italy calls the **Flesh-colored Rose** <note>original: *incarnatam Rosam*</note>, is similar to the Alabandic rose; the Germans call them **Flesh-colored Roses** <note>original German: *Liebfarbige Rosen*, referring to a pale pink or skin-tone hue.</note>.

3 **The third** is called the **Provincial Rose** by the French; one may suspect, not without reason, that this is the "Milesian Rose" <note>A variety famous in antiquity from Miletus.</note>.

4 **The fourth**, the French call **French Roses** <note>original: *Rosas Franchas*</note>; Pliny might perhaps have called these the "Trachinian" or the "Praenestine" rose.

5 **The fifth** is the **Musk Rose** <note>original: *Moschata*</note>, named, as we have said, for its scent of musk; it may be the "Coroneola" rose, for the *Coroneola* is an autumn rose according to Pliny. Some also call this the "Damascene Rose" <note>The Damask Rose.</note>; the Italians call it the **Musk Rose** <note>original Italian: *Rosam moschettam*</note>; the French, **Musk or Muscadelle Roses** <note>original French: *Roses musquées ou muscadelles*</note>; the Belgians, **Musk Roses** <note>original Dutch: *Musket Roosen*</note>.

6 **The Wild** Rose is called **Wild Rose** or **Hedge-rose** by the Germans <note>original German: *wilder Rosen / heckrosen*</note>; by the Brabanters <note>Inhabitants of the Duchy of Brabant, in the modern-day Netherlands and Belgium.</note>, **Wild Roses** <note>original Dutch: *wilde Roosen*</note>; and by the French, **Wild Roses** <note>original French: *Roses sauuages*</note>. In Pliny (Books 8 and 25), it is the **Dog-rose** <note>original Greek: *κυνόροδος* (kynorodos)</note> or **Dog-roses**, that is, the *Rosa Canina*; some also call it the "Dog-brier" <note>original: *Canina sentis*</note>.

Truly, those hairy little balls <note>These are rose galls, caused by the larvae of the gall wasp.</note> found around the twigs and branches of this Dog-rose are most aptly and properly called "Little Sponges of the Wild Rose." The apothecary shops have wrongly called them **Bedeguar**. For to the Arabs, *Bedeguar* is a species of Thistle, which the Greeks call the **White Thorn** <note>original Greek: *ἄκανθα λευκὴ* (akantha leuka)</note>.

7 **Other** wild Roses are also species of Dog-rose; 8 including that which the Belgians have named the **Cinnamon Rose** <note>original Dutch: *Caneelrooskens*</note>; 9 as well as the yellow African rose; and also the smallest one, called by our people the **Dune Rose** <note>original Dutch: *Duynrooskens*</note>, named for the sandy mounds facing the sea where they grow spontaneously, which are commonly called "Dunes."
10 **There is** also a species of Dog-rose, or Rose-canina, that is scented,

# ON ROSES

This species, which our people call **Eglantier** or **Aglantier**, and the French call **Englenterium** <note>original: *Englenterium*</note>—according to the testimony of Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537) was a French physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides and helped bridge ancient and Renaissance botany.</note>—is suspected by him to be the *Cynosbaton* <note>From the Greek *kynosbatos*, literally "dog-brier."</note> or **Dog-brier** <note>original Latin: *Canirubum*</note>. Regarding this, Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician whose *De materia medica* was the foundational text for botany for 1,500 years.</note> writes in these words:

<margin>Dog-brier <note>original: *Cynosbatos*</note></margin>
> The Dog-brier, which some call the sharp-thorned bush <note>original Greek: *oxyacantha*</note>. It is a shrub much larger than the common bramble, growing like a tree; it bears leaves much wider than those of the myrtle. It has strong thorns along the branches, a white flower, and an oblong fruit similar to an olive pit, which turns reddish-yellow when it ripens; the interior is woolly.
*That is: The Dog-brier, or Dog-bush, which some call the Sharp-thorn, is a shrub far larger than the Bramble, tree-like in form; it bears leaves wider than the myrtle: strong thorns around the branches: a white flower: an oblong fruit, like the pit of an olive, reddish when mature, and downy inside.*

**There also exists** a description of the Dog-brier in the works of Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC), a student of Aristotle often called the "Father of Botany."</note> of this kind:
> The Dog-brier has a reddish fruit, very similar to that of the pomegranate; it is intermediate between a tree and a shrub, and is similar to pomegranates, and the leaf is shining <note>original Greek: *aglodes*</note>.
<margin>Restored passage of Theophrastus.</margin>
(*The common edition has the word "unknown" <note>original Greek: *agnodes*</note>, but Athenaeus <note>Athenaeus of Naucratis (c. 200 AD), a Greek rhetorician whose works preserve many fragments of lost older texts.</note>, quoting Theophrastus, more correctly reads "shining" <note>original Greek: *aglodes*</note>*) that is: *The Dog-brier has a sub-red fruit, similar to a pomegranate: it is halfway between a tree and a shrub, like the pomegranate tree: the leaf, moreover, is shining.*

**Since** each of these descriptions seems to fit the **Scented Dog Rose** <note>The author is likely describing *Rosa rubiginosa*, known today as Sweetbrier or Eglantine.</note> quite well. For it is a shrub larger than a bramble, having thick woody stems or rods like a pomegranate or some other woody and tree-like plant, armed with hard prickles and thorns. Its leaf is not indeed a single piece, but each individual part (being a part of the whole compound leaf) is wider than myrtle leaves and is smooth and shining on the upper side. The fruit is similar to an olive pit, which, when ripe, is red in color, containing woolly tufts and seeds within. All these features show the similarity of this wild and scented Rose to the *Cynosbaton*.

# ON ROSES

<margin>
A corrected passage of Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), the Roman author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*.</note>.
</margin>
There is indeed another Dog-brier <term>Cynosbatus<gloss>from the Greek *kynosbatos*, literally "dog-bush"</gloss></term>, which Pliny describes in Book 24, Chapter 14, which others call *Cynospaston* <note>original: *Cynospaston*</note> (though Cornarius <note>Janus Cornarius (1500–1558), a German humanist physician known for his editions of Greek medical texts.</note> reads it as *Cynapauxin* <note>original: *Cynapauxin*</note> based on an ancient manuscript), and still others call *Neuropaston* <note>original: *Neuropaston*, meaning "strung with nerves."</note>. It has a leaf similar to a human footprint. It also bears a black grape-like fruit, which has a fiber <term>neruum<gloss>literally "nerve," referring here to a thread-like or fibrous part within the seed</gloss></term> inside the berry; for this reason, it is called *Neuropastos*. But the scented wild rose (provided the description is legitimate and not corrupted) is by no means similar to this Dog-brier, just as it is not similar to Capers nor to the Prickly Ivy <term>Smilaci asperæ<gloss>Smilax aspera</gloss></term>, both of which are also called Dog-brier by some authors.

But let us return to Roses; the petals of these flowers, because they consist of various parts, also possess different medicinal properties. For there are in them certain terrestrial and astringent parts; others that are moist and watery; and some spiritual and airy parts, which nevertheless do not behave the same way in every individual flower. Indeed, in one kind these qualities excel, while in another, those predominate. Generally, however, they all have a cold temperament <note>In the humoral theory of the time, plants were classified by their "complexion" or temperament (Hot, Cold, Moist, Dry).</note>, yet close to a moderate temperament, *specifically from the first order of cooling agents.*

*The moist, airy, and spiritual parts predominate in the white, the pale red, and also in the Damask or Musk roses: which Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist.</note> records as being superior in their powers; so much so that even twenty of their petals, when consumed, can loosen the bowels <term>aluum subducant<gloss>acting as a mild laxative</gloss></term>. For that reason, a liquid of most pleasant and sweet scent is extracted from these three, which is almost scentless when taken from other Roses.*

*This distilled water of Roses is useful both for the strengthening of the heart and the restoration of the spirits,* as well as for all things that require gentle cooling. When mixed into many sweets <term>tragematis<gloss>sugar-coated nuts or fruits</gloss></term>, cakes, seasonings, and other things prepared for the sake of provoking pleasure, it imparts a grace of flavor: *it soothes the hot pains of the eyes: it induces sleep:* which even the fresh Roses themselves provoke by their sweet scent.

The JUICE of Roses, especially those of a pale red color, and,

# ON ROSES

*...as some even say, of those shining with a deep purple color, it softens and moves the bowels.*
<note>Parts of the text on this page are underlined in red ink by a later hand.</note>

*The infusion of these same roses performs the same task, as does the Rose-honey or Syrup <term>Drosatum<gloss>A medicinal honey or syrup made from rose infusion; the name relates to the Latin for "dew," *ros*</gloss></term> or <term>Serapium<gloss>A term for a medicinal syrup, likely derived from the Arabic *sharab*</gloss></term> which is cooked from it; the apothecary shops call it "laxative rose syrup" <note>original: *syrupum rosatum solutiuum*</note>, because it must be prepared from an infusion or dilution in which many fresh rose petals have been soaked repeatedly. It is useful for softening or moving the bowels when a more powerful purgative is either not needed or not appropriate. For it does not draw out anything other than the waste sticking to the intestines, or the raw, phlegmatic, and occasionally bilious matter in the nearby and primary veins, unless it is mixed with other more powerful medicines. This syrup also moistens and cools: therefore, it also calms the excessive heat of burning fevers, mitigates inflammation of the internal organs, and extinguishes thirst. However, it is of little use for a weak and moist stomach, for it leaves it laxer and weaker.*

*In the red Roses, which are common everywhere, and others of a deep purple which they call Provincial <note>original: *Prouinciales*; likely referring to the *Rosa gallica*, often called the "Rose of Provins."</note>, there is more of an earthy substance and a drying and astringent quality, though not without some mixture of moisture. This moisture is indeed present while they are still fresh, but it is lost when they are dried. For this reason, their juice and infusion also serve to move the bowels, though less so than the others mentioned before.*

*When these Roses are dried, however, and the moisture is now consumed, they bind and dry. They also cool, but less than when fresh. They bring strength to the heart and help with its trembling and palpitations. They strengthen the liver, kidneys, and other weak internal organs. They dry and invigorate a flaccid, weak, and moist stomach. They restrain the "female flow" <note>original: *fluorem muliebrem*; likely referring to leukorrhea or general discharge.</note> and excessive menstrual purging; of blood...*

<meta>This page continues the description of the medicinal properties of red and Provincial roses, transitioning from their physical qualities (astringency and dryness) to their specific therapeutic applications for skin conditions, internal ailments, and as ingredients in various pharmacological preparations.</meta>

# ON ROSES

...they stop eruptions everywhere; they restrain sweats; they tighten the bowels when they are loose with excessive flow. They are usefully mixed into all antidotes and also other medicines, whether applied externally or taken internally, to which an astringent and strengthening quality is a beneficial addition.

The **ROSE-HONEY** <term>Rhodomeli<gloss>A medicinal preparation made by boiling rose petals with honey.</gloss></term> which is made from them is by far the most useful for wounds, ulcers, eruptions, and generally for all things that require cleansing and drying.

**ROSE OIL** settles all heats and prevents inflammations, or mitigates those already beginning.

The **FLOWERS** of roses—namely the yellow stamens and tips <note>original: *stamina & apices*</note>—dry and bind in a similar way, and indeed more powerfully than the petals of the roses themselves. The calyxes and buds <term>alabastri<gloss>Immature flower buds, so named for their resemblance to small alabaster perfume jars.</gloss></term> can do the same; but since all these lack scent, they are less useful and less agreeable to human nature.

<note>The following sections are underlined in red ink in the original manuscript.</note>
**ROSE HEADS**, according to Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), author of the *Natural History*.</note>, also stop blood and the belly [diarrhea]; the claws <note>original: *vngues*; the white bases of the petals where they attach to the stem</note> are healthy for watery eyes <term>epiphoris<gloss>Excessive tearing or inflammation of the eyes.</gloss></term>.

The **ROOT** of the wild rose is the unique remedy for the bite of a mad dog, discovered by an oracle, as the same author writes in Book 8, chapter 41.

The **LITTLE SPONGES** <term>Spongiolæ<gloss>Mossy galls found on wild roses, caused by gall wasps, commonly known today as "robin's pincushions."</gloss></term> that grow in the midst of its thorns, if their ash is mixed with honey, can fill in the hair lost to <term>Alopecia<gloss>Baldness or patchy hair loss.</gloss></term> of the head, as the same author says in Book 25, chapter 2.

<margin><image-desc>A hand-drawn manicule in red ink points to the beginning of the following paragraph.</image-desc></margin>
**FUCHSIUS** <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a prominent German physician and one of the "fathers of botany."</note> affirms that the little sponges and heads of the wild rose are effective against stones <term>calculum<gloss>Kidney or bladder stones.</gloss></term> and difficulties of urine if they are administered ground into a powder. However, they may be beneficial not as diuretics <note>Medicines that promote the production of urine.</note> or as things that wear down the stone, but like certain other astringents, by strengthening weak kidneys; these are not infrequently more useful to those with stones than many diuretics, especially powerful ones. For by the frequent or excessive use of such diuretics, it happens that the kidneys are much weakened and often become overheated; and the stones are not only not broken up, worn down, or expelled,

...not expelled, but more numerous and harder ones are frequently generated. For they <note>The author refers here to "stronger diuretics."</note> separate that which is thin, aqueous, and serum-like in the blood; but that which is thicker, they condense, and the stronger diuretics cause it to harden: and similarly also other things, through more frequent use.

### ON THE CISTUS. CHAPTER XXIX.

**The Cistus** <term>Cistus<gloss>Commonly known as the Rockrose, a genus of flowering shrubs found in dry, rocky areas.</gloss></term> is seen by some as a species of **Rose**. The ancients identified two kinds of it: male and female, besides a third called *Ledum* <term>Ledum<gloss>Likely refers to *Cistus ladanifer*, the resin-bearing rockrose from which labdanum is harvested.</gloss></term>.
**Both** are woody shrubs, branched with many stems; yet they are low-growing, not very tall, and have thin branches. Their leaves are broad, somewhat wrinkled and woolly <note>original: *lanuginosa*</note>, almost whitish, similar in a way to the leaves of garden Sage or another variety of Sage. The flowers at the top of the twigs are like small wild Roses, yet fleeting and quickly perishing; in color, the male is almost reddish-purple or blue-red, while the female is white. From these come small, somewhat round heads in which the small seed is kept; the roots are woody, split, and stretching in different directions.
**There grows** occasionally under the shrub itself at the roots a certain kind of *Limodorum* called **Hypocistis** <term>Hypocistis<gloss>A parasitic plant (*Cytinus hypocistis*) that grows on the roots of rockroses; it lacks chlorophyll and was historically used for its astringent properties.</gloss></term>, which is thick, fat, fleshy, full of juice, and without leaves. It consists entirely of many parts resembling the small pods of Henbane or the flower buds <note>original: *cytinis*</note> of a pomegranate. Its color in one kind is ruddy <note>original: πυῤῥῷ (*pyrrhō*) or *ruffo*</note> or reddish; in another white; and in yet another green <note>original: χλωρῷ (*chlōrō*) or *virenti*</note> or herbaceous, as Dioscorides <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist from the 1st century AD.</note> says.
**The Cistus grows** in Italy, Spain, Narbonnese Gaul <note>A Roman province located in what is now Southern France.</note>, and in many other warmer provinces of Europe, in dry and rocky places. In one region it may have more abundant leaves, in others sparser, elsewhere whiter and softer, and in most places less white and less hairy. Change...

# ON THE ROCKROSE
<note>original: "DE CISTO"</note>

...for the Rockrose also undergoes some variation according to the diversity of regions. It blooms in the summer months.

The Greeks call it <term>Kiston<gloss>Κίστον</gloss></term> or <term>kisthon</term>, some call it <term>kissaron</term>, and others <term>kitharon</term>. Among the Latins, it is similarly called **Cistus** and **Wild Rose**, and by some even the **Dog Rose**, as the physician Scribonius Largus reports. A growth emerging from the roots is called <term>hypocistis<gloss>ὑποκιστὶς; literally "under the Cistus"</gloss></term>, because it grows at the base of the Rockrose. Some call this <term>rhobethron</term> or <term>orobethron</term>. Others call it <term>kytinos</term>, including Paulus Aegineta; he applies this name not to the plant growing beneath the Rockrose, but to its juice. From this may have come the word *hypoquistidos*, which is the "barbaric" <note>The author uses "barbaric" here to refer to non-classical or corrupt Latin used by medieval pharmacists</note> name the apothecary shops give to this juice.

**PROPERTIES**
Furthermore, as Galen says, the Rockrose dries quite vigorously, being of nearly the second order of drying agents; yet it is so cold that it partakes of only a lukewarm heat. The leaves and first buds, when crushed, are only drying and astringent, such that they are able to bind together ulcers <note>In this context, "ulcers" refers generally to open wounds or sores</note>. The flowers are more effective, so much so that when drunk with wine they heal dysentery, weaknesses of the stomach, and various fluxes or discharges. When applied as a poultice, they assist with the rotting parts of these same ulcers. Dioscorides records that an application of it restrains <term>nomas<gloss>νομάς</gloss></term>—that is, "creeping" or eating sores—and when mixed with wax, it heals burns and long-standing wounds.

**HYPOCISTIS**
The **Hypocistis** <note>A parasitic plant that grows on the roots of the Rockrose</note> is much more astringent and is an effective remedy for all conditions involving "fluxes" <note>An excessive flow of bodily fluids</note>, such as the spitting of blood, excessive menstrual flow, and intestinal or dysenteric disorders. Indeed, if the intent is to strengthen a part of the body that has been weakened by too much moisture, this plant adds strength and firmness quite readily. Thus, it is surely mixed into topical applications for the stomach and liver; it is also added to the **Viper Theriac** <note>A famous ancient "universal" medicine and antidote containing dozens of ingredients, including the flesh of vipers</note> specifically so that it may confirm and strengthen the body, according to Galen.

# ON THE MASTIC ROCKROSE AND LABDANUM. CHAPTER XXX.
<note>original: "DE CISTO LEDO, ET LADANO. CAP. XXX."</note>

**Cistus** Ledon <note>Commonly known today as the Mastic Rockrose or Gum Rockrose.</note> is a small shrub very similar to the *Rockrose*, but with longer, darker, and sticky leaves; the flowers are small and very similar to tiny roses, white in color. The leaves collect a certain fatty, resinous, and fragrant substance, and they do so in the springtime, according to *Dioscorides*; but, according to *Pierre Belon* <note>A 16th-century French naturalist who traveled extensively in the Levant.</note>, an eyewitness <gloss>αὐτόπτης</gloss>, it is gathered in the midst of summer and during the most scorching heat of the Dog Days <note>The 'Dog Days' (dies caniculares) refer to the hottest period of the summer, historically associated with the rising of the Dog Star, Sirius.</note>. This substance is collected from the plants themselves in our time with extraordinary care and diligence, and no less industry; it is no longer combed out from the beards or hair of goats <note>Ancient authors like Herodotus and Pliny claimed that labdanum was collected by scraping it off the fur of goats that had brushed against the shrubs.</note>. Once collected, it is strained and formed into masses to be stored for medicinal uses. On the island of *Crete*, as the same *Belon* testifies, the *Ledon* grows everywhere in wild places; it is also found in *Cyprus*, according to *Pliny*.

The shrub itself is called <term>Ledon<gloss>Λήδον</gloss></term> or <term>ladon<gloss>λάδον</gloss></term>, and it is a species of *Cistus*. However, the fatty substance collected from the leaves is called <term>ladanum<gloss>λάδανον</gloss></term> or <term>ledanon<gloss>λήδανον</gloss></term>. The *Latins* preserve the *Greek* names, though the apothecary shops corruptly pronounce it *Lapdanum*. According to *Dioscorides*, the most highly prized variety is fragrant, yellowish-green <gloss>ὑπόχλωρον</gloss>, easily softened, fatty, free of sand, not easily broken, but resinous.

**In its first** completed degree, it is warm, *Galen* says, possessing also a little astringency <note>Astringency refers to the ability to shrink or constrict body tissues.</note>. In addition to this, it is of a thin substance, and therefore it is softening, and likewise moderately digesting and even concocting <note>In traditional medicine, "concocting" or "coction" refers to the process by which the body's heat ripens or matures morbid humors so they can be expelled.</note>. Therefore, it is no wonder if it is uniquely suitable for ailments of the uterus, especially since it possesses that small amount of astringency mentioned; for this reason, it also prevents hair from falling out. For whatever bad humor settles at the roots of the hair, it consumes it all; then it contracts the pores in which the hair is fixed.

**It has**, says *Dioscorides*, a power that is astringent and warming...

[warming] <note>The text begins mid-sentence; based on the preceding context of Ladanum's properties, the missing word is likely 'warming' (calefacientem).</note>, softening, and opening: it holds back thinning hair when mixed with wine, myrrh, and myrtle oil; it makes scars more beautiful when applied with wine. **FLOWER OF THE SUN.** Furthermore, there is found a certain plant similar to the Mastic Rockrose (*Ledon*), but smaller; it is sometimes creeping unless it is supported by certain stakes: its stems are numerous, thin, and slender: the leaves are oblong, smaller than those of the *Ledon*, fleshy, slightly hairy, and contain a sticky juice: the flowers are tiny, like small roses, but smaller: equal in shape and size to the flowers of *Argentina* <note>The Silverweed (Potentilla anserina).</note>, also called *Potentilla*; they are yellow or white in color: the roots are thin, woody, and reddish. It grows in rough, dry, and sunny places, both in fields and mountainous regions. It flowers in the summer months.

**TRAGVS** <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), a pioneering German botanist.</note> calls it in German **Heyden ysop** <gloss>Heathen hyssop</gloss> and **feld ysop** <gloss>field hyssop</gloss>: most call it in Latin *Gratia Dei* <gloss>the Grace of God</gloss>. There is, however, another herb called *Gratiola* <note>Hedge hyssop.</note> by more recent authors. Valerius Cordus names it *Helianthemum* <gloss>Sun-flower</gloss> and *Flos Solis* <gloss>Flower of the Sun</gloss>.

<margin>Helianthe.</margin> **PLINIVS** records that *Helianthe* <note>original: "Helianthen"</note> grows in the region of Themiscyra and on the coastal mountains of Cilicia, with a leaf like the Myrtle. He writes that the Magi and the Kings of the Persians are anointed with this plant boiled with lion's fat, with added saffron and palm wine, so that the body might become pleasing in appearance: for this reason, the same plant is named *Heliocalliden* <note>original: "Heliocalliden" — from the Greek for 'sun-beauty'.</note>. Whether this is the same as our Flower of the Sun or something different, no one could easily explain given Pliny's brevity. Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and commentator on Dioscorides.</note> reports that *Helianthemum* or Flower of the Sun is thought by some to be *Panaces Chiromum* <gloss>Chiron's all-heal</gloss>, but it has no resemblance to it.

According to the same author, it knits together **WOVNDS**; [blood] ... <meta>catchword: nem (completing "sanguinem")</meta>

## ON THE PEONY. CHAPTER XXXI.

<margin>1</margin> There are three types of **PEONY**: one male and two female.
In the **MALE** Peony, the stems and the stalks of the leaves are red: the leaves are broad and composed of several parts, almost similar to walnut leaves, except that walnut leaves consist of seven or nine leaflets joined together along one central vein or <term>rhachis</term> <note>original: ῥάχιν — the central axis or spine of a compound leaf.</note>; whereas those of the Peony are joined from only five, and occasionally from only three leaflets: the flowers at the tops of the stems are red, like large roses in deep crimson; with purple stamens in the center, from which hang yellow tips: the pods are downy on the outside and open of their own accord when ripe; the seeds within are black, round, and about the size of a vetch-seed <note>original: ochri — a type of wild pea or pulse.</note>; there are also certain grains that shine with a scarlet color: the roots underneath are long, thick, and descend deep, sometimes being split into two or three; they are white inside and fragrant.

<margin>2</margin> The stems of the **FIRST** female Peony have little or no red color: the leaves are irregular, divided much like those of Alexanders <note>original: hyposelinum, which the ancient botanist Theophrastus calls *Smyrnion* — a celery-like herb used in antiquity.</note>, and the individual leaflets are narrower than those of the male, greener on the top side and whiter underneath: the flowers are like those of the male Peony, sometimes a beautiful red and sometimes a pale red: the seeds, in pods resembling almonds, are also similar: but the roots are different and of another sort: for this plant does not have a single root, but many tuberous <note>original: glandulosæ — literally "gland-like," referring here to the swollen, tuber-like roots characteristic of certain peonies.</note> roots descend from a single head.

## ON THE PEONY. CHAPTER XXXI.

<margin>1</margin> There are three types of **PEONY** <note>original: Pæonia</note>: one male and two female <note>In early botany, "male" and "female" designations usually referred to the plant's robust or delicate appearance rather than biological sex.</note>.
In the **MALE** Peony, the stems and the stalks of the leaves are red. The leaves are broad and composed of several parts, almost similar to walnut leaves, except that walnut leaves consist of seven or nine leaflets joined together along one central vein or <term>rhachis</term> <note>original: ῥάχιν — the central axis or spine of a compound leaf.</note>; whereas those of the Peony are joined from only five, and occasionally from only three leaflets. The flowers at the tops of the stems are red, like large roses in deep crimson; with purple <term>stamens</term> <note>The pollen-bearing organs of the flower.</note> in the center, from which hang yellow tips. The pods are downy on the outside and open of their own accord when ripe; the seeds within are black, round, and about the size of a vetch-seed <note>original: ochri — a type of wild pea or pulse.</note>; there are also certain grains that shine with a scarlet color. The roots underneath are long, thick, and descend deep, sometimes being split into two or three; they are white inside and fragrant.

<margin>2</margin> The stems of the **FIRST** female Peony have little or no redness at all... <note>The description of the first female variety begins here, noting its lack of the male variety's characteristic red pigmentation in the stems.</note>
