--- Page 116 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 111, the text continues the physical description of the Male Peony's roots before transitioning into a comparison with female varieties and the differing opinions of ancient authorities.</meta>

...roots, similar to the roots of the true **Asphodel** <note>A genus of perennial plants in the lily family, often associated with the underworld in mythology.</note>, but much thicker and longer; from which often two or three others proceed, of the same form entirely.

3 The **OTHER** female [Peony] has stems and leaves similar [to the male], however the leaves are less irregular, and in their greenness brighter, and on the underside even whiter: the flowers are smaller, of a more diluted red: the roots are also glandular <term>glandular</term> <note>Meaning tuberous or shaped like acorns or glands.</note>, but each is much longer and thinner. **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author and naturalist.</note> seems to have taken this for the male: for to him the roots are many, and each a palm-width long and white, such as he himself attributes to the male. The female, he says, is considered to be that whose roots, or "acorns" <note>original: balani</note>, are longer, with about eight or six adhering: the male has more, since it is not supported by a single root, of a palm's height, and white. But this could also be another species of female Peony, and also considered **Dioscorides'** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and botanist whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical text for centuries.</note> male, if he had not written that the leaf of the male is like a **Walnut** <note>original: nucis iuglandis</note>, and the root is single. For he attributes to his male such a root as is any one of the third Peony, torn from those roots which consist of many individual ones. But nevertheless, from the fact that he declared the leaves of the male Peony to be like the **Royal Nut** <note>original: βασιλικῇ καρύᾳ (basilikē karya)</note>, that is, the walnut, and the root one in number, not seven or eight as he says the female has, he shows that he did not call this third one but the first one the male: which indeed has leaves of the walnut or Royal nut, and a single root.

<margin>Pliny's carelessness.</margin>
**Pliny** also says that the leaves of the female smell of **Myrrh**; but there is an evident suspicion that he took "Myrrh" <note>original: σμύρναν (smyrna)</note> instead of "**Smyrnium**" <note>original: σμυρνίου (smyrnion); an aromatic herb also known as "Alexanders."</note>; or for *Smyrnium*, he rendered "Myrrh" incorrectly.

The **PEONY** grows, as **Dioscorides** left written, in high and steep mountains: **Pliny** truly says, in shady mountains, and in another place in forests: **Apuleius** <note>Referring to Pseudo-Apuleius, the author of a popular 4th-century herbal.</note> says in the mountains of **Crete** and **Sicily**: **Gesner** <note>Conrad Gesner (1516–1565), a Swiss naturalist famous for his encyclopedic works on animals and plants.</note> had heard that it is found on the mountain called **Generoso** in **Switzerland** <note>original: Heluetia</note>: among the **Belgians** and elsewhere in this age it is kept in gardens, and indeed the first female is very common; then the other:

# ON THE PEONY.
->H<-
<meta>catchword: any</meta>

...then: the male is very rare. They all flower in the month of May, and the seed reaches maturity in July.

TO THE GREEKS, it is known as *paionia* <note>original: "παιόνια"</note> and *glykysidē* <note>original: "γλυκυσίδη"</note>: and by some as *pentoboron* <note>original: "πεντόβορον"</note>; similarly to the Latins, it is called *Paeonia* and *dulcisida* [sweet-root]. In the pharmacies, it is *Pionia*; to the Germans, **Peony flowers** / **Peony roses** / **Gout-root** <note>original: "Gichtwurtz"</note> / **King’s flower** <note>original: "Kunigblum"</note> / **Pentecost roses** <note>original: "Pfingstrosen"</note>: to the Belgians, generally **Peonies** <note>original: "Pioenen"</note>: yet to the Flemish, **Mast-flowers** <note>original: "Mastbloemen"</note>. To the French, *Piuoine*: to the Spaniards, *Rosa del monte* [Mountain Rose]. Indeed, it has many other names, rejected among the illegitimate terms, some of which are also found in the writings of Apuleius, such as: *orobelion*, *orobax*, *haimagogon* [blood-bringer], *paisaide*, *menogeneion*, *menion*, *panthikeratos*, *Idaean finger* <note>original: "ἰδαῖος δάκτυλος"</note>, *aglaophotis*, *theodonion*, *selenion*, *selenogonon*, *dichomenion*, *Rosa fatuina* [Fool’s Rose], and *herba casta* [Chaste Herb]. The names *Menogenion* however, as well as *Menion*, *Selenion*, *Selenogonion*, and *Dichomenion*, are derived from *mene* <note>original: "μήνης"</note> and *selene* <note>original: "σελήνης"</note>; both of which words signify the "Moon" to the Latins. Indeed, it can be called the "Lunar" or "Moon Peony" because it assists those suffering from the Comitial disease <term>Comitial disease</term> <note>An ancient term for epilepsy, so-named because the Roman *Comitia* (assemblies) were suspended if a participant had a seizure.</note>, whom most people call "Lunatics." The name "Idaean fingers" <note>original: "Idaei dactyli"</note> fits the female plant, whose tuberous roots resemble those fingers. These "Idaean fingers" are actually certain gemstones shaped like a human thumb, found on the island of Crete. It is called *Aglaophotis*—that is, "shining brightly"—because of its grains that gleam with a scarlet color.

<margin>Aglaophotis.</margin>
Two kinds of **AGLAOPHOTIS** are found described in Aelian’s fourteenth book: one marine (chapter 24) and the other terrestrial (chapter 27).

1 THE MARINE is a species of seaweed <term>fucus</term> <note>Historically referring to various seaweeds or rock-lichens used for dyes.</note> which grows on deep rocks, reaching the size of a Tamarisk <note>original: "Myricae"</note>, with the fruit of a Poppy; this fruit, opening at the Summer Solstice, emits a certain fiery and sparkling splendor at night.

2 THE TERRESTRIAL, which is called by another name, *Cynospastus* [dog-plucked], he says hides during the day among other herbs, and cannot [be seen by any...] <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ullo" (any).</note>

<summary>This page catalogs the diverse nomenclature for the peony across Europe and antiquity, explaining the plant's etymological links to the moon and its use in treating epilepsy ("the lunar disease"). It also introduces the Aglaophotis, a legendary bioluminescent variety of the plant described by Aelian.</summary>

# THE SECOND FEMALE PEONY.
<note>original: "PAEONIA FOEMINA ALTERA"</note>

*This botanical woodcut illustrates the "second" variety of the female peony in its entirety, from root to bloom. At the base of the plant, we see a cluster of thick, tuberous roots. These roots were commonly known in the German tradition as **Gout-root** <note>original: "Gichtwurtz"</note>, as they were frequently used in historical medicine to treat seizures and joint pain. From this root crown, several sturdy stems rise, adorned with dense, deeply lobed leaves. The leaflets are narrow and pointed, distinguishing this variety from the "Male" peony which typically has broader, more rounded leaves.*

*The illustration showcases the plant at the height of its season—the month of May—featuring a large, multi-petaled flower in full bloom at the top. To the right, a secondary bud remains tightly closed, demonstrating the growth stages of the plant. In the following months, usually by July, these flowers would transition into seed pods, which the previous text associated with protective charms and the phases of the moon.*

# ON THE PEONY.

...be recognized in any way; but at night, shining like a star and flashing with fiery splendor, it easily comes into view.

And this terrestrial **Aglaophotis** <note>original: "Aglaophotis" - from the Greek for "bright light," referring to the plant's supposed glowing properties.</note> or **Cynospastus** <note>original: "Cynoſpaſtus" - meaning "dog-dragged," referring to the method of harvest described below.</note> is indeed the Peony; of which even the seeds, red with the color of grain, Apuleius says, shine at night like a lamp, and are most often found and gathered by shepherds at night.

**It is called** indeed this Aglaophotis by Josephus, the writer of the *Jewish War*, Book 7, Chapter 25, **Baara** <note>original: "Baaras" - Josephus describes this as a flame-colored root in the valley of Baaras that was difficult to capture.</note>, from the place where it is found. This is manifest to anyone comparing what Aelian writes of the terrestrial Aglaophotis, or *Cynospastus*, with what Josephus writes of Baara. For Aelian says the *Cynospastus* is not uprooted without danger, and the rumor is that he who, being unskilled in its nature, first touched it, perished: therefore, a marker should be fixed at its roots at night, and a hungry dog tied to them, who, moved by the scent of roasted meat placed before it, should uproot it by its roots in its rush toward them. So too Josephus relates that the Baara shines like a ray of light around evening, and is not easy for those approaching and wishing to uproot it; but either woman’s urine or menstrual blood must be poured over it, or it must be dug around and a dog tied to it, and only thus can it finally be uprooted.

**But all these things are most vain**: for with no trouble and without any danger, the root of the Peony can be gathered and uprooted at any hour of the day or night. For which reason, those things which Theophrastus and Pliny report as being handed down about the Peony are similarly vain: namely, the instruction that it should be gathered at night. For if anyone should gather the fruit by day, it is said his eyes would be in danger from being seen by a **Woodpecker of Mars** <note>original: "Pico martio" - the green woodpecker, which was sacred to the god Mars and said to guard the plant.</note>; but if he should cut the root, there is a danger that his rectum might prolapse.

**It should not seem marvelous**, however, that nonsense and most vain superstitions of this kind, as well as most impious ceremonies, are found scattered here and there in the books of the most ancient authors:

<meta>Catchword: [found...] ...here and there:</meta>

<summary>This page recounts the legendary and superstitious methods of harvesting the peony, including the myth of using a dog to uproot it to avoid death, while the author dismisses these ancient tales as baseless nonsense.</summary>
<keywords>Paeonia, Aglaophotis, Cynospastus, Baara, Josephus, Aelian, superstitions, dog-uprooted, Woodpecker of Mars, botanical folklore</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 116, which dismissed the superstitious and magical harvesting rituals (like using dogs to uproot the plant) as the fabrications of "tricksters" and "men of vanity."</meta>

...men: for there were many things in their times, born of great vanity for the sake of show, as Pliny truly testifies, fashioned by the Egyptians and other tricksters. One follower of these was once the physician Andreas, whom Galen says introduced trickery and lies into the study of medicines; for this reason, Galen instructs that one should abstain from reading him and others of similar falsehood.

# ON THE PEONY.

**Furthermore**, the root of the Peony, as Galen says, is slightly astringent with a hint of sweetness. It also possesses a certain bitter pungency. Its temperament is not significantly hot, being only slightly warmer than a balanced state; however, it is drying and consists of fine parts. For women who have not been cleansed after childbirth, Dioscorides says it is given in dried form. When drunk in the quantity of an almond, it brings on the menses; Galen adds it should be taken in honey-water <note>original: "mulsa" — a mixture of honey and water.</note>. It is beneficial for those with jaundice <note>original: "auriginosis" — from the Latin for "gold," referring to the yellowing of the skin.</note> and those suffering from pains in the kidneys and bladder. It clears an obstructed liver and kidneys, soothes belly pains, and stops discharges when boiled in wine and drunk. Galen discovered through a certain and clear experiment that the root hung from the necks of children is effective against the falling sickness <note>original: "comitialem morbum" — epilepsy, so named because an outbreak of a seizure during a Roman "comitia" or assembly was seen as a bad omen that would end the meeting.</note>. It is also used, says Pliny, against ailments of the mind; four drachms are sufficient for healing. For these uses, the root of the male Peony is preferred.

**The red grains** <note>seeds</note>, 10 or 12 taken in a dry, dark wine, stop the "red flux" <note>excessive menstrual bleeding</note> in women. Eaten, they benefit those with stomach issues who feel gnawing pains. When eaten by children or taken in a drink, they expel the beginnings of stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>.

**The black grains**, specifically from the female plant, 15 in number taken in a drink, help with "nightly suppressions" which they call **ephialtes** <note>original: "ἐφιάλτας" — nightmares or sleep paralysis, which ancient physicians often attributed to a physical weight or "demon" pressing on the chest.</note>, as well as strangulations and pains of the womb. According to Pliny, both types of seeds help those bitten by a snake.

# ON THE WHITE LILY. CHAPTER XXXII.

**Regarding the Fair or White Lily**, which surpasses all others in beauty, luster, and utility, its leaves are oblong, broad, fleshy, and smooth, shining with a grassy and pale green color. The stems grow more than two cubits <note>About three feet or 90 centimeters</note> high, thick with many leaves that gradually taper toward the top, which emerge from between the leaves. The flowers at the summit of the stem are beautiful, white, fragrant, and large, expanding from a narrow base into a wide opening, shaped like a basket <note>original: "calathi forma" — referring to the flared, bell-like shape of the lily</note>. They consist of six small petals <note>original: "foliolis"</note> that are oblong and streaked on the outside, with stamens springing forth from the center, from which yellow tips <note>anthers</note> hang. The root is bulbous, consisting of segments and composed of many parts joined together, full of a slow-moving and sticky juice with which both the leaves and stems are also filled. Lilies are planted everywhere in gardens. However, in Syria and in Pisidia in Pamphylia <note>Regions in modern-day southern Turkey</note>, Dioscorides says those most effective for making ointments grow. Among the Germans, Belgians, and French, the Lily flowers in the month of June; in August the leaves come out, remaining green all through the winter; in Spring, the stem breaks forth.

**The stock of Lilies** is called *krinonia* <note>original: κρινώνια</note> by the Greeks; the flower indeed is called *krinon* <note>original: κρίνον</note> and *leirion* <note>original: λείριον</note>. However, the Narcissus is also called *leirion*. By some, it is called *kalleirion* <note>original: καλλείριον — "beautiful lily"</note> and *krinanthemum* <note>original: κρινάνθεμον — "lily flower"</note>. By the Latins, it is called the *Lilium* and the *Rose of Juno*.

**It is called the Rose of Juno**, however, because it is said to have been born from her milk falling to the earth. For the poets imagine that Hercules, whom Jupiter had sired by Alcmena, was applied to the breasts of the sleeping Juno. After he suckled, a great amount of milk flowed out; one part of it drenched the sky, and the other the clods of the earth. From the latter, Lilies were born; from the former, the Milky Way <note>original: "lacteum circulum"</note> appeared in the heavens. The Germans commonly call this lily *white lily* <note>original: "weiſz gilgen" or "weiſz lilgen"</note>. The Belgians call it *white lilies* <note>original: "witte Lelien"</note>, and the French call it *white lily* <note>original: "Lys blanc"</note>.

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics, growth cycle, and geographical origins of the White Lily (Lilium candidum). It also details the plant's various names in Greek and Latin, including the mythological origin of the flower from the milk of the goddess Juno.</summary>

<keywords>White Lily, Lilium candidum, botanical description, Dioscorides, Juno, Hercules, Milky Way, nomenclature, Syria, horticulture</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the medicinal applications and properties of the White Lily, continuing from the botanical description and illustration on the previous pages. It draws heavily on classical authorities such as Galen, Dioscorides, and Pliny the Elder.</meta>

# CONCERNING THE WHITE LILY.

The flower of the **LILY**, as Galen says, possesses a mixed temperament; it is composed partly of a thin [subtle] essence and partly of an earthy essence.

The **ROOT** and leaves, when ground by themselves, dry and cleanse, and moderately dissipate <term>digerunt: in early medicine, this meant to dissolve or disperse "morbid humors" or swelling</term>.

The **LEAVES** of the plant, Dioscorides says, when applied as a poultice, are helpful against snake bites. These same leaves, when boiled, are beneficial for burns; when preserved in vinegar, they become suitable for healing wounds.

The **JUICE**, mixed with honey and vinegar and cooked in a bronze vessel, creates a medicine useful for old ulcers and fresh wounds.

The **ROOT**, roasted and ground with rose oil <note>original: "rosaceo"</note>, heals burns; it softens the womb and promotes menstruation; it leads ulcers to scarring. When ground with honey, it heals injuries to the nerves and dislocations; it clears away skin spots <note>vitiligines</note> and leprosy; it wipes away dandruff and weeping sores of the head; it cleanses the face and removes wrinkles. When ground with vinegar, henbane leaves <note>original: "hyosciami"</note>, and wheat flour, it soothes inflammations of the testicles. For corns on the feet, Pliny says the roots of the Lily should be boiled in wine and not removed for three days. Boiled with fat or oil, they restore hair to burned areas. Drunk with honey-wine <term>mulsum: a common Roman beverage of wine sweetened with honey</term>, they draw out "useless blood" <note>this likely refers to internal bruising or "corrupt" humors</note> through the bowels; they are beneficial for the spleen, and for those who are ruptured or strained.

**MODERN AUTHORS** <note>original: "RECENTIORES" — referring to Renaissance physicians contemporary to the author</note> mix cooked Lily roots into poultices <term>cataplasma: a soft, moist medicated mass, usually warm, applied to sores</term> with the greatest benefit for maturing abscesses and bringing them to suppuration <note>the process of forming pus to allow a wound to drain</note>. These same roots are also usefully boiled in mouthwashes for the same purpose. For they ripen all abscesses, cause them to suppurate, and soothe pain at the same time.

The **SEED**, Dioscorides says, when drunk, works against the bites of serpents. A poultice made from the seed and leaves is applied to erysipelas <note>a bacterial skin infection characterized by large, raised red patches</note>.

An **OIL** or ointment is made from the flowers, which is called *Lirinum* or *Susinum* <note>original: "Lirinum aut Susinum" — "Lirinum" is derived from the Greek "leirion" and "Susinum" from the Hebrew "shushan," both meaning lily</note>, which is suitable for softening the nerves, and especially useful for hardness of the womb.

<meta>catchword: CONCERNING</meta>

<summary>This page details the extensive medicinal uses of the White Lily, from treating snake bites and burns to clearing skin conditions and softening internal tissues. It synthesizes knowledge from ancient Greek and Roman physicians with contemporary Renaissance medical practices.</summary>

# CONCERNING PURPLE LILIES, THE INSCRIBED HYACINTH, AND COMOSANDALUM. CHAPTER XXXIII.

There are two kinds of **PURPLE LILIES**: one larger, the other smaller. For the larger purple lily, the stems are tall and long, often taller than those of the white lily; the leaves, however, are darker and narrower, many around the stem, but none before it sprouts. The flowers at the top of the stem are numerous—10, 12, or more, even up to 18, 20, and beyond—arranged above one another in an uncertain order. They have the shape and size of the other lily, but the color is red inclining toward saffron; they are sprinkled with many tiny, black, almost dot-like marks, like the first sketches of certain letters <note>This likely refers to the "inscribed" nature of the petals, which ancient observers thought looked like Greek letters or markings of mourning</note>. The roots are large bulbs, also made of many scales like the white lily, but larger.

In Italy, in the fields among the crops around harvest time; also in the mountains and valleys, this Lily shines forth, according to the testimony of Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note>. The Belgians plant them in gardens. It blooms a little before the white lily, and after the smaller purple lily, together with the Roses.

**MATTHIOLUS** says that it is called the "wild lily" in Italy, and that it is the <term>Hemerocallis: ἡμεροκαλλὶς, literally "beautiful for a day," usually identifying the day-lily</term>. But the <term>Hemerocallis</term>, as the author Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and author of "De Materia Medica"</note> writes, produces three or four flowers on individual branches, having a color that is <term>strongly yellow: ἰσχυρῶς ὠχρὰν</term>, that is, very golden-yellow. Furthermore, <term>Apophyses: Ἀπόφυσες</term> or <term>Apophyai: ἀποφύαι</term> are what we call branch-like outgrowths or appendages. The color of "ochre" is similar to honey-yellow or lemon-yellow, taking its name from the Citron fruit. This Lily, however, has no branch-like outgrowths; and the flowers on the stem are not only three or four, but not rarely 18 or 20, and the color is not yellow, but a purple-saffron. For these reasons, it appears that it is not indeed the Hemerocallis, but that Lily which Dioscorides writes—based on reports—is found in the chapter on the Lily as the <term>purple: πόρφύρον</term> lily; and which Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist</note> calls "reddish," as do the Greeks, according to him.

-> **THE GREAT PURPLE LILY** <-

# CONCERNING PURPLE LILIES, THE INSCRIBED HYACINTH, AND COMOSANDALUM. CHAPTER XXXIII.

There are two kinds of **PURPLE LILIES**: one larger, the other smaller. For the larger purple lily, the stems are tall and long, often taller than those of the white lily; the leaves, however, are darker and narrower, many around the stem, but none before it sprouts. 

The flowers at the top of the stem are numerous—10, 12, or more, even up to 18, 20, and beyond—arranged above one another in an uncertain order. They have the shape and size of the other lily, but the color is red inclining toward saffron; they are sprinkled with many tiny, black, almost dot-like marks, like the first sketches of certain letters <note>original: "primas quasdam literarum lineas." This likely refers to the "inscribed" nature of the petals, which ancient observers thought resembled the Greek letters "AI AI," a cry of grief associated with the myth of Hyacinthus</note>. The roots are large bulbs, also made of many scales like the white lily, but larger.

In Italy, in the fields among the crops around harvest time; also in the mountains and valleys, this Lily shines forth, according to the testimony of **MATTHIOLUS** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist whose commentaries on Dioscorides were standard in the Renaissance</note>. The Belgians plant them in gardens. It blooms a little before the white lily, and after the smaller purple lily, together with the Roses.

**MATTHIOLUS** says that it is called the "wild lily" in Italy, and that it is the <term>Hemerocallis: ἡμεροκαλλὶς, literally "beautiful for a day," usually identifying the day-lily</term>. But the <term>Hemerocallis</term>, as the author **DIOSCORIDES** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical authority for 1,500 years</note> describes it, has leaves like a leek, though wider...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of purple lilies, specifically addressing their various names in antiquity and the debate over whether they are the same as the "Hemerocallis" mentioned by classical authors. It concludes by linking these botanical observations to the mythological origin of the Hyacinth as described by the Roman poet Ovid.</meta>

-> **ON PURPLE LILIES.** <-

They call it *krinon* <note>original: "κρίνον," the Greek word for lily</note>, but the flower *Cynorbodon* <note>original: "Cynorbodon," literally "dog-rose." In ancient botany, this term was occasionally confused or used synonymously with certain lilies or wild briars</note>: and it is most praised in *Antioch* and *Laodicea* in Syria, then in *Phaselis*, with the one growing in *Italy* holding fourth place. Unless perhaps there are two *Hemerocallis* flowers: one having *apophyses* <note>original: "apophyses," meaning outgrowths or projections, likely referring to the stamens or specific petal structures</note>; the other not having them, and that one indeed being purple: for in this way even that Lily might be a *Hemerocallis* <note>literally "beautiful for a day," usually referring to daylilies</note>, and especially *porphyranthes* <note>original: "πορφυρανθὲς," meaning purple-flowered</note>: by which name the *Hemerocallis* is also said to be called, found among the "bastard" names <note>In early botany, "nothas voces" (bastard voices) referred to unofficial, localized, or suspect synonyms for a plant</note>. But it is likely it is not the *Hemerocallis*; for there is a certain *Hemerocallis* with a purple flower to which the name *porphyranthes* properly belongs.

ANOTHER and smaller purple Lily has a stem that is much shorter, namely only a cubit <note>A cubit is roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</note> or less: with leaves similarly dark, narrower, and smaller. Flowers likewise break out from the top of the stem, each sitting on its own stalk, sometimes shorter, sometimes longer; they are also of a purple-saffron color, adorned similarly with certain black and very tiny marks; they have the division of a Lily, but are a little smaller: for roots there are also smaller bulbs, to which fibers <note>small rootlets</note> adhere below and above. Together with other Lilies, it is cultivated in gardens among the *Germans*, *Belgians*, and elsewhere. It blooms toward the end of May, preceding the other Lilies.

There is also a purple LILY, and *krinon porphyron* <note>original: "κρίνον πόρφυρον," Greek for purple lily</note>, which for the sake of differentiation from the preceding one is nicknamed "the lesser."

<note>The following paragraph and the final two lines of the blockquote are underlined in red in the original manuscript.</note>
OVID, in book 10 of the *Metamorphoses*, calls a Lily of this kind the *Hyacinthus*, after the youth Hyacinthus, from whose blood he imagines this flower was born, when he had died while playing with Apollo; upon which he says Apollo inscribed the marks and letters of his own grief, writing thus:

> Behold, the blood which, poured on the ground, had stained the grass,
> Ceases to be blood, and more brilliant than Tyrian purple <note>original: "Tyrioque nitentior ostro." Tyrian purple was the most expensive dye of the ancient world, derived from sea snails</note>
> A flower arises, and takes the shape of lilies, except that
> These have a purple color, while those have a silvery one.

It connects these flowers to the mythological Hyacinth from Ovid's Metamorphoses, which was said to have sprung from the blood of Apollo's beloved.</summary>

# THE SMALLER PURPLE LILY.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration of a botanical specimen. It depicts a plant with a large, scaly bulb at the base and fibrous roots. A single straight stem rises from the bulb, densely covered with narrow, pointed leaves that spiral upwards. > *Phoebus <note>The sun god Apollo</note> was not satisfied with this (for he was the author of the honor);*
> *He himself inscribed his own groans upon the leaves, and "ai ai"* <note>original: "ai ai," the Greek cry of lament (αἰ αἰ). In Greek mythology, Apollo's grief for the dead youth Hyacinthus was said to be etched into the petals of the flower that sprang from his blood</note>
> *Is written upon the flower, and the mournful letter has been traced.* <note>These lines are from Ovid’s "Metamorphoses," Book 10, describing the origin of the hyacinth flower</note>

Theocritus <note>A Greek poet of the 3rd century BCE, famous for his pastoral "Idylls"</note> also mentions this *Hyacinth* in the *Epitaph of Bion*, Idyll 19 <note>Bion of Smyrna was a Greek poet; the "Lament for Bion" is a famous funeral poem which, in the early modern period, was often attributed to Theocritus or included in his collections</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "smaller purple lily," which the author identifies with the Hyacinth of classical mythology. The previous page ended with references to Ovid and Theocritus regarding the flower's origin from the blood of the youth Hyacinthus. # ABOUT PURPLE LILIES.

> Now, hyacinth, speak your letters, and take even more "ai ai" <note>original: "αἰ αἰ" (ai ai), the Greek cry of mourning. Ancient Greeks believed the petals of the hyacinth were marked with these letters as a sign of Apollo's grief</note>
> Upon your petals.

This plant is also sometimes called *rusty* <note>original: *ferrugineus*</note> by the Latin poets, as by *Virgil* in the fourth book of the *Georgics*:

> *And the oily pine torch, and the rusty hyacinths.*

Similarly, it is also mentioned by *Columella* <note>A Roman writer on agriculture from the 1st century AD</note> in his tenth book:

> *Fill the rush-basket with rusty hyacinths.*

<margin>The color of rust.</margin>
The hyacinth is called *rusty* because it is red in a way that resembles iron rust. For iron rust <note>original: *ferrugo*</note> is the decay of iron, just as verdigris is the decay of copper; from its reddish color, it is called *rubigo* <note>rust or mildew</note>. Hence, the "rusty" color is a red like that of rust. There are some who would argue that the hyacinth is not named "rusty" because it is reddish like rust, but because it has marks of mourning inscribed on its leaves; they say it is called "rusty" as if to mean mournful and somber. Indeed, they report that "rusty" <note>original: *ferrugineum*</note> is a dark and somewhat obscure color. When Virgil calls the hyacinth "rusty," he is alluding to its myth, which imagines the hyacinth was born from the blood of the youth Hyacinthus; for this reason, the somber and mournful boat of Charon <note>The ferryman who carried souls across the river Styx to the underworld</note> is also called "rusty" by him. But *Virgil* himself contradicts these people in the third poem of his *Bucolics*, where he writes that the hyacinth is "sweetly"—that is, not intensely—red, in these words:

> *And Phoebus loves me; his own gifts, the laurels, are always with me,*
> *As is the sweetly blushing Hyacinth.*

*Ovid* also, in the passage cited above, clearly shows it to be purple <note>original: *purpureus*</note>. Indeed, every color in flowers that is red has commonly been called "purple."

Most of the more recent writers call this the "Poetic Hyacinth," specifically to distinguish it from another which *Jodocus Willich* <note>A 16th-century German physician and scholar who wrote commentaries on classical texts</note> calls the "Historical Hyacinth" in his commentaries on Virgil's *Georgics*. But we prefer to call this the "inscribed" hya-
<meta>catchword: cinthum (hyacinth)</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes the various descriptions of the hyacinth's color in Roman literature, specifically debating whether the term "rusty" refers to a literal reddish hue or a symbolic association with mourning and the underworld.</summary>
<keywords>Hyacinth, Virgil, Ovid, Columella, Jodocus Willich, color terminology, rust, purple, mythology, Charon, Georgics, Bucolics</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "purple lily" (Hyacinth), specifically focusing on classical accounts of the flower's markings and its use in ancient rituals, before transitioning to a new chapter on the Wild Lily (likely the Martagon or Turk's cap lily).</meta>

...to be called a hyacinth. The other indeed—of which we will speak below in its proper place—is "unwritten" <note>This refers to the distinction between the "inscribed" hyacinth of myth, which bears mourning marks, and other similar flowers that do not.</note>: for in this one, mournful marks are found inscribed; in the other, however, there are none.

PAUSANIAS, in the second book of his *Description of Greece* <note>original: *Corinthiacon*</note>, recorded that the "written" hyacinth, or a flower most similar to it, is called *Comosandalon* by the people of Hermione. He records the rites which are performed by them in honor of the goddess Chthonia <note>An epithet for Demeter as a goddess of the earth</note> on fixed festival days. "The procession," he says, "is led by the minor priests and those who hold annual magistracies; women and men follow; it is also customary for even children to lead the goddess in the procession. They walk wearing white garments, carrying crowns on their heads. These garlands are woven from a flower which
<margin>Comosandalus.</margin>
the inhabitants call *komosandalon* <note>original Greek: κομοσάνδαλον</note>. I believe that flower to be the hyacinth: for it is very similar to it both in size and color; furthermore, it has those same letters indicating grief." Thus says Pausanias.

FURTHERMORE, the medicinal use of these purple lilies is unknown, and their properties have not been fully discovered; however, they have found favor among flowers <note>Meaning they are valued for their beauty as ornamentals rather than for medicine.</note>.

# ON THE WILD LILY.
## CHAPTER XXXIV.

The WILD Lily produces round stems two cubits <term>cubit: an ancient unit of length, approximately 18 inches or the length of a forearm</term> or higher. The leaves are long, broad, and pointed; they are not arranged one above the other in an uncertain order, but surround the stem in certain places, radiating like a star <note>This describes "whorled" leaves, which grow in a circle around the stem at the same level.</note>. The flowers hang downward from their own stalks, divided like the Lily, but smaller, of a dull purple color, shining with several purple spots, and with stamens of the same color in the middle. Their petals are reflexed backward, almost curving back into a circle or orb. The roots are bulbous, as if joined from many kernels, of a golden or yellow color, from which fibers hang. The lily grows...

-># WILD LILY.<-

<margin>Martagon lily</margin>

## CHAPTER XXXIV.

The WILD Lily <note>original: *Lilium sylvestre*; likely the *Lilium martagon*</note> produces round stems two cubits <term>cubit: an ancient unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters</term> high, which are often of a reddish color and of a certain thickness. 

The leaves are similar to those of the common garden lily, though they are somewhat smaller and narrower. These leaves are arranged around the stem at intervals in distinct circles, resembling the spokes of a wheel <note>In modern botany, this arrangement is called "whorled" leaves</note>. 

At the summit of the plant, several flowers hang downward, bowing toward the earth. The petals of these flowers are curved and reflexed back toward the stalk <note>This distinctive "curled back" shape is why it is commonly called the Turk’s cap lily</note>. In color, they are usually a deep purple or a pale flesh-tone, speckled with small, dark spots. 

The root is a bulb made of many yellow scales, appearing much like the scales of a fish, with numerous small, fibrous roots spreading out from the base.

<summary>This page introduces the Wild Lily (Martagon lily), providing a detailed botanical description of its reddish stem, whorled leaves, and distinctive reflexed, spotted flowers.</summary>

<keywords>Wild Lily, Martagon, Lilium sylvestre, botanical description, whorled leaves, bulbous roots, Turk's cap lily</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the Wild Lily (Martagon) from the previous page, detailing its natural habitats and various regional names before transitioning to Chapter 35, which introduces "Non-Bulbous Lilies," known today as Daylilies.</meta>

<page-num>128</page-num>
<header>ON THE NON-BULBOUS LILY.</header>

This plant is found in many parts of Germany, in forests and mountains, as witnessed by Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a founding father of German botany</note> and Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), a Swiss naturalist</note>. The Belgians, along with other lilies, keep it in their gardens. It blooms toward the end of May or the beginning of June, appearing earlier than the white lilies.

The **GERMANS** call it **Gold-root** <note>original: *Goldtwurtz*</note>, because of the yellow color of the bulb, and also **Heathen flowers** <note>original: *Heydnisch blumen*</note>. Our people <note>The author likely refers to the Dutch or Flemish</note> call it **Little Lilies of Calvary** <note>original: *Leliekens van Caluarien*</note>. The French call it **Wild Lily** <note>original: *Lys sauvage*</note>. However, there is another wild lily known by another name, *Hemerocallis* <note>Daylily</note>, which is discussed in the following chapter. Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist</note> says that in Italy it is called **Martagon** by the Chemists <note>original: *Chymistis*; referring to Alchemists or early pharmacists who sought medicinal properties in plants</note>.

<margin>2</margin> To this genus of Lily, it seems we should also refer what Matthiolus names "the second Hemerocallis," which he reports is found in the mountains of Japidia <note>A region in the western Balkans, modern-day Croatia and Slovenia</note>. It is depicted by him with a leafy stem and many leaves arranged around it in an irregular order; the flower has petals that curve backward, similar to the Wild Lily, and roots made of many parts, like small bulbs clustered together.

However, neither of these types of Lily has any **USE** in medicine.

### ON THE NON-BULBOUS LILY.
### CAP. XXXV.

There are two types of Non-bulbous Lily <note>These are likely species of *Hemerocallis* (Daylilies), which have fleshy roots rather than true scaled bulbs</note> to be found, distinguished by the color of their flowers. One has a yellow flower; the other has a more faded red or purple hue. In the yellow category, the flower of one is smaller, while the other is slightly larger; both, however, are smaller than the purple variety, which is the largest and nearly equal in size to the white lily.

The Non-bulbous Lily has long, narrow leaves, similar to the leaves of a Leek or an Asphodel. The stem is taller than a cubit <term>cubit: approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters</term>, round, smooth, and without leaves. At the top, it is divided into *apophyses* <note>original: *apophyses*; branch-like projections or offsets</note>, that is, two or four small branch-like extensions or shoots. On each of these, three or four flowers [grow]...

<page-num>130</page-num>

# ON THE NON-BULBOUS LILY <note>original: *DE LILIO NON BVLBOSO*</note>

*...resembling [true lilies] in their form and division: in one kind the color is a yellow Ochre <note>original: *Ochra*; a natural earth pigment</note>, as we mentioned; in another, it is a reddish-purple, though the center of the flower is yellow. After the flowers, thick, triangular pods <note>original: *siliquæ*</note> are produced; in which there is a black and shining seed, similar to the seed of a Peony, but smaller. Beneath are roots that are not bulbous, but rather like those of the yellow Asphodel <note>original: *Asphodeli lutei*</note>, many in number, oblong, and clinging together; in the kind with larger or purple flowers, the roots are thicker and more knotted; in the other with smaller flowers, they are thinner and longer. From where these join, certain thicker fibers creep out, by which—not unlike the yellow Asphodel—the plant easily multiplies into many more. This Lily is a stranger to the Low Countries <note>original: *Belgio*; referring to the region of modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands</note> and was brought in from elsewhere. It blooms together with the larger purple Lily, or a little before, preceding the white Lilies <note>original: *Lilia candida*; the Madonna Lily</note>.*

*Some call it the yellow Lily; but it is more correctly named the non-bulbous Lily. Others call it* Liliago, *most* Liliastrum: *but the ancients called it* Hemerocallis <note>Greek for "beauty for a day"</note>. *Among those ancient writers, two types of* Hemerocallis *are found: one is a shrub, the other a flower.*

<margin>The *Hemerocallis* shrub.</margin>

*The* HEMEROCALLIS *shrub is of the genus used for garlands and is woody, consisting of small and slender leaves; it grows from seed and has a woody root, according to the author Theophrastus in Book 6 of his* Inquiry into Plants <note>original: *de historia stirpium*</note>. *Pliny, in Book 21, Chapter 10, counts this* Hemerocallis *among those plants that are used for garlands for their leaves alone.*

<margin>The *Hemerocallis* flower.</margin>

*THE OTHER* Hemerocallis *is a flower which withers at night but sprouts when the Sun rises, according to Athenaeus. Such is this non-bulbous Lily; especially the purple kind, which is very fleeting and flourishes for only a single day; for having risen in the morning, it wilts in the evening.*

*DIOSCORIDES writes that the* Hemerocallis *has offshoots <note>original: *apophyses*; literally "projections" or "outgrowths"</note> or branch-like growths, and on each, three or four flowers of an ochre color. The stalks of the non-bulbous Lily, in...*

<meta>catchword: propa- (likely "propagating")</meta>

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the Daylily, specifically its unique tuberous roots and short-lived flowers, while reconciling its identity with the "Hemerocallis" described by ancient Greek and Roman naturalists.</summary>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of the "non-bulbous lily" (Hemerocallis/Daylily). It picks up from the description of the plant's branching structure and moves into a critical analysis of ancient sources, specifically challenging Dioscorides's classic description.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>

<header>ON THE NON-BULBOUS LILY.</header>

...offsets, or small branches; each flower produces three or four blooms, and in one kind, they are indeed the color of ochre <note>original: *ochræ*; a yellow earth pigment</note>. Now, there is also a plant found called the *Porphyrathes*, also known as the *Hemerocallis* <note>Greek for "beauty for a day," usually referring to the Daylily</note>: this name for a purple flower fits the non-bulbous Lily. All these things clearly demonstrate that the non-bulbous Lily is the true and legitimate *Hemerocallis*.

<margin>Dioscorides's description is suspect.</margin>

Dioscorides attributes a bulbous root to the *Hemerocallis* as well; this suggests that the description of the *Hemerocallis* in Dioscorides's work is corrupted. It has been expanded either by the addition of details that are superfluous and contrary to the truth, or perhaps the descriptions of two different Lilies have been compressed into one—specifically, the *Hemerocallis* and some other Lily. This is unless, perhaps, there exists some bulbous Lily that produces side-shoots <term>apophyses: lateral growths or offsets from the main stem or root</term> and has a yellow flower that lasts only a single day; however, it is not yet certain that such a plant has been found anywhere.

The description of the **Hemerocallis** is recorded in these words by Dioscorides: <note>original Greek: ἡμεροκαλλὶς φύλλα ἔχει καὶ καυλὸν ὅμοια κρίνῳ. χλωρὰ δὲ ὥσπερ πράσα. ἄνθη δὲ ἐν αὐτῷ καθ᾿ ἑκάστην ἀπόφυσιν γ ἢ δ· τὴν σχίσιν τῷ κρίνῳ ἐοikότα ὅταν ἄρξηται χαίνειν. τὴν δὲ χρόαν ἰσχυρῶς ὠχρὰν. ῥίζαν ὁμοίαν βολβῷ εὐμεγέθει.</note> *The Hemerocallis has leaves and a stem similar to a lily, but green like a leek. On the plant itself, near each side-shoot, there are three or four flowers; their shape is like a lily when they first begin to open. The color is a deep ochre, and the root is like a large bulb.*

The **Hemerocallis** is also called *hemerocatalachton*, wild lily, lily-flower, purple-flowered, blood-bulb, anti-beetle <note>original: ἀντικάνθαρον (*antikantharon*); an ancient name suggesting a remedy against beetles or insects</note>, Wild Lily, and Sea Lily. The non-bulbous Lily can be called by all these names, with the exception only of the "blood-bulb" name, which better fits some kind of bulbous Lily.

**Furthermore**, the medicinal powers <note>original: *facultates*; the inherent properties or virtues of a plant in early medicine</note> of the non-bulbous Lily are undiscovered—

<sig>I 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: —covered (original: tæ sunt, completing the word incompertæ)</meta>

<header>ON THE LILY OF THE VALLEY.</header>

<meta>catchword: things</meta>

...[the flowers] are [similar], though in this age, there is no use for them in medicine or food.

**DIOSCORIDES** <note>a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note>, however, says that the root of the *Hemerocallis* <note>original: "beauty for a day"; the Daylily</note>, when ground and drunk, or when applied with honey and wool in a pessary <note>a medicinal plug</note>, draws out water and blood. He also claims that the crushed leaves, when applied, soothe inflammations of the breasts after childbirth and of the eyes; furthermore, both the root and the leaves are usefully applied to burns.

## ON THE LILY OF THE VALLEY.
### CHAPTER XXXVI.

**WHAT** the more recent age calls *Lilium convallium* <note>literally "Lily of the valleys"</note> puts forth two or three leaves, which are green, smooth, and somewhat broad—similar to the leaves of a Lily, but smaller. The stem is thin and a palm’s length; around the upper half of this stem grow small, round, gaping flowers, which are notched and turned back at the edges, whitish and fragrant. From these come round fruits, not much different from the red berries of Asparagus, but smaller; the seed within these is hard and somewhat bitter. The roots are thin, long, slightly jointed, and creeping.

**IT** grows spontaneously in woods and shady places. It blooms around the Kalends <note>the first day</note> of the month of May.

**THE GERMANS** name it <term>Meyenblumlin</term> <note>May little flower</note> and <term>Meyenrysz</term> <note>May spray/branch</note> because of this; the Belgians call it <term>Meyenbloemkens</term> <note>little May flowers</note>; and the French also call it <term>Muguet</term>. However, there is another plant called *Muguet* by them, namely a species of *Lappago* <note>a plant with burrs</note> which Pliny called *Asperugo* <note>Madwort</note>. Gesner <note>Conrad Gesner, a famous 16th-century Swiss naturalist</note> thinks this flower can be called *Callionymus* <note>Greek for "beautiful name"</note>, but our current age calls it Lily of the Valley.

**THE FLOWERS** and fruit share a hot and dry quality <note>referring to the Galenic theory of the four humors, where plants were classified by their "complexion"</note>. The flowers are said to be helpful against apoplexy <note>a sudden loss of consciousness, often a stroke</note>, paralysis <note>original: *neruorum resolutiones*; a "loosening" of the nerves</note>, vertigo, the falling sickness <note>original: *morbus comitialis*; epilepsy</note>, and other diseases associated with a cold and moist imbalance of the brain—

<summary>This page concludes the medicinal description of the Daylily and introduces the Lily of the Valley (Lilium convallium), detailing its physical characteristics, various regional names, and its use in treating neurological disorders.</summary>

<keywords>Lily of the Valley, Lilium convallium, Hemerocallis, Dioscorides, medicinal herbs, apoplexy, epilepsy, May flower, botanical naming</keywords>

...beneficial things. There are also those who maintain that these flowers strengthen the heart and are beneficial to those with heart conditions <note>original: "cardiacis"; individuals suffering from heart ailments</note> in whatever way they are taken; likewise, dimness of the eyes is dispelled by a decoction or the juice of them.

<header>ON THE ASPHODEL.</header>

**IT IS REPORTED** *by some that the powder of dried small roots is effective against the plague-boil <note>original: "pestilentem bubonem"; refers to the swelling of lymph nodes characteristic of the bubonic plague</note>, if at the very start of the illness, a weight of one drachma <note>A drachma was an ancient unit of weight, roughly 3.5 grams or about a teaspoon of powder</note> is given mixed with vinegar and wine, or water.*

---

### ON THE ASPHODEL. CHAPTER XXXVII.

**DIOSCORIDES** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> *states there is only one kind of **ASPHODEL**; however, Pliny <note>A Roman naturalist and author</note> reports that there are two kinds, and that Dionysius said there were female and male varieties. A later age has observed two or three kinds of Asphodel, including a bulbous kind mentioned by Galen <note>An influential Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note>.*

<margin>1</margin> **THE FIRST** *kind might be called the white; the second, however, is the yellow.*

**THE FIRST KIND** *has many leaves which are oblong, narrow, and similar to the leaves of a Leek, but pointed at the tip. Its stalk is round, smooth, and longer than a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</note>; from the middle upward, many open flowers with five small petals adorn it. These are white in color, or a pale red slightly tending toward purple, with stamens in the middle. These are followed by small rounded heads containing the seeds, which are hard, triangular, and of a dull blackish color. Many oblong roots emerge from a single head; they are thicker at the bottom, somewhat resembling large acorns <note>original: "glandibus"; specifically referring to the nut-like shape of the tubers</note>, juicy, with a mild bitterness and an astringent taste. It grows wild in Provence in France, Italy, Spain, and other places; among the Germans and Belgians, it only grows if it has been planted. It blooms here in May and June, flower by flower, starting from the bottom, a few years after it has been sown.*

<margin>2</margin> **SIMILAR TO THIS ONE** *in its flowers, leaves, and seeds, is another smaller Asphodel, which has fibrous roots rather than acorn-shaped ones. It blooms immediately in its first year and then dies off; for this reason, it is not inappropriately called the "Pseudo-Asphodel" <note>False Asphodel</note>.*

<meta>catchword: ASPHO-</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>

# ON THE ASPHODEL.
<note>original: "DE ASPHODELO"</note>

I 5
<meta>catchword: frankincense,</meta>

The **Asphodel** is called *asphodelos* <note>Greek: ἀσφόδελος</note> by the Greeks, and by the Latins it is called **Albucum** or **Albucus**, and **royal spear** <note>original: *hastula regia*</note>. The stalk with its flowers is called *antherikos* <note>Greek: ἀνθερικὸς</note>. Pliny reports that the flower-bearing stalk is named ***Anthericon***, but the root—that is, the bulbs—is named ***Asphodelum***.

**Hesiod** mentions this Asphodel in his *Works and Days*, where he says that fools do not know how great a benefit lies in **Mallow and Asphodel**. For the roots of this particular Asphodel are especially edible. However, **Galen** believes that he [Hesiod] did not write about this Asphodel, but about the bulbous variety; yet he himself [Galen] testifies that its bulbs are not made edible except by many boilings. It is not likely that **Hesiod** would have recommended such a thing, as he seems to have suggested some food that is easy to prepare through "Mallow and Asphodel." His words are:

> Fools, they know not how much more the half is than the whole,
> Nor how great a benefit lies in mallow and asphodel.
> <note>original Greek: Νήπιοι οὐδ’ ἴσασιν ὅσῳ πλεὸν ἥμισυ παντὸς, Οὐδ’ ὅσον ἐν μαλάχῃ τε καὶ ἀσφοδέλῳ μεγ’ ὄνειαρ.</note>

Furthermore, as **Dioscorides** relates, the roots of the Asphodel, when drunk, provoke urine and menstruation; they also heal pains in the side, coughs, and ruptures or convulsions when a weight of one drachma <term>drachma: an ancient Greek unit of weight, approximately 3.4 to 4.3 grams</term> is drunk with wine. Taken in such a quantity with food, it makes one more prone to vomiting. A weight of three drachmas is usefully given to those bitten by a serpent; but it is also necessary to apply the leaves, roots, and flowers, crushed in wine, to the bites. Likewise, the root boiled in wine dregs cures foul and spreading ulcers, inflammations of the breasts and testicles, swellings <term>phymata: inflammatory tumors or boils</term>, and boils; however, for fresh inflammations, it helps when mixed with barley meal <term>polenta: here referring to a poultice made of barley meal</term>.

The **JUICE** of the root, with the addition of old sweet wine, myrrh, and saffron, becomes an extremely useful medicine for anointing the eyes if they are boiled together. For discharging ears, the same juice by itself or with—

<header>ON THE YELLOW ASPHODEL:</header>

ground with frankincense, honey, wine, and myrrh, it is beneficial. Poured into the opposite ear <note>A common practice in early medicine was to treat ear or tooth pain by applying remedies to the ear on the opposite side of the ailment.</note>, it soothes toothaches.

The ash of the **BURNT** root, when applied to hair loss <note>original: *Alopecias*; literally "fox-mange," referring to patches of baldness</note>, restores the hair.

**OIL** boiled over a fire in hollowed-out roots is useful as an ointment for ulcerated chilblains <note>painful inflammation of small blood vessels in the skin in response to cold</note> and burns: and poured into the ears, it helps their pains. The applied root removes white patches on the skin <note>original: *Vitiliginem albam*; often used for vitiligo or similar skin conditions</note> if the skin has first been rubbed with a linen cloth in the sun.

The **SEED** and flowers especially, when drunk in wine, are an excellent remedy against centipedes <note>original: *Scolopendras*</note> and scorpions.

---

### ON THE YELLOW ASPHODEL.
### CHAPTER XXXVIII.

**BESIDES** the Asphodel described in the previous chapter, there is another known as the "yellow" by its surname; its leaves are oblong, narrow, and ribbed, of a bluish-green color, and are longer and narrower than those of the first kind. The stalk is round and a cubit <term>cubit: an ancient measurement roughly the length of a forearm, about 18 inches</term> high: on it, from the middle upwards, are many flowers; they are quite similar to the other kind, but yellow in color, with stamens in the middle. Round heads follow the flowers, in which the seed is contained. The roots are likewise numerous, but each one is oblong and thin—much thinner and longer than the first kind—and yellowish in color. Above these roots, certain lateral fibers emerge, through which the plant itself easily multiplies into many others. This Asphodel is also a foreigner to the Low Countries <note>original: *Belgio*</note>, and is found nowhere else but in gardens. It blooms similarly in the month of May or June; most of its leaves stay green in winter, unless the season has been remarkably harsh; in Spring, new leaves emerge.

**RECENT** authors call it the Yellow Asphodel from the yellow color of its flowers; it may seem to be the plant called the "female" by Dionysius, and the "second kind" mentioned by Pliny <note>referring to Pliny the Elder, the Roman naturalist</note>.

**FURTHERMORE**, what use this Asphodel may have either as food or in medicines has not yet been investigated.

# ON GALEN’S BULBOUS ASPHODEL. CHAPTER XXXIX.

<meta>catchword: first</meta>

**BULBOUS Asphodel** has leaves like a leek <note>original: *porracea* — referring to the thick, succulent leaves of the leek, *Allium porrum*</note>, yet they are narrower and shorter than those of the first Asphodel. It has a straight, round stalk, similarly surrounded by many flowers, though they are more spaced out. These flowers are not much different from the flowers of the first Asphodel, being composed of six small, pointed petals with several stamens in the middle. It also bears small triangular heads, though they are more oblong, which contain the seed. The root beneath is bulbous, the size of an edible bulb or larger. In the Low Countries <note>original: *Belgas*</note>, it is found only as a garden plant. It blooms at the same time as the first variety, or a little later.

The fact that the stalk is similar to **Anthericum** <note>a genus of plants in the asparagus family</note> shows it to be a species of Asphodel; for this reason, it is clearly the same Asphodel that Galen mentions in the second book of *On the Properties of Foods* in these words:

The **ROOT** of the Asphodel is somewhat similar in size, shape, and bitterness to the root of the Squill <note>original: *Scillæ* — *Drimia maritima*, a medicinal but acrid sea onion</note>. However, if it is prepared like a Lupin, it loses the greatest part of its bitterness; in this, it differs from the Squill, for the quality of the Squill is very difficult to wash away. Hesiod certainly praises the Asphodel when he says: *Nor do they know how much good lies in Mallow and Asphodel.* <note>original: "Nec quanta in Malua & Albuco sint bona, norunt." Hesiod used these plants as symbols of a simple, frugal, and virtuous diet.</note> Indeed, I have known some country folk who, when famine was raging, finally rendered it edible through many boilings and soakings in fresh water. Furthermore, this root also possesses the power of thinning and opening <note>referring to the "opening" of blockages in the body's humors</note>, just as **Dragon Arum** <note>original: *Dracontij* — *Dracunculus vulgaris*</note> does. Accordingly, some offer its "asparagus" <note>the young, tender shoots</note> as a primary remedy for those suffering from jaundice <note>original: *ictero*</note>. Regarding this same Asphodel, the same Galen writes in his book *On the Properties of Simple Medicines* that the roots of the Asphodel are similar in power to **Arum**, **Arisarum**, and **Dragon Arum**; such are certainly the qualities of this plant, not...

# GALEN’S BULBOUS ASPHODEL. 141

->[Illustration: The Bulbous Asphodel]<-

<note>This detailed woodcut illustrates the **Bulbous Asphodel** (*Asphodelus bulbosus*). The plant is shown with its defining rounded **bulb** at the base, from which small roots extend downward. A cluster of long, slender leaves—described by the author as leek-like—radiates from the crown. A tall, straight central stalk emerges, bearing a **raceme** <note>a flower cluster with separate flowers attached by short stalks along a central stem</note> of star-shaped flowers, each with six petals, along with several elongated buds yet to bloom.</note>

The medicinal and dietary properties of this plant, as previously noted, were highly regarded by the ancient physician **Galen**. He observed that the root, while bitter like a **Squill** <note>a plant in the asparagus family often used as a diuretic, but acrid when raw</note>, can be made palatable through boiling. This process allows the Asphodel to be eaten as a simple, rustic food, a practice famously alluded to by the poet **Hesiod** when he spoke of the virtues of a modest life.

The plant is distinguished from other varieties of Asphodel by its bulbous root and the specific structure of its flowering stalk, which botanists of this period often referred to as the **Anthericum**.

The words of **Galen**, in this passage, concerning the **Asphodel**, are these: "And its root is useful, just like that of the **Arum** <note>original: *Ari*</note>, **Arisarum** <note>a smaller relative of the Arum</note>, and **Dragon-plant** <note>original: *Dracontij*; likely the Dragon Arum</note>, having a cleansing power. ## ON THE IRIS. CHAPTER XL.

**IRIS** species differ in the color of the flower, as **Dioscorides** <note>an influential Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> indicates there are many. For he says the flowers are varied: either white, yellow, black, purple, or blue. Regarding this diversity of color, **Antonius Musa** <note>the personal physician to Emperor Augustus</note> is an eyewitness <note>original: *αὐτόπτης*</note> that these are found in different plants, and not in a single flower as **Andreas Lacuna** <note>a 16th-century Spanish physician known for his commentaries on Dioscorides</note> supposed. Musa writes in his examination of simples <note>medicinal plants used on their own rather than in compounds</note> that those he saw in **Illyria** <note>the region along the eastern Adriatic coast</note> were mostly white, others pale, some yellow, certain ones purple, and very many blue.

The **LEAVES** of all are oblong, in the likeness of a sword or a blade: the stems are round, smooth, a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> long, or higher in some; at the top of these, the flowers are arranged in a parallel or alternate order <note>original: *παράλληλοι*</note>, in threes or fours, and are fragrant. The flowers consist primarily of six small leaves <note>petals/sepals</note>, three of which are bent back downward like an arch, while the other three are curved upward and toward each other. The color of the white variety is indeed white throughout the flower; the yellow is yellow; the blue or black variety shines in a deep blue-purple, resembling the color of the **black Violet** <note>original: *μελανίου* or *Violæ nigræ*</note>; in the purple variety, however, it is purple within the blue. Yet, in that variety which is common in **Belgium**, it is not the same in all petals nor in every part; for those reflexed upward are paler and of a more diluted color, especially in one where they are very pale and approach the **dark blue** <note>original: *κυάνεον*</note> color of water; those curved downward are pale in the lower part, but in the upper part, they most closely express the beautiful diluted color of the purple or black Violet, with white lines interspersed at intervals,

# THE IRIS.

-> THE IRIS <-

<note>This page is dominated by a botanical woodcut, a common feature in 16th-century herbals. These illustrations were intended to help apothecaries and physicians identify plants in the wild.</note>

**[Illustration of an Iris plant]**
<note>The woodcut depicts the plant's anatomy with scientific precision: the sword-like leaves, the distinctive "bearded" or "veined" petals of the flower, and the thick, horizontal rhizome at the base.</note>

...longer and broader than those of the **Gladiolus** <note>original: *gladioli*; literally 'little sword,' referring to the sword-lily</note>, though they share a similar shape. The **STALK** <note>original: *caulis*</note> is upright and firm, supporting the flower at its summit. The **ROOT** <note>referring here to the rhizome</note> is divided into several joints, appearing knotted and segmented; it is of a white or pale color, possessing a remarkably sweet fragrance when dried.

The most excellent roots are brought from **Illyria** <note>the eastern coast of the Adriatic Sea</note> and **Macedonia**. One should choose those that are thick, heavy, and difficult to break, with a scent that is both sharp and pleasant.

### THE MEDICINAL POWERS OF THE IRIS

According to the ancient authorities, the Iris root has a warming and thinning <term>extenuandi|In Galenic medicine, the ability of a substance to 'thin' or break down thick, sticky bodily fluids like phlegm</term> quality. 

**Dioscorides** states that the root, when prepared and taken in a drink with honey-water <note>original: *mulsa*</note>, is exceptionally useful for:
*   **Purging Phlegm:** It helps to draw out thick humors from the chest.
*   **Inducing Sleep:** It is known to provoke rest in those suffering from insomnia.
*   **Healing Pains:** It soothes the griping pains of the belly and helps with "suffocation of the womb" <note>a historical medical term for various gynecological distresses believed to be caused by a 'wandering' uterus</note>.

<margin>The root is also used in perfumes and to sweeten the breath.</margin>

When applied externally as a plaster with honey or vinegar, it heals **ulcers** and cleanses the skin of spots and freckles. It is also said that the root is effective against the bites of venomous creatures, particularly when mixed with wine.

It highlights the plant's effectiveness in treating respiratory issues, inducing sleep, and healing skin ailments based on Dioscoridean tradition.</summary>

# ON THE IRIS.

-> ON THE IRIS <-

...which, from the very growth and base of the petal <note>original: *folij*; early botanists often used the word for 'leaf' to describe flower petals</note>, and even from the yellow fringe itself, produce a pleasing mixture and variety of color. This **fringe** <note>This refers to the "beard" of the iris, the fuzzy line of hairs found on the lower petals of many species</note> begins at the lower part of the petals where they are attached to the calyx, and from there it extends upward to the middle. It is composed of many thin, short, yellowish hairs, resembling a human eyebrow or a **hairy caterpillar** <note>original: *Erucae hirsutae*</note>. In addition to these six main petals, there are three other oblong, narrow leaflets, split into two at the very top, which somewhat cover that fringe; beneath each of these are the individual stamens.

The **roots** <note>These are technically rhizomes—thick, horizontal underground stems</note> of the Iris lie beneath; they are long, solid, knotted, and jointed, about the thickness of a finger or more. They occasionally spread into several offshoots growing from the sides and cling to the surface of the soil; they are white inside. When these are still fresh and have not yet dried out, they possess a somewhat burning flavor, which is not noticed immediately, but a little while after they have been chewed. For then, attacking the throat and the base of the tongue, the root leaves a trace of fiery quality—**something more fiery** <note>original: *πυρώτερον τὶ* (pyroteron ti)</note>—which is not easily washed away. When dried, however, they have a more pleasant scent and a less sharp taste.

The Iris **grows** in Illyria, Macedonia, Africa, and in many provinces of Europe. In **Belgium**, the purple variety is the most familiar; it grows of its own accord not only in gardens but also in certain marshy places, especially the type whose flowers reflect a diluted purple and a water-blue color. The **white** Iris is found only in gardens. The **yellow** variety has been cultivated in the gardens of a few people, having first been brought to our knowledge only a few years ago. We have heard that the **blue** variety, which has a very fragrant flower, grows wild in many places in Spain.

The **best** Iris is produced among the Illyrians <note>regions along the eastern Adriatic coast</note>, and it is of better quality than that in Macedonia; however, it does not come from the coastal areas, but from the inland regions situated further to the North; as **Theophrastus** <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and the 'father of botany'</note>...

# ON THE IRIS.

-> ON THE IRIS <-

<margin>A passage of Pliny restored.</margin>
...he says. Pliny also prefers the **Illyrian** <note>Illyria was a region in the western Balkans</note> variety to all others, and indeed [notes that it grows] not in maritime areas, but in marshy ones (*the copies usually have "syluestribus" [forest-grown], but less correctly*), born near the **Drylo and Narona** [rivers]: which Nicander also commends in his *Theriaca* <note>A famous Greek poem about venomous creatures and their antidotes</note>, writing thus:

> And the Iris which the bank of the Drylon and Narona nourished. <note>original Greek: *Ἴριν θ' ἢν ἔθρεψε Δρίλων κῂ Νάρονος ὄχθη*</note>

Moreover, these rivers are placed by Ptolemy in the province of **Dalmatia**, near Illyricum. The commentator on Nicander, however, says the **Drylo** is a river of the Illyrians and Nari which separates the **Illyrians and Liburnians**; there, Cadmus and Harmonia lived after being transformed into serpents <note>In Greek mythology, the founder of Thebes, Cadmus, and his wife Harmonia were eventually turned into snakes and sent to the Elysian Fields or, in some versions, lived in Illyria</note>.

Dioscorides says that among the **Illyrian and Macedonian** varieties, the most excellent is the one with a root that is dense, small, resistant to breaking, of a reddish color, very fragrant, with a burning taste, a pure scent not smelling of mold, and which causes sneezing while it is being crushed.

According to Pliny, the **Macedonian** iris is next in quality. Galen (in the 7th book of *On the Composition of Medicines according to Kind*) includes it in a certain soothing ointment <note>original: *acopo*; a medicinal salve used to relieve weariness or pain</note> by Asclepiades, although in other compositions the **Illyrian** is almost always the one mixed in.

In third place is the **African** iris, which Galen (in his first book *On Antidotes*) testifies is much inferior to the **Illyrian**. For he says if you compare that which is brought to Rome in great abundance from vast **Libya**, it is as far from the **Illyrian** as a dead body is from a living one.

Those found in colder regions are worse and weaker in their medicinal powers. Theophrastus <note>An ancient Greek philosopher often called the "Father of Botany"</note> believed these to be entirely without scent. In **Thrace**, he says, and other colder places less suitable for "cooking" <note>original: *cocturæ*; Theophrastus used the term 'concoction' or 'cooking' to describe how the sun's natural heat matures a plant's essential oils and properties</note>, it lacks scent entirely. However, this is not absolutely true. For even the **Belgian Iris**, when dried out, is not entirely without scent, although...

# ON THE IRIS.

-> ON THE IRIS <-

<meta>catchword: ...ly [likely the end of a word like 'gratiam']</meta>

...in scent, than the **Italian** variety which is inferior; this variety is, in this age, preferred over the others in **Germany** and elsewhere, and is known by the name **Florentine**.

The flowers of the **Iris** come forth in the month of May: the leaves are almost always green: the roots are long-lasting and are hardly ever harmed by the injuries of winter; except perhaps for the white variety, whose roots occasionally die off due to excessive winter cold.

**Iris** is called *iris* by the Greeks <note>original Greek: ἶρις</note>: *Athenaeus* and *Theophrastus* read it as *ieris* <note>original Greek: ἴερις</note>, as if it were a "consecrator" <note>The author suggests a link to the Greek word *hieros*, meaning sacred or holy</note>, by which name it is also called by the **Latins**: by some it is called the **Marica** root, or rather the **Naronica** root, from the river **Naron**, around which the best and most abundant grows, as we have said. It is also called *ourania* <note>original Greek: οὐρανία</note>, or "heavenly," from the celestial bow [the rainbow], and for the same reason *thaumastos* <note>original Greek: θαυμαστὸς</note>, meaning "wonderful"; for the Poets sometimes call the rainbow *thaumantion* <note>original Greek: θαυμάντιον</note>. It is also named *delpide* or *dalpide* <note>original Greek: δελπίδη / δαλπίδη</note>, *kathairon* <note>original Greek: καθαίρων, meaning "purging"</note>, and *hypertritos* <note>original Greek: ὅπερτρίτος</note>. *Philinus* reports that the flowers of the Iris are called *lykoi* <note>original Greek: λύκους</note>, that is, **Wolves**, because when they are curved downward in the manner of a bow, they reflect a certain appearance of a **Wolf’s** lips. To the Germans, it is **Bilgen** or **Schwertel** <note>meaning "little sword," referring to the leaf shape</note>; to our people <note>referring to the Dutch/Flemish speakers of the author's region</note>, **Lisch ouer Zee** <note>literally "Iris from over the sea"</note>. To the French, it is *Flambe*. To the Italians, it is named *Giglio* [Lily], *Iride*, and *Iris*.

<margin>1</margin> A species of **Iris** with a white flower was once called *iris* in the apothecary shops by Greek chance: the Germans call it **Violwurtz** [Violet-root] or **weiß Violwurtz** [White violet-root]: Our people call it **wit Lisch**: the French call it *Flambe blanche*. Many prefer this variety to the others and assert that its root is more fragrant.

<margin>2</margin> **Another** species of *Iris* with a bluish-purple flower retains the name *Iris*: however, it is called *Lilialis* [Lily-like] by many, and in **Apulia** it is called *Spatula*, as the brothers of the Franciscan order, *Angelus Palea* and *Bartholomew* from Orvieto <note>original Latin: "ab vrbe veterum," referring to the city of Orvieto in Italy</note> write: the Italians call it *Giglio azurro* [Blue lily] and *Giglio celeste* [Heavenly lily]. The Germans call it **blauw Bilgen**, **blauw Schwertel**, or **himmel Schwertel** [Heavenly sword-lily]: the Brabantians call it **blauw Lisch ouer Zee**. Most call this variety, which has a paler flower, the wild *Iris*; to distinguish...

# ON THE IRIS.

-> ON THE IRIS <-

<meta>catchword: DE [On]</meta>

...are also white, while others are of the garden variety. There are also other wild Irises mentioned by the ancient writers, namely the **Xyris** <note>original Latin: *Xyris*, often identified as the "stinking iris"</note> and the **non-lethal Ephemeron** <note>original Latin: *Ephemeron non lethale*, likely a species of spiderwort or similar plant</note>. **Luigi Anguillara** <note>A renowned 16th-century Italian botanist and director of the Orto Botanico di Padova</note> believes that **Palladius** calls this Iris the **Hyacinth**; another might say the name of **Hyacinth** was attributed to both the **Iris** and the **Gladiolus**. The words of **Palladius** <note>Rutilius Taurus Aemilianus Palladius, a 4th-century Roman writer on agriculture</note> are found in Book I *On the Bee-yards*, title 37: "Let the ground nourish herbs: **Oregano**, **Thyme**, **Wild Thyme**, **Savory**, **Balm-leaf**, **Wild Violets**, **Asphodel**, **Lemon Balm**, **Marjoram**, and the **Hyacinth**, which is called **Iris** or **Gladiolus** because of the similarity of its leaves," etc.

FURTHERMORE, fresh **Iris** roots, while still full of juice, are exceedingly hot, reaching almost the fourth degree of heat <note>In the Galenic system of medicine, substances were graded from 1 to 4 based on their primary qualities of heat, cold, moisture, or dryness; the 4th degree was the most intense</note>. Their juice powerfully draws out not only bile, but especially watery humors, and remarkably purges those suffering from **dropsy** <term>dropsy: an old term for edema, or swelling caused by fluid retention</term>, when drunk with milk whey or another liquid that might somewhat temper its heat.

DRIED roots are in the second order of intense heating and drying agents. They thin out thick and sticky humors [fluids] that are expelled with difficulty; they are very beneficial in an **electuary** <term>electuary: a medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</term> for difficulty breathing, an old cough, and all chest ailments arising from these causes. They also help those with spleen problems, convulsions, snake bites, chills, shivers, and those suffering from a "flow of seed" <note>original Latin: *genitura profluit*, referring to involuntary seminal discharge or spermatorrhea</note>, when drunk with vinegar, as **Dioscorides** <note>The 1st-century Greek physician whose work *De Materia Medica* was the standard pharmacological text for 1,500 years</note> says. Taken with wine, they stimulate menstruation. A decoction is useful for women’s sitz-baths, for it softens and opens the "places" <note>A common euphemism for the womb or birth canal</note>. For pains of the hips [sciatica], however, an infusion is used. It fills **fistulas** <note>abnormal narrow passages or holes in the body</note> and cavities with new flesh. When inserted with honey like a **pessary** <note>original Latin: *collyrij*, here referring to a medicated suppository or insert</note>, they extract a fetus. Boiled and applied as a poultice, they soften **scrofulous swellings** <term>strumas: swellings of the lymph nodes, usually in the neck</term> and old hardened tissues. In dry form, they fill up ulcers; with honey, they cleanse them and cover bare bones with flesh. They are beneficial for headaches when applied with vinegar and rose oil. They wipe away "lentils" <note>freckles</note> and **sunspots** <note>original Latin: *ephelidas*</note> when applied with **White Hellebore** and double the amount of honey. They are mixed into medicinal inserts and **poultices** <term>malagmatis: softening external applications for the skin</term>; in general, it has many uses.
