### ON THE SMALLER OR NARROW-LEAVED IRIS. CHAPTER XLI.

<meta>catchword: zotomos,</meta>

BEYOND the Irises described in the previous chapter, certain others are found which must be discussed in order. One of these is not without reason called the **smaller and narrow-leaved Iris** <note>original Latin: *Iris minor, et tenuifolia*</note>.

THIS plant is similar to others, but smaller and more slender. It has shorter, narrower, and darker leaves, or of a bluish-gray <note>original Latin: *caesii*, a pale blue or gray-green color</note> hue, which, when rubbed together, give off a somewhat heavy odor, very much like the **Stinking Iris** <note>original Latin: *Spatula foetida*, referring to *Iris foetidissima*, known for its unpleasant smell when crushed</note>, though it is less offensive. The stalks are likewise shorter, often scarcely a **cubit** high <term>cubit: an ancient measure of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</term>. On these are two or three alternating flowers of a deep blue color, similar in form to other Irises, except that its individual petals are narrower. The upper petals do not curve back toward one another as much, but stand almost upright. The lower petals, which are bent downwards, have no fringe <note>likely referring to the "beard" or crest common to many iris varieties</note>, but only a yellow line, which is nonetheless somewhat hairy near the **calyx** <term>calyx: the leaf-like structure that encloses and protects the flower bud</term>. The roots are also slanted, knotty, and tangled together, sometimes denser than those of other varieties, sending down many long fibers. They are reddish on the outside and whitish or yellowish on the inside, with a pungent taste like the others.

IT GROWS in **Germany** in sunny and flat meadows, and in brushy, damp fields, as witnessed by **Valerius Cordus** <note>A 16th-century German physician and botanist famous for his foundational botanical descriptions</note>, and not far from **Zurich** <note>original Latin: *Tigurum*</note>, as **Gessner** <note>Conrad Gessner, a 16th-century Swiss naturalist often called the father of modern zoology and botany</note> says. In **the Low Countries** <note>original Latin: *Belgas*, referring to the region of modern Belgium and the Netherlands</note>, it only grows in gardens. It blooms at roughly the same time as the others.

THEY call it, as we have said, the **smaller and narrow-leaved Iris**. To some, it appears to be the **Root-cutter** <note>original Latin: *Rhizotomos*, a Greek term meaning "root-cutter" used by Pliny to describe specific varieties</note> of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note>, because its root is somewhat reddish, short, and dense. However, the *Rhizotomos* is a species of the **Illyrian Iris**, and indeed a superior one according to Pliny’s authority; this variety, by contrast, is inferior to others in scent and potency, and is found in Germany rather than Illyria. Therefore, it is not the Illyrian or Rhi-

...[the Root-cutter] <note>original: *Rhizotomos*, completing the word from the previous page</note>, but it is a certain narrow-leaved German Iris of its own kind, which could be considered a species of **Stinking Iris** <note>original: *Spatula fœtida*, referring to *Iris foetidissima*</note> if the seed produced in the pods were not flat like other Irises, but round like that of the Stinking Iris. Now, the words of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist</note> regarding the **Root-cutter** <term>Rhizotomos: a Greek term for 'root-cutter' used to identify a specific potent variety of Iris</term> are these: "The Illyrian Iris is of two kinds: the *Raphanitis*, so named for its resemblance [to a radish]; and the superior kind, the *Rhizotomos*, which is somewhat reddish."

MOREOVER, the potency of this root is intensely hot and burning; in its quality and strength, it is not inferior to other Irises. For that reason, they are useful for the same purposes as the others, especially when fresh.

## ON THE DWARF IRIS. CHAPTER XLII.

THE **DWARF IRIS** <note>original: *Chamæiris*, literally 'ground-iris'</note> is the smallest among the Irises; it has leaves smaller, narrower, and fleshier than the **Sword-lily** <note>original: *Gladiolo*</note>. Its stem is the height of a **palm** <term>palm: an ancient unit of measurement, approximately 3 to 4 inches or 7 to 10 centimeters</term>, which is shorter than the leaves. On this stem, the flowers do not burst forth in alternating order and position as in others, but all together from the top of the little stalk, each sitting on its own **pedicel** <term>pedicel: the small structural stalk that supports a single flower</term>. They are fragrant, with the scent and somewhat the color of a ripe plum. They are smaller than the flowers of the narrow-leaved Iris but otherwise similar in form. Indeed, the petals of this flower are also narrower and bent downwards, and they lack that fringe resembling a human eyebrow <note>this refers to the 'beard' or fuzzy crest found on the lower petals of many Iris species</note>. The roots are small and slanted, reddish on the outside, white within, hard, and burning to the taste. This Iris is foreign not only to **the Low Countries** <note>original: *Belgio*</note> or **Germany**, but also to **Italy**, as it is said; and it is found nowhere except in gardens.

IT BLOOMS in the month of May; the leaves perish in winter, and new ones burst forth in the Spring.

**Hermolaus Barbarus** <note>a prominent 15th-century Venetian scholar and diplomat</note> believed this to be the **Illyrian Iris** when he once saw it in the garden of Felix Sophia in **Padua** <note>original: *Patauij*</note>; but **Antonius Musa Brasavola** <note>an Italian physician and botanist famous for his study of medicinal plants</note>, in his *Examination of Simples*, sufficiently demonstrated that it differs from the true and legitimate Illyrian Iris. # ON THE STINKING IRIS.
## CHAPTER XLIII.

**STINKING IRIS** <note>original: *Spatula foetida*, known today as *Iris foetidissima* or Roast-beef plant</note> is similar to the common iris in its stem and leaves; however, the leaves are darker and emit a heavy and foul odor, especially if they are rubbed and crushed between the fingers. The flowers are also similar but smaller, and are a purple color that is dull and faded <note>original: *obsoleti coloris*</note>. Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus (1515–1544), a notable German botanist</note>, however, reports that some specimens please the eye with a deep, saturated purple. Large triangular pods follow the flowers, which split open into three parts as they gape. The seeds within these are round, the size of a bitter vetch <term>Eruilia: a type of pulse or pea used in ancient agriculture</term>, red and shining; when dried, the seed shrinks and turns black. In young plants, the roots are round, like the head of a bulb or some type of onion, from which thicker fibers hang down. In old plants of many years, however, many roots are tangled together, forming a large clump; they are fiery to the taste, as is the seed itself. It grows wild in Tuscany <note>original: *Hetruria*</note> and other parts of Italy; among the Belgians <note>referring to the people of the Low Countries/Netherlands</note> it is hardly found anywhere except in gardens. It flowers in July and August, and the seed ripens in Autumn.

**EVERYWHERE** people call it the Stinking Iris <note>original: *Spatulam foetidam*</note>, and many also call it the Wild Iris. The Germans call it **Bedbug-weed** <term>Wandtlauszkraut</term> because of its power to kill bedbugs. The French call it **Stinking Gladwin** <term>Glayeul puant</term>. The Belgians call it **Bedbug-weed** <term>Wantluyscruyt</term> or **Stinking Iris** <term>stinckende lisch</term>. Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel, a French physician and translator of Dioscorides</note> thinks it is the *Sparganium* <note>bur-reed</note>, but we have presented another herb as *Sparganium* which better matches the description given by Dioscorides <note>the 1st-century author of *De Materia Medica*</note>. To others it seems to be the **Xyris** <note>a Greek name for a plant with "razor-like" leaves</note>; this is the consistent opinion of Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs, author of famous herbals</note>, Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli</note>, and others, and it once pleased us as well, but now it cannot be proven. For if the description of Xyris...

<meta>This page continues the botanical comparison between the Stinking Iris (Iris foetidissima) and the Xyris described by ancient authorities. The author is concluding a critique of why the Stinking Iris should not be confused with the Xyris of Dioscorides based on its physical characteristics.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>ON THE XYRIS ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES.</header>
<meta>catchword: DIOS-</meta>

*If the description is carefully weighed, it will be found that the Stinking Iris <note>original: Spatula foetida</note> corresponds very little with it. For the leaves of the Stinking Iris are not broader, but narrower than the common Iris; the flower does not burst forth from any pod, much less a triangular one, but is naked and covered by no garment before it opens; it is also sometimes purple, but by no means flame-colored <note>original: phoeniceus</note> in the center. The roots also are not long and reddish, but white, and for the most part short. All these things show that the Stinking Iris differs from the Xyris. Indeed, the leaves of the Xyris are wider than those of the Iris; the flower bursts from a pod, flame-colored in the middle, and the roots are somewhat reddish, long, and jointed.*

Furthermore, regarding the **PROPERTIES** and powers of the *Stinking Iris*, the roots and seeds are hot, reaching the third degree of heat and dryness. The juice of the leaves kills bedbugs when smeared on walls and bed-frames.

---

## ON THE XYRIS ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES.
### CAP. XLIIII.

**XYRIS** has leaves similar to the Iris, but broader and pointed at the tip; from their center grows a stem a cubit <term>Cubit: an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches, representing the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</term> long and quite thick, on which are triangular pods, and from these emerge purple flowers with a flame-colored center. The fruit is in pods like beans, or, as the manuscript of Oribasius <note>Oribasius (c. 320–403 AD) was a Greek medical writer and the personal physician to the Roman Emperor Julian</note> has it, similar to bitter vetch <term>Bitter vetch (Eruum): a Mediterranean pulse used in antiquity for both livestock and as a medicinal plant</term>, round, red, and sharp. The root is jointed, long, and reddish <note>original: πυῤῥὰ / pyrra</note> or tawny.

It is called *xyris* in Greek and Latin, and by some *Xeris* and *Xyrida*, according to Galen. Likewise, it is called *wild iris* <note>original: ἶρις ἀγρία</note>, and *smoke* <note>original: καπνὸς / kapnos</note> or *stem* <note>original: καυλός / kaulos</note>. Commonly, however, the plant is unknown.

Galen says it is of **THIN** parts, possessing the power to draw out, digest, and certainly also to dry, both in the root and even much more so in the fruit; this fruit indeed promotes urine and is able to heal hardened spleens.

<summary>This page distinguishes the Stinking Iris from the true Xyris of Dioscorides by comparing their leaves, pods, and roots, while listing the medicinal virtues of both plants according to Galen.</summary>
<keywords>Xyris, Dioscorides, Stinking Iris, Iris foetidissima, Galen, Oribasius, medical botany, bedbugs, spleen remedies, botanical description</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient medicinal authorities, shifting from the specific identification of Xyris to the medicinal applications of the Iris group, specifically the False or Yellow Iris (Iris pseudacorus). It bridges the botanical descriptions of Dioscorides and Pliny with the vernacular names used in the 16th century.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>ON THE FALSE IRIS.</header>

**DIOSCORIDES** says the root is effective against head wounds and fractures, extracts splinters, and draws out any weapon <note>likely referring to arrowheads or thorns</note> without pain, when a third part of flower of copper <term>Flower of copper: also known as flos aeris, a green acetate or oxide formed on copper, used in ancient medicine for its corrosive and cleansing properties</term>, a fifth part of Centaury root, and honey are added. It heals swellings <note>original: oedemata</note> and inflammations when applied with vinegar. It is drunk for convulsions, ruptures, hip pains, the dripping of urine, and diarrhea when ground with raisin wine <note>original: passo</note>. The fruit, in the weight of three obols <term>Obol: an ancient Greek unit of weight; three obols is approximately 1.5 to 2 grams</term> drunk with wine, powerfully moves the urine. Indeed, the spleen is diminished when it is drunk with vinegar.

**XYRIS** root, on the authority of Pliny, disperses scrofula <note>original: strumas; glandular swellings in the neck</note>, tumors, or groin swellings. He adds that it is prescribed to be gathered with the left hand and kept for these uses; those gathering it should state for which person and for what ailment they are removing it <note>This reflects a common ancient practice of "sympathetic magic" where the ritual of harvesting was believed to transfer the ailment to the plant</note>.

### ON THE FALSE IRIS, OR THE WILD YELLOW IRIS.
### CHAPTER XLV.

**PSEUDO-IRIS** <note>The False Iris, today known as Iris pseudacorus or the Yellow Flag Iris</note> also bears leaves like the Iris, but longer and narrower, with the central rib standing out somewhat on both sides. The stems are smooth, not unlike the Iris, sometimes two cubits <term>Cubit: a measure of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches</term> high; upon these are several flowers, just like other Irises, except that their upper petals <note>original: foliola</note> are quite small—mere sketches or first outlines of those seen in true Irises. Thick triangular pods follow, in which many seeds are compressed together, arranged in order; these are flat, cartilaginous, and larger than lentils. The roots are oblique, long, jointed, fringed with fibers, and divided into offsets; they are pale reddish in color and astringent in taste. It grows in marshy places and moist, watered tracts, and is familiar to Italy, Germany, France, and Belgium. It flowers in May and June.

**COMMONLY**, the Germans call it **Yellow Sword-lily** <note>original: gel Schwertel</note> and **Dragon-**

# ON SWEET FLAG.

...root <note>original: "wurtz"</note>: Our people call it *yellow lily* <note>original German: "geel Lilch"</note>; the French call it *Gladiolus* or *Bastard Iris* <note>original French: "Glayeul ou Flambe bastarde"</note>. The pharmacies <term>officinæ|the workshops or laboratories where medicinal compounds were prepared</term> formerly gave it the name *Acoron* incorrectly, and with even greater error, used its roots in place of the legitimate Sweet Flag. For it differs greatly from the Sweet Flag, not only in form—which might perhaps agree in some respects—but especially in its medicinal powers. Because of this, many call it *Pseudo Acoron* (False Sweet Flag); however, we have preferred to call it the *wild yellow iris*, or rather the *false iris*, as it is more similar to the Iris than to the Sweet Flag.

*The root of this plant has an astringent <note>a substance that causes the contraction of body tissues</note> quality, being cold and dry in the second degree <note>the intensity of a plant's "humoral" qualities was measured on a scale of one to four</note>; it restrains dysentery and other flows of the belly: it stops the menstrual flow and suppresses bleeding from any part, whether taken as a drink or otherwise. There are even those who claim that merely carrying the root on one's person will protect against any flow of blood.*

## ON SWEET FLAG ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES, THEO-
## PHRASTUS, AND OTHERS.
### CHAPTER XLVI.

**SWEET FLAG** <note>original: "Acoro"; referring to Acorus calamus</note> *has an affinity and similarity with the* Iris; *its leaves, as* Dioscorides <note>an ancient Greek physician and the author of "De Materia Medica"</note> *says, are like those of the* Iris, *but narrower; the roots are not dissimilar, being tangled together, not descending straight down, but growing sideways along the surface of the earth, marked by joints, whitish, sharp to the taste (and slightly bitter, adds* Galen <note>the most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note>), *and with a not unpleasant scent.* Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> *ranks the Sweet Flag among those plants that produce flowers along with their seeds.* Apuleius *writes that it is rare to find, and cannot be recognized except when it has produced its little flower.* Pliny’s *history of* Sweet Flag, *found in Book 25, Chapter 13, would agree with* Dioscorides, *had he not recorded the roots as black, adding that they are less veined and easy to break.* Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a 16th-century botanist famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides</note> *depicts the Sweet Flag with the leaf of an* Iris, *and a long, slanting root, distinguished by joints on the surface, which...*

# ON SWEET FLAG.

...recorded that the legitimate plant was sent to him by Ogier Ghiselin de Busbecq <note>original: "Augerio à Bousbeeck," a Flemish diplomat famous for introducing the tulip to Europe</note>, Ambassador of Emperor Ferdinand to the Emperor of the Turks.

**SWEET FLAG GROWS** in Colchis <note>an ancient region on the eastern coast of the Black Sea, in modern-day Georgia</note>, Galatia <note>a region in central Turkey</note>, and on the island of Crete. Pliny says that the best roots of **Sweet Flag** are from Pontus; next are those of Galatia, then those of Crete. However, the very best are found in Colchis, near the Phasis river, and wherever it grows in watery places. Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> reports that specimens were sent to him that had been collected from a lake of great size near Nicomedia. Indeed, Nicomedia is a city of Galatia, near Colchis; and Colchis is a province neighboring the Euxine Sea <note>The Black Sea</note>.

**THE BEST**, according to Dioscorides <note>an ancient Greek physician whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical text for 1,500 years</note>, is **Sweet Flag** that is dense, white, not decayed, full, and fragrant. Pliny, however, says the Cretan variety is whiter than the Pontic ones, though by his own account, the Cretan ones are less esteemed than those from Pontus.

*Akoros* or *Akoron* in Greek; it is called *Acorum* in Latin. According to Apuleius <note>referring to the author of an influential 4th-century herbal</note>, some call it *Aphrodisias*, *Venerea*, nautical root, *syngentiana*, or *unguentaria* <note>meaning "of the perfumer"</note>. The pharmacies <term>Officinæ|The specialized shops where medicinal herbs and compounds were prepared and sold</term> of Europe, with great error, called it *Calamus odoratus* or *aromaticus* <note>sweet-smelling reed</note>, incorrectly substituting the roots of the False Iris for the true **Sweet Flag**, as we demonstrated in the previous chapter. To some, *Acoros* is also the root of *Oxymyrsine* <note>Butcher's Broom</note>; therefore, some prefer to call this "wild Sweet Flag," as Pliny says.

**THE ROOT OF SWEET FLAG**, according to Galen <note>the most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note>, is of the third order of heating and drying <note>In the "humoral" system of medicine, plants were graded 1 to 4 based on the intensity of their effects on the body's balance</note>. Its decoction <note>an extract made by boiling the plant material</note>, Dioscorides says, promotes the flow of urine; it assists with pains of the side, chest, and liver; likewise for griping <note>sharp abdominal pains</note>, ruptures, and convulsions. It consumes <note>reduces the swelling of</note> the spleen. It is beneficial for those afflicted by the dribbling of urine <note>original: "stillicidio urinæ"</note> and for those bitten by serpents. The juice of the root clears away things that hinder the clarity of the eyes. This root is also usefully mixed into antidotes.

# ON THE GLADIOLUS. CHAPTER XLVII.

The **GLADIOLUS** has leaves that are oblong, narrow, and grooved, pointed like a sword <note>original: "gladij modo," referring to the Roman short sword or *gladius*</note>, though smaller and narrower than those of the Iris. The stem is smooth, rounded, and longer than a cubit <note>an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or the length of a forearm</note>. Near its top, it bears six or seven spaced-out flowers arranged one above the other in a row on one side of the stem; these are composed of six small petals and shine with a reddish-purple color. These are followed by pods that are oblong in their roundness. Within these are the seeds, which are thin and chaff-like. The root consists of twin bulbs sitting one upon the other, of which the upper is smaller and more vigorous at the beginning of Spring, while the lower is larger but more flaccid; this lower one perishes shortly thereafter, having been multiplied into several others. The Gladiolus emerges in meadows and in cultivated fields among the crops in many places throughout the fertile lands of Italy. Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus, a 16th-century German physician and botanist</note> writes that it also grows in brushwood and grassy areas. In some locations, a larger and taller variety is found, while in others, it is smaller and shorter. It blooms in the month of May.

TO THE GREEKS it is called *xiphion* <note>original: "ξίφιον," from the word for sword</note>, to the Latins *Gladiolus*, and by some it is called *ensis* <note>another Latin term for sword</note>. There are those who name it *phasganon*, *machaironion* <note>little knife</note>, *machairophyllon* <note>knife-leaf</note>, *anaktorion* <note>princely or royal</note>, *limnikēn* <note>of the lake</note>, *arion*, Corn Gladiolus, and Hart's Tongue <note>original: "lingua Cervina"</note>. Pliny even calls it *Cypirus*, as in Book 21, Chapter 17. He says that some count the root of the *Cypirus*—that is, the Gladiolus—among the types of bulbs; he notes it is sweet, and when boiled and kneaded into dough, it makes bread more pleasant and heavier. Likewise, in Chapter 18, he states: *Cypirus* is the Gladiolus, as we have said. However, those things found in Chapter 17 are translated word-for-word from Theophrastus <note>an ancient Greek philosopher often called the "Father of Botany"</note> regarding the *phasganon*. 

<margin>Pliny's error.</margin> 
But *phasganon* is the same as *xiphion*. From this, it is evident and clear that Pliny wrongly gave the name *Cypirus* to the Gladiolus, and in this way confused it with the *Cypirus* <note>likely referring to a species of Cyperus or sedge</note>.

VALERIUS Cordus calls the Gladiolus the "Female Victory-plant" <note>original: "Victorialem feminam"</note>; others call it the "Round Victory-plant," and in German, **round Victory-root** <note>original: "**runde Siegwurtz**"</note>, to distinguish it from a certain other genus...

<meta>catchword: Others</meta>

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the Gladiolus, particularly its sword-shaped leaves and unique double-bulb root system. It also provides a detailed etymological history, correcting a long-standing error by Pliny the Elder who confused the plant with the Cypirus.</summary>

# ON THE SWORD-LILY. CHAPTER XLVII.

The **SWORD-LILY** <note>original: "Gladiolus"</note> has leaves that are oblong, narrow, and grooved, pointed like a sword <note>original: "gladij modo," referring to the Roman short sword or *gladius*</note>, though smaller and narrower than those of the Iris. The stem is smooth, rounded, and longer than a cubit <note>an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or the length of a forearm</note>. Near its top, it bears six or seven spaced-out flowers arranged one above the other in a row on one side of the stem; these are composed of six small petals and shine with a reddish-purple color. These are followed by pods that are oblong in their roundness. Within these are the seeds, which are thin and chaff-like. 

The root consists of twin bulbs <term>corms: underground storage organs similar to bulbs but solid</term> sitting one upon the other, of which the upper is smaller and more vigorous at the beginning of Spring, while the lower is larger but more flaccid; this lower one perishes shortly thereafter, having been multiplied into several others. The Sword-lily emerges in meadows and in cultivated fields among the crops in many places throughout the fertile lands of Italy. Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus (1515–1544), a German physician and botanist famous for his pharmacopoeia</note> writes that it also grows in brushwood and grassy areas. In some locations, a larger and taller variety is found, while in others, it is smaller and shorter. It blooms in the month of May.

TO THE GREEKS it is called the sword-plant <note>original: ξίφιον (xiphion)</note>, to the Latins the little sword <note>original: Gladiolus</note>, and by some it is called the blade <note>original: ensis</note>. There are those who name it the sword <note>original: phasganon</note>, the little knife <note>original: machaironion</note>, the knife-leaf <note>original: machairophyllon</note>, and the royal plant <note>original: anaktorion</note> because it is said that its root, when it becomes one, is a remedy for the "king’s evil" <note>scrofula, a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck</note>.

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the Gladiolus, emphasizing its sword-shaped leaves and its unusual "twin" bulb structure. It also lists the various Greek and Latin names for the plant, most of which derive from words for weaponry.</summary>

<keywords>Gladiolus, Sword-lily, Valerius Cordus, botany, corm, xiphion, medicinal plants, Italian flora</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the medicinal properties and varieties of the Gladiolus (Sword-lily) before transitioning into Chapter 48, which describes the Sisynrichium (likely the Barbary Nut or a species of Iris).</meta>

Others have a long root, which is found on the highest ridges of the Alps and is called by the Germans *Seigwurtz* <note>original: "Seigwurtz," a folk name for *Victorialis*, likely referencing its protective "victory" qualities</note>, *Victoriæ radix* <note>Victory root</note>, and *Victorialis longa*. The flowers of the Sword-lily are named *Monacuccie* <note>Literally "little monks," likely referring to the hood-like shape of the petals</note> by the Italians, as Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> testifies.

FURTHERMORE, the upper root of the Sword-lily possesses an attracting, dispersing, and even a drying quality, as Galen <note>The influential 2nd-century Greek physician</note> says. Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and author of "De Materia Medica"</note> states that this root, when applied with wine and frankincense, extracts splinters and thorns; with darnel flour <note>original: "Lolij farina"</note> and honey-water <note>original: "hydromelite"</note>, it disperses inflammatory swellings <note>original: "phigethla," likely referring to swollen glands or tumors</note>, and for that reason, it is added to plasters of this kind. He also says that when used as a suppository, it draws out the menses. On the authority of the same author, they say that the upper bulb, taken with wine, excites Venus <note>acts as an aphrodisiac</note>, whereas the lower one makes people sterile. Moreover, the upper bulb is usefully given in water to children suffering from intestinal hernias <note>original: "enterocelicis"</note>. Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, Roman naturalist</note> adds that it induces urine in infants and is applied for bladder defects. Theophrastus <note>The "father of botany" and successor to Aristotle</note> records that the root of the *Phasganon* <note>original: "Phasgani," the Greek name for the Gladiolus, meaning "little sword"</note>, that is, the Sword-lily, is sweet when boiled, and when ground and mixed with flour, it makes the bread sweet and harmless.

## ON THE SISYNRICHIUM.
### CHAPTER XLVIII.

The **SISYNRICHIUM** <note>identified as *Iris sisyrinchium* or *Moraea sisyrinchium*, also known as the Barbary Nut</note> has two or three grass-like leaves, oblong and narrow. The stem is rounded and about nine inches long <note>original: "dodrantalis," a Roman measurement of three-quarters of a foot</note>, on which are three or four beautiful little flowers, blue in color, but fleeting and lasting only a few hours. They emerge alternately in a form somewhat like the flowers of the Iris; specifically, they are composed of nine small petals. Three of these are bent downwards and have a spot on the fringe that is mostly yellow; another three are raised somewhat upwards; and the remaining three, corresponding to those that cover the fringe of Iris flowers, are proportionally larger and thinner for this little flower, standing upright and holding the middle of the bloom, while their highest and outermost part is

# ON THE SISYNRICHIUM. <note>original: "DE SISYNRICHIO." The plant is likely the Barbary Nut (*Moraea sisyrinchium*), a Mediterranean iris-like plant with edible bulbs.</note>

divided: from there, oblong capsules succeed, in which the seed is round and small like that of a Turnip <note>original: "Rapi"</note>. The root is a double bulb, one bulb placed on top of the other, just like the Sword-lily <note>the Gladiolus</note>; it is sweet in flavor and edible, and is covered in a certain reticulated <note>net-like</note> casing. This casing is indeed thicker in the larger varieties, but less thick in the smaller ones: for there seem to be two kinds of this plant, differing only in size. It grows in Portugal and elsewhere among the Spaniards. It flowered at Mechelen in the month of May, in the most spacious and well-stocked garden of Lord Jean Brancion <note>Ioannes Brancio, a 16th-century nobleman and renowned botanical collector</note>, a most noble man.

The SPANIARDS call them *Nozelhas* <note>meaning "little nuts," modern Spanish: *Nuececillas*</note>, as if they were little kernels or small nuts. To us, this seems to be that genus of edible bulb which is called *sisyrinchion* or *sisyringchion* <note>original Greek: σισυρίχιον ἢ σισυρίγχιον</note> in Greek, and *Sisynrichium* by Pliny in Latin. Indeed, Theophrastus writes in Book 7 that it is characteristic of the *Sisynrichium* that the lower part of the root increases first; but as Spring appears, this part withers away, while the upper part takes on its edible growth. By these words, he shows that the root of the *Sisynrichium* is double and edible; thus, from this, it appears we should conclude that this bulb, which the Spaniards call *Nozelhas*, is the true *Sisynrichium*. For the root is double, as we have written, and it is eaten commonly throughout Spain.

### ON THE LILY. CHAPTER XLIX. <note>original: "DE LIRIO. CAP. XLIX."</note>

The word *Lirium* has many meanings <note>original: "polysemos"</note> and is common to several flowers: <insert>for the *Lirium* is the lily <note>original: "lilium"</note>;</insert> and the Narcissus is a *lirium*, about which we shall speak in their proper places. But besides these, there are two others properly called *Liria*: one larger, emerging in Spring; the other smaller, an Autumn variety; both of which must be dealt with in this chapter.

The LARGE Lily has leaves similar to the Leek <note>original: "Porro"</note> or the Asphodel: a stalk a cubit <note>an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches</note> long: the flower at the top is quite similar in form to the flower of the Iris or the *Sisynrichium*, but larger than the Si-

# ON THE LIRIUM <note>original: "DE LIRIO." While "Lirium" is a Latin form of the Greek *leirion* (lily), the author is here describing plants we now identify as bulbous Irises.</note>

...of the **Sisynrichium** <note>The Barbary Nut or Spanish Iris, continued from the previous text.</note>: in color, it is either entirely yellow, or a bluish-purple with yellow lines that serve as a fringe on the lower petals. The root is bulbous, oblong, and similar to an onion. It grows wild in many places in Baetic Spain <note>The Roman province of Hispania Baetica, roughly modern-day Andalusia.</note> and Portugal. It bloomed in Antwerp in the garden of Peter Coldenberg, a most faithful and diligent pharmacist, in the month of June.

We have learned that the Portuguese call this the **Lirio**. And certainly, it seems to be that same *leirion* <note>original Greek: "λείριον"</note> which is recorded by Theophrastus in Book 6 to emerge in the spring after the Lily; it is distinct from both the Lily and the Narcissus.

<margin>The *Hierobulbus* of Apuleius</margin>
Perhaps this same bulb is also the **Hierobulbus** <note>Literally "Sacred Bulb."</note> of Apuleius, which he mentions in Chapter 21. Since the Iris was called *hieris* <note>original: "hieris," a Greek-derived term for "sacred."</note> by the ancients, and this bulb has the flower of an Iris, it could not improperly be called *Hierobulbus*—as if you were to say, "a bulbous Iris." Unless, of course, someone prefers to interpret *hierobulbus* <note>original Greek: "ἱεροβόλβον"</note> as meaning a great and large bulb. For it is beyond doubt that great and grand things were nicknamed "sacred" <note>original Greek: "ἱερα"</note> and "holy" by the ancients. Some believe that the *Hierobulbus* is actually the *Colchicum* <note>The Autumn Crocus or Meadow Saffron.</note>; we mention their opinion here more willingly than we refute it, but let us return to the *Lirium*.

<margin>2</margin>
The **second Lirium** puts forth narrow and shining leaves, very similar to the leaves of the six-petaled bulbous Snowflake <note>original: "Leucoij bulboſi hexaphylli."</note>. Between these, a stem three or four inches high emerges, bearing flowers like those of the German *Colchicum*, colored honey-yellow or bright yellow. They last for ten or twelve days, but close toward evening, just as the Marigold and certain other plants do. A bulbous root lies beneath. It is also found in many places in Spain. It flowers in autumn along with the *Colchicum*.

Among the **Portuguese**, it is called *Cibollina Alboraña* <note>Meaning "Little Wild Onion" or "Little Sea Onion."</note>, using a diminutive name to distinguish it from the *Scilla* <note>The Sea Onion or Squill.</note>, which they call *Cibolla alboraña*. It appears to be the **"other leirion"** <note>original Greek: "λείριον ἕτερον"</note> of Theophrastus, which in Book 6 he says blooms along with the Cro— <meta>catchword: co flo- (likely "Crocus")</meta>

### ON THE CROCUS. CHAPTER 50.

The **Crocus** flower is also numbered among the flowers. It has oblong leaves that are very narrow; the flowers are beautiful, blue in color, and similar to the flowers of the **Meadow Saffron** <note>original: *Colchici*. While similar in appearance, the Crocus and Colchicum are different botanical genera.</note>, but shining with a more saturated purple. In each flower, there are three or four oblong stamens <note>These are botanically the stigmas, which are harvested to make the spice saffron.</note> of a reddish, flame-like color, with a scent that is strong and fills the head when they have dried. These parts are preferred over the rest; they are collected and sold alone under the name of **Saffron** <note>original: *croci*</note>. Beneath is a bulbous root that is fleshy, abundant, and hardy.

The Crocus is sown in many kingdoms and provinces, where it grows green across the fields just like grain: such as in Cilicia, Cyrene, Sicily, Italy, Pannonia <note>A Roman province covering parts of modern-day Austria, Hungary, and the Balkans.</note>, Germany, Gaul, and Britain. **Theophrastus** <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and the "father of botany."</note> says that it rejoices in being trampled by feet; and for that reason, it grows most beautifully next to paths and springs. It is sown by planting the bulbs of the roots.

The best, says **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and author of the influential herbal *De Materia Medica*.</note>, is the **Corycian** <note>From Mount Corycus in Cilicia, modern-day Turkey.</note>. The second place is held by that which grows near Corycus toward Lycia, and on Mount Olympus in Lycia; then that from Aegae in Aetolia. The varieties from Cyrene and Centuripe <note>original: *Centirupinus*, a town in Sicily.</note> are the least potent of all those in Sicily. **Mattioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist.</note> says that the variety brought from the city of L'Aquila <note>original: *Aquila Vestinorum*, a city in the Abruzzo region of Italy.</note> claims the first place in Italy; however, the variety that grows around Vienna in Pannonia (or, as it is commonly called, Austria) is even more excellent than the one from L'Aquila. In Gaul, it is nowhere more vigorous than in the territory of Agen and Narbonne, according to the testimony of **Jean Ruel** <note>original: *Ruellio*, a French physician and botanist.</note>. That which grows in the southern part of the island of Britain, which they call **England**, is also approved; but from the other and northern part, which the **Picts** and **Scots** hold, it is rejected.

# ON THE CROCUS.

The best variety, wherever grown, is that which is the most succulent <note>original: *pinguissimus*, literally "fattest" or "richest," referring to the oil content of the saffron stigmas</note>, fresh, of a fragrant scent, and a reddish-orange color. The worst is shriveled and smells of mold.

The **CROCUS** begins to flower in Autumn, immediately—as **Theophrastus** <note>An ancient Greek philosopher known as the "father of botany."</note> says—following the first rains at the setting of the **Pleiades** <note>original: *Vergiliarum*. This astronomical event occurs in late autumn and was a traditional marker for the agricultural calendar.</note>. It produces its flower before the leaves, which follow shortly after; these remain green throughout the winter but are seen nowhere in the summer.

To the **GREEKS**, it is similarly known as *krokos* <note>original: κρόκος</note>; in **Latin**, it is also called *Crocum*; to the **Moors**, it is *Zaffaran*; and from there, in **Italy**, it is called *Zafferano*; to the **Germans**, **Saffron** <note>original: **Saffran**</note>; and to the **French**, *Safran*.

AS FOR THE REST, the Crocus possesses a slightly astringent quality, but its warming nature is more dominant, so that its entire essence is of the second degree of heating and the first degree of drying <note>In Galenic medicine, drugs were categorized by "degrees" of intensity in the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>. Consequently, it possesses a certain power to aid digestion <note>original: *concoquendi*, referring to the "cooking" or ripening of humors in the body</note>, assisted in this by its slight astringency, as **Galen** <note>A prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> states.

<note>The following section from 'Sodam' to the end of the page is in italics in the original and contains several passages underlined in red ink by a historical reader.</note>
*Sodam, that is, a **headache** <note>original: *Cephalalgiam*</note>, as **Avicenna** <note>A Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note> says, is caused by it, and it harms the head—which it can do not from a small amount, but from excessive use. Indeed, in large quantities, it induces wakefulness, which, when exceeding moderation, harms the head and the senses. However, the moderate use of Crocus benefits the brain, makes the senses more vigorous, drives out sleep and lethargy, and produces joy. The Crocus also strengthens the heart, brings raw humors of the chest to digestion <note>original: *concoctionem*</note>, opens the lungs, and frees them from obstruction; and it is so effective a remedy for these things that it sometimes restores the breath to those suffering from **consumption** <note>original: *phthisicis*</note> even at the ultimate crisis of life and almost at the point of death, prolonging life for several days. It is administered in a dose of half a **scruple** <note>original: *scriptuli*, a small unit of weight equal to about 1.3 grams</note>, or at most a whole one, with **raisin wine** <note>original: *passo*</note> or **Pramnian wine** <note>original: *vino Pramnio*, a famous dark wine mentioned by Homer</note>, or some other sweet wine. When given in this manner, it has been found by us on more than one occasion to immediately remove—in the blink of an eye—the most severe attacks of **shortness of breath** <note>original: *dyspnoeas*</note> that rush in suddenly without fever. **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and pharmacologist, author of 'De Materia Medica.'</note> records that, taken in this way, it also wards off the effects of a hangover. It is praised for obstructions of the liver and gallbladder, and...*

*...not for the Royal disease <note>original: "Regium morbum," a historical name for jaundice, so-called because it was said to be treated with expensive remedies fit for a king, or perhaps because the patient's skin turned the color of gold.</note>; and for this reason, **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD.</note> writes that **Saffron** produces a healthy complexion. By the same author's account, it is added to all potions that are prepared for the sake of the intestines: but in our age, it is added especially to those which draw out the menses, the fetus, and the afterbirth. It promotes urine and stimulates sexual desire: it is added to poultices for the womb and the seat <note>The rectum or anus.</note>, as well as to plasters and waxes that disperse old tumors and pains. It soothes inflammations of an erysipelas <note>original: "herisipelatodes," referring to a painful, red bacterial skin infection.</note> nature: it is usefully mixed into ear preparations. When applied, it protects the eyes from swellings and eruptions if mixed with milk, or the distilled liquid of fennel or **Roses**, and it restrains the flow of humors into them.*

### ON THE UNWRITTEN HYACINTH.
### CHAPTER LI.

<note>original: "Hyacintho non scripto." In Greek mythology, the hyacinth flower was said to have the letters "AI AI" (meaning "Alas") inscribed on its petals, representing the grief of Apollo. This specific variety, the common bluebell, was called "unwritten" because it lacks those markings.</note>

*To this **Hyacinth**, the leaves are oblong, narrow, smooth, and shining, narrower and smaller than the leaves of a* **Leek**: *the stalk is bare of leaves, smooth, and round; its head of flowers, weighed down by the mass and weight as they first begin to open, is curved downward. The flowers are oblong, roundish, and hollow, spreading wider at the edges and turned back at the rims; their color is for the most part blue, though occasionally they are whitish or pure snow-white, and sometimes they reflect the diluted purple of the dark* **Violet**: *the seed, contained in triangular pods, is small and round. The root is bulbous, white, and perennial, and—like the whole plant—is full of a sticky juice.*

THIS **HYACINTH** is found in many regions, being larger and earlier-blooming in some, and smaller and later in others. Indeed, that variety which **Italy** and **Belgium** call the "Oriental" has thicker and broader leaves, sturdier stalks, and larger, more open flowers that emerge sooner. The **German** and **Belgian** variety is smaller in its individual parts, yet it produces more flowers, though it comes forth later in the season. It is found in **Germany**, as

### ON THE UNWRITTEN HYACINTH.
### CHAPTER LI.

<note>In Greek mythology, the hyacinth flower was said to have the letters "AI AI" (meaning "Alas") inscribed on its petals, representing the grief of Apollo over the accidental death of his companion, the youth Hyacinthus. Renaissance botanists called this specific variety "unwritten" because its petals are plain and lack the dark, vein-like markings that could be interpreted as letters.</note>

# ON THE UNWRITTEN HYACINTH.
<meta>catchword: SEED</meta>

Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus (1515–1544) was a German physician and botanist</note> says [it grows] on certain sunny hillocks; in Belgium, however, it is also found in the fields and along the edges of fields planted with shrubs or trees; and especially near the city of Mechelen <note>Latin: Mechlinia</note>, where it grows abundantly.

IT FLOWERS in the months of April and May; the Oriental variety <note>The Eastern Hyacinth</note> also flowers in March.

<margin>//</margin> The Greeks call it *Hyakinthos* <note>original Greek: Υ̓άκινθον</note>, and the Latins similarly call it *Hyacinthus*. According to the authority Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note>, they also call it *Vaccinium*. However, the "black violet" is also called *Vaccinium*, as we have written above. This plant is named the "Unwritten Hyacinth" specifically to differentiate it from the other variety described earlier, which bears inscribed marks of mourning. <gloss>(123. 16.</gloss> <note>These marks refer to the "AI AI" letters of the mythical hyacinth, said to have formed from the blood of the youth Hyacinthus.</note>

DIOSCORIDES names this Hyacinth without any addition to the name; for it seems he encompassed the other Hyacinth under the name of the "purple lily." Columella <note>A Roman writer on agriculture from the 1st century AD</note> mentions this in Book 10, encompassing the diversity of color found in the flowers in a single verse:

> And likewise [there are] either snowy-white or sky-blue Hyacinths. <note>original Latin: "Nec non vel niueos vel cæruleos Hyacinthos"</note>

The Belgians, retaining the Greek name, call this Hyacinth *Hyacinthen*. Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides</note> states that it is called *Vaciet* by the French. There are truly many other species of this kind of Hyacinth, as more recent authors judge, which will be discussed in the following chapter.

FURTHERMORE, the root of this Hyacinth is of the first degree in drying, and of the full second or beginning of the third degree in cooling. <note>These "degrees" refer to the ancient Galenic system of medicine, which classified plants based on their perceived "temperament" or effect on the body's humors.</note>

THE SEED lightly cleanses and binds, drying to some extent in the third degree, but otherwise remaining midway between heat and cold, according to the authority Galen <note>Aelius Galenus, the most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note>.

THE ROOT, says Dioscorides, when applied to boys with white wine, is believed to keep them long from reaching puberty; it stops the bowels; when drunk, it provokes urine, and it helps those bitten by the *Phalangium* spider <note>A venomous spider frequently mentioned in classical herbalism</note>.

The SEED is helpful against serpents; drunk with wine, it purges the Royal Disease <note>A historical term for jaundice, so named because it was supposedly curable by the "royal touch" or because the patient’s skin turned a golden, "regal" color.</note>.

### CHAPTER LII. ON OTHER SPURIOUS HYACINTHS OF THE MODERNS.

In the preceding chapters, we described two Hyacinths: one formed in the likeness of a Lily and inscribed [with markings], and another small one that is uninscribed. But besides these, there are many others that are judged to be Hyacinths in the opinion of modern scholars. Indeed, they classify almost all nameless flowers emerging from bulbs as either Hyacinths or Narcissi; the larger ones they consider Narcissi, while the smaller ones they consider Hyacinths.

AMONG these modern Hyacinths, the first has narrow, almost round leaves of a healthy green color, for the most part spread across the ground. Between them grow round, smooth stems, longer than a palm’s width, at the top of which are small, round, hollow, gaping flowers of a blue color—sometimes pale, sometimes deep. These are followed by triangular, membrane-like pods containing small black seeds. The root underneath is bulbous. To us, this bulb is a foreigner; it does not grow wild anywhere in Belgium, yet when sown in gardens, it takes root easily and quickly multiplies into many bulbs. The leaves appear before winter, the stems in early spring, and the flowers in March.

FUCHSIUS <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a founding father of German botany</note> calls this a Hyacinth and identifies it as a second species. To us, however, it seems to be a type of edible bulb. There are, in fact, many kinds of edible bulbs, as Pliny and Theophrastus record.

AND as for the types, Pliny says the Greeks identified these: the *Bulbine*, the *Setanion*, the *Pythion*, the *Acrocorion* (which some read as *Acroscorodon* <note>meaning "top-garlic"</note>), the *Aegilops*, and the *Sisynrichion*. Theophrastus also records these, though not all of them, in Book 7, Chapter 13. Those which he himself mentions are: the white violet <note>original Greek: λευκόϊον (*leukoion*)</note>, and the little bulb <note>original Greek: βολβίνη (*bolbinē*)</note>, and the *pition* <note>original Greek: ὁ πιτιών</note>...

It also references classical botanical authorities like Pliny and Theophrastus to compare these plants with ancient edible bulbs.</summary>

# ON OTHER SPURIOUS HYACINTHS.

...the knix <note>original Greek: ἡ κύϊξ (he kyix), an unidentified bulbous plant mentioned by ancient authors.</note>, and in a certain way the sisyrinchium <note>original Greek: τὸ σισυρίχιον (to sisyrinchion), likely the Barbary Nut or a type of Iris.</note>. The **Lower Stock-gilliflower** <note>original Latin: *Leucoion inferius*. In this period, *Leucoion* could refer to Wallflowers, Stocks, or Snowdrops.</note> will be described below. Pliny says that the Greeks call a plant **Bulbine** <note>original: *Bulbinen*. Pliny the Elder was a Roman naturalist whose *Natural History* was the standard reference for centuries.</note> which has leek-like leaves and a reddish bulb. **Setanium** is a certain kind of onion, as both Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus was a student of Aristotle and is often called the "Father of Botany."</note> and Pliny testify. Regarding the **Sisyrinchium**, we have already written of it above. As for the **Pytion**, **Cyix**, **Acroscorodon**, and **Aegilops**, it has not yet been possible to determine with certainty what kind of bulbs these are; it seems suspected that the *knix* and the **Acroscorodon** <note>original Greek: ἀκροσκόροδον, literally "garlic with a head" or "top-garlic."</note> are the same plant. For in the works of Theophrastus, the *knix* follows the *pytion*; in Pliny, the *Acroscorodon* follows in the same order. It seems likely that this genus commonly called "Hyacinth" is one of these many bulbs; however, which one it might be cannot be explained due to the excessive brevity of the ancient writers. If one may use conjecture, that bulb is the *Acroscorodon*; if not, others claim the *Acroscorodon* is a wild genus. Indeed, the leaves have a certain similarity to the others, and the name *Acroscorodon* indicates some likeness to **Garlic**.

<margin>2</margin>
**TO THIS** one, another spurious Hyacinth is similar, with leaves likewise spread across the ground, but wider and more similar to leek leaves, with a thicker stem and whitish flowers that are larger than those of the other variety. It is found in gardens and blooms at the same time as the former. Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist known for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note> seems to depict this as the **vomiting bulb** <note>original Latin: *bulbo vomitorio*, likely the Tassel Hyacinth (*Muscari comosum*), known for its emetic properties.</note>, though he describes it without any specific notes; he supports his opinion with no testimonies or reasons, but merely presents a bare image of some kind with the name inscribed. We have described the vomiting bulb as something entirely different below; we judge this plant to be among the number of edible ones.

<margin>3</margin>
**THE** third among these modern Hyacinths has long, fleshy, somewhat wide leaves; long stems, often taller than nine inches <note>original Latin: *dodrante*, a Roman unit of measurement equal to three-fourths of a foot or nine inches.</note>; many small, hollow flowers from the middle upward all around the stem, which are purple at first; but when they wither, they become a faded color (they are also found white...

-> ### THE THIRD MODERN HYACINTH <note>original Latin: *Hyacinthus Neotericorum Tertius*. "Moderns" (*neoterici*) refers to 16th-century scholars who were expanding upon the plant knowledge inherited from ancient Greek and Roman authorities.</note> <-

[The page is dominated by a detailed botanical woodcut]

The illustration depicts a plant with a large, rounded **bulb** <note>the fleshy underground storage organ</note> from which several fibrous roots grow downward. Three long, narrow, ribbon-like leaves emerge from the top of the bulb, curving gracefully toward the ground. 

Rising from the center is a single tall flower stalk, bearing a **raceme** <note>a cluster of flowers where each flower is attached to a central stem by a small individual stalk</note> of numerous small, bell-shaped or globular flowers. The flowers are clustered more densely at the top of the stalk and become more spaced out toward the bottom. This structure is highly characteristic of what we now identify as the **Grape Hyacinth** <note>genus *Muscari*</note>.

-> ## ON OTHER SPURIOUS HYACINTHS <-

...having] a tufted top: the root, like that of the others, is bulbous, reddish, and of a somewhat purple color. According to the testimony of Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a German physician and one of the "fathers of botany."</note>, it is found in the groves of Germany; but according to Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an Italian doctor and botanist famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note>, it grows among the crops and in the forests. It flowers in April or May.

Fuchs, Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), who used the Latinized name "Tragus," another primary figure in early German botany.</note>, and Mattioli identify this as a species of Hyacinth, and indeed the first variety; some call it the **Wood Hyacinth**. In certain parts of Germany, it is called *Breunling*, and in others *Honds knoblauch* <note>German: *Hundsknoblauch*, literally "Dog's Garlic."</note>, which means **Dog’s Garlic**. However, it is not actually a species of Garlic, but rather a type of edible bulb, such as the *Bulbina* <note>a term used by ancient writers for various small bulbs</note>; it is certainly most similar to that. For according to Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist of the 1st century AD.</note>, the *Bulbina* has a red bulb and leaves like a Leek. Moreover, this appears to be the bulb from Africa which they nickname the "African bulb." For Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD whose work "De Materia Medica" was the standard botanical text for 1,500 years.</note> says that this plant has a *pyrrhon* <note>original Greek: πυῤῥόν, meaning "flame-colored" or "reddish-yellow."</note>, that is, a reddish bulb. There are, however, those who would argue that this third spurious Hyacinth is a species of *Scilla* <note>Squill</note> or *Pancratium* <note>Sea Daffodil</note>; we do not approve of their judgment for the reason that the bulbs of *Scilla* and *Pancratium* are large, while the bulb of this plant is small and tiny by comparison.

<margin>4</margin> Following this is the **fourth** of the spurious Hyacinths depicted by Mattioli, which has oblong and wide leaves: a somewhat long stalk, clothed with little flowers almost from the bottom to the top: and a bulbous root—at least as much as can be gathered from his illustration. For Mattioli did not write down what sort of plant it was or what name he gave it, but only displayed the image, as if the picture itself were enough for its recognition.

He names it, however, the **second Oriental type**; and he makes it the second variety after the "Oriental" and "legitimate" Hyacinth, which we mentioned in the preceding chapter.

<margin>5</margin> The **fifth** plant called Hyacinth, which is a late-blooming variety, has narrow leaves that are quite small and smaller than those of the first variety; it has short stalks, three or four inches high; the flowers at the top are sparse and blue; the stamens in the center are tiny; the seed...

<meta>catchword: in fili- (likely "in siliquis," referring to seed pods)</meta>

# ON OTHER SPURIOUS HYACINTHS

...they call it the early variety <note>original: "rotinum," likely a fragment of "praecotinum," meaning early-ripening or early-blooming</note>; nor do we know any other name for it.

<margin>6</margin> BESIDES these, Leonhart Fuchs describes a certain other bulb in place of a Hyacinth, with sometimes two, sometimes three leaves, somewhat broad, yet smaller than those of a small Leek <note>term: Porrum (Leek)</note>: with flowers scattered around the top of the little stalk, opening wide into the likeness of a radiating star, blue or white in color: these are followed by small round heads the size of a vetch <note>original: "ochri"</note>, in which there is seed similar to millet; with a bulbous, round root. It is found in many places in Germany, in lush, rich, but uncultivated soil. In March, and sometimes even in February, the flower emerges.

The GERMANS call them **bloß gertzenblumen** <note>literally "bare candle-flowers"</note> and **bloß Honungs blumen** <note>literally "bare honey-flowers"</note>. Fuchs depicted this as a species of Hyacinth. Most call it the Starry Hyacinth <note>likely *Scilla bifolia*</note> from the very shape of the flower. We once thought it was a *Satyrium* <note>a type of orchid</note>, but this opinion no longer pleases us. For the *Satyrium* has a stalk a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> long; leaves like a Lily, and a bulb the size of an apple: but this bulb has very small, narrow leaves, a tiny bulb, and a short little stalk.

FURTHERMORE, as far as the use of these spurious Hyacinths is concerned, among our people there is none, either for food or in medicines; consequently, their powers and properties remain unexplored: only the little flowers have found favor in gardens for their beauty.

### ON THE WHITE-FLOWERED BULB, OR STAR-OF-BETHLEHEM. CHAPTER LIII.
<note>original: "DE BVLBO LEVCANTHEMO, SIVE ORNITHOGALO." Leucanthemo means "white flower"; Ornithogalum literally means "bird's milk."</note>

The WHITE-FLOWERED bulb has many narrow leaves, smaller than grass: among these a stalk emerges less than a span <note>original: "dodrante," approximately 9 inches</note> high, round, smooth, and without leaves: at the top, six or seven scattered flowers shine forth, of a herbaceous green on the outside and white within, composed of six small petals, resembling tiny lilies, especially before they open; which indeed open when the Sun shines,

# CONCERNING THE WHITE-FLOWERED BULB

*...they open, but at night and when the air is cloudy they remain closed even during the day. Small triangular heads follow, similar to the seeds of the Cachrys <note>A genus of plants in the carrot family, known for their thick-winged seeds.</note>, in which there is a tiny seed. The root is bulbous, full of a slow-thickening juice, and for the most part is tasteless and watery in flavor, though with a certain hidden sharpness. It is found in grain fields, in many places among the people of Brabant <note>A historical region in the Low Countries, covering parts of modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands.</note>. It produces flowers in the month of May.*

<margin>Matthioli’s *Ornithogalum*</margin>
*In Belgium, it is called **Field onion** <note>original Middle Dutch: "Velt ayeuyn"</note>, and by the Greeks <term>Ornithogalon <note>literally "bird’s milk"; a Greek idiom for something rare or wonderful, here referring to the milky-white color of the flowers</note></term>, which is called *Ornithogale* by Pliny. Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist.</note> depicts an entirely different bulb for *Ornithogalum*, namely one with a leafy stem, differing for that reason from our *Ornithogalum*, which is the legitimate one described by Dioscorides <note>The ancient Greek physician whose work "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical authority for centuries.</note>.*

<margin>Ruellius’s *Ornithogalum*</margin>
*Furthermore, Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides into Latin.</note> describes another *Ornithogalum* in Book 2, Chapter 77, which is dug up by peasants in the fields of Soissons <note>A region in northern France.</note>. He says it has a root presenting a bulbous appearance, from the middle of which a small stalk emerges, with three or four flowers attached at the top. The leaves are thinned out into certain hair-like filaments. The flowers are small, herbaceous <note>greenish</note> on the outside and shining with a milky whiteness on the inside. When clustered together, these flowers stick together in scales like a firm, compact tuft, swelling out into a little ball representing the cone of a pine tree. The root is gathered almost into a circle, with a dark skin and very white flesh inside, or a pulp that is extremely fragrant. It is green with foliage in the spring or summer; in winter or autumn when the fields are plowed, they are pulled up by the roots. It can be kept for a long time, and when a lack of provisions presses hard, it satisfies the hunger and fasting of the country folk in the manner of chestnuts. Nothing else grown from the earth is more readily turned into food when a scarcity of crops spreads. It is wonderfully sought after by pigs; the country people know of no more pleasing food born spontaneously from the fields. It does not even shrink from the more elegant tables of the wealthy. The roots are not unknown to children, who—because they stand by when the earth is cut open by the plow or furrowed into wider ridges—collect those torn out by the plowshare, being much [sweeter] than chestnuts—*

<meta>catchword: sweeter- <note>The Latin catchword "suauio-" is the beginning of "suauiores" (sweeter), which concludes the comparison to chestnuts on the next page.</note></meta>

<summary>This page describes the physical traits of the Star-of-Bethlehem (Ornithogalum), noting how its flowers respond to sunlight. It details the plant's various regional names and its historical significance as a wild food source used by peasants and even the wealthy during famines.</summary>
<keywords>Star-of-Bethlehem, Ornithogalum, botany, Brabant, Soissons, famine food, bulbous plants, Matthiolus, Ruellius, Dioscorides</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "White-flowered Bulb" (Ornithogalum) from page 178, concluding a reference to Ruellius before moving into a comparison with the Bulbocastanon (Earthnut). It then begins Chapter 54, describing the "Wild Bulb."</meta>

[...more] pleasant to the taste; they eat these both raw and cooked in the ashes. The territory of Soissons <note>A city in northern France; the local dialect referred to here is likely a regional French name for the plant.</note> is accustomed to calling them *Churlos*. Thus far, Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides.</note>.

But to us, a root of this kind seems not to be <term>Ornithogalum <note>Star-of-Bethlehem</note></term>, but a certain <term>Bulbocastanon <note>Literally "bulb-chestnut," likely the Earthnut or Pignut (*Conopodium majus*).</note></term>. For the root has the shape of a bulb, with the flavor of a chestnut; the name itself sufficiently indicates that it is a *Bulbocastanon*.

<margin>Earthnut</margin>
However, we have described another and different *Bulbocastanon* in our histories of plants published in the common tongue <note>Referring to the author's previous botanical works written in Dutch.</note>; namely, one with leaves thinner and smaller than Celery, approaching Dill: with a thin stem, nine inches long: with white flowers in umbels <note>A flat-topped flower cluster where all the stalks arise from a single point.</note>: with a small, fragrant seed: with a round root, wrinkled when dried, black on the outside, white on the inside, with a flavor halfway between a Parsnip and a Chestnut. This is found in the grain fields of Zeeland, Holland, and also Britain, and it is very similar to the <term>Bulbocastanon <note>original Greek: Βολβοκαϛάνῳ</note></term> or <term>wild chestnut <note>original Greek: ἀγριοκαϛάνῳ</note></term> of Alexander of Tralles <note>A 6th-century Byzantine physician known for his medical compendium.</note>. But let the discourse return, diverging back to *Ornithogalum*.

Dioscorides says that the fruit of *Ornithogalum* is cooked along with bread, like <term>Melanthium <note>Black cumin or Nigella seeds, commonly used as a spice on bread in antiquity.</note></term>; but the bulbous root is eaten both raw and cooked.

## ON THE WILD BULB.
### CHAPTER 54.

A bulb seems not much different from the one described in the previous chapter, which Leonhart Fuchs and Hieronymus Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), who used the Latinized name "Tragus." Along with Fuchs, he was one of the "fathers of botany."</note> call the "wild" one: however, this one has fewer leaves, and only one or two, narrow and leek-like: a short, tender, round stem, nine inches long, with some [flowers] attached at the top, in which the small flowers are star-like and radiating, smaller than the white-flowered bulbs, with a color fading from grassy green to yellow;

->DE NARCISSO.<-

...with hair-like stamens in the middle; the small seed-bearing heads are triangular; the root is bulbous, with a sticky juice, having no scent, but in flavor it reflects the **Onion** <note>original: *Cepam*</note> somewhat, though very faintly. It is said to grow in Germany in shaded spots at the edges of meadows. It flowers and matures its fruit in March and April.

**THE GERMANS** call it *Feldzwibel* or *ackerzwibel* <note>Modern German: *Feldzwiebel* (field onion) or *Ackerzwiebel* (field-crop onion)</note>; as if you were to say "field onion." It is among the number of bulbs that grow wild and spontaneously. By what name it was called by the ancients is not established.

Its **POWERS** <note>original: *FACVLTATES*, referring to medicinal properties</note> are unknown. However, Hieronymus Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), a pioneering German botanist</note> reports that a poultice made from the bulb cooked under ashes and mixed with honey is usefully applied to "consuming" ulcers <note>ulcers that spread or "eat" into the flesh</note>.

### ON THE NARCISSUS. CHAPTER 55.

There are two types of **NARCISSUS**, on the authority of Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician whose work was the standard botanical text for 1,500 years</note>. The flower of both is white: but one has a purple center; the other, yellow.

<margin>1</margin> The **PURPLE-CENTERED** type has green, oblong leaves, similar to the leaves of a Leek: the stem is angular, without leaves, nine inches or a foot tall, and at the top the flower breaks out from a membrane—usually a single one, though occasionally a pair—moderately large, fragrant, and composed of six white petals. In the middle of these is a short <term>calyx <note>The cup-shaped central part of the flower, often called a "corona" in modern narcissus descriptions</note></term> of a circular roundness, purple-colored along the edges, inside of which are short stamens with yellowish tips. The seed-heads are thereafter angular, containing black seeds. The root is bulbous, like an **Onion**, which, by putting forth offsets, is easily multiplied into many bulbs. However, three kinds of this variety are found, differing not at all in form, color, or scent, but in their time of flowering; they also differ somewhat in size, though only slightly. The mid-season ones are larger; the early and late-blooming ones are smaller.

<summary>This page concludes a description of the wild "field onion" and its medicinal use for ulcers before introducing the Narcissus, detailing its two primary types and the physical characteristics of the purple-rimmed variety.</summary>

### ON THE NARCISSUS.

**2** *A SECOND Narcissus, yellow in the middle, has longer and wider leaves that are somewhat less green: the stalks are taller and thicker, and on each stalk are three or four flowers, similar to the previous ones but with a yellow center; the seeds and the bulbous root are similar.*

**3** *Of this genus there is also a certain third Narcissus, with leaves not only wider and longer than the first, but even than the <term>Asphodel</term> <note>A hardy plant of the lily family, often associated with the underworld in mythology.</note>, with four or more flowers on each stalk; these are white and smaller, with a yellow center and a more prominent <term>calyx</term> <note>the central cup or corona of the flower</note>, though much shorter than that of the wild yellow Narcissus: the root is similar and bulbous.*

**4** *NOW there is also a certain fourth Narcissus, similar in form to the aforementioned ones, but white throughout the whole flower and in the middle as well.*

**5** *THERE ARE also Narcissi with multiple flowers, both of the first and the second kind, not unlike others in their leaves and bulbs. Thus, nature is always varied and fertile in multiplying the types of plants and herbs; and prone to changing them, especially if their cultivation is aided by <term>mangonio</term> <note>original: "mangonio"; a term for horticultural artifice or "doctoring" plants to change their appearance, often for display or sale</note>, or if the plants are moved to another location. Indeed, we have written elsewhere that these factors have the greatest power to change plant types.*

*THOSE which are purple in the middle come forth in many places in <term>Gaul</term> <note>The historical region encompassing modern-day France and its surroundings.</note>, and among the <term>Hedui</term> and <term>Sequani</term> <note>Ancient Celtic tribes of eastern Gaul, in the regions of modern Burgundy and Franche-Comté.</note>, in the meadows. <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>An ancient Greek physician and botanist whose work was the primary source for medicinal plants for 1,500 years.</note> records that they grow primarily in the mountains. <term>Theocritus</term> <note>The creator of ancient Greek pastoral poetry.</note> is a witness that the Narcissus is born in meadows, in <term>Idyll</term> 20 <note>A short poem or "little picture" of rural life.</note>, where he sings in these verses that <term>Europa</term> <note>A Phoenician princess from Greek myth who was carried off by Zeus in the form of a bull.</note>, having entered the meadows with her companions, plucked the sweet-smelling Narcissus:*

> *But after the girls had come into the flowery meadows,*
> *they delighted their hearts with different flowers, one after another;*
> *one indeed with the sweet-smelling Narcissus, and another with the Hyacinth.*
> <note>original Greek: "Αἱ δ’ ἐπεὶ οὖν λειμῶνας ἐσήλυθον ἀνθεμόεντας, Ἄλλη ἐπ’ ἀλλήλαισι τότ’ ἄνθεσι θυμὸν ἔτερπον, Τῶν ἣ μὲν νάρκισσον ἐΰπνοον, ἡ δὲ ὑάκινθον." The author also provided a Latin translation: "Florida sed postquàm venêre in prata puellae, His illa: hae alijs se floribus oblectabant; Narcisso illa quidem bene olente, atq; illa Hyacintho."</note>

# ON THE NARCISSUS.

In the gardens of the Low Countries, the early Narcissi of the first kind bloom at the beginning of April; those of the middle sort bloom toward the end of April; and the later-blooming ones flower in the month of May. The yellow varieties of the middle sort bloom at nearly the same time as the middle-blooming plants of the first kind.

The Greeks call flowers of this sort *Narkissous* <note>original Greek: Ναρκίσσους</note>, and the Latins similarly call them *Narcissi*. The Belgians <note>referring to the people of the Low Countries</note> call them *Narcissen* and *Spaensche Jenetten* <note>literally "Spanish Jennets," a common name for certain daffodils</note>. The Germans call them *Narcissen roszlin* <note>literally "little narcissus-roses"</note>. *Plutarch, in his "Symposia," reports that it is named Narcissus because it numbs the nerves and causes a sluggish heaviness* <note>The author is highlighting the linguistic connection between the flower and the Greek word "narke," meaning numbness or stupor</note>. *For this reason, Sophocles calls it the "garland of the great underworld gods," because those who have passed away and are sluggish in death are rightfully crowned with a "sluggish" flower.* It is also called *leirion* <note>original Greek: λείριον; a general term for lilies or similar flowers</note>, and according to Apuleius, it is known as *anydros* <note>original Greek: ἄνυδρος; "waterless"</note>, *bolbos emetikos* <note>original Greek: βολβὸς ἐμετικὸς; "vomiting bulb"</note>, and *autogenes* <note>original Greek: αὐτογενές; "self-born"</note>. Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> calls it *Lirium*, describing the one which has a wider leaf and a longer, honey-colored flower, such as the third kind; he also says it sprouts spontaneously. For this reason it can be called *autogenes*, *that is, born from itself, or growing by its own power*. There are also other *Liria* of their own kind, which we wrote about above; and the Lily too is a *Lirium*. *Julius Pollux is the authority for the claim that Homer encompasses every kind of flower under the name "Lirion."* Specifically, the *anydros* Narcissus is a mountain variety, so called because it grows far from water. The name *bolbos emetikos* <note>"vomiting bulb"</note> comes from its effect: *for according to Dioscorides, the Narcissus also induces vomiting*. There is, however, another "vomiting bulb" properly so called, which we will discuss in the following chapter.

Theophrastus (in Book 7) seems to have written about the first of these Narcissi, where he reports that the Narcissus flowers three times, just as the Squill <term>Scilla</term> <note>A genus of bulbous perennial herbs in the asparagus family</note> does; and that it indicates three times for plowing, just as the Squill does. *For he says that the stalk of the Squill shows three flowerings. However, since the Narcissus does not have a second stalk beside the one that bears the flower, it is impossible for it to flower three times from the same stalk*

<meta>catchword: thrice</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the names and blooming seasons of the Narcissus, citing classical authorities like Plutarch and Theophrastus to explain its medicinal effects and symbolic associations with death.</summary>
<keywords>Narcissus, nomenclature, etymology, Plutarch, Theophrastus, medicinal plants, blooming seasons, Low Countries, Greek botany, mythology</keywords>
