<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of the Narcissus, specifically addressing its blooming cycles and its identification in the works of classical authors like Pliny the Elder, Virgil, Ovid, and Theophrastus. It clarifies that what ancient authors sometimes described as a single plant blooming multiple times was actually three distinct varieties blooming in succession.</meta>

# ON THE NARCISSUS.

...blooms three times. Consequently, it follows that there are three different bulbs that bloom at three different times, just as we have already written that the Narcissus with the purple center does. <margin>Pliny, Book 21, chapter 19.</margin> Indeed, those who find all three in one and the same place and notice they are different bulbs might think this plant blooms three times. This Narcissus with the purple center is the very one which Pliny, in Book 21, chapter 19, writes has a purple flower. He says that physicians recognize two kinds of Narcissus: one with a purple flower and another that is grass-like <note>original Latin: *herbaceum*</note>. For he gives the name "purple-flowered Narcissus" to that which has a purple center, as he shows in chapter 5 of the same book, explaining his own meaning. He says the other kind has a white flower and a purple cup <note>original Latin: *calyce*</note>. By "cup," he means the part in the middle of the flower, shaped like a circle, which contains the stamens and the tips <note>The "apices" or anthers of the flower</note>. Regarding this Narcissus, Virgil also writes in the fifth eclogue of his *Bucolics*:

<margin>Virgil.</margin>
> In place of the soft Violet, in place of the purple Narcissus,
> The thistle and the Christ's-thorn <term>Paliurus: a spiny Mediterranean shrub</term> rise with sharp thorns.

**OF THE SECOND** and especially the third kind of Narcissus, Ovid makes mention in the third book of his *Metamorphoses*. There, he describes the transformation of the beautiful youth Narcissus into the flower of his name with these lines:

<margin>Ovid.</margin>
> The body was nowhere to be found; instead of a body, they found
> A saffron-colored flower, with white petals surrounding its center.

<margin>Theophrastus.</margin>
**THEOPHRASTUS** also wrote about the third kind in his sixth book. In fact, he discusses a different one here than he does in the seventh book. In the seventh book, he assigns it a narrow leaf, just as the first kind has; but in the sixth, he says it is wider than that of an Asphodel <note>A hardy perennial herb in the lily family</note>, as is the case with the third kind. This proves that he wrote about different Narcissi in different places. Moreover, Theophrastus writes in Book 6 that the flower of the Narcissus is yellow, or honey-colored <note>original Latin: *melini coloris*</note>, which we have already shown, by Ovid's testimony, should be understood as referring to the cup in the center of the flower. <margin>Hermolaus; Marcellus.</margin> Almost all copies of the text have "black in color" <note>original Greek: μέλανα τῆι χροιᾷ; this is a common scribal error where *melini* (honey/quince colored) was confused with *melana* (black)</note>, which gave Hermolaus and Marcellus <note>Hermolaus Barbarus and Marcellus Virgilius Adriani, famous Renaissance scholars and commentators on ancient botany</note> the occasion to think that the flower of the Narcissus is black...

<summary>This page reconciles classical descriptions of the Narcissus with botanical reality, arguing that ancient reports of "triple-blooming" plants actually referred to three distinct varieties. It cites Pliny, Virgil, Ovid, and Theophrastus to identify specific characteristics like the purple "cup" and leaf width.</summary>
<keywords>Narcissus, Pliny the Elder, Virgil, Ovid, Theophrastus, botanical identification, flower anatomy, calyx, classical literature, Renaissance botany</keywords>

# ON THE NARCISSUS.

<margin>
A passage of Theophrastus corrected
</margin>
...they might have thought. But instead of "black" <note>original Greek: μέλανα (*melana*)</note>, "quince-yellow" <note>original Greek: μήλινα (*melina*)</note> should be restored. For the "Melinus" color is the same as ochre, or yellow.

**IT IS ALSO THOUGHT** *that this Narcissus is considered late-blooming by Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BCE) was the "father of botany" and a student of Aristotle</note>, and only flowers in Autumn, because in the extant copies, these words immediately follow his description: "for after the rising of Arcturus the flowering occurs, and around the equinox" <note>original Greek: μετὰ γὰρ ἀρκτοῦρον ἡ ἄνθησις, καὶ περὶ ἰσημερίαν</note>, that is, the production of the flower is after the star Arcturus appears, and near the equinox. But these things do not pertain to the history of the Narcissus: for between the description of the Narcissus and those words, there is a deficiency and a gap in the text. Nor do they even relate to the Saffron <term>Crocus</term>, as Conrad Gessner <note>A 16th-century Swiss polymath and author of a massive botanical work</note> writes in the appendix which he added for the gardens of Germany: but they belong to the history of another Lily, which Theophrastus says in the same book, a little lower down along with the Saffron, flowers "at the first rains" <note>original Greek: τοῖς πρώτοις ὕδασι</note>, that is, in Autumn; as will be immediately manifest to those carefully weighing both passages.*

*And indeed, we have happened to observe just as many of those which we believe are properly called Narcissi. There are, however, certain others called yellow Narcissi, concerning which we shall speak in the chapters immediately following.*

**MATTHIOLUS** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century physician known for his commentaries on Dioscorides</note> *also presents images of many; however, with the exception of the first, we judge these not to be Narcissi, but flowers of an entirely different kind. For those which he depicts in the second, third, and eighth places seem to be species of Squills or coastal bulbs; such as sprout naturally in many places, both elsewhere and in Narbonese Gaul <note>A Roman province in what is now southern France</note>. The fourth is similar to that bulb which Marcellus Virgilius <note>Marcello Adriani, a Florentine scholar who translated Dioscorides</note> describes in his commentaries in the chapter on the Narcissus with these words:*

<margin>
The Narcissus of Marcellus Virgilius.
</margin>
> *In these Apennine mountains of ours, we have seen a frequent flower blooming in the months of August and September, in the shape of a Lily, three-quarters of a foot <term>dodrantali: about nine inches</term> in height, with leaves such as are described in the Narcissus, with a single stalk, and the same as all the others, ex-*

# ON THE NARCISSUS.

...of a received color, which is neither white nor purple, as Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 CE), author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note> teaches, but between white and yellow <note>original Latin: *luteum*</note>, of no or little scent; we have also seen it growing on milder hills, but not as beautiful and more whitish. The fifth [kind], Gessner and almost everyone else call the Tulip. The sixth and seventh are bulbous Snowdrops <note>original Latin: *Leucoia*</note>, flowers very different from Narcissi, about which [we write] below.

**Moreover**, the root of the Narcissus, as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 CE) was a Greek physician whose medical theories dominated Western science for over a millennium</note> says, possesses such a powerful drying faculty that it glues together even the largest wounds, even as far as incisions that occur around the tendons; indeed, it has something cleansing and drawing. The same [root], according to Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 CE), the author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary source for herbal medicine in antiquity</note>, whether eaten or drunk, induces vomiting; crushed with honey, it benefits those burned by fire; applied, it joins together severed nerves; it also assists with dislocated ankles and long-lasting joint pains when crushed with honey and applied. It clears away freckles <note>original Latin: *ephelin*</note> and white spots <note>original Latin: *alphum*</note> when used with nettle seed and vinegar; but with bitter vetch <term>ervum</term> and honey, it cleanses the filth of ulcers and bursts abscesses that are slow to ripen; it also draws out embedded thorns when used with darnel <term>Lolium</term> flour and honey.

## ON THE YELLOW RUSH-LEAVED NARCISSUS,

**The leaves** of this Narcissus are oblong, narrow, sometimes almost round, very smooth and flexible, similar to rushes <term>iuncis</term> both in smoothness and color, and somewhat in flexibility, as well as roundness; from the middle of which emerges a stalk; at the top of which are three or four, or more, fragrant little flowers, similar in shape to Narcissi, but smaller and yellow throughout the whole flower: the root is bulbous, white, [covered] with a thin black membrane...

# ON THE RUSH-LEAVED NARCISSUS. <note>original Latin: *Narcissus junifolius*</note>

*...is covered by a membrane. It grows of its own accord in many places in Spain. In Belgium, it produces its flower in the month of April.*

THE SPANIARDS, from the similarity which the leaves have to rushes, call them **Jonquils** <note>original: *Ionquillias*</note>; we might call it the **Rush-leaved Narcissus**. It is the **Emetic Bulb** <note>Greek: *Bolbos emetikos*</note> of Dioscorides, or the "vomiting bulb." This can be gathered from the very description of the vomiting bulb, which goes as follows: "The bulb called 'emetic' has leaves more strap-like <note>Greek: *himantodestera*</note> and much longer than the edible kind; a root similar to a bulb, around which is a black skin." *That is to say, the bulb called 'vomiting' has leaves that are more flexible and much longer than the edible kind; a root like a bulb, around which there is a black membrane. This description is very similar to this Rush-leaved Narcissus: for its leaves are much more* **strap-like** <note>Greek: *himantodestera*</note> *or flexible than those of any other bulb, having almost the suppleness of a rush; they are also exceedingly long, much longer than those of the edible bulb; and the root is hidden within a thin, black membrane.*

*Regarding the powers with which the Rush-leaved Narcissus is endowed, there is no evidence or testimony from any recent experiments. However, Dioscorides recorded that if the root of the vomiting bulb is eaten, or if its decoction is drunk, it helps with ailments of the bladder and induces vomiting.*

<header>ON THE BULBOUS SNOWFLAKE. 191</header>
<meta>catchword: others</meta>

Germany, and also in Spain: it is also transplanted into gardens. It blooms in the month of February: for this reason, the Germans call it **Yellow February flowers** <note>original German: *Geel Hornungs blumen*</note> / that is, in our own dialect, **yellow Sporkel-flowers** <note>original: *geel Sporkelbloemen*; "Sporkel" is an archaic Dutch name for February</note>. For February is called **February month** <note>original: *Hornungs mont*</note> by the Germans / and **Sporkelle** by the Belgians.

The Belgians also call this Narcissus **yellow timeless-flowers** <note>original Dutch: *geel Tydeloosen*</note>: in Latin, however, it is commonly called the Yellow Narcissus <note>original: *Narcissus luteus*</note> and the False Narcissus <note>original: *Pseudo-Narcissus*</note>.

AS FOR what pertains to its medicinal virtues, it has been discovered through the experiments of certain modern authors that a decoction of the roots of this wild yellow Narcissus purges viscous and phlegmatic humors <term>phlegmatic humors: according to ancient medicine, thick, cold bodily fluids that caused sluggishness</term>, as well as watery fluids, through the bowels; it is beneficial to those who are burdened by an abundance of raw humors. A small amount of anise seed and ginger are added so that it causes less harm and is less distressing to the stomach.

## ON THE BULBOUS SNOWFLAKE <note>original Latin: *Leucoium bulbosum*</note>.
### CHAPTER LVIII.

We have happened to observe three kinds of bulbous snowdrops: one may be named the three-petaled <note>original: *trifolium*</note>, the second the six-leaved <note>original: *hexaphyllon*</note>, and the third the many-flowered <note>original: *polyanthemon*</note>.

<margin>2</margin>
THE FIRST produces two leaves from each bulb, which are oblong, narrow, and a whitish green, very close in color to the leaves of the Yellow False Narcissus; between these, a central stalk emerges a palm’s breath high <term>palm’s breath: a historical measurement of about 3 to 4 inches</term>, at the top of which a flower breaks out from a small oblong sheath, hanging downward from a thin stem. It consists of three larger white petals, within which are three other shorter and smaller petals, pale green and somewhat striped; in the middle of the flower are stamens with yellow tips. The root is bulbous, dark on the outside and the color of a chestnut kernel; it easily multiplies into many bulbs. This flower is a stranger to Belgium, but is found in Italy and

### ON THE BULBOUS SNOWFLAKE <note>original: *De Leucoio Bulboso*</note>

<sig>N</sig>

...common in other regions. It emerges prematurely in the month of February, preceding almost all other flowers.

This appears to be the bulbous **Leucoion** <note>A name used in antiquity for various white fragrant flowers, here referring to the Snowflake or Snowdrop</note> of **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note>, which he mentions in Book 7 where he catalogs bulbs; and which he says in Book 6 is the first to show itself and spring forth where the climate is mild, even before winter has ended, though later where the weather is more severe. To distinguish it from the varieties that follow, we have thought it not unfitting to name it the **Bulbous Three-leaved Clover** <note>original: *Trifolium bulbosum Triphyllon*</note> and the **Bulbous Three-leaved Violet**, specifically because of the three petals <note>original: *foliolis*, literally "little leaves"</note> of the flower.

<margin>2</margin>The leaves of the **next kind** are similarly narrow, resembling the Leek and the **False Narcissus** <note>the Wild Daffodil</note>, but they are shorter, greener, smoother, and more reflective, bending back somewhat toward the ground. The stalks are slender and a palm's breadth high; the flowers grow one to a stalk, very rarely in pairs, and also hang downward. They are white in color, composed of six slightly striped petals, with yellow-tipped stamens in the center. The seed is small, round, smooth, and yellow, held in round seed-heads. The root is white and bulbous, and it easily multiplies into many. It is found in many places in Upper Germany in shaded and damp woods; among the Belgians <note>the people of the Low Countries</note>, it adorns the garden beds. It blooms at the same time as the one described above, or not much later.

The **Germans** call them *white February flowers* <note>original: *weiß Hornungs blumen*</note>; our people call them *white early-bloomers* <note>original: *witte Tydeloosen*</note>.

It is clearly a genus of bulbous Snowflake; it is not without reason called the **Six-leaved** <note>original: *exaphyllum*</note> due to its six flower petals. **Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, an influential 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> classifies both this and the previous one as a species of Narcissus, as was written in the chapter on the Narcissus.

<margin>3</margin>The **third** bulbous Snowflake is similar to the second in its stem and leaves, yet it is larger and taller. It produces not a single flower on each stalk, but five or six, which bloom sequentially. In the form of its flowers and...

->ON THE TULIP<-

**It is undoubtedly a species of violet or bulbous Leucoion <note>original: *Leucoji bulboſi*; often used for Snowflakes or Snowdrops</note>; but a late-blooming one.**

But concerning the medicinal properties <term>facultates</term> of these *Snowflakes*, we have nothing to report, since nothing has been recorded about them by the *ancients* <note>referring to classical authorities like Dioscorides or Pliny</note>, and nothing has been observed by *more recent authors* as well.

## ON THE TULIP. CHAPTER LIX.

Among the number of bulbous flowers, the *Tulip* <note>original: *Tulipa*</note> is also an exotic flower <term>peregrinus</term>; of which there are different *species*: some are *larger*, others *smaller*.

<margin>1</margin> **THE LARGER ONES** all have a fairly similar form; they have two or three leaves, thick, oblong, broad, occasionally slightly turned back at the edges, which when they first emerge appear slightly reddish, but when full-grown are whitish: a stem <term>caulis</term> rises between them, a foot or slightly higher, upon which sits a single flower looking upward, made of six petals, in an open, quite deep, and somewhat narrower-rimmed cup <term>cyathus</term>, or somewhat like an inverted cap of this shape; with saffron-colored stamens in the middle: the color of the flower is sometimes yellow, sometimes a pale purple as it were, not rarely red, yet with a yellow center, and sometimes even the edges of the petals are yellow; no scent; the seed is cartilaginous, flat, and grows in small heads <term>capitula</term>, which split into three parts when the seed is ripe. The root is bulbous, not unlike the bulb of a *Narcissus*. The flowers differ somewhat among themselves in size; the larger is generally yellow, the smaller red; the medium-sized one is purple.

<margin>2</margin> **THE SMALLER** *Tulips* have narrower leaves, almost like those of a leek <note>original: *porraceis*</note>; with thinner and shorter stems: smaller flowers that do not spread out as wide: and smaller bulbs.

The *Tulip* grows in *Thrace* <note>original: *Trachia*</note> and *Cappadocia*: it is an exotic flower to *Italy* and *Belgium*. The smaller ones are found here and there in *Narbonnese*

<meta>catchword: Gaul (nenſi)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the Tulip as an exotic bulbous plant, distinguishing between larger and smaller species and describing their morphology, colors, and origins in the Eastern Mediterranean.</summary>
<keywords>Tulip, Tulipa, bulbous plants, Thrace, Cappadocia, botanical description, flower morphology, exotic plants</keywords>

# TULIP.

In others, the color is more saturated; in some, it is paler; in others, it is even marked with lines; and in others, it is plain. Again, in others, the entire flower is yellow with a black base.

They bloom at the beginning of spring, and the flower lasts until the end of April; soon after, they drop the stem while the leaves are still green.

<margin>2</margin> Likewise, there are different species of the **SMALLER ONES**: some are earlier-blooming <term>praecociores</term>, while others are later-blooming <term>serotinores</term>. The earlier ones are called **DALMATIAN TULIPS**, because they were first brought to us from Dalmatia <note>A region along the Adriatic coast, in modern-day Croatia</note>. The leaves of these are longer and thinner, and more curved back at the edges, lying flat upon the ground itself. They also produce a shorter stem, at the top <term>fastigio</term> of which a small flower appears, ending in a point; it is saffron-colored on the inside, but more reddish on the outside, and also possesses a not unpleasant scent.

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut of a tulip plant. The illustration depicts the complete plant from the roots to the flower. At the base is a rounded bulb with fibrous roots emerging from the bottom. A single, straight stem grows vertically from the bulb. Two large, broad leaves with wavy or undulate edges are attached to the stem. The stem is topped with a single, upright flower with visible petals, shown in a partially closed position. Faint ink bleed-through from the illustration on the reverse side of the leaf is visible in the background.</image-desc>

They believe the name was bestowed by the Byzantines <note>People of Constantinople, modern-day Istanbul</note> because the inverted flower resembles the Turkish headdress, which they call a *Tulipan* <note>original: *Tulipan*; the ancestor of the modern word "turban"</note>.

-># ON THE TULIP.<-
<note>original: "DE TVLIPA"</note>
->N 4<-

...[at...] are said to be born. In the month of April, Belgium sees the Tulip in bloom.

**MODERN** writers call this plant by the Turkish and foreign name **TULIPA**, derived from the Dalmatian cap called a *Tulipa* <note>A reference to the 'tulipan' or turban, which the flower's shape resembles when open</note>, the form of which the opening flower is seen to reflect. By what name this flower was called by the ancients is not certain. One might suspect it to be the *pytion* <margin>*pytion* <note>original Greek: πυτιών</note></margin>, if the bulb <term>bulbus</term> <note>The underground storage organ of the plant</note> were edible and effective for curdling milk. For indeed, the *pytion* is recorded by Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and the "father of botany" (c. 371 – c. 287 BC)</note> among the edible bulbs; and it is a herb with which milk is curdled, as Hesychius <note>A 5th-century Alexandrian grammarian</note> says. Conrad Gesner <note>A celebrated Swiss naturalist (1516–1565)</note> thought the Tulip was a species of *Satyrium* <note>A name used by ancients for various orchids and bulbous plants, often associated with aphrodisiac qualities</note>, and specifically the *Erythronium* <note>Literally "red-plant"</note>, because one species has a red flower; or perhaps some other kind of *Satyrium*. Whether the tulip truly matches these, and to what extent, we leave to be more carefully considered from the descriptions of the *Satyriums* written below.

**CONCERNING** the powers and properties of the Tulip, we have found nothing certain, nor have we found anything written by anyone.

### ON SATYRIUMS ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES AND OTHERS.
### CHAPTER 60.

**BESIDES** those called *Orchies* <note>The Orchids</note>, which Pliny and Apuleius also call *Satyria*, Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and author of "De Materia Medica" (c. 40–90 AD)</note> records that there are two kinds of *Satyrium*: one called *triphyllon* <note>original: "trifolium" or "three-leafed"</note>, the other named *erythronium* <note>The red variety</note>.

**THE THREE-LEAFED** variety bears three leaves turned downwards, similar to Dock <note>original: "Lapathum"</note> or the Lily, but smaller and reddish. It has a bare <margin>bare <note>original Greek: ψιλὸν (psilon)</note></margin> stem—that is, thin or smooth—a cubit long. The flower is of the Lily type and white. The root is bulbous, like an apple, and tawny <margin>tawny <note>original Greek: πυῤῥὰν (pyrran)</note></margin> or reddish-yellow, white inside like an egg, sweet to the taste, and pleasant to the mouth. Pliny <note>Roman naturalist and author of "Natural History" (23–79 AD)</note>, in Book 26, Chapter 10, describes the same *Satyrium*, but somewhat differently: he says its leaves are not like Dock or the other Lily, but like the Red Lily, and much narrower.

->### ON THE SATYRIUMS ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES.<-
<meta>catchword: queat;</meta>

...and they are smaller than white Lilies: and it has twin roots, quite contrary to what Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD), author of 'De Materia Medica'</note> describes. The words of Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of 'Natural History'</note> are: *The Greeks report that the Satyrium has leaves smaller than the red Lily, and no more than three emerging from the earth: with a smooth, cubit-long, bare stem: a twin root, of which the lower and larger part generates males; the lower and smaller generates females.* <margin>A passage of Pliny is noted.</margin> However, what is added regarding the twin root pertains to the *Orchis* <note>The name for various orchids, derived from the Greek word for 'testicle' because of the shape of the paired tubers</note>, and not truly to the history of the Satyrium. Dioscorides says the root of this Satyrium ought to be boiled in dry wine and drunk for **opisthotonon** <term>opisthotonon</term> <note>A condition where severe muscle spasms cause the body to arch backward</note>: and it should be used by those who wish to have sexual intercourse; for it is said to excite the power for Venus <note>The Roman goddess of love, here used as a euphemism for sexual desire or performance</note>.

2 **ERYTHRONIUM** *Satyrium or the Red-plant, has seeds similar to linseed, but larger, robust, shining, and smooth, which itself is also said to excite sexual desire, just like the Skink <note>original: "Scincus," a lizard whose flesh was historically used as an aphrodisiac</note>: the bark of the root is thin and tawny, but the inner part is white, pleasant to the taste, and sweet. It grows in sun-exposed and mountainous places. The root is said to incite sexual desire even if held in the hand: but much more so when taken with wine. Pliny says almost the same things about the Erythronium; except that he attributes to it a seed not of flax, but larger and smoother than that of the chaste-tree <note>original: "viticis"</note>, and he ascribes more powers to it based on the reports of others: namely, that it is given in drink to sluggish rams and he-goats; and by the Sarmatians <note>Ancient nomadic peoples of the Eastern European steppes</note>, to horses that are too slow in mating due to constant labor; a condition they call **prosedamum** <term>prosedamum</term> <note>A term for sexual exhaustion or impotence in pack animals</note>.*

**BUT** after both Dioscorides and Pliny record that such great powers are attributed to the Erythronium Satyrium for inciting sexual desire, it seems not unreasonable to suspect that this Satyrium is that herb which Theophrastus <note>The "father of botany" (c. 371–287 BC)</note> says in his ninth book that a certain Indian man brought; most effective for sexual matters: especially since Theophrastus makes no mention of this or any other Satyrium elsewhere. For if the Erythronium has such an effective power, that even when taken in the hand it can excite desire...

<keywords>Satyrium, Erythronium, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, aphrodisiacs, Orchis, botanical history, ancient medicine, Theophrastus, veterinary medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of aphrodisiac plants from the previous section, concluding the comparison between the Satyrium and the Erythronium before moving into a detailed classification of various orchid species based on the works of classical authors and contemporary botanists like Valerius Cordus.</meta>

...could be; it must not differ much in power from that herb of the Indian (if it is not the same). For the herb which the Indian had brought was wonderfully powerful in the matter of Venus <note>The Roman goddess of love, used here to refer to sexual potency and desire</note>; for they said the power was so intense not only for those eating it, but also for those merely touching it, that they were able to engage in intercourse as often as they wished; as Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC), a Greek philosopher and the "father of botany"</note> reports. However, it cannot be affirmed with certainty that this is the same as the **Erythronium** for that reason alone, since plants that are dissimilar in form are not infrequently found to possess the same or similar powers. We have indicated our conjecture, but we leave a more exact judgment to the studious.

### ON THE ORCHIDS. CHAPTER LXI.

Many and different species of the **ORCHIS** <note>The name "Orchis" is derived from the Greek word for "testicle," referring to the paired tubers of the root</note> are found, which the ancients categorized into fewer groups, but we have thought they should be understood in five kinds. The first kind we call the *Cynosorchis* <note>original: "Cynosorchin," literally "Dog's testicle"</note>; the next the *Morio* orchid; the third the *Tragorchis* <note>original: "Tragorchin," literally "Goat's testicle"</note>; the fourth the *Serapias* orchid; and the fifth we have called the sweet-scented or dwarf orchid, following Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus (1515–1544), a German physician and botanist known for his descriptions of plants</note>.

<margin>Cynosorchis. 1</margin>
Several species of **CYNOSORCHIS** are found. The **FIRST** has broad, fleshy leaves, almost similar to those of a lily; the stem is a foot or more in height and angular; and upon it are many flowers, arranged in a spike, of a faint reddish-purple color, resembling a hood or an open helmet. From the lower part of these flowers, a certain ragged appendage hangs down, similar to the hide of a dog or another four-footed animal; it is also faintly purple and dappled with more intense purple spots. For roots, besides the fibers clinging above, there are twin bulbs like testicles, somewhat oblong in shape; one of these is fuller and more vigorous, the other more wrinkled.

<margin>2</margin>
The **SECOND** species has similarly smooth, oblong, and

-># THE SECOND DOG ORCHID<-
<note>original Latin: "Cynosorchis altera". The name is derived from the Greek *kyon* (dog) and *orchis* (testicle), referring to the shape of the paired root tubers.</note>

<margin>Dog orchid...</margin>

**Botanical Illustration:**

The woodcut on this page depicts the entire structure of the **Second Dog Orchid**. 

The root system is characterized by two rounded, <term>testiculate</term> <note>shaped like testicles; a defining feature of many orchids in early botanical texts</note> tubers, with several smaller fibrous roots spreading outward. At the base of the plant, a short sheath gives rise to several long, narrow, lance-shaped leaves, which feature prominent parallel ribbing. 

A single, sturdy central stalk grows from the center of the foliage, terminating in a dense, cone-shaped <term>raceme</term> <note>a flower cluster where individual flowers are attached to a central stem by short stalks</note> of many small flowers. To the left of the main plant, a single blossom is shown in isolation to highlight its specific morphological features, particularly its distinct <term>labellum</term> <note>the specialized "lip" or petal of an orchid flower</note>.

### ON ORCHIDS

The leaves are broad, but smaller and narrower than those of the preceding plant. There are five or six of them, some of which clasp the stem. The stem is about nine inches long <note>original: "dodrantalis"; a Roman unit of measure representing three-quarters of a foot</note>. The flower spike is dense and short, containing many flowers. Their color is a purplish-white, adorned with many purple spots and, on the interior, numerous tiny dark lines. In shape, they resemble a helmet or a gaping hood. From each flower hangs a small body resembling a four-legged animal or a "little man" <note>original: "homunculi"</note> with outstretched arms and spread legs but without a head, in much the same way that small children are usually depicted hanging from the mouth of Saturn <note>A vivid reference to the mythological theme of Saturn devouring his children; the author is describing the "labellum" or lip of the orchid, which often looks like a human torso</note>. Beneath are two globe-shaped roots, the size of a nutmeg, with several thick fibers attached where they join the plant.

<margin>3</margin>
The **THIRD species of Dog Orchid** <note>original: "Cynosorchis"; from the Greek "kyon" (dog) and "orchis" (testicle)</note> produces narrow, ribbed leaves, somewhat similar to the leaves of narrow-leafed plantain. The stem is nine inches long. The flowers are numerous in a short, dense spike; their color on the outside is a deep red or dark purple, while on the inside they are whitish. They have the same shape as the previous species but are smaller; there also hangs from them a rough and formless little likeness of a four-legged creature. The roots underneath are similar to those of other orchids.

<margin>4</margin>
To this, there is **another Dog Orchid** very similar in all its flowers, yet with wider leaves like those of the second species; one might not inappropriately categorize this as the fourth species of Dog Orchid.

<margin>5</margin>
Now, a **fifth species** is also found, with leaves like the "dog's testicle" <note>original: "canis testiculus"; the literal Latin name for this orchid group</note> described second. It has a stem a foot long and a long spike of flowers that are a greenish, grass-like color. The part hanging down, which resembles a fringe, is oblong and almost four-sided. From among these, either the first or the second (or perhaps both) is the Dog Orchid described by Dioscorides <note>An ancient Greek physician and the father of pharmacognosy; his descriptions were the gold standard for Renaissance botanists</note>. Both of these have smooth leaves, similar to the leaves of the "soft" or "Epimenidian" Squill <note>original: "Scillæ mollioris siue Epimenidiæ"; the Squill is a bulbous flowering plant in the asparagus family</note>. Some of these leaves are upright and clasp the stem, while others are inclined toward the ground—

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of the "Cynosorchis" or Dog-Orchid, focusing on reconciling ancient descriptions from Dioscorides, Theophrastus, and Pliny the Elder. It follows the previous page's morphological description of orchid varieties.</meta>

# ON ORCHIDS.

<margin>Dioscorides’ Dog-Orchid.</margin>
*...inclined: the stem is often taller than nine inches <note>original: "dodrante"; a Roman unit of measure representing three-quarters of a foot</note>: the flowers are purplish: the root bulbs are like oblong olives, especially the primary one. For Dioscorides writes that such is the Dog-Orchid.* The Orchid, *he says*, which some call the Dog-Orchid <note>original: Ὄρχις, οἱ δὲ κυνὸς ὄρχιν καλοῦσι</note>, has leaves spread upon the ground around the stem and the base, similar to the soft squill <note>term: Scilla; a genus of bulbous plants, some of which were used medicinally in antiquity as "sea onions"</note>, but narrower, smoother, and longer; a stem nine inches in length, upon which are purplish flowers; a bulbous root, oblong, double, and narrow like an olive; one upper, the other lower; and one indeed full, the other soft and wrinkled. *That is to say: the Orchid which they call the Dog-Orchid has leaves both spread across the ground and surrounding the stem and the spike, similar to the soft Squill, but narrower, soft, and smaller: a stem of nine inches in height: upon which are purplish flowers: a bulbous root, oblong, twin, and narrow like an olive; one superior, the other inferior, and one indeed full, the other soft and wrinkled.*

<margin>Dioscorides' passage restored.</margin>
In this description, the Greek manuscripts are incorrectly read as "soft olive" <note>original: "ἐλάιᾳ μαλακῇ"</note> instead of "soft squill" <note>original: "σκίλλῃ μαλακῇ"</note>; for indeed no olive is ever called "soft," and the leaves of an olive are much smaller and narrower than those of the Orchid. Among the Squills, however, there is one that is softer and less rough. For according to Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman author and naturalist</note> in Book 19, Chapter 5, there are three species of Squill: one male with whiter leaves, another female with darker leaves—both being quite rough—and a third called "Epimenidian" <note>named after the Greek seer Epimenides</note>, with narrower and less rough leaves. Dioscorides calls this the "soft squill," and he says the soft and smooth leaves of the Dog-Orchid are similar to its leaves, but narrower.

<margin>Squill.</margin>
Theophrastus **CONFIRMS** this reading in his *History of Plants*, Book 9, where writing about the Orchid he says: *it has a leaf like a squill, but smoother and smaller* <note>original: "ἔχει δὲ φύλλον σκυλλῶδες, λειότερον δὲ καὶ ἔλαττον"</note>. And similarly Pliny in Book 26, Chapter 10, says: *Some say its leaf is that of a squill, but smoother...*

<summary>This page provides a philological analysis of the Dog-Orchid (Cynosorchis), correcting a common error in Greek manuscripts by comparing the plant's leaves to the "soft squill" rather than the "soft olive." The author cites classical authorities Dioscorides, Pliny, and Theophrastus to establish an accurate botanical description.</summary>
<keywords>Cynosorchis, Dog-Orchid, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Theophrastus, Squill, Scilla, botanical philology, orchid roots</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and botanical critique of ancient orchid descriptions. Having just discussed the errors in Dioscorides' text regarding "soft olives" versus "soft squills," the author now turns to similar corruptions found in the works of Theophrastus and Pliny the Elder before moving into a detailed morphological description of the "Fool’s Orchis" (modern Green-winged Orchid).</meta>

# ON ORCHIDS.

<margin>Corrupted passages in Theophrastus and Pliny.</margin>
...and smaller. However, the description of this Orchid is corrupted in the works of both authors. For instead of "squill-like" <note>original: "σκυλλῶδες"</note>, as Marcellus reads it, certain copies of Theophrastus mistakenly read "silphium-like" <note>original: "σιλφῶδες"; silphium was a now-extinct medicinal plant from Cyrene</note>, which provided the occasion for Gaza’s <note>Theodorus Gaza, a 15th-century translator of Theophrastus</note> poor translation. There follows: "the stem is most similar to a pear tree" <note>original: "ἀπίῳ"</note>, or "to one without fire" <note>original: "ἀπύρῳ"</note>, or "to a thorn" <note>original: "ἀκάνθῃ"</note>, which seems entirely corrupted. In Pliny, it is read as "with a thorny stem," and is similarly corrupted: for indeed the stem of the Orchid does not have thorns. Cornarius <note>Janus Cornarius, a 16th-century Saxon physician and humanist</note>, however, relying on this reading of Pliny, thought that the passage in Theophrastus should be restored in this way: "the stem is thorny, but the orchid bulb is most like a pear." We leave it to be more diligently weighed how these corrupted passages of Theophrastus and Pliny ought to be restored and emended.

<margin>The male Fool's Orchis.</margin>
**THE FOOL'S ORCHIS** <note>term: Testiculus Morionis; today known as the Green-winged Orchid (Anacamptis morio). The name "Morio" refers to a jester or fool, likely due to the flower's resemblance to a fool's cap</note> *is twofold: namely, male and female. The male has five or six leaves, long, wide, and smooth, indeed similar to the leaves of a lily but smaller, marked on the upper side with several blackish spots; of these, one and occasionally a second also embrace the stem. The stem is nine inches long <note>original: "dodrantalis"; three-quarters of a foot</note>. The flowers in the spike are purple, whiter around the center, fragrant, and pleasing to the sight; they have a hanging spur <note>original: "corniculum"; literally 'little horn'</note> at the back, almost similar to the spur of the King’s flower <note>term: Regius flos; likely the Larkspur (Consolida), known for its distinct spur</note>. At the front, they resemble the eared and crested hood of a Fool <note>original: "Morionis"</note>. For the little flower gapes in the manner of a hood or an open helmet; the narrow leaflets that rise from the sides represent the ears, and that which rises from the middle represents the crest. The roots are twin globules, similar to the kernel of a nutmeg, from above which the fibers emerge.*

<margin>The female Fool's Orchis.</margin>
**THE FEMALE** *similarly has smooth leaves, but narrower and somewhat veined or striated, in some way similar to the narrow-leaved Plantain. The flowers are also gaping, and like open hoods, with a spur hanging from the back of each, but the leaflets, which are attached in the manner of a crest or ears, are not erect.*

<summary>This page analyzes textual corruptions in classical botanical descriptions by Theophrastus and Pliny and provides a detailed morphological study of the male and female "Fool’s Orchis" (Anacamptis morio).</summary>
<keywords>Theophrastus, Pliny the Elder, Orchids, Fool's Orchis, Green-winged Orchid, botany, philology, plant morphology, Testiculus Morionis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and botanical critique of ancient orchid descriptions from page 205. Having just discussed errors in Dioscorides regarding "soft olives" versus "soft squills," the author now reconciles similar corruptions in Theophrastus and Pliny the Elder before transitioning to a formal description and illustration of the Male Fool's Orchis.</meta>

# ON ORCHIDS.

<margin>Corrupted passages in Theophrastus and Pliny.</margin>
...and smaller. However, the description of this Orchid is corrupted in the works of both authors. For instead of "squill-like" <note>original: "σκυλλῶδες" (skyllōdes)</note>, as Marcellus <note>Marcellus Virgilius Hadrianus, an Italian humanist who produced a famous 1518 edition of Dioscorides</note> reads it, certain copies of Theophrastus mistakenly read "silphium-like" <note>original: "σιλφῶδες" (silphōdes); silphium was a legendary medicinal plant from Cyrene, now extinct</note>, which provided the occasion for Gaza’s <note>Theodorus Gaza, a 15th-century scholar known for translating Greek science into Latin</note> poor translation. There follows: "the stem is most similar to a pear tree" <note>original: "ἀπίῳ" (apiō)</note>, or "to one without fire" <note>original: "ἀπύρῳ" (apyrō), perhaps suggesting a plant that hasn't been dried or scorched</note>, or "to a thorn" <note>original: "ἀκάνθῃ" (akanthē)</note>, which seems entirely corrupted. In Pliny, it is read as "with a thorny stem," and is similarly corrupted: for indeed the stem of the Orchid does not have thorns. Cornarius <note>Janus Cornarius, a 16th-century German physician and philologist</note>, however, relying on this reading of Pliny, thought that the passage in Theophrastus should be restored in this way: "the stem is thorny, but the orchid bulb is most like a pear." We leave it to be more diligently weighed how these corrupted passages of Theophrastus and Pliny ought to be restored and emended.

## THE MALE FOOL'S ORCHID.
<note>original: TESTICVLVS MORIONIS MAS.</note>

->[Image: A detailed woodcut of a Green-winged Orchid. Two rounded tubers are visible at the root system. Long, slender leaves cluster at the base of a single stem, which terminates in a dense spike of flowers. A separate, enlarged detail of a single flower appears to the right.]<-

<margin>The male Fool's Orchis.</margin>
**THE FOOL'S ORCHID** <note>term: Testiculus Morionis; known today as the Green-winged Orchid (*Anacamptis morio*). The name "Morio" translates to "jester" or "fool," referring to the flower's resemblance to a fool's cap</note> is twofold: namely, male and female. The **Male Fool's Orchid** <note>original: Testiculus Morionis mas</note> produces leaves that are narrow, long, and similar to those of the Narrow-leaved Plantain, though smaller and slightly broader. From the middle of these leaves, a stem rises to a height of about nine inches, bearing at its summit flowers of a dark purple color, and occasionally of a paler hue. These flowers are shaped like a hood or a fool's cap, which is why it is given the name "Morio." The roots consist of two tubers, which are rounded like small stones or testicles <note>The word "Orchis" itself comes from the Greek for "testicle," referring to this twin-tuber root structure</note>. One of these tubers is always firm and full, while the other is soft and wrinkled.

<summary>This page concludes a scholarly debate over linguistic errors in ancient botanical texts by Theophrastus and Pliny before providing a formal description and woodcut of the Male Fool's Orchid (Anacamptis morio).</summary>

# ON ORCHIDS.

*...upright, but leaning so closely into the hood of the flower itself that they are not easily observed: these also have twin globules similar to testicles <note>The name "Orchid" comes from the Greek "orchis," meaning testicle, referring to the shape of the plant's twin tubers.</note>, and several fibers emerge above the attachment.*

1. **Of** *this genus, one variety indeed has purple flowers, almost the color of a dark violet, whiter in the middle and variegated with several dots; they grow many together in a dense spike, and the stem is longer than that of the others.*

2. **ANOTHER** *has flowers that are a pale red; the small stem is shorter, and the leaves are somewhat smaller.*

3. **THE THIRD** *has flowers that reflect an exquisitely reddish purple, even a vermilion color: it has a dense and short spike; the flowers are smaller; the leaves are somewhat <term>striated<gloss>marked with thin, parallel grooves or lines</gloss></term>, most of them standing upright and clasping around the stem. There are some who wish to identify this "testicle-plant" as the Erythronion Satyrion <note>original: "Satyrion Erythronion." This refers to a plant mentioned by ancient authorities like Dioscorides, but the author argues here that orchids are a distinct category from the Satyrions.</note>, but they have paid too little attention to the fact that Orchids differ from Satyrions.*

4. **THERE ARE** *indeed even smaller orchids of this genus, which have a short stem: five or six very small leaves, with one or two standing upright around the stem; the flowers in the short spike are sparse; their color is sometimes a pale bluish-purple, sometimes white, and occasionally reddish, but of a dull color and an unpleasant smell.*

From the Greek "tragos" (goat) and "orchis" (testicle/orchid).</note> *similarly bears smooth, wide, and long leaves, very close to those of lilies, and larger than those of other Orchids. It often has a stem a cubit <note>An ancient unit of measure, approximately 18 inches or the length of a forearm.</note> high, with several leaves entwined at the base: it bears many flowers along the stem in a long spike, which are foul and smell like a goat <note>original: "hircum olentes"</note>. The seed produced in the oblong and striated pods is very small. Large tubers lie beneath; several fibers are attached above. # GOAT ORCHID, GOAT'S TESTICLE. 209
<note>original: "Tragorchis, Testiculus hirci." The name is derived from the Greek *tragos* (goat) and *orchis* (testicle), referring to the plant's strong, animal-like odor and the shape of its roots.</note>

*...occasionally reddish, and they are nearly devoid of scent.*

-> [An illustration of the Goat Orchid, featuring a tall flowering spike with long, ribbon-like petals and two rounded tubers at the root.] <-

The **GOAT ORCHID** <note>original: "Tragorchis." Identified as *Himantoglossum hircinum*.</note> is a robust plant, easily distinguished from the smaller varieties by its size and its remarkable blossoms. The leaves are large, broad, and lance-shaped, similar to those of a lily but more elongated, and they wrap around the lower part of the stem.

The most characteristic feature of this plant is the flowering spike. Each individual flower possesses an extraordinarily long, thin, and twisted <term>lip<gloss>the labellum, or the specialized bottom petal of an orchid flower</gloss></term> that hangs downward like a ribbon or a small tail. This gives the entire cluster a wild, shaggy appearance.

The root system is composed of two prominent, rounded **tubers**, which are firm and solid. Several smaller, fibrous roots emerge from the base of the stem just above these tubers.

This plant is most famous—or perhaps infamous—for its powerful and unpleasant scent, which closely resembles the pungent odor of a male goat. It is from this smell that the plant receives its name in both the ancient Greek and Latin traditions.

It serves as a primary example of how early botanists categorized plants based on both visual form and sensory characteristics like smell.</summary>

# ON ORCHIDS. 210
<meta>catchword: testicles</meta>

*...when it is in bloom, it resembles the goat-orchis <note>original: "τραγόρχιν" (tragorchis)</note>, and we have preferred to call it Goat’s testicle.*

<margin>Serapias Orchid.</margin>
**ORCHIDS** <note>original: "TESTICVLI," literally "testicles." In early botany, orchids were almost exclusively referred to by this name because of the shape of their paired underground tubers.</note> *named by us after Serapias <note>Named for the Graeco-Egyptian god Serapis. Today, "Serapias" remains a specific genus of orchids.</note>, we have observed three kinds: the flower of the first resembles a butterfly, the second a drone bee, and the third a fly.*

1 **THE FIRST** *kind, therefore, produces two or three leaves, smooth and somewhat broad, yet much smaller than the leaves of a lily: the stem is nine inches <term>dodrantalem<gloss>a Roman unit of measurement equal to about nine inches or three-quarters of a foot</gloss></term> or higher; the flowers upon it are few, white, and in a certain way similar to butterflies with wings spread; they are composed of three small petals—one at the top and two at the sides representing wings—and a longer, thin part like the body of a butterfly with a hanging tail, containing a honey-like and sweet liquid; these are attached to the stem by a somewhat twisted little stalk: beneath are two white, round tubers, and attached above them are several fibers serving as roots.*

2 **THE SECOND** *kind consists of five or six leaves, which are veined and somewhat broad, to some extent similar to the narrow-leaf plantain, but smaller, with some reflexed toward the ground and others surrounding the stem; the stem is four to nine inches high: with five or six flowers at the top composed of several small petals, of which the lower part seems to very closely resemble a drone bee <note>original: "fucum." This is an early description of the Bee Orchid group (Ophrys), which uses visual mimicry to attract pollinators.</note> in its shape and its dull, darkish color: two tubers are underneath as in the others. Two species of this kind are found: the flower of one has larger, whiter, and somewhat purplish upper petals: the other has entirely smaller and greenish petals. The stem of the latter is shorter; that of the former is taller.*

3 **THE THIRD** *Serapias orchid has smaller and fewer leaves, and a shorter and thinner stem than the second, to which it otherwise appears quite similar: it has three or four flowers, made of three tiny upper petals and a fourth lower petal that is rather long, of a somber blackish-purple color, somewhat variegated, with a shape similar to the body of a certain fly: the testicles* <note>The text cuts off here, referring again to the "testiculi" or tubers.</note>

Now, we have named these three kinds "Serapiades" because their flowers resemble certain fertile and lustful insects. They take their name, as it were, from Serapis <note>A Graeco-Egyptian deity whose cult was associated with fertility and healing.</note>, the former God of the Alexandrians. Leonhart Fuchs also, before our time, identified that genus which resembles a drone <note>original: *fucum*, referring to a drone bee</note> in its flower as the "Female Serapias." Likewise, Dioscorides, when describing the Serapias Orchid, expresses one of these quite clearly. His words are: *Another Orchid, which others call Serapias, has leaves resembling Horehound <note>original Greek: *prasio*; Latin: *Marrubio*. # THE THIRD SERAPIAS ORCHID #

<image-desc>A botanical woodcut illustration of an orchid plant on the left side of the page. It shows a tall, thin stem with a spike of small flowers at the top. ...glossy. If our second *Serapias*, especially the larger variety, is compared with this description, it will be found to match perfectly. Indeed, its leaf is longer and broader than that of Horehound <note>original: *Marrubij*. This plant, also known as Marrubium, has fuzzy, textured leaves.</note> and it is smooth and bent back: the stem is nine inches <note>original: *dodrantalis*; a measure of three-quarters of a foot.</note> high: the upper petals of the flowers reflect a pale reddish-purple: and the tubers <note>The author uses the Latin *testiculi* (little witnesses/testicles) to describe the paired underground bulbs of the orchid.</note> are small. *Pliny* <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of the Natural History.</note> also mentions this same plant, but he attributes to it the leaves of a Leek <note>original: *Porri*.</note> rather than Horehound; this gave the translators of *Dioscorides* the opportunity to render the Greek phrase *resembling horehound* <note>original Greek: ἐοικότα πρασίω (eoikota prasio)</note> as *resembling a leek* <note>There is a linguistic confusion here between the Greek *prasion* (horehound) and *prason* (leek).</note>. *Pliny* says: "Among the few remarkable herbs is the *Orchis* or *Serapias*, with leaves of a Leek, a stem a span high, a purple flower, and a twin root resembling testicles." *Paulus Aegineta* <note>A 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician.</note> writes that the *Serapias* is also called the *Triorchis*, as if having three testicles. For this reason, *Fuchs* <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a German physician and botanist.</note> added three bulbs to his depiction of the *Serapias*. However, *Pliny* attributes only twin bulbs to it; and it has never happened that we observed more than two, either in this one, or the one *Fuchs* depicts, or in other *Orchids*.

# FRAGRANT ORCHID. FRAGRANT TESTICLE-PLANT. #

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut illustration of an orchid plant (Orchis) is featured on the left side of the text. At the base of the plant are two small, ovoid tubers (bulbi gemini). A few narrow, lanceolate leaves emerge from the lower part of a single, tall, slender stem. The stem is topped with a dense, elongated spike of numerous small flowers.</image-desc>

...surrounding them: there are two twin bulbs underneath, small and oblong.

ORCHIDS EMERGE for the most part in marshy and moist places, in woods and meadows: some grow in rich and clayey soil, such as the one called *Tragorchis* <term>Tragorchis: Literally "Goat Orchid" (*Himantoglossum hircinum*), so named because its scent was thought to resemble a male goat.</term>, which is born in the richest soil: others are born in leaner soil. The Fragrant variety is found in many places in high, uncultivated, and dry areas.

THEY ALL BLOOM in the months of May and June, except for the fragrant dwarf variety, which only produces its flowers in August or September.

ALL ARE NAMED *Orchies* by the Greeks, and *testiculi* <note>original: *testiculi*; the Latin term for "testicles," referring to the anatomical shape of the paired tubers.</note> by the Latins. The Germans call them **Knabenkraut** <note>German: "boy-herb," reflecting the folklore that the plant influenced the sex of a child.</note> and **Standelkraut**: the Belgians, **Standelcruyt**: the French call them **du Satyrion**, retaining the name used in the apothecary shops. Indeed, the shops call the Orchids *Satyria*, and they mix their bulbs into the medicines and compounds used as *Satyria* <note>Satyria refers to medicines intended to stimulate sexual desire, named after the lustful Satyrs of mythology.</note>. Apuleius <note>Referring to Pseudo-Apuleius, the author of a famous 4th-century herbal that blended botanical knowledge with folklore.</note> seems to have provided the occasion for this confusion, as he makes no distinction between Orchids and *Satyria*, but confuses both *Satyrion* with both types of *Orchis*—namely the one called "dog-like" <note>original: *canis dicto*; referring to the *Cynosorchis* or Dog Orchid.</note> and the one named *Serapias*. He says: "It is called *Satyrion* by the Greeks, *Cynosorchis* by others, *Entaticos* <term>Entaticos: A Greek-derived term meaning "stimulating" or "causing tension," used for aphrodisiacs.</term> by others, as well as *Panion*, *Serapion*, and *Orchis*. The Gauls call it *Uram*: the Italians, *Priapiscon* <note>A name derived from Priapus, the god of fertility.</note>: others call it *Orminalem*: and others *testiculum leporinum* <note>Latin: "hare's testicle."</note>."

NOW, THE POWER of the *Cynosorchis* roots is hot and mo-

--- Page 219 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal and reproductive properties of orchid bulbs, expanding on their use as aphrodisiacs or contraceptives according to ancient authorities like Galen and Dioscorides. It also provides a detailed botanical explanation of the orchid’s dual-bulb root system and how to identify the most potent specimen based on its growth cycle.</meta>

# ON ORCHIDS #

...moist: but the larger one seems to have much surplus and flatulent humidity, and for that reason, when drunk, it excites **Venus** <note>original: *Venerem*; a common classical euphemism for sexual desire or the goddess of love.</note>. The other smaller one, on the contrary, has a temperament tending toward the hotter and drier: and so this root is so far from stimulating **intercourse** <note>original: *coitum*.</note> that it even clearly restrains and represses it; the author is **Galen** <note>Galen of Pergamon (129–c. 216 AD), an influential Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire.</note>. And concerning the root, **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary botanical manual for centuries.</note> says it is told that if men eat the larger, they father males; if women eat the smaller, they bear females. They add that in **Thessaly** <note>A region in Greece famous in antiquity for witchcraft and herbal medicine.</note>, women give the tender root to be drunk with goat's milk to excite Venus, but the dry root to inhibit it.

**SERAPIAS** <note>original: *SERAPIADEM*; a genus of orchids named after the Graeco-Egyptian god Serapis.</note>, Galen says, possesses a drier quality and is not as suited for Venus.

**OUR AGE** uses the bulbs of all Orchids to excite Venus: and pharmacists indiscriminately mix any of them into compositions made for that purpose: however, the bulbs of the **Goat Orchid** <term>Tragorchios: Literally "Goat Orchid" (*Himantoglossum hircinum*), prized for its potency, though notorious for its strong, goat-like odor.</term> are superior. Moreover, both bulbs should not be taken, but rather the harder, fuller one having more juice: the flabbier and more wrinkled one is less suitable, or not suitable at all.

HOWEVER, the fuller one is not always the larger, but often the smaller: specifically if the bulbs are collected before the stalk has finished flowering, or as soon as the stem breaks out. For the juicier one does not surpass the other in size before the seed has begun to ripen. For since one bulb swells in alternating years while the other subsides and perishes, it cannot be that the harder and juicier one is always the larger. Indeed, when the leaves break out, then at last—or not long before—the fuller one begins to grow; as it gradually takes on growth, the other becomes flaccid and contracts wrinkles, until, once the seed has ripened, it eventually subsides and perishes along with the leaves and small stems; meanwhile, the one that grew in the interim remains vigorous and full of juice.

<summary>This page discusses the reproductive folklore and medicinal properties of orchid bulbs, explaining how their physical condition relates to their potency as aphrodisiacs. It highlights the biological cycle of the "twin" bulbs, where one grows while the other withers.</summary>
<keywords>Orchids, Galen, Dioscorides, aphrodisiacs, fertility, Goat Orchid, botanical growth cycle, herbal medicine</keywords>

<meta>The previous page concludes a discussion on the medicinal properties of various orchids, specifically their use in fertility and sexual health as described by Galen and Dioscorides. This page transitions into a botanical classification of the "Royal Orchid" (Satyrium basilicon), distinguishing between the male, female, and smaller varieties based on their physical characteristics.</meta>

# CHAPTER LXII. ON THE ROYAL ORCHID #

**WHAT** the modern era <note>original: *recentior ætas*</note> calls the **Royal Orchid** <note>original: *Satyrium basilicon*; the name *Satyrium* refers to the Satyrs of Greek myth, known for their lust, while *basilicon* means "royal." Today, these are often identified as species of palmate orchids, such as *Dactylorhiza*.</note> is not of just one kind. For there is one larger variety and another smaller; some with unspotted leaves, and others with spots.

<margin>1</margin> **THE LARGER** **Royal Orchid**, which most call the male, has oblong, wide, and smooth leaves, smaller than lily leaves, and sprinkled with either no spots at all or spots that are barely visible. The stems are a foot or more in height, not without leaves growing along them. At the top, the flowers are spiked, pale purple in color, and sprinkled with small dots of deep purple. They are shaped like the **Male Fool’s Orchid** <note>original: *testiculi Morionis maris*; likely referring to the Green-winged Orchid (*Anacamptis morio*).</note>, except that they lack a crest. Beneath these flowers, individual pointed leaflets grow. The roots are twin and shaped like the palms of hands, each divided into four "fingers"; one of these is flabbier and almost spongy, while the other is vigorous and solid, with a few fibers growing out from where they join.

<margin>2</margin> **THE OTHER** larger kind, which they call the female—though it is smaller than the first—produces leaves of the same shape, but smaller and sprinkled with many black spots. The flowers are similar to hoods, crested and gaping, like the **Male Fool’s Orchid**. Their color is sometimes white, other times turning toward purple or red, or pale leaning toward blue, but they are always decorated and variegated with more purple dots. The roots are similar to the other variety.

<margin>3</margin> **THE SMALLER** kind has narrow leaves and, as **Mattioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist who wrote influential commentaries on the works of Dioscorides.</note> says, they are like those of **Saffron** <note>original: *croci*.</note>. The stem is nine inches long <term>dodrantali: A Roman measurement of approximately nine inches, representing three-quarters of a foot.</term>, reed-like and smooth, at the top of which a purple flower shines forth, rivaling the **Amaranth** <note>A deep red or purple flower often used in ancient times as a symbol of immortality.</note>. When fresh, it gives off a most sweet scent. The roots are similar to the others, but smaller in size.

**THESE** **Royal Orchids** grow mostly in damp, swampy places, in meadows, and in woods. They bloom in **May and June**.

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical description of the "Royal Orchid" (Satyrium basilicon), categorizing it into male, female, and small varieties based on leaf spotting, flower color, and the distinctive hand-shaped structure of their roots.</summary>

<keywords>Royal Orchid, Satyrium basilicon, Mattioli, botany, plant classification, palmate roots, medicinal herbs, floral morphology</keywords>

# CHAPTER LXII. ON THE ROYAL ORCHID #

**WHAT** the modern era <note>original: *recentior ætas*</note> calls the **Royal Orchid** <note>original: *Satyrium basilicon*; the name *Satyrium* refers to the Satyrs of Greek myth, known for their lust, while *basilicon* means "royal." Today, these are often identified as species of palmate orchids, such as *Dactylorhiza*.</note> is not of just one kind. For there is one larger variety and another smaller; some with unspotted leaves, and others with spots.

<margin>1</margin> **THE LARGER** **Royal Orchid**, which most call the male, has oblong, wide, and smooth leaves, smaller than lily leaves, and sprinkled with either no spots at all or spots that are barely visible. The stems are a foot or more in height, not without leaves growing along them. At the top, the flowers are spiked, pale purple in color, and sprinkled with small dots of deep purple. They are shaped like the **Male Fool’s Orchid** <note>original: *testiculi Morionis maris*; likely referring to the Green-winged Orchid (*Anacamptis morio*).</note>, except that they lack a crest. Beneath these flowers, individual pointed leaflets grow. The roots are twin and shaped like the palms of hands <note>This "palmate" or hand-shaped root is a defining characteristic of the genus *Dactylorhiza*.</note>, each divided into four "fingers"; one of these is flabbier and almost spongy, while the other is vigorous and solid, with a few fibers growing out from where they join.

<margin>2</margin> **THE OTHER** larger kind, which they call the female—though it is smaller than the first—produces leaves of the same shape, but smaller and sprinkled with many black spots. The flowers are similar to those of the male, but they are often clustered in a shorter, denser spike and display a more vivid purple color.

<image-desc>A botanical woodcut illustration of a terrestrial orchid, likely a species of *Dactylorhiza* <note>The name *Dactylorhiza* is derived from the Greek words for "finger" and "root," referring to the hand-shaped tubers.</note>. To the right of the main flower spike is a larger, isolated depiction of a single flower, showing its tripartite labellum <note>the large, three-lobed "lip" or petal that serves as a landing pad for pollinators</note>. Several lanceolate <note>tapering, sword-shaped</note> leaves are attached to the stem; the larger, lower leaves are marked with dark spots or mottling, a characteristic that led historical botanists to distinguish this from the "first" variety. The root system at the base features characteristic fleshy, palmate <note>hand-shaped or divided into finger-like lobes</note> tubers and thin fibrous roots.</image-desc>

<note>Translator's note: The "Second Royal Orchid" described here corresponds to what modern botanists classify as palmate orchids, such as the Spotted Orchid. In Renaissance botany, the presence or absence of spots on the leaves was a primary method for distinguishing between species of *Satyrium*.</note>

<meta>This page follows the botanical illustration of the "Second Royal Orchid" on page 218. # ON THE ROYAL ORCHID.
<note>original: *DE SATYRIO BASILICO*</note>

Our age calls these *Satyria Basilica* or "Royal Orchids": likewise also "Hands of Christ" <note>original: *Palmas Christi*</note>. There is, however, another plant with this same name, namely the Castor oil plant <term>Ricinus; a plant whose seeds are used for oil, though its name was often confused with the hand-shaped orchids due to the palm-like shape of its leaves</term>; for many commonly call this the Hand of Christ as well. The Germans call the Royal Orchid *Crutzblum* <note>literally "Cross-flower"</note>: the people of the Low Countries call it *Handekens cruyt* <note>literally "Little-hands herb," referring to the hand-shaped tubers</note>: the French call it *Satyrio royal*.

There are those who wish this to be the *Buzeiden* or *Buzidan* of the Arabs. But Avicenna <note>a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age</note> says that *Buzeiden* is an Indian woody medicine. And Serapio <note>an influential Arabic medical writer</note> says that the roots of *Buzeiden* are hard, white, and similar to white *Behen* <note>likely referring to *Centaurea behen*, a root used in medieval pharmacy</note>, and that it is a medicine from India. The roots of this royal orchid, however, are anything but woody, and they are not sought from India, but are found everywhere in all the provinces of Europe: thus, they are far removed from *Buzeiden*.

Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> maintains that the Royal Orchid is the "Citron Fingers" <note>original: *Digitos citrinos*</note> of Avicenna, and he rebukes the Monks who published commentaries on the pharmaceutical compositions of Mesue <note>an 8th-century Christian physician in Baghdad whose works were foundational to European pharmacy</note>. These Monks were hesitant to make a definitive identification, leaving the judgment to the reader. To us, however, the hesitation of the Monks is more pleasing than the assertion of Matthiolus. For the roots of this orchid are not hard; rather, when fresh they are vigorous and succulent, and when dried they become entirely flaccid: they are not at all citron-yellow or powdery.

<margin>Citron Fingers.</margin> Citron fingers, according to Avicenna, are hard, and some are citron-yellow with a powdery quality. His words are: "What is *Asabasafra* <note>the Arabic term for Citron Fingers</note> or citron fingers? Its shape is like the palm of the hand, a mixture of citron-yellow and white, and it is hard, possessing a little sweetness; and a variety of it is citron-yellow with a powdery nature without whiteness." Now Rhazes <note>Al-Razi, another foundational Persian physician</note> also, in the last book of the *Continens*, calls these "Saffron Fingers" <note>original: *digitos croceos*</note>, as the manuscripts have it; and he says it is a gum or a dyer's material. But the roots of the Royal Orchid are anything but saffron-colored, and they are considered not at all useful for dyeing. All these facts clearly show that the plant commonly called the Royal Orchid differs from the "Citron Fingers" of Avicenna and Rhazes.

<meta>catchword: BUT</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the naming of the Royal Orchid (Satyrium basilicon), correcting its confusion with the Castor oil plant and Indian medicines like Buzeiden. The author critiques Matthiolus's identification of the orchid with the "Citron Fingers" mentioned by Arabic physicians like Avicenna and Rhazes.</summary>

<header>ON THE FALSE ORCHID.</header>

But the roots of the Royal Orchid <note>original: *Satyrij basilici*</note> resemble the *Orchis* in flavor and taste, and for that reason, they are thought to have similar properties. Moreover, Nicolaus Nicolus <note>Niccolò Falcucci, an influential 15th-century Italian physician</note> says in his chapter on the cure of quartan fever that it has the power to purge through both the upper and lower belly <term>referring to emetic and laxative properties</term>; and against long-standing quartan fevers <term>a form of malaria where paroxysms occur every fourth day</term>, after general purges have been performed, a piece of the root the length of a thumb is crushed and usefully administered with wine before the onset of the fever. By taking this remedy three times, a certain Biliotus was freed from the quartan fever after his 44th or 45th attack.

### ON THE FALSE ORCHID, OR TWAYBLADE. CHAPTER 63.

To the Orchids, the more recent age also assigns what they call *Bifolium* <note>literally "two-leaf"</note>, which is a certain bastard and false kind of orchid <note>original: *Orchion nothum & adulterinum*</note>. It has no more than two leaves, and these are positioned opposite each other on the stem; they are broad, veined, and similar to the leaves of the Broad-leaved Plantain <note>original: *Plantaginis latifoliæ*</note>, but smaller. The stem is about nine inches long <term>original: *dodrantalem*; a Roman measurement of three-quarters of a foot</term>; the flowers are arranged in order along it, somewhat similar to the "Third Serapias Orchid" <note>a classification used by early botanists for certain orchid species</note>; but they are somewhat green or a whitish-green and narrower, very closely resembling the shape of gnats. The roots are fibrous, extending in different directions. It grows in damp and marshy places. It flowers along with the Orchids in May and June.

<margin>Ophrys.</margin>
Our age calls it *Bifolium*: the Germans call it *Zueyblat* <note>modern German: *Zweiblatt*</note>; our people [in the Low Countries] call it *Tweeblat* <note>modern Dutch: *Tweeblad*</note>. It is thought to be the plant called *Ophris* by Pliny <note>the Roman naturalist Pliny the Elder</note>, which he says is an herb similar to a jagged-edged vegetable, having two leaves. But it cannot be asserted that it is the true *Ophrys* so long as it remains unknown what that "jagged-edged vegetable" is that the *Ophrys* is said to resemble. To others it seems to be *Alisma* <note>Water Plantain</note>, but how little it agrees with *Alisma* is easy for anyone to gather from what was written above in Chapter 22, where the nature of *Alisma* [is shown...]

<meta>catchword: is shown (ostensum)</meta>

### ON GOAT’S BEARD AND SCORZONERA. CHAPTER 64.

*Goat’s Beard* <note>original: *Tragopogon*</note> *has leaves that are oblong, narrow, and pointed, being wider and larger than the leaves of Saffron. The stem is round and divided into several branches; at the top are large cups <term>the calyx, or the whorl of protective leaves at the base of a flower</term>, from which emerge large, manifold flowers with many petals. These are similar in size and appearance to the herb commonly called Dandelion* <note>original: *Dentis Leonis*, literally "Lion’s Tooth"</note>*. Their color is either yellow or a deep reddish-purple; they open when the sun rises, but close at noon. As they fade, they turn into gray, downy tufts* <term>pappus; the feathery bristles on seeds that allow them to be carried by the wind</term>*, beneath which lies the seed, which is long, blackish, and pointed. The root is white and long. The entire plant, along with the root, contains a milky sap that flows out when it is wounded. It grows wild in many uncultivated and damp places in both regions of Germany, especially the yellow-flowered variety. It also grows taller and fuller in gardens, with larger and more beautiful flowers, especially the purple variety. It blooms in June and July.*

*Among the Greeks it is called* τραγοπώγων <note>original: *tragopōgōn*</note> *and* κομὴ <note>original: *komē*, meaning "hair"</note>*. In Latin, it is called Hirci barba [Goat's beard] or Coma [Hair]. In German, it is* **Bocks bart**. *In the language of the Low Countries, it is* **Bockbaert** *or* **Josephs bloemen** <note>original: "Joseph’s flowers," a common folk name because the flower closes at midday, often associated with Joseph of Nazareth's working hours</note>*. In French, Barbe de bouc. In Italian, according to Mattioli, it is Sassefrica* <note>original: *Sassefrica*, likely referring to Salsify</note>*.

*The herb, as Dioscorides says, is suitable for eating; in our time, the root is eaten by many in the winter months, mixed into salads.*

<margin>Scorzonera.</margin>
*Of this genus is the plant which Spain calls Scurzonera or Scorzonera, with leaves that are oblong, somewhat white, fleshy, and point-* <note>catchword: *acumi-* / pointed</note>

### ON GOAT’S BEARD AND SCORZONERA. CHAPTER 64.

*Goat’s Beard* <note>original: *Tragopogon*</note> *has leaves that are oblong, narrow, and pointed, being wider and larger than the leaves of Saffron. The stem is round and divided into several branches; at the top are large cups* <term>the calyx, or the whorl of protective leaves at the base of a flower</term>*, from which emerge large, manifold flowers with many petals. The root is white and long. The entire plant, along with the root, contains a milky sap that flows out when it is wounded. It also grows taller and fuller in gardens, with larger and more beautiful flowers, especially the purple variety. It blooms in June and July.*

-># SCORZONERA.<-

This illustration depicts the **Scorzonera**, commonly known today as *Viper’s Grass* or *Black Salsify*. 

The woodcut displays the plant’s distinctive anatomy:
*   **The Root:** A thick, elongated, and somewhat gnarled horizontal root, which was highly valued in traditional medicine.
*   **The Foliage:** Several vertical stems emerge from the base, bearing leaves that are lance-shaped <term>long and narrow, tapering to a point like a spear-head</term> with distinct, irregularly wavy or serrated margins.
*   **The Flowers:** At the top of the stems are flower heads in various stages of development. One large, fully open composite flower <term>a flower head made up of many small individual flowers</term> with many thin petals is shown at the top center. Surrounding it are several closed, elongated buds encased in pointed, overlapping bracts <term>small, leaf-like structures that protect the base of the flower</term>.

<note>The name "Scorzonera" is thought to derive from the Old French *escorzon*, meaning viper, referring to the root's supposed efficacy against snake venom, or from the Italian *scorza nera*, meaning "black bark," referring to the dark skin of the root.</note>

<summary>This page features a botanical woodcut of Scorzonera (Viper's Grass), focusing on its gnarled root and composite flowers, and concludes the Greek etymology for the related Goat's Beard plant.</summary>
<keywords>Scorzonera, Viper's Grass, Black Salsify, Tragopogon, Goat's Beard, botanical illustration, medicinal roots, composite flowers</keywords>
