<meta>This page continues from page 224, concluding the physical description and medicinal properties of Scorzonera (Viper's Grass) before introducing a new chapter on Chamomile.</meta>

*...pointed, wider, and occasionally slightly wavy along the edges: with a round stem, a foot or more in height; a composite yellow flower entirely similar <note>Referring to the Goat's Beard described on the previous page</note>, which also dissolves into downy tufts: with oblong, whitish, and smaller seeds; and a long, single, white root, covered by a thin, blackish bark.*

**Scorzonera grows** *in most mountains of Spain, in damp, dark, and marshy places: it is also found in Thuringia and Bohemia in Germany, in woods and mountains in similar locations. Among the Spaniards, the flower appears in the month of May: in Germany, however, it appears later, namely in June and July.*

**The Spaniards** *call it Scorzonera, which in Latin sounds like Viperina, Viperaria, or Serpentaria <note>original: "Viperinam siue Viperariam aut Serpentariã" — all names derived from the words for vipers and serpents</note>; it is so named because it is considered effective against the poisons of vipers and snakes. For the Spaniards call a viper a Scurzo. Among the Germans and other nations, it has not found a [specific native] name.*
<note>The sentence "For the Spaniards call a viper a Scurzo" is underlined in red ink in the original.</note>

**They say** *moreover that this plant wards off the contagion of the plague, resists all poisons, and especially heals the bites of venomous vipers, whether by drinking the juice of the root or the herb: it is also beneficial for those suffering from heart troubles <term>cardiacis</term> and those afflicted by fainting <term>syncope</term>, as well as those subject to the falling sickness <term>comitiali morbo — epilepsy</term> and dizziness: the chewed root is said to bring about cheerfulness and dispel all sadness: the milky juice of the root, when applied to the eyes, sharpens the vision.*
<note>The phrase "bring about cheerfulness and dispel all sadness" is underlined in red ink in the original.</note>

# ON COMMON CHAMOMILE.
## CHAPTER LXV.

**The flower of Chamomile**, *although it is not a garland flower <note>original: "coronarius" — flowers traditionally used for weaving wreaths or crowns</note>, has nevertheless claimed its place among flowers because of its frequent use in medicine.*

**There are**, *however, several kinds of Chamomiles. One is the legitimate kind, most suitable for medicine and in common use, which for that reason is called "Common Chamomile": then there are others that are wild...*

# ON COMMON CHAMOMILE

...wild ones of no or little use; the third is the fragrant garden variety. Of that which is most familiar, we shall treat first.

IT HAS slender little stems divided into many branches, about a cubit long <term>cubit: an ancient measurement roughly the length of a forearm, about 18 inches</term>: the leaves are thin, finely divided, and numerous: the flowers are at the top, yellow in the middle but white on the outside; specifically, many small white petals surround the yellow disk of the flower itself, each of which is narrow and oblong in shape: the roots are thin and fibrous. It emerges everywhere in fields along with the grain; and it is familiar throughout all of Europe. The flowers are collected especially in the month of June.

THE apothecary shops <note>original: "officinæ"</note> call it *Camomilla*; in common speech *Camille* and *Camomillebloemen* <note>Flemish/Dutch: "chamomile flowers"</note>. The Germans call it *Chamill*, and the French *Camomille*. Among the ancients it is *chamaimelon* <note>Greek: χαμαίμηλον; literally "earth-apple"</note> or Chamæmelon, and a type of *Anthemis*, which is called *leukanthemon* <note>Greek: λευκάνθεμον; "white-flower"</note> because of those white petals surrounding the yellow circle of the flower. For according to Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and the author of "De Materia Medica," a primary source for botanists.</note>, there are three types of *Anthemis*, and indeed all have flowers yellow in the center, but along the circumference, one has white petals, the second yellow, and the third purple. That which consists of yellow petals around the edge is named *chrysanthemon* <note>Greek: χρυσάνθεμον; "gold-flower"</note>: the third, with purple petals, is called *eranthemon* <note>Greek: ἠράνθεμον; "spring-flower"</note>, because it clearly blooms in the spring.

INDEED, it is called Chamæmelon because the flowers have the scent of apples <note>original: "μηλᾶ" (mela) or "mali"</note>, a scent which is evident and clear to perceive in the common Chamomile; so that even from this one may conclude that the common variety is the true and legitimate one; which its very properties also more clearly demonstrate. For the properties of common Chamomile are entirely such as Galen <note>A highly influential 2nd-century Greek physician in the Roman Empire.</note> attributed to Chamæmelon.

CHAMÆMELON, Galen says, warms and dries in the first degree <term>first degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating the mildest intensity of a physical quality</term>, and is composed of "thin parts" <note>This refers to its ability to penetrate and disperse fluids in the body</note>; it has the power of digesting, relaxing, and thinning. Also in another place: it is very similar to the Rose in its "thinness," though in its heat it approaches closer to the properties of oil for hu—

DE CHAMÆ. VVLGARI.
<note>On Common Chamomile</note>

*...most familiar and temperate. Therefore, it helps against weariness, if anything else does, among the first, and settles and mitigates pains: furthermore, it relaxes and loosens tensions: it softens those things which are moderately hard, and thins those that are congested <note>original: "constipata," referring to blocked or dense tissues</note>. Indeed, daily experience itself demonstrates that all these powers exist in our common* **Chamomile**. *For it warms moderately, dries but little, and consists of very fine parts <note>A reference to "thinness of substance," a concept in Galenic medicine suggesting the medicine can penetrate deep into the body</note>; it is endowed with a singular and wonderful power for digesting <note>In historical medicine, "digesting" or "digerendi" meant the process of maturing or breaking down "crude" or morbid humors</note> and relaxing; in short, it has many uses for everything that needs to be gently warmed, loosened, dispersed, and soothed. It eases stones and colicky pains: it moderately provokes urine: it settles griping and gnawing of the belly: it softens pains in the sides: it softens hard tumors, and disperses raw humors <term>humors: in ancient medicine, the four vital fluids of the body; "raw" humors were those not yet properly processed by the body's heat</term>. The oil made from the flowers provides the same benefits; it is a remedy against all weariness and is usefully mixed into all preparations applied to put any pain to sleep.*

# ON WILD CHAMOMILES,
## OR MAYWEEDS.
### CHAPTER LXVI.

Of the **Wild Chamomiles**, which they commonly call *Cotulas* <note>The genus *Anthemis* or *Matricaria*, often called Mayweeds</note>, there are three kinds: one stinking, and two others not stinking: of these, one has a flower that is white around the circumference, while the other is entirely yellow.
**Stinking Wild CHAMOMILE**, or *Stinking Mayweed*, produces round, green, fragile, and succulent stems, divided into many branches <note>original: "alas," literally "wings"</note>, thicker and often taller than the true **Chamomiles**: the leaves are similarly larger and of a dark green: the flowers are similar in shape and color. The whole herb stinks and emits a heavy odor: from which the surname "stinking" was given...

### ON WILD CHAMOMILES.

*...given. It grows in fields, along paths, and at the borders of farmlands. It blooms at the same time as Chamomile.*

**A P O T H E C A R I E S** <note>original: "OFFICINÆ"</note> *and herbalists everywhere call it* **Stinking Mayweed** <note>original: "Cotulam fætidam"</note>; *most call it* **Stinking Chamomile**. *The Germans call it* **Toad-dill / Dog-dill / Dog-flower** <note>original: "Krottendill / Hundsdill / Hundsblum"</note>. *The Belgians call it* **Toad-flowers / Stinking Chamomile** <note>original: "Paddebloemen / stinckende Camille"</note>. *The French call it* **Espargoutte**; *by some it is called* **dog-flower** <note>original: "κυνανθεμὶς" (kynanthemis)</note>, *by others* **dog-herb** <note>original: "κυνοβοτανὴ" (kynobotane)</note>. *Leonhart Fuchs* <note>A prominent 16th-century German physician and herbalist.</note> *maintained that it was* **Parthenium**. *But the true and legitimate* **Parthenium** *is what is commonly called* **Feverfew** <note>original: "Matricaria"</note>, *which has a much heavier scent and a very bitter taste, with thin leaves similar to Coriander. Stinking Mayweed, however, smells less heavily, and its leaves do not resemble Coriander.*

<margin>2</margin> **A SECOND VARIETY** *is the* **Non-stinking White Mayweed**, *which is also called* **Ox-eye** <note>original: "Oculus bouis"</note> *by most; it produces many stems, which are thinner, harder, and more flexible than those of* **Stinking Mayweed**; *around them are thin leaves, a little longer and whiter than Chamomile leaves. The flowers are yellow in the middle and white around the circumference, similar to the flowers of Chamomile and Stinking Mayweed, but larger, and with little or no scent. The roots are hard and woody, with attached fibers, which remain for several years and sprout again in the spring. It is found in fields and grain lands along with Chamomile. It blooms in the summer months.*

**O U R** *age calls this non-stinking Mayweed, and* **White Mayweed**, *while most call it* **Ox-eye** *and* **Buphthalmum** <note>term: Buphthalmum|From the Greek for "ox-eye," a name used by ancient botanists for plants with large, daisy-like flower heads.</note>. *The Germans call it* **Cow-dill / Dog-eye / Cow-eye** <note>original: "Kudil / hundsaug / kuaug"</note>; *the Belgians,* **Cow-dill / Cow-eye** <note>original: "Coedille / Coeooghe"</note>. *Indeed, it was the opinion of Leonhart Fuchs that this was the true* **Buphthalmum**. *But to us, it seems that the only true and legitimate variety is that which is described below under the name Buphthalmum. For according to the authority of Dioscorides* <note>An ancient Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical reference for centuries.</note>, *the* **Buphthalmum** *has* **fennel-like** <note>original: "μαραθροειδῆ" (marathroeidē)</note> *leaves; that is, having a resemblance to the leaves of fennel, and a honey-yellow flower. This plant, however, does not have leaves like fennel, nor is the flower entirely yellow or honey-colored, but rather...*

### ON WILD CHAMOMILES.

...the first part, namely the white circumference; which alone moved **Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist.</note> to deny that this **White Mayweed** <note>original: "Cotulam hanc albam"</note>, or **Common Ox-eye** <note>original: "vulgare Buphthalmum"</note>, was the true and legitimate **Ox-eye** <note>original: "Buphthalmum"</note>. Therefore, it is not **Ox-eye**, though it is called by that name by most, but is instead a species of **Mayweed** <note>original: "Cotulæ"</note> and **Wild Chamomile**.

<margin>Melandrium</margin>
Pliny, in book 26, chapter 7, mentions the herb **Melandrium**, which grows in grain fields and meadows, with a white and fragrant flower. Whether this **Melandrium** is indeed **Non-stinking Mayweed** <note>original: "Cotula non fœtida"</note> must be more diligently investigated; for on account of Pliny's brevity, no one can easily affirm what kind of herb **Melandrium** might be.

**A THIRD VARIETY**, **Yellow Mayweed** <note>original: "Cotula lutea"</note>, emerges with many small stems; its leaves are larger, thicker, and whiter than those of **White Mayweed**. The flowers, both in the center and around the circular edge, are honey-colored or yellow. The root is hard, woody, and long-lasting. It grows in **Germany and France**, in the same and similar places as **White Mayweed**. Among the **Belgians**, it is a garden plant. It puts forth its flower in **June and July**.

**GERMANS** call it **Streichblumen** <note>literally "stroke-flowers"</note> and **Steinblumen** <note>literally "stone-flowers"</note>. The Belgians call it **Strÿckbloemen**. **Fuchs** <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a key figure in the history of botany.</note> identifies it as a **species of Anthemis**, and **Golden-flowered Chamomile** <note>original: "Chamæmelum Chryſanthemum"</note>; however, in its leaf it resembles the **White-flowered Chamomile** <note>original: "Chamæmelum Leucanthemum"</note> very little, and in its scent not at all. **Matthiolus** seems to have depicted it as the legitimate **Ox-eye** <note>original: "Buphthalmo"</note>; yet it lacks the **Fennel-like leaf** <note>original: "folium Marathroides"</note> which **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, the primary ancient Greek authority on herbal medicine.</note> testifies that **Ox-eye** possesses. For these reasons, we have thought it better to place it among the **Mayweeds and Wild Chamomiles** rather than rashly assert it to be either one.

**THE USE** of these Mayweeds in medicine or food is none; therefore, their properties are unknown. They seem, however, to be all somewhat hot and dry, similar in their powers to the common use of **Chamomile**, but far inferior and weaker. The one which is called stinking...

<header>ON SCENTED CHAMOMILE.</header>

*It is recommended for diseases of the womb; but especially by those who believe it to be **Feverfew** <note>original: "Parthenium"</note>, and who have transferred the powers of **Feverfew** to this plant. ### ->ON SCENTED CHAMOMILE.<-

#### ->CHAPTER LXVII.<-

**SCENTED Chamomile** <note>original: "Odoratum Chamæmelum"</note> *is of two kinds: one may be called **White-flowered** <note>original: "Leucanthemum"</note>, and the other **Golden-flowered** <note>original: "Chrysanthemum"</note>.*

<margin>1</margin>**FROM THE WHITE-FLOWERED** *scented Chamomile, many stems emerge, not upright, but drooping and as if creeping along the ground: the leaves are thin and divided, like those of the common Chamomile, but larger and greener. The flower is also similar, namely yellow in the middle and surrounded by white petals <note>original: "foliolis," literally little leaves</note> along the circumference: the root is fibrous and durable in nature. The leaves and flowers give off a very pleasant and delightful scent with a certain weightiness, very similar to the scents of aromatic spices, which remains for a long time even when the plant is dry.*

**THERE IS** *also a certain variety of this kind which produces a dense multiplication of the petals surrounding the center of the flower, with the yellow part compressed into a narrow circle; in scent and form, it is in all ways similar to the preceding one.*

<margin>2</margin>**THE GOLDEN-FLOWERED** *scented Chamomile similarly produces thin, weak stems that lie prostrate on the ground: the leaves are also thin, like the first kind, but whiter and a little smaller. Instead of the flower which—in all other kinds of Chamomile and Mayweed—is a yellow disc in the middle, this one is naked and not surrounded by any encircling petals; the root is similar. This herb is also fragrant, similar to the first in scent and medicinal property.*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Scented Chamomile from the previous page's illustration, moving from its physical description to its nomenclature and medicinal properties, before beginning a new chapter on the "Spring-flower" or Pheasant's Eye.</meta>

<page-num>234</page-num>
<header>ON SCENTED CHAMOMILE <note>original: "DE CHAMAE. ODORATO."</note></header>

BOTH [varieties] are garden plants in the Low Countries <note>original: "Belgio"</note>; they flower in the months of July and August.

It is properly called **Scented Chamomile** <note>original: *Chamaemelum odoratum*</note>. Tragus <note>Hieronymus Bock (1498–1554), a pioneering German botanist</note> calls it **Noble Chamomile** <note>original: *Chamæmelum nobile*</note>. Our common folk call it *Roman Chamomile* <note>original: *Roomſche Camille*, that is, *Chamæmelum Romanum*</note>. The one that is white in flower can be called **White-flower** <note>original: *Leucanthemum*</note>; the other **Gold-flower** <note>original: *Chryſanthemum*</note>. However, it does not seem entirely certain that these were the plants used by Dioscorides, Galen, or other ancient authorities; it is more likely that they were overlooked or unknown to them.

FOR their potency is much more intense than that which Galen attributes to Chamomile. Indeed, in terms of heat and dryness, they greatly exceed that mild and balanced quality—familiar to the living body—which Galen attributes to Chamomile, and which daily experience shows it to possess; for these are **hot and dry in the second degree** <term>second degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating a moderate level of intensity on a scale of one (mildest) to four (strongest)</term>. Since they also possess a "thinness of substance" <note>a quality allowing the medicine to penetrate deeply into the body</note>, they have a greater power to digest <note>to break down or process bodily humors</note> and to thin [the humors]; however, their power to soften and to soothe or mitigate pain is weaker. Therefore, where there is a need for heating or thinning beyond what common Chamomile can achieve, these may be usefully applied—as in baths prepared for the purpose of inducing sweat and opening the pores <note>original: *rarefaciendæ cutis*, literally "thinning the skin"</note>, in which the use of these Chamomiles should not be neglected. For they also loosen skin that is too constricted, draw out sweat, and provide a pleasant scent to the body.

<page-num>236</page-num>

# ON THE COMMON SPRING-FLOWER. <note>original: "DE ERANTHEMO VVLGARI"; likely referring to *Adonis annua*, commonly known as Pheasant's Eye.</note>

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing the description from the previous chapter's text.</meta>

*...and similar things: the flowers are not large, shaped like the flowers of **Buttercups** <note>original: "Ranunculi"</note>, beautifully red like cinnabar; these are followed by an oblong head, gathered from many seeds that are round, pointed, and blackish-green in color. The roots are fibrous. The plant has a grassy smell, somewhat heavy, but less so than **Stinking Mayweed** <note>original: "Cotulæ fœtidæ"</note>, and not at all like **Scented Chamomile**. It grows in many provinces of Europe, in fields together with **Spelt** <note>original: "Zea"</note>, **Wheat**, or other grains. The Belgians plant it in gardens. It blooms in the summer months: May, June, and July.*

Our people call it in their own language **Bruynettekens** <note>A Flemish/Dutch name meaning "Little Brownings," likely referring to the dark, brownish-black center of the red flower.</note>; some of the Germans call it **Field Rose** <note>original: "Feldroszlin"</note>; many in this age call it the **Spring-flower** <note>original: "ἐράνθεμον" (eranthemon).</note>. However, no one could easily affirm that this is the third kind of *Anthemis* <note>A genus including chamomiles and daisies.</note> which is specifically called Eranthemum. For, if one trusts **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the standard botanical text for 1,500 years.</note>, the Eranthemum has a flower shaped like Chamomile, golden or yellow in the middle, but with a purple circle around the edge. This plant, however, has a purple flower, but its center is not yellow; this very fact shows that it is not a species of *Anthemis*."

**Hieronymus Tragus** <note>Jerome Bock (1498–1554), one of the "German Fathers of Botany."</note> identifies the **Starwort** <note>original: "Astera Atticum"</note>—whose flower is yellow in the center and blue around the circumference—as the Spring-flower. **Fuchs** <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a physician and botanist famous for his beautifully illustrated herbal.</note>, however, identifies it as **Larkspur** <note>original: "Consolidam regalem"</note> or the Royal Flower, which we have discussed above.

In the Starwort, however, which **Virgil** <note>The Roman poet (70–19 BCE), who described plants in his agricultural poem, the *Georgics*.</note> calls the flower **Amellus**, the plant lacks a leaf like Chamomile, and the color of the flower is different. In the Royal Flower (Larkspur), both the color and the shape of the flower are lacking. The flowers of both of these are blue, whereas that of the Spring-flower is rose-red, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 CE), a Roman naturalist.</note> testifies in Book 26, Chapter 8. For this reason, neither of these can be the Spring-flower.

**Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an Italian doctor famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note> calls this present Spring-flower the **Flower of Adonis**, but the Flower of Adonis is believed to be the **Anemone**. Indeed, **Ovid** <note>The Roman poet (43 BCE – 17 CE); he describes the origin of the Adonis flower in his *Metamorphoses*.</note> writes that the flower of Adonis is [shaken off by the wind...] <note>The text cuts off mid-word "scribit" (writes) as a catchword for the next page.</note>

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the Spring-flower (Pheasant's Eye) and recounts the scholarly debates among Renaissance botanists over whether it is the same plant mentioned by ancient authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny.</summary>

<keywords>Spring-flower, Eranthemum, Adonis, botanical description, Dioscorides, Hieronymus Tragus, Leonhart Fuchs, Pliny the Elder, Matthiolus, floral identification</keywords>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>ON OX-EYE.</header>

...Ovid writes in the 10th book of his *Metamorphoses* <note>The Roman poet Ovid famously described the origin of the Anemone from the blood of the youth Adonis.</note> at the end. However, the flower of the winds is the **Anemone**. For *Anemone* takes its name "from the wind" <note>original Greek: "ἀπὸ τοῦ ἀνέμου" (apo tou anemou).</note>, as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of the monumental "Natural History."</note> testifies in book 21, chapter 23. *The flower*, he says, *never opens unless the wind is blowing; and from this it received its name.* It is therefore manifest that the **Spring-flower** <note>original: "Eranthemum"</note> which we have described does not match the Anemone.

FURTHERMORE, there is no medicinal use for this Spring-flower; however, as far as its taste indicates, it possesses some heat <note>This refers to the "humoral theory" of medicine, where plants were classified as hot, cold, moist, or dry to balance the body's humors.</note>, though not a great deal.

### ON OX-EYE. CHAPTER LXIX.

ON the plant to which we have given the name **Ox-eye** <note>original: "Buphthalmi"; likely referring to *Anthemis tinctoria* (Yellow Chamomile) or a similar species.</note>, three, four, or more thin stalks emerge from the roots, reaching a foot or more in height. Around these are leaves, finely divided and green, resembling the leaves of **Fennel**, but much smaller. The flowers at the top of the stalks are large, quite similar to **Marigolds** <note>original: "Calendulae"</note>, but of a more diluted yellow color, with yellow stamens in the middle. These are followed by a small seed-head, like that of the Spring-flower described above, composed of many seeds crowded together. The roots are thin and fibrous, similar to the roots of **Black Hellebore** <note>original: "veratri nigri"</note>. It grows wild in many provinces of **Germany** and **Bohemia** in uncultivated places; among the **Belgians**, it is a garden plant. It blooms in **May** or **June**.

REGARDING the naming of this plant, the opinions of more recent scholars vary. There are those who would have it be a species of **Black Hellebore**; some call it **Setterwort** <note>original: "Consiliginem"</note>; others **Sesamoides**; and another **False Hellebore** <note>original: "Elleborastrum"</note>.

<margin>Black Hellebore.</margin>
AS FOR Black Hellebore, two types are found among the ancient authors: one with a leaf like **Laurel** and fruit like **Sesame**; the other with a leaf like a **Plane-tree** and a seed like **Safflower** <note>original: "Cnici"</note>.

**Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher often called the "Father of Botany."</note> mentions the first type in his 9th book; and to this genus should be referred those plants which are everywhere called Black Hellebore...

<meta>catchword: ...re</meta>

### ON OX-EYE <note>Latin: *Buphthalmum*</note> ###

...whose leaves are gathered from several oblong parts: especially because it does not produce a central stalk, but instead brings forth flowers from the very roots, sitting on short stems, which are initially white and finally of a grassy <note>greenish</note> color. Indeed, its leaves consist of many parts, the individual segments of which are hard, smooth, and in some way similar in form to Bay leaves <note>Latin: *Laurinis*</note>. The seed is produced in oblong pods, five or six joined together in the manner of Sesame: the roots are numerous, like fibers, and are black, possessing an extraordinary purging power. There are, however, others of this genus, the remaining Black Hellebores <note>Latin: *Veratra nigra*; a plant historically used as a powerful but dangerous laxative</note>, which do produce stalks and have flowers of a grassy color; their individual leaves are also oblong and somewhat similar to the former plant, and the fruits are similar. The roots are effective for purging, black, and just like the other variety.

<margin>2</margin>
**DIOSCORIDES** *describes the* **LATTER** *Black Hellebore in Book 4; it has black leaves like those of the Plane tree; a stalk like a fern; flowers in an umbel <note>A flower cluster where several stalks spread from a common center, resembling an umbrella.</note>; whitish, oblong seeds; and thin, black, fibrous roots. However, it is very well known that the Ox-eye described by us in this place does not match either of those.*

<margin>Consiligo <note>Likely *Helleborus foetidus*, or Setterwort, used in traditional veterinary medicine.</note></margin>
**CONSILIGO**—*what the form of this herb is, is not found among the ancient writers. Pliny says that in his age it was discovered among the Marsi <note>An ancient Italic people famous for their herbal knowledge and snake-charming.</note>, and that it is a ready remedy for the lung disease of pigs and all cattle, even if it is only passed through a pierced ear: Book 26, Chapter 7. Columella, in Book 6, Chapter 5, also says that it grows most abundantly in the Marsian mountains and is extremely healthy for all cattle; he teaches by what reason and in what manner it ought to be inserted into the ear. It is also said that the roots of our Ox-eye cure certain diseases of cattle if they are inserted through a perforated ear: but for that reason, it should not follow that it is Consiligo. Indeed, it is not unusual for different plants of similar or the same faculty <note>medicinal property</note> to be found. For Pliny, in Book 25, Chapter 5, testifies that the roots of Black Hellebore can do the same. He says that the mucus* <meta>The catchword "tuitas" completes the word "pituitas" (mucus/phlegm) which begins the next page.</meta>

### ON OX-EYE <note>Latin: *Buphthalmum*</note> ###

...cures chronic lung conditions <note>The word "tuitas" likely completes "phtisicuitas" from the previous page, referring to consumption or respiratory ailments.</note> by passing a small twig through the ear and removing it at the same hour the next day. This is very well proven by the experiments of the farmers of our own age, who treat the diseases of their livestock with the roots of the <term>Common Black Hellebore <note>Latin: *Veratri nigri vulgaris*</note></term>. The roots of <term>White Hellebore <note>Latin: *Veratri albi*</note></term> provide a similar effect, as <term>Absyrtus <note>A 4th-century Greek veterinary surgeon to the Roman army.</note></term> and <term>Hierocles <note>Another Greek writer on veterinary medicine (mulomedicina).</note></term> after him write; however, they insert the small roots of <term>White Hellebore</term> not into the ear, but under the skin of the chest. In this same way, <term>Vegetius Renatus <note>Author of "Digestorum artis mulomedicinae," an influential late Roman veterinary manual.</note></term> uses <term>Consiligo <note>Historically identified as Setterwort (*Helleborus foetidus*), used in "setoning," a practice of inserting a thread or root through skin to drain infection.</note></term> in Book 1, Chapter 12 of his *Veterinary Medicine* concerning the treatment of diseases under the skin; although in Book 3, Chapter 2, regarding <term>Malleus <note>A contagious and often fatal disease of horses, known today as Glanders.</note></term>, he writes that they should even be inserted into the ears. These things sufficiently show that different plants occasionally have similar medicinal powers; for that reason, it by no means follows that our <term>Ox-eye</term> is <term>Consiligo</term> simply because it treats the diseases of cattle just as <term>Consiligo</term> does. But if a guess must be made based on these medicinal powers, <term>Consiligo</term> would actually be <term>White Hellebore</term>. For <term>Vegetius</term> uses <term>Consiligo</term> in exactly the same way that <term>Absyrtus</term> and <term>Hierocles</term> use <term>White Hellebore</term>. This suspicion is increased by the fact that <term>Vegetius</term> is thought to have received this method of treatment from the Greeks. For this reason, <term>Consiligo</term> seems to many to be nothing other than <term>White Hellebore</term>. If this is so, the present <term>Ox-eye</term> will be very different from <term>Consiligo</term>, since it is in no way similar to <term>White Hellebore</term>.

->**IT IS**<- likewise better known than it needs to be refuted that this plant is not <term>Sesamoides <note>Latin: *Sesampoides*; a plant so named for seeds resembling sesame.</note></term>, whether of the first or second kind.

->**HELLEBORE-LIKE**<- <note>Latin: *Helleborastrum*</note> is also an improper name for this plant: for that can properly be called "hellebore-like" which has the form and appearance of <term>Veratrum</term> (Hellebore); however, this <term>Ox-eye</term> has no appearance of <term>Veratrum</term>.

**FOR** all these reasons, it has seemed that none of these names suit this plant, but only the name of <term>Ox-eye <note>Latin: *Buphthalmum*</note></term>. Since this plant matches most perfectly with the description of it found in <term>Dioscorides <note>A first-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary pharmacological text of the era.</note></term>, it is the proper and legiti-

### ON OX-EYE ###

<note>Latin: *De Buphthalmo*. The name literally means "Ox-eye" due to the appearance of its flower.</note>

We consider this to be the legitimate Ox-eye. For the Ox-eye, as <term>Dioscorides <note>A first-century Greek physician and botanist, author of *De Materia Medica*.</note></term> says, sends out small stems that are soft and thin, and leaves that are <term>fennel-like <note>Greek: *marathroeidē*</note></term>—that is, bearing the appearance and likeness of fennel leaves. It has a yellow flower, larger than the <term>Chamomile <note>Latin: *Anthemis*</note></term>. This describes the present plant exactly, which, by the thinness and multiple divisions of its leaves, so exquisitely expresses that appearance of fennel leaves that no other small-leaved plant could do so better. Thus, beyond all doubt, this plant appears to be the legitimate Ox-eye.

It is **NAMED**, however, Ox-eye, and also *Cachla*, or rather *Caltha*. Now, the *Caltha* is the <term>Marigold <note>Latin: *Calendula*</note></term>, which we said most closely resembles our Ox-eye in its flower. There are indeed those who would have it that Ox-eye is the same as the <term>Chrysanthemum <note>Literal meaning: "Golden Flower."</note></term>, and that Dioscorides described the same herb in separate places under different names. But if these people had weighed Dioscorides' words a little more carefully, they would think otherwise. For although their descriptions agree in many respects, there is nonetheless some specific quality present that shows the plants to be different species. The leaves of the Chrysanthemum are described as <term>much-cleft <note>Greek: *polyschidē*</note></term>, that is, divided and cut into many parts; whereas those of the Ox-eye are <term>fennel-like</term>. Not everything that is jagged and cut into many parts has the appearance of fennel leaves. Furthermore, Dioscorides says the Chrysanthemum produces a flower that is <term>strongly shining <note>Greek: *ischyrōs silbonta*</note></term>, that is, very reflective; but he does not record the Ox-eye as being reflective. Nor indeed does the flower of the plant depicted by us shine so much that it can or should be called "very reflective." Do these things not show an evident difference between the Ox-eye and the Chrysanthemum, and confirm that the Ox-eye depicted by us is the legitimate one? Let those who wish think otherwise, but it seems so to us; and let those who want the Chrysanthemum to be the same as the Ox-eye look for another Ox-eye if they deny this is the one. For that which is both by others and by us [considered] as the Chrysanthemum...

<meta>The text cuts off here at the catchword "depin-", likely leading to "depingitur" (is depicted/illustrated) on the following page.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal investigation into "Ox-eye" (Buphthalmum). The previous page established the plant's identification based on its fennel-like leaves. Here, the author examines its practical uses in veterinary and human medicine, questioning its effectiveness compared to the more potent Black Hellebore.</meta>

### ON OX-EYE ###

*...is depicted, it cannot be the legitimate Ox-eye: for its leaves are not <term>fennel-like <note>Greek: *marathroeidē*</note></term>, as those of the legitimate Ox-eye must certainly be.*

**AS** for its medicinal properties: <term>Matthiolus <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note></term> says that in Bohemia, all physicians and pharmacists use the roots of this plant in place of <term>Black Hellebore <note>Latin: *Veratrum nigrum*. A powerful but toxic plant used historically as a purgative.</note></term>, specifically for the diseases of cattle; however, he does not assert that these roots are <term>substitutes <note>Greek: *antemballomenas*</note></term> for Hellebore roots in human medications. I remember observing once that some doctors added the roots of this plant in quite large quantities to <term>decoctions <note>Concentrated extracts made by boiling plant material in water.</note></term> prepared for purging the bowels, but they moved the bowels no more than if they had not been added at all. This raised a suspicion in me that the roots of this herb are endowed with no purging power, and that they cure the diseases of cattle when passed through the ears by an entirely different property than that of hellebore. The roots of <term>Gentian <note>A plant with very bitter roots still used today in tonics.</note></term> are excellent for dilating the openings of <term>fistulas <note>Narrow, tube-like sores or passages in the flesh.</note></term> that are too narrow; the roots of Birthwort and <term>Bryony <note>Latin: *Aristolochia* and *Bryonia* respectively.</note></term> can do the same, as can pieces of sponge. Yet these materials differ greatly from one another in their other medicinal powers. Therefore, although the roots of Ox-eye may share some mechanical similarity with Black Hellebore, they cannot for that reason do everything that Hellebore can. Thorns, prickles, wood shavings, and similar things cause pain, excite inflammation, and draw out the fluids of neighboring parts if they are driven into some area of the body. No part can be injured without pain: this pain is intensified by anything passed through or placed upon the wound. Perhaps even if anything else were placed into a pierced ear, the same effect would follow as when the root of this plant is inserted. We affirm nothing here; we are merely opening the way for the curious to explore the powers of this plant more diligently.

**DIOSCORIDES** says that the flowers of Ox-eye, ground with <term>wax salve <note>Latin: *cerato*, a topical ointment made of wax and oil.</note></term>, disperse swellings and hardened lumps; and it is said that if it is drunk immediately after leaving the baths, it provides a good complexion for some time...

<summary>This page critiques the medicinal efficacy of Ox-eye, particularly its use as a substitute for Black Hellebore in livestock, and suggests its healing effects in ears may be mechanical rather than chemical. It also cites Dioscorides on the use of its flowers for skin conditions and complexion.</summary>

[...to be useful] for those with <term>jaundice <note>Latin: *ictericis*; a condition causing yellowing of the skin and eyes.</note></term>. It seems, however, that these properties were transferred here from the chapter on the <term>Chrysanthemum <note>Literal translation: "Golden Flower."</note></term> by those who believed there was no difference between Ox-eye and Chrysanthemum. This is no different from how certain properties of the <term>Attic Starwort <note>Latin: *Aster Atticus*.</note></term> were added to the Black Violet in the writings of <term>Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), author of *Natural History*.</note></term>; or how some details of the <term>Smooth Bindweed <note>Latin: *Smilax levis*.</note></term> were added to the <term>Dolichos <note>A type of legume or bean.</note></term> in the copies of <term>Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century physician and the author of *De Materia Medica*.</note></term>, as we have noted in the appropriate places. However, what the specific powers of Ox-eye may be, we have not yet discovered.

## ON THE CHRYSANTHEMUM.
### CHAPTER LXX.

The stems of the **Chrysanthemum** are branched and smooth; the leaves are oblong, nearly the width of a finger, cut into many segments and finely divided. The flowers are like those of <term>Chamomile <note>Latin: *Anthemis*.</note></term> but larger; not only is the central 'button' yellow, but the circular petals are also yellow and shine with a certain golden luster; they have a scent that is not unpleasant. The roots are fibrous. It is found in fields, in gardens among the vegetables, and elsewhere in various places; in some locations the leaves are more withered, in others less so. It blooms in June, July, and sometimes in August.

By the Germans it is called <term>**Sant Johans blum** <note>English: "St. John’s Flower."</note></term> and <term>**Genßblum** <note>English: "Goose-flower."</note></term>. By our own people it is commonly called <term>**Gokelaer** <note>An old Dutch/Flemish name for the Corn Marigold (*Glebionis segetum*).</note></term>, and sometimes <term>**Geel ganse bloemen** <note>English: "Yellow Goose-flowers."</note></term>. The Greeks call it <term>Chrysanthemum <note>original Greek: χρυσάνθεμον; "Golden Flower."</note></term> because of the golden brilliance of its flowers. There is, however, also a certain species of Chamomile called Chrysanthemum, as we wrote above. <term>Crowfoot <note>Latin: *Batrachion*.</note></term> is also called Chrysanthemum, according to <term>Democritus <note>Refers to Bolus of Mendes (c. 200 BC), often called Pseudo-Democritus, whose agricultural writings appear in the *Geoponica*.</note></term> in the <term>Geoponica <note>A 10th-century collection of agricultural lore.</note></term>. Likewise, the <term>Everlasting Flower <note>Latin: *Elichrysum*.</note></term> is a Chrysanthemum. But these other Chrysanthemums differ from the one present here. Indeed, many also call this Chrysanthemum by the names **Ox-eye** and **Caltha**; but these names properly belong to that Ox-eye which was described in the preceding chapter. There are also those who

# CHRYSANTHEMUM

<page-num>244</page-num>

->**CHRYSANTHEMUM**<-

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut of a Chrysanthemum plant. The illustration shows a horizontal, fibrous root system at the base, from which several upright stems grow. These stems are adorned with many deeply lobed and serrated leaves, typical of the species. At the top of the stems are three large, fully opened composite flowers with prominent central disks and radiating petals, alongside several smaller flower buds in various stages of growth.</image-desc>

## ON THE CHRYSANTHEMUM
### CHAPTER LXX

The stems of the **Chrysanthemum** <note>original: *Chrysanthemum*; derived from the Greek words *chrysos* (gold) and *anthemon* (flower).</note> are branched and smooth; the leaves are oblong, measuring nearly the width of a finger, and are cut into many segments and finely divided. The flowers are similar to those of **Chamomile** <note>Latin: *Anthemis*; a common flowering herb often used in teas.</note> but are larger in size. In these flowers, not only is the central "button" yellow, but the surrounding petals are also yellow, gleaming with a distinct golden luster; they possess a scent that is not unpleasant. The roots are fibrous. This plant is commonly found growing in open fields, in gardens among the vegetables, and in various other locations.

<summary>This page provides a botanical description of the Chrysanthemum, detailing its physical characteristics such as its branched stems, segmented leaves, and golden-yellow flowers, while distinguishing it from similar plants like Chamomile.</summary>

<keywords>Chrysanthemum, botany, golden flower, Chamomile, herbal medicine, plant anatomy, Renaissance botany</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the chapter on the Chrysanthemum, specifically addressing its medicinal uses and its distinction from the Daisy, before beginning Chapter LXXI, which provides a detailed botanical study of the Daisy (Bellis).</meta>

<page-num>245</page-num>

->DE BELLIDE<-
<note>original: *De Bellide*; On the Daisy.</note>

...[they] may make it a species of Daisy, and call it the **yellow Daisy**. However, the **Daisy** is a plant of a different kind.

The **stems of the Chrysanthemum**, as Dioscorides records, are eaten in the manner of vegetables. The flowers, when ground with a wax ointment, are said to disperse fatty tumors <note>original: *steutomata*; sebaceous cysts or growths.</note>. The herb itself, when drunk in the baths after leaving the tub, quickly restores a healthy color to those afflicted by the **Royal Disease** <note>original: *morbus Regius*; a historical term for jaundice, characterized by yellowing of the skin.</note>.

### ON THE DAISY. CHAPTER LXXI.

<margin>1</margin> The **Daisy** produces many leaves from its roots; these are small, smooth, fleshy, oblong-round, and very lightly notched, with most of them spread flat along the ground. From among these, single small flowers emerge, each sitting on its own thin stalk. They are almost the same shape as **Chamomile** flowers but smaller; their center is yellow, which many tiny white petals surround in a circle. These petals are somewhat reddish, appearing almost purple amidst the white. The roots underneath are fibrous. It grows in meadows and similar marshy or damp places in many locations. It blooms in the spring months.

<margin>2</margin> There is another species similar to this, but found only in gardens. It has multiple, most beautiful flowers with such a dense weave of tiny petals that the yellow circle is hardly, if at all, visible. It occupies the entire circumference and center of the flower. The colors are sometimes red, sometimes white, and sometimes a mixture of both; rarely are they herbaceous or green. They have no scent. Sometimes—though not frequently—other small flowers, five or six or even more, surround these, arising from under or out of the flower cups <note>original: *calycibus*.</note> themselves; these are similar to their "mother" flowers but smaller. This Daisy also blooms in the months of April and May.

The Germans call it *Maszlieben* <note>modern German: *Maßliebchen*.</note> and *Massuselen*. The Belgians <note>referring to the Dutch-speaking Low Countries.</note> call it *Madelieuen* <note>modern Dutch: *Madeliefje*.</note>, *Kerstouwen*, and *Margrieten* <note>meaning "pearls" or "Marguerites".</note>.

<meta>catchword: French,</meta> <note>original catchword: *Galli*; indicating the next page begins with the French names for the plant.</note>

# GARDEN DAISY.

<image-desc>A detailed botanical woodcut of a daisy plant (Bellis hortensis). The illustration shows a thick, fibrous root system extending downward. Above the roots is a dense rosette of spatulate leaves. Several long, leafless stalks emerge from the center, topped with composite flower heads in full bloom and some in bud.</image-desc>

<margin>A corrupt passage in Pliny.</margin>
The French call these **Pasquettes** and **Marguerites**. Pliny mentions the **Daisy** <note>original: *Bellis*</note> in Book 26, Chapter 5. The Daisy, he says, grows in meadows, with a white flower that is somewhat reddish. There is also a passage in Pliny’s Book 21, Chapter 8, concerning the **Bellium**, which we do not cite here because the text is corrupt <note>In botanical philology, a "corrupt" passage refers to a section of an ancient manuscript that has been so poorly copied over the centuries that its original meaning is debated or illegible.</note>, and learned men have so far labored in vain to restore its original meaning. More recent authors also call the Daisy the **Lesser Comfrey** <term>Consolida minor; a name historically given to plants believed to "consolidate" or heal wounds</term>; most call it the **Pearl-herb** <note>original: *margarita*; likely referring to the white, pearl-like appearance of the buds</note>; others call it the **Primrose**... 
<meta>The text cuts off at the catchword "mulam," which completes the word "Primulam."</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the various names for the daisy in French and Latin, citing classical references from Pliny the Elder and noting the plant's medicinal association with wound healing.</summary>

<keywords>Daisy, Bellis hortensis, Pliny the Elder, Consolida minor, Margarita, botanical history, French plant names</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of naming conventions for the Daisy (Bellis) and transitions into its medicinal applications, before beginning a new chapter on the Golden Everlasting (Elichryson).</meta>

<page-num>247</page-num>
<header>ON THE DAISY.</header>
<sig>Q 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: disk</meta>

...rose of spring. There are, however, others also called **Primroses of spring**, which are species of Mullein <note>original: *Phlomis*</note>, about which I will speak elsewhere.

<margin>3</margin>
Some also refer to the Daisy genus a certain wild variety called the **Greater Daisy** <note>likely the Ox-eye Daisy, *Leucanthemum vulgare*</note>. This plant produces thin stalks a cubit <term>an ancient measurement of roughly 18 inches, or the length of a forearm</term> high or longer. The leaves around the stalks are oblong, somewhat serrated, and nearly a finger's width. The flowers at the top of the small stalks are white around the circumference and yellow in the center—similar to Ox-eye Chamomile and White Mayweed—and have no scent. The roots are fibrous. It grows in meadows and along the edges of fields in shady spots.

As we have said, they call it the **Greater Daisy**. The Germans, according to Leonhart Fuchs <note>A 16th-century German physician and one of the "fathers of botany"</note>, call it the **Goose-flower** <note>original: *Genßblum*</note>; some also call it **Middle Comfrey** <term>Consolida media</term>. However, there are other plants known as Middle Comfrey, such as those called **Bugle** and **Self-heal**, both of which are considered types of Middle Comfrey.

MOREOVER, the leaves of the **Lesser Daisy** are endowed with the power of moistening and cooling. Indeed, they moisten in the "second degree" of intensity and cool in the "second degree" of mildness <note>Galenic medicine categorized plants by four "degrees" of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness to balance the body's humors</note>. They alleviate all pains, but especially those of the joints and gout resulting from hot and dry humors. They are most effective when ground with fresh, unsalted butter and applied to the aching areas, particularly if Mallow leaves are added. These same leaves, when added to pottage <note>original: *oleribus*; leafy vegetables or pot-herbs</note>, loosen the bowels; in burning fevers and inflammations of the intestines, they are mixed into enemas with great benefit.

## ON THE GOLDEN EVERLASTING. CHAPTER LXXII.

The **Golden Everlasting** <note>original: *Elichryson*; likely *Helichrysum*</note> produces slender, green, woody stalks, occasionally divided into wing-like segments. The leaves are similar to those of Southernwood <note>original: *Abrotanum*</note> and are somewhat whitish. The flowers cluster in "umbels" <term>a flat-topped flower cluster where the individual stalks spring from a single point</term> at the top of the stalks, shining with the yellow color of gold, in the shape of Tansy flowers or small central disks, or...

<page-num>248</page-num>

# GOLDEN EVERLASTING <note>original: *Elichryson*; likely the species *Helichrysum stoechas* or a related Everlasting flower</note>

->[Illustration: A detailed botanical woodcut of the Golden Everlasting plant. The image displays the entire plant from its gnarled, fibrous root system at the base up to the flowering tops. Several upright stems branch out, densely covered with small, narrow leaves. Each branch terminates in tight, rounded clusters of small flower heads, which in life would appear golden and papery.]<-

<note>The name *Elichryson* is derived from the Greek words *helios* (sun) and *chrysos* (gold), referring to the brilliant, shimmering yellow of the flower heads that maintain their color even after being dried.</note>

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical illustration of the Golden Everlasting (Elichryson), focusing on its physical structure from its gnarled roots to its characteristic clustered blossoms.</summary>

<keywords>Golden Everlasting, Elichryson, Helichrysum, botanical illustration, herbals, Renaissance medicine, medicinal plants</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Golden Everlasting (Elichryson) from page 248, detailing its historical uses, names in various languages, and medicinal properties, before transitioning to a related species, Yellow Stoechas.</meta>

<page-num>249</page-num>
<header>ON THE GOLDEN EVERLASTING.</header>
<sig>Q 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: color</meta>

The centers of the flowers resemble those of Chamomile <note>original: *Anthemidis*</note>; if they are gathered before they begin to wilt, they preserve their appearance and beauty for a very long time. For this reason, Pliny records that the statues of the gods were often crowned with them, and that Ptolemy, the King of Egypt, observed this custom most diligently. It grows in many uncultivated places in Italy and Spain, in meadows with thin soil, and around riverbeds. In the gardens of Belgium, it blooms in August or September; however, Theophrastus and Pliny number the Golden Everlasting among the flowers of spring.

**DIOSCORIDES** calls it *helichryson* <note>original Greek: ἑλίχρυσον, meaning "sun-gold"</note>, while some call it *chrysanthemon* <note>original Greek: χρυσάνθεμον, meaning "gold-flower"</note>, and others—including Galen—call it *amaranton* <note>original Greek: ἀμάραντον, meaning "unfading"</note> because of the flower’s long life. Yet there is another *Amarantus* that is purple, which was described above. Pliny names this plant *Heliochryson*; Theophrastus similarly calls it *heleiochryson* <note>original Greek: ἑλειόχρυσον, meaning "marsh-gold"</note>, while Gaza translates it as *Aurelia* <note>Latin: "the golden one"</note>.

**ITS PROPERTIES**, Galen says, are <term>cutting and thinning</term> <note>Galenic medical terms referring to the ability to break down and dilute thick or stagnant bodily fluids (humors)</note>. According to Dioscorides, the flower head, when drunk in wine, is beneficial against difficulties in urination, snake bites, pains in the hips <note>sciatica</note>, and for ruptures. It also provokes menstruation. When taken with honeyed wine <note>original: *mulso*</note>, it dissolves blood clots in both the belly and the bladder. It stops "fluxes from the head" <note>likely referring to catarrh or the common cold</note> when three <term>obols</term> <note>an ancient Greek unit of weight; three obols equal about half a dram</note> by weight are given in diluted white wine to a patient who has fasted. If placed among clothes, it keeps them preserved and uncorrupted <note>by acting as a moth repellent</note>. Theophrastus records that the Golden Everlasting, when roasted and mixed with honey, is also effective for treating burns.

### ON YELLOW STOECHAS, OR
### GOLDY-LOCKS. CHAPTER LXXIII.

The golden-colored head is similar to the Golden Everlasting; it is called **Yellow Stoechas** <note>original: *Stœchas citrina*; likely *Helichrysum stoechas*</note> by more recent authors. It produces thin stalks about nine inches long <note>original: *dodrantales*</note>. The leaves are oblong and narrow; both the stalks and leaves are hoary and woolly. The flowers at the top of the stalks grow in a loose and disordered cluster, like an umbrella; they are fragrant and resemble tiny bubbles.

# YELLOW STOECHAS <note>original Latin: *Stoechas Citrina*; this plant is often identified today as *Helichrysum stoechas*, known for its straw-like, everlasting yellow flowers.</note>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of Yellow Stoechas from the text preceding the illustration on page 250. The author is describing the physical characteristics, habitat, and various regional names of the plant.</meta>

### ON YELLOW STOECHAS

shining with a yellow color, which, if collected before they have fully ripened, retain their color and beauty for a long time without withering. The roots are slender and black. It grows in rough and sandy places and in valleys; it is common near the Rhine, especially between Speyer <note>original: *Spiram*</note> and Worms <note>original: *Vormaciam*</note>. It blooms in the month of June and after the Summer Solstice.

Because it grows in the regions near the Rhine, the **GERMANS** call it **Rhine-flowers** <note>original: *Rheinblumen*</note>; likewise, they call it **Youth-flower**, **Moth-flower**, or **Moth-herb** <note>original: *Jungling/Motten blumen/Motten kraut*; so named because the dried flowers were used to repel moths from clothing</note>. The **Belgians** call it **Rhine-flowers** or **Moth-herb** <note>original: *Rhÿn bloemen / Motten cruyt*</note>. In **Latin**, it is called *Tinearia* <note>from *tinea*, meaning moth</note>. In the **apothecary shops**, it is known as *Stoechas* or *Stichas citrina*. The botanists Leonhart Fuchs and Hieronymus Bock <note>referred to here by his Latinized name *Tragus*</note> identify it as *Eliochrysum* <note>Gold-everlasting</note>. However, it is clear from what was written in the previous chapter that this plant matches that description not at all, or only very little.

There are also those who believe this plant is *Ageraton* <note>Greek for "not-aging," referring to the everlasting nature of the flowers</note>, because the flower is spherical like a bubble and does not easily or quickly wither. Indeed, the flower of the *ageraton* is "bubble-like" <note>original Greek: ἀγηράτου... πομφολυγῶδες</note>, that is, it is rounded like a bubble and is preserved in its own colorful appearance for a very long time. However, the leaf of the *Ageraton* is similar to the leaf of Marjoram <note>original: *Origanum*</note>, whereas the leaf of Yellow Stoechas is not like that at all, being, as we have said, oblong and narrow. Therefore, it is evident that this is not the *Ageraton* described by the ancient physician Dioscorides.

To us, Yellow Stoechas seems to have the most similarity with *Chrysocome* <note>Greek for "golden hair"</note>, which is also called *Chrysitis*. For it is a short herb with a head of golden color, which is divided and resembles clusters <note>term: <term>corymbs</term>A botanical term for a flower cluster where the individual stalks grow to different lengths to form a flat or slightly curved surface.</note>; the roots are thin and black. Dioscorides left a written record that *Chrysocome* was exactly like this. He says: "Chrysocome <note>original Greek: χρυσόκομη</note> is a small rod about nine inches long <note>original: *dodrantalis*; a span</note>, having a cluster-like head similar to Hyssop; a hairy, thin root like black hellebore, not unpleasant to the taste, and similar to Cyperus <note>Galingale</note>."

<summary>This page discusses the naming and identification of Yellow Stoechas, comparing it to the "everlasting" plants described by ancient authorities like Dioscorides and debating its classification among Renaissance botanists.</summary>

<keywords>Yellow Stoechas, Rhine-flowers, Moth-herb, Chrysocome, Ageraton, Dioscorides, Leonhart Fuchs, Hieronymus Bock, botanical nomenclature, Speyer, Worms</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of Yellow Stoechas from page 251, moving from its common names and identification to its physical differences from other herbs and its medicinal applications. It then begins a new chapter on Santolina.</meta>

<header>ON YELLOW STOECHAS.</header>

In this description, it should be observed that the true and legitimate *hyssop* <term>hyssop: a fragrant herb used in ancient rituals; here the author distinguishes between the classical "true" plant and the common garden variety</term> of the ancients produces a certain likeness to clusters [corymbs] at the tips of the small stems; and for that reason, the hyssop in common use is by no means the genuine kind: specifically because it does not bear its flowers in clusters or the appearance of clusters, but produces them along the stems next to each individual leaf, in the manner of *Savory* <term>Thymbria: likely Satureja or Summer Savory</term>, of which it is undoubtedly a species, and indeed a larger one.

<margin>2</margin>Regarding Yellow Stoechas <note>original: *Stoechadi citrinæ*</note>, it happened that I observed another similar herb with many thin branches from the root and narrow leaves, becoming somewhat white, but much less so than Yellow Stoechas: it has yellow flowers in an umbel <term>umbel: a flower cluster where stalks spread from a common center like umbrella ribs</term>, yet they are not spherical, but like the central disk of *Chamomile* <note>original: *Chamæmeli*</note> flowers, though smaller. Where it grows wild we have not discovered, but it has been seen by us in the gardens of Belgium. It is *Anonymous* and without a name. Some consider it a narrow-leafed species of *Everlasting* <note>original: *Eliochrysi*; refers to the genus Helichrysum</note>.

FURTHERMORE, the flowers of Yellow Stoechas boiled in wine are said to expel intestinal worms <note>original: *ventris lumbricos*</note> when drunk; and when used with lye <note>original: *lixiuio*; an alkaline solution used for washing</note>, they destroy lice and nits.

Regarding GOLDY-LOCKS <note>original: *Chrysocome*</note>, Dioscorides <note>a famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> says the root is warming and astringent, and is suitable for those with liver complaints or pneumonia; it is also taken boiled with honey-water <note>original: *hydromelite*</note> for cleansing the uterus.

# SANTOLINA <note>original: Santolina; commonly known as Lavender Cotton</note>

This woodcut displays the plant's entire structure in fine detail. It begins with a **fibrous** <term>fibrous: a root system composed of many thin, hair-like roots of similar size</term> and somewhat gnarled root system at the bottom, which leads into a woody, branching base. # ON SANTOLINA <note>original: DE SANTOLINA; commonly known today as Lavender Cotton</note>

...abundant with small, oblong, narrow, and toothed [leaves]: from the top of the twigs, single little disks emerge, packed with short stamens, similar to the flowers of **Tansy** <note>original: *Tanacetum*</note> and the central disks of **Chamomile** <note>original: *Chamaemelum*</note> flowers, but yet somewhat broader, yellow in color, which in the "female" plant are changed into a darker color: the root is woody. The shrub itself is whitish in its stalks and leaves, and is sweetly scented with a certain heavy fragrance.

2  **TO THIS** a second species is similar, yet with longer, greener, and less white leaves; the flower is paler.

3  **A THIRD** with very short, very thin leaves, closest to **Heath** <note>original: *Erica*</note>: with yellow flowers, and similar to the others.

4  **A FOURTH** has leaves that are less toothed and more similar to **Cypress** <note>original: *Cupressus*</note>.

5  **FOR THE FIFTH**, the little stalks do not rise, but creep along the ground: the leaves are thicker and more woolly, becoming more white: the flowers are golden in clusters <term>corymbis: a flower cluster where the individual flower stalks grow to different lengths to form a flat-topped head</term>, similar to the other **Santolinas**.

**IN** Belgium, all these species are foreign, and are found nowhere but in gardens. The first kind is the most common; the rest are rarer. They bloom in July and August. These are all called by the single name **Santolina**: the Belgians commonly name them **Cypress** <note>original: *Cypres*</note>. Many identify them as **Ground Cypress** <note>original: *Chamaecyparissum*</note>, but **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> is so brief and succinct regarding Ground Cypress that, in the opinion of these men, it can neither be rejected nor proved from his writing. The apothecary shops <note>original: *Officinæ*</note> call the seed **wormseed** <note>original: *semen contra vermes*</note>, and **holy seed** <note>original: *semen sanctum*</note>. Some, being ignorant of which plant it is collected from, think it is the seed of **Santonicum** <note>a type of wormwood from the region of the Santones in Gaul</note>; others think it is **Seriphium** or **Sea Wormwood** <note>original: *Absinthium marinum*</note>. Both, however, wander from the truth, for it belongs to no other than the **Santolina**. Those also hallucinate <note>the author uses *hallucinantur* to suggest they are greatly mistaken</note> not a little who wish **Santolina** to be **Female Southernwood** <note>original: *Abrotanum femina*</note>. For indeed, Female Southernwood has leaves like the male—namely, **Seriphium**—but it is taller and grows like a shrub into a tree-like form. **Santolina** is lower and is not **Seriphium**,

<summary>This page describes five varieties of Santolina, distinguishing them by their leaf shape and growth habits, while correcting common naming errors made by apothecaries and other herbalists.</summary>
<keywords>Santolina, Lavender Cotton, species varieties, botanical classification, wormseed, Pliny the Elder, Ground Cypress, Southernwood, Sea Wormwood</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of Santolina (Lavender Cotton) and its variants, specifically comparing it to Southernwood and Helichrysum, before transitioning to a new chapter on Marjoram. The text references classical authorities like Pliny the Elder and Dioscorides to resolve naming disputes.</meta>

[Sea-wor]mwood <note>original: *Seriphium*</note>, but it more closely resembles **Heath** <note>original: *Erica*</note> in its leaf. **Helichrysum** <note>original: *Eliochryson*; likely the Strawflower</note> has more similarity with **Female Southernwood** <note>original: *Abrotanum femina*</note>, for it reflects the leaf of **Southernwood** and is close to it in scent; however, it is not Southernwood, but a herb of its own kind.

**The** seed of **Santolina** is bitter in taste, and in its properties it is hot and dry in the third degree <note>referring to the Galenic system of humoral medicine, where "degrees" measured the intensity of a plant's effect on the body</note>: when consumed, it kills intestinal worms and is effective against them.

**PLINY** says that the herb **Ground-Cypress** <note>original: *Chamaecyparissum*</note>, when drunk in wine, is powerful against the poisons of all serpents and scorpions.

## ON MARJORAM, OR MARUM.
### CHAPTER 75.

**After** the flowers, several "garland herbs" <note>original: *coronariae*; plants traditionally used to weave wreaths or festive crowns</note> must be described: among which is **Marjoram**, which is a low and shrubby plant, whitish and of a very sweet scent, standing a span and a half or a foot high; with thin little stalks or forks, divided into many branches; around which are small leaves that are almost round, soft, and grayish-white; and at the top are very tiny white flowers, emerging from spikes similar to **Heracleotic Oregano**, namely small, oblong, and packed together like scales: with thin roots. This is found sometimes larger, and then thinner and smaller, and more excellent in scent. Both are garden plants. Both also bloom in summer.

**THE ITALIANS** call it *Persa*, and the smaller variety *Persa gentile*; the **Germans**, *Mayoran* and *Meyran*; the **Belgians**, *Marioleyne*; the **French**, *Mariolaine* and *Marone*; the **Apothecaries** <note>original: *Officinae*</note> and people everywhere call it **Marjoram** <note>original: *Maiorana*</note>. It is believed by many to be the **Sweet Marjoram** <note>original: *Sampsychum*</note> of the ancients. But **Sweet Marjoram** <note>original: *Sampsychon*</note> is a herb, according to the authority of **Dioscorides**, that creeps along the ground; **Marjoram**, however, does not creep, but [rises] straight and high...

<keywords>Santolina, Marjoram, Helichrysum, Pliny the Elder, Dioscorides, botanical classification, medicinal herbs, intestinal worms, garland herbs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of Marjoram (Maiorana) from the previous chapter, concluding the physical description and transitioning into a discussion on the plant's classification and nomenclature compared to classical sources.</meta>

# MARJORAM, OR MARUM.

[The plant] rises up; which clearly suggests that it is not *Sampsychum* <note>original: *Sampsychum*; a Greek name used by Dioscorides for marjoram, though often debated by Renaissance botanists</note>, or at least not that plant alone.

**WE HAVE WRITTEN** <note>original: *Scripsimus*; the author refers to his previous botanical works</note> elsewhere that **Marjoram** is the legitimate **Marum** <note>original: *Marum*; a fragrant herb mentioned by ancient authors like Pliny, often identified today as Cat-Thyme or a specific variety of Marjoram</note>...

The plant is covered in small, ovate leaves arranged in pairs. At the tips of many branches are dense, cone-like flower clusters or bracts. The plant is shown with its full root system, consisting of several thick, branching roots.</image-desc>

<summary>This page concludes the physical description of the marjoram plant and begins an academic argument identifying modern marjoram with the "Marum" described by ancient authorities.</summary>

<keywords>Marjoram, Maiorana, Marum, Sampsychum, botanical identification, Renaissance herbals</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of Marjoram (Maiorana) from page 256, where the author was arguing for its identification with the plants described by ancient Greek and Roman authorities.</meta>

# ON MARJORAM, OR MARUM.

...to be true; our opinion is confirmed by the name of **Marjoram**, which seems to be derived from **Marum** <note>original: *Marum*; a fragrant herb mentioned by ancient authors, often identified today as Cat-Thyme or a specific variety of Marjoram</note>; but it is confirmed even more clearly by the description of Marum itself, with which it agrees exactly. Indeed, it is a shrubby herb, with a flower (as we have said) like that of **Oregano** <note>original: *Origani*</note>, but with much whiter leaves and a more fragrant flower. Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist whose *De Materia Medica* was the primary authority on herbs for 1,500 years</note> left written that Marum is of this kind. *Marum,* he says, *or isobryon, is a well-known herb, shrub-like, similar in its flower to oregano; however, its leaves are much whiter, and the flower is more fragrant.* <note>original Greek: Μάρον , ἢ ἰσόβριον , πόα γνώριμος , φρυγανώδης , ὁμοία τῷ ἄνθει ὀριγάνῳ , τὰ μέντοι φύλλα τούτου λευκότερα πολλῷ , καὶ τὸ ἄνθος εὐωδέστερον</note> That is: Marum, or **Isobrion** <note>an ancient alternative name for Marum</note>, is a well-known herb, twiggy, similar to Oregano in its flower; however, its leaves are much whiter, and the flower is more fragrant. And it should not be considered strange that Marjoram was substituted for **Sampsychum** <note>another name for Marjoram used by the ancients</note>, since even in the time of Galen <note>Aelius Galenus, a famous Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note>, Marum was put in the place of Sampsychum in **Amaracinum ointment** <note>a famous scented salve made from Marjoram</note>, as he himself testifies in book 1 of *On Antidotes*, where he says: *Marum is far more fragrant than Sampsychum; and for that reason, omitting the latter, Marum was added to the medicinal compositions.* This provided an opportunity for those who came after to treat Marum (or Marjoram) in place of Sampsychum, and to call it by that name.

**Marjoram** heats and dries, and in both qualities it is of the **second degree** <note>In Galenic medicine, herbs were classified by four "degrees" of intensity for the four qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry. A second-degree herb has a noticeable effect but is not as intense as those of the third or fourth degrees.</note>. It helps with "cold" diseases of the brain and head in whatever way it is taken. Placed in the nostrils, it causes sneezing and draws out **phlegm** <note>original: *pituitam*; one of the four bodily humors, thought to cause congestion if in excess</note>. It soothes toothaches when chewed or applied to the area. Taken in a drink, it provokes urine and watery humors, and encourages the **menses** <note>the monthly period</note>. It is also mixed into antidotes.

## ON SAMPSYCHUM, OR AMARACUS, ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES, THEOPHRASTO, AND OTHERS. CHAPTER LXXVI.

# ON MARJORAM.

*...having hairy and round leaves, similar to small-leafed Calamintha <note>original: *Calaminthae tenuifoliae*; a type of aromatic mint</note>: it is very fragrant and warm, and is woven into crowns. The varieties from Cyzicus and Cyprus are the most highly praised. The Egyptian variety holds second place. It is called **Amarakon** or **Amaracum** by the people of Cyzicus and Sicily.*

**THEOPHRASTUS** *lists Marjoram <note>original: *Amaracum*</note> in his catalog of **sub-shrubs** <note>original: *phryganikon* (φρυγανικῶν); low, woody plants</note> that are cultivated and used for garlands in Book 6. He says it is superior because of the abundance of its seed, which is **fragrant** <note>original: *euosmon* (εὐοσμον)</note>, that is, of a good and sweet scent. It grows from seed and from cuttings; it is a summer plant and is mixed into perfumes.*

**PLINY**, *according to Book 21, Chapter 11, states that the physician Diocles and the Sicilian people called it **Amaracum**, while Egypt and Syria called it **Sampsychum**. It is planted in both ways, by seed and by branch; the seed of Amaracum is as plentiful as that of Southernwood <note>original: *Abrotano*; a flowering plant in the wormwood family</note>.*

**VIRGIL**, *in Book 1 of the Aeneid, shows that the Marjoram is a flower-bearing shrub, writing thus:*

->*— Where soft Marjoram embraces him*<-
->*With flowers and a sweet-breathing shade.*<-

*The same is testified by **Catullus** in the Wedding Song of Julia and Manlius:*

->*Bind your temples with the flowers*<-
->*Of sweet-smelling Marjoram.*<-

**GALEN** *distinguishes between **Amaracum** and **Sampsychum** as different plants; he treats both in separate sections and attributes specific powers to each. However, Galen’s Amaracum is actually **Feverfew** <note>original: *Parthenium*</note>; indeed, as Dioscorides testifies, some call Feverfew "Amaracum"; and in his books "On the Power of Simple Medicines," Galen nowhere mentions Feverfew.*

**FURTHERMORE**, *Marjoram <note>original: *Sampsuchum*</note>, as Galen says, is made of fine parts and has a resolving <note>original: *digerentis*; a faculty to disperse or "digest" morbid humors</note> faculty; it dries and warms in the third degree. Dioscorides records that a decoction of it, when drunk, is beneficial for those with **dropsy** <note>original: *hydropicis*; a historical term for swelling or edema</note> at the start of the disease, and for those suffering from difficulty in urination and griping pains <note>original: *torminibus*; intestinal cramps</note>. Indeed, the dried leaves [applied] with honey...*

## ON COMMON MARUM, OR CLINOPODIUM. CHAPTER LXXVII.

**What is now called Marum** by most people is a small shrub with a very sweet scent, standing about a foot and a half or eighteen inches high: it has slender, branchy, woody stalks, and small leaves around them, similar to the leaves of tougher Thyme or common Wild Thyme <note>original: *Serpylli*</note>, but whitish, brighter, and somewhat larger. At the top, it has woolly little heads and surrounding whorls <note>original: *verticillos*; circular arrangements of leaves or flowers around a stem</note> from which emerge very tiny white flowers: the roots are thin and woody. In Spain, it grows everywhere alongside French Lavender <note>original: *Stoechade*</note> in dry and deserted places; among the Belgians and elsewhere, it is planted in gardens with no small amount of effort and care. In summer, the whorls and woolly heads burst forth with flowers.

**Many in this age call this plant Marum;** however, it is by no means Marum, but rather the *Clinopodium* of Dioscorides <note>Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician and the father of pharmacology</note>. Dioscorides records that *Clinopodium* is a <term>shrub</term> <note>original: *thamnon* (θάμνον)</note>, that is, a small bush, <term>stick-like</term> <note>original: *phryganōdē* (φρυγανώδην)</note> or shrubby, eighteen inches high, growing among rocks, and having leaves similar to Wild Thyme; the flowers, however, look like the feet of a bed <note>the name *Clinopodium* literally means "bed-foot" in Greek</note>, spaced at intervals like those of Horehound <note>original: *Marrubij*</note>. This fragrant plant agrees well enough with that description. Indeed, it is shrubby and rises to a height of eighteen inches; its flowers are arranged in whorls such that some surround the stem like Horehound, while others stand out at the top like the rounded head of a bed-foot: the leaves are similar in color to the leaves of true Wild Thyme, namely whitish; such as...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Clinopodium (Common Marum) from the previous page, focusing on its botanical comparisons and medicinal virtues before beginning a new chapter on Rosemary.</meta>

->ON COMMON MARUM<-
<sig>R 3</sig>

Dioscorides shows that its leaves are like those of **Wild Thyme** <note>original: *Serpylli*</note>, though he says they are brighter than **Oregano** <note>original: *Origani*</note>: Theophrastus testifies that these same leaves are thin, just like **Wild Thyme**. All these features show a very great similarity between this plant and **Clinopodium** <note>literally "bed-foot," likely named for the flower whorls resembling the casters of a bed frame</note>. It is indeed called *klinopódion* in Greek, and by some also *Cleonicum*, *Ocymoides* <note>meaning "basil-like"</note>, *Zopyron*, and in Latin *lectipes* <note>meaning "couch-foot"</note>.

According to Galen, **Clinopodium** possesses a **POWER** of heating, with a subtle essence; it is ranked in the third degree of both heating and drying agents <note>In Galenic medicine, plants were classified in four "degrees" of intensity; the third degree indicates a potent medicinal effect</note>. Dioscorides writes that the herb and its decoction are drunk to treat the bites of venomous beasts, convulsions, ruptures, and the dripping of urine; it also induces **menstruation**. If drunk for several days, it is said to expel the fetus and remove warts <note>original: *acrochordonas*; a type of hanging wart</note>. The same decoction, when drunk, stays the bowels <note>treats diarrhea</note> on the third day; it should be taken in wine by those without fever, and in water by those with fever.

## ON CROWN ROSEMARY.
### CAP. LXXVIII.

**Crown Rosemary** <note>original: *Rosmarinus coronarius*; "coronarius" refers to its use in making garlands or crowns</note> is a woody shrub with a trunk that is sometimes three or four feet high. It has many slender branches and crowded leaves that are oblong, narrow, and somewhat hard—whitish on the underside but a deep, saturated green on top, possessing a scent that is heavy but not unpleasant. The flowers are small, ranging from blue to whitish, and emerge from the base of the leaves. The seed is tiny and black; the roots are thin, fibrous, and do not descend deeply into the earth.

**It grows in France, Spain,** and other provinces in wild and uncultivated places. In **Narbonese Gaul** <note>the Roman province in Southern France</note> it is so abundant that the inhabitants commonly burn it as firewood. In **Italy**, it is used to hedge garden plots; it also adorns the gardens of the **Belgians**. It blooms at the very beginning of **Spring**.

The Greeks call it *Libanotida stephanomatikēn* <note>original: Λιβανωτίδα στεφανωματικὴν; meaning "garland-making frankincense-herb" due to its scent</note>, and the Latins call it *Rosmarinus coronarius*. It is called "Crown" [coronarius]...
