<header>ON COMMON MARUM.</header>

[...is named], to distinguish it from other umbrella-bearing Frankincense-herbs <note>original: *Libanotidum*; plants named for their incense-like scent. "Umbrella-bearing" refers to the umbel shape of the flower clusters</note>. Indeed, there are several species of Frankincense-herbs; some are umbrella-bearing, while one is not umbrella-bearing and differs in form from the others—the "crown" variety, which we are discussing now. The Germans call this **Rosemary**, and the French call it the same.

**Crown Rosemary** <note>original: *Rosmarinus coronarius*; "coronarius" refers to its use in making garlands or crowns</note> is **HOT** and dry in the second degree, and at the same time partakes of an astringent power; as it is composed of a more abundant mixture of an earthy essence. Therefore, it stops all chronic discharges of the uterus and suppresses flows of blood. It is also beneficial—especially the flowers—for all ailments of the head and brain arising from cold and moist causes: for they dry the brain, sharpen the senses and memory, and strengthen the nervous parts.

**Serapio** <note>a prominent 11th-century Arab physician</note> testifies that the **Rosemary** used by garland-makers heats and dries significantly; and on the authority of **Abin Mesuai** <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a 9th-century physician</note>, he says it helps with a "cold catarrh" <note>original: *corizae*; a runny nose or head cold</note> of the brain.

**Dioscorides** <note>the 1st-century author of *De Materia Medica*</note> records that it cures the "royal disease" <note>original: *regium morbum*; jaundice, so named because it was supposedly treated with expensive remedies like wine and exercise fit for a king</note> if one drinks it boiled in water before exercise, then bathes after the exercise and drinks wine.

### ON LAVENDER.  CAP. LXXIX.

**Lavender** is a fragrant and garland-making plant, but low-growing; it produces slender, four-angled, jointed branches or twigs, a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> or longer; with leaves arranged in pairs around each joint, oblong, fleshy, and whitish on both sides, much longer and wider than the leaves of **Crown Rosemary**; and at the top, it bears slender, oblong spikes filled with blue flowers. The roots are woody and divided into fibers. Both the flowers and leaves breathe out a heavy scent that fills the head.

<meta>This page (264) continues the botanical description of Lavender (Lavandula) from the previous page, specifically focusing on its geographical distribution, various names used across Europe, and a scholarly debate regarding whether it is the plant referred to as "Casia" by ancient poets like Virgil or "Cneoron" by naturalists like Pliny and Theophrastus.</meta>

<page-num>264</page-num>

->## ON LAVENDER<-

*...[with] longer [leaves], which is more fragrant; the other with smaller and thinner leaves: it is also found having white flowers.*

In Spain and Narbonese Gaul <note>Southern France</note>, many mountains and deserted fields are **clothed in Lavender**. In the rest of France, among the Germans, and elsewhere, it is sown in gardens. The flowers come out in June and July.

The **ITALIANS** call the larger variety "Italian Nard," "Spike," and in the common tongue, *Spigo*; the smaller, *Lavanda* and *Lavandula*. Germany and Belgium call both *Lavandula* and *Lavendula*: specifically calling the larger "male lavender" and the smaller "female"; commonly called **Lavender**. It is thought to be the fragrant herb <term>Casia: A sweet-smelling plant of the Mediterranean, often confused in antiquity with the spice cinnamon</term>, which Virgil mentions from time to time, as in the second Eclogue of the *Bucolics*:

> *Then weaving in Casia and other sweet herbs,*
> *she colors the soft blueberries with yellow marigold.*
> <note>original: "Tum Casia atque alijs intexens suauibus herbis / Mollia luteola pingit Vacinia Caltha." — Virgil, Eclogues 2.49-50</note>

And similarly in the fourth book of the *Georgics*, where he discusses the location and site to be chosen for bees and the establishing of an apiary, with these words:

> *Around these let green Casia flourish, and widely-scented*
> *wild thyme, and a plenty of heavy-breathing savory.*
> <note>original: "Hæc circum Casiæ virides, & olentia latè / Serpilla, & grauiter spirantis copia Thymbræ / Floreat." — Virgil, Georgics 4.30-32</note>

<margin>Cneoron <note>A name used by ancient botanists for several different shrubs, possibly related to Daphne or Genista</note>.</margin>
There **IS**, however, another Casia <note>Greek: σύριγξ (*syrinx*) or *fistula* (pipe)</note> which is everywhere at this time called Cinnamon and *Canella* <note>The rolled, quill-like bark of the cinnamon tree</note>. Pliny reports that Casia was called *Cneoron* by Hyginus. According to the authors Theophrastus and Pliny, there are two types of *Cneoron*: one white, with a fleshy, oblong leaf, somewhat similar in shape to an olive, and fragrant; the other black, with no scent. Both have roots that go down deep and are large, with many thick, flexible branches; they sprout and flower after the autumnal equinox and bloom for a long time. Lavender, however, is not at all like this, as it has neither a thick nor a deep-reaching root; its branches are not flexible, and it produces flowers in summer, not autumn. Therefore, it is not even a species of *Cneoron*.

<meta>Catchword: EST (translated as "IS" at the start of the final paragraph)</meta>

<summary>This page describes where lavender grows wild and in gardens, its various regional names, and corrects historical misidentifications of the plant by comparing it to descriptions in Virgil, Pliny, and Theophrastus.</summary>

<keywords>lavender, Lavandula, Virgil, botanical identification, Casia, Cneoron, medicinal herbs, Renaissance botany, geography of plants</keywords>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues the medical properties and contraindications of Lavender (Lavandula), transitioning from its botanical description to its practical application in medicine, followed by the introduction of a related species, Stoechas (French Lavender).</meta>

<page-num>265</page-num>
<header>ON LAVENDER.</header>
<sig>R 5</sig>

Lavender is hot and dry in its potency and strength, specifically to the third degree <note>In Galenic medicine, herbs were graded 1 to 4 based on the intensity of their effects on the body's humors</note>. It consists of fine parts and is composed of many "airy" and "spirituous" elements. For this reason, it is helpful when applied in any manner to "cold" diseases of the head, especially those that do not originate from an excess of fluid <note>humors</note>, but primarily from the quality of the ailment itself.

Consequently, its fragrant distilled liquid, when rubbed onto the temples and forehead, restores those suffering from <term>Catalepsy: A condition of trance or seizure with rigid muscles</term>, mild <term>hemiplegia: Paralysis affecting one side of the body</term>, and occasionally the <term>Falling Sickness: A historical name for epilepsy, so called because the sufferer falls during a seizure</term>, as well as frequent <term>syncope: Fainting or sudden loss of strength</term>.

However, where there is an abundance of humors—especially those mixed with blood—the use of this plant is not entirely safe. The same is true for a medicinal compound made from distilled wine in which these types of herbs, flowers, seeds, or certain spices have been soaked, which many people administer recklessly and without distinction. For by using such "hot" remedies that fill the head, the disease is intensified and the patient is put in danger, especially when bloodletting or purging has not been performed beforehand.

We thought it necessary to give this warning because everywhere, many unlearned, reckless, and bold doctors, over-eager pharmacists, and other foolish women immediately pour such compositions and similar things into those afflicted not only with <term>apoplexy: A sudden loss of consciousness, often a stroke</term>, but also with <term>Catochus: A state of rigid, comatose stupor</term> accompanied by fever. Nothing worse could be given to them, as these remedies do a great deal of harm and not infrequently kill the patients themselves.

### ON STOECHAS. CHAPTER LXXX.

**Stoechas** <note>Also known as French Lavender or Topped Lavender (*Lavandula stoechas*)</note> has a trunk like **Lavender**, short and woody, but thinner. It has many thin branches or twigs; it is clothed with leaves that are oblong, narrow, whitish, and smaller than those of **Lavender**. At the top, it bears leafy heads or spikes that are dense, compact, short, and thick, from which come small purple flowers and certain prominent little leaves. The seed in these is somewhat black; the roots are woody.

# STOECHAS <note>original: "Stoechas"; This plant is now commonly known as French Lavender or Topped Lavender (*Lavandula stoechas*). The name derives from the Stoechades, a group of islands near Marseilles where the plant was known to grow abundantly.</note>

[**Botanical Illustration**]

*A detailed woodcut botanical illustration of a **Stoechas** plant. The plant is depicted with its root system visible at the base. From a central woody stem, numerous narrow, lance-shaped leaves grow in dense clusters. Four long, leafless stalks rise high above the foliage, each topped with a thick, cylindrical flower spike. These spikes are crowned with distinctive large, petal-like bracts forming a tuft at the peak.* <note>In historical herbals, these "tufts" were often described as looking like little wings or ears, distinguishing this species from common lavender.</note>

<meta>This page follows the illustration of Stoechas (French Lavender) on page 266. It provides the botanical description, geographic distribution, and medicinal applications of the plant, transitioning into a new chapter on Thyme.</meta>

<page-num>267</page-num>

# OF STOECHAS <note>original: "DE STOECHADE"; French Lavender or Topped Lavender.</note>

**SPAIN**, Narbonnese Gaul <note>The southern region of Roman France, including modern Provence and Languedoc.</note>, and the Stoechades islands <note>Now known as the Îles d'Hyères.</note> located opposite Marseille, are abundant with the Stoechas herb or shrub. In Germany and the Low Countries, it grows easily from seed in early summer; but in winter, it dies unless it is moved indoors or into storage cellars; for it is truly intolerant of winter and cold.

It is called *stichas* <note>original: "στιχας"</note> by Dioscorides, and *stoichas* <note>original: "στοιχας"</note> by others and by Galen, with a diphthong in the second syllable: in Latin, *Stoechas*. In the apothecary shops, they call the flower *Stoechas*.

**FURTHERMORE**, regarding Stoechas, Galen says its temperament is complex, consisting of a small amount of a cold earthy essence, which makes it astringent; and a larger portion of a refined earthy essence, from which it is bitter. Because of the harmony of both these qualities, it is naturally suited to free obstructions, to thin [thick humors], to cleanse, and to strengthen both all the internal organs and the whole body. Dioscorides records that a decoction of it, much like that of hyssop, is effective for ailments of the chest, and is usefully mixed into antidotes. Later physicians affirm that Stoechas, and especially its flowers, are very effective against headaches and all diseases of the head arising from cold or a "cold cause" <note>In the humoral theory of medicine, "cold" illnesses were thought to be caused by an excess of phlegm or black bile.</note>. For this reason, they are mixed into almost all compositions used against long-term Cephalalgia <term>Cephalalgia: a persistent, severe headache</term>, Apoplexy <term>Apoplexy: a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, often a stroke</term>, Catalepsy <note>A condition involving muscular rigidity and a trance-like state.</note>, Epilepsy, and similar conditions.

## ON THYME. CHAPTER 81.

**OF THYME**, there are two kinds according to the ancients: one called *Cephaloton* <note>original: "Cephaloton"; meaning "headed" thyme, likely Syrian Oregano or a similar bushy variety.</note>; the other a hardier type.

**CEPHALOTON** grows with many slender and thin branches, surrounded by small and abundant leaves: with little heads at the tips almost like those of Stoechas, but smaller; from which little purple flowers emerge. It grows abundantly on the island of Crete:

# ON THYME <note>original: "DE THYMO"</note>

... [it is found] there, whence it was given the surname Cretan. Dioscorides writes that it grows in thin and stony soil.

<margin>2</margin> **THE HARDER KIND** *Thyme is also branchy <note>original: "furculosum"; meaning shrub-like or twiggy.</note>, and has very small little leaves; those which are most common are green in color and sweet-smelling; but the white ones are more pungent-smelling, which is rarer: indeed, the flowers are not found in little heads or spikes, but next to the leaves: the roots are thin. Deserted and uncultivated places of Spain and Narbonnese Gaul <note>The Roman province in southern France, including modern-day Provence.</note> abound in Thyme of this kind: In Germany, the Low Countries, and elsewhere, it is diligently cultivated in gardens.*

**AMONG THE GREEKS** *it is called thymos <note>original: "θύμος"</note>; in Latin, Thymus and Thymum. The first kind is called "headed" <note>original: "κεφαλωτός" (kephalotos)</note> and thyrsion <note>original: "θύρσιον"</note>: the other is called "harder" <note>original: "σκληρότερος" (skleroteros)</note> by Dioscorides in the chapter on Epithymum. Pliny reports that there are two kinds of Thyme: one he calls white; the other darker or blackish. He says the white is superior and grows on hills (Book 21, Chapter 21). Both seem to refer to the harder genus, for as we have written, some of the harder Thyme has whitish leaves; another indeed has green ones, which is called blackish because it is green with a deep, saturated color and not whitish. The Germans and Belgians call it* **Thymum/Thymus**: *the French call it du Thym.*

**AS FOR THYME**, *as Galen records, it must be placed in the third degree of heating and drying <note>In the humoral system, the third degree indicates a very potent medicine that causes significant changes in the body’s temperature and moisture.</note>; it cuts through [thick humors] and heats vehemently; for this reason, it provokes both urine and menstruation, expels the fetus <note>It was used as an abortifacient.</note>, purges the internal organs, and is useful for clearing the chest and lungs. Dioscorides writes that when drunk with salt and vinegar, it draws out phlegm through the bowels. A decoction of it with honey is beneficial for those suffering from orthopnea <note>Difficulty breathing while lying down.</note> and asthma. It expels intestinal worms, the menses, the fetus, and the afterbirth: it promotes the flow of urine. Mixed with honey and taken as a lickable medicine <note>electuary</note>, it allows for easy coughing up of phlegm from the chest. Applied with vinegar, it disperses recent swellings and dissolves blood clots. It also treats "thymi" <note>A type of wart named for its resemblance to a thyme bud.</note> and acrochordons <note>original: "ἀκροχορδόνας"; skin tags or hanging warts.</note> or hanging...*

<summary>This page describes the 'harder' variety of Thyme, its growth habits in Europe, and its nomenclature across Greek, Latin, and modern languages. It details Thyme's medicinal potency as a 'third-degree' heating agent used for respiratory issues, digestive purging, and skin growths.</summary>

It helps those suffering from sciatica <note>pain affecting the back, hip, and outer side of the leg</note> when applied as a poultice with wine and polenta <note>a thick paste of grain or meal</note>. Taken in food, it is beneficial for dimness of sight. It is also useful as a seasoning during times of good health.

# ON COMMON WILD THYME <note>original: "DE SERPILLO VULGARI"</note>
## CHAPTER LXXXII.

**COMMON** Wild Thyme has many thin stalks, which are nonetheless hard and woody; some rise straight up to the height of a palm <note>roughly 3 to 4 inches</note>, while others creep along the ground, attached to the surface in some places by tiny fibers. The leaves are small, green, and similar to Thyme. At the tips are round little heads from which small flowers emerge, reddish-purple in color, though not infrequently white. The roots are very thin and fibrous.

**IT GROWS** everywhere in uncultivated places, along the edges of fields, especially in rich and fertile soil; it is also found occasionally in stony places. It grows wild not only in Germany or Belgium, but also in Italy and France. Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist</note> reports that it grows most abundantly on Mount Salustino near Gorizia. It is in flower throughout almost the entire summer.

**THE GERMANS** call it *Quendel*, *kumel*, and *kienlin*; the **BELGIANS**, *Quendel*, *wilden Thymus*, and *onser Vrouwen bedstroo* <note>literally "Our Lady’s bedstraw"</note>; the **FRENCH**, *Pillolet*; the **APOTHECARIES**, *Serpillum*, though many also call it *Pulegium montanum* <note>Mountain Pennyroyal</note>. It is widely thought to be the *Serpillum* of the ancient authors; yet it corresponds not so much to their *Serpillum* as it does to the *Saxifraga* <note>literally "stone-breaker," plants often used for kidney stones</note> of Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of the foundational pharmaceutical text "De Materia Medica"</note>. For if it is carefully weighed against the descriptions of both *Serpillum* and *Saxifraga*, it will be found to be very little like *Serpillum* and much more like *Saxifraga*. For the first kind of *Serpillum*—besides the fact that its branches and leaves are like Oregano—grows to whatever height it can when supported by a prop or leaning on neighboring stalks, and sometimes [resembles] Basil <note>original: "Ocymo"</note>...

<summary>This page describes Common Wild Thyme (Serpillum), detailing its physical appearance, its preference for fertile or stony soils across Europe, and the debate among botanists regarding its classification compared to the writings of Dioscorides.</summary>
<keywords>Wild Thyme, Serpillum, Mattioli, Dioscorides, Saxifraga, medicinal herbs, botany, sciatica, eye health</keywords>

But the other kind does not creep, and its leaf is longer than that of Rue <note>original: "Rutæ"</note>—that is, St. John’s Wort <note>original: "Hyperici"</note> or Lesser Centaury <note>original: "Cētaurij minoris"</note>, as we have shown elsewhere. However, Common Wild Thyme is low-growing; it does not climb up shrubs, nor does it arise from Basil <note>original: "ex Ocymo"</note>; and its leaves are much smaller and shorter than those of St. John’s Wort or Lesser Centaury—evidently quite tiny and similar, as we have said, to the leaves of Thyme. For he says that Saxifrage is an herb similar to Thyme, growing among rocks. <page-num>271</page-num>
# ON COMMON WILD THYME.

*It bears a resemblance to Thyme; it is called Wild Thyme <note>original: "Thymum sylvestre"</note> both by the Belgians and by others.*

<margin>The passage of Dioscorides restored.</margin>
*The WORDS of Dioscorides regarding Saxifrage are: "Sarxiphagon," or by some "Sarxiphangon," and by others "Empetrum": the Romans call it Saxifrage <note>original: "Saxifranga"; literally "stone-breaker"</note>; it is a twiggy shrub growing among rocks and in rough places, similar to Thyme <note>original Greek: Σαρξίφαγον, οι δε σαρξίφαγγον, οι δὲ ἔμπετρον : ρωμαῖοι σαξίφραγγα... θυμῷ ἐμφερής</note>. That is, Saxifrage is a twiggy shrub growing in rocks and harsh places, similar to Thyme. Certain copies read "similar to Epithymum" <note>original: "ἐπιθυμῷ ἐμφερής"</note>, but the more corrected versions have "Thyme" <note>original: "θυμῷ"</note>.*

*In its POWER <note>medicinal properties or "faculties"</note>, Common Wild Thyme is hot and dry, almost in the third degree; it is likewise of thin parts and incisive <note>term: "incidens"; a medicinal quality that cuts through or thins out thick bodily fluids or "humors"</note>: it calls forth the menses; it stirs the urine; it expels stones; and used in baths and fomentations <note>warm, medicinal compresses</note>, it calls forth sweat.*

*Dioscorides recorded nearly the SAME things regarding Saxifrage: he says that when cooked in wine, it helps those suffering from fevers; it is beneficial for difficulty in urination; it calms hiccups; it breaks bladder stones; and it stirs the urine.*

### ON WILD THYME ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES,
### THEOPHRASTUS, AND OTHERS.
#### CHAPTER LXXXIII.

*According to Dioscorides, Wild Thyme <note>original: "Serpillum"</note> is of two kinds: the garden type and the wild type.*

<margin>1</margin>
*The GARDEN type is used for wreaths <note>original: "coronarium"; referring to plants grown for garlands</note>, and reflects the scent of Marjoram <note>original: "Sampsychum"</note>: it has leaves and branches similar to Origanum, though much whiter; when left near hedges, it grows taller.*

<margin>2</margin>
*THE OTHER is not creeping but erect; it sends out thin, twiggy branches: filled with leaves similar to Rue <note>original: "Rutæ"</note>, yet narrower, longer, and harder: the flowers [are sharp] in taste* <meta>The text cuts off here; the catchword "acres" indicates the next page begins with "sharp" or "pungent".</meta>

<page-num>272</page-num>

<header>ON WILD THYME ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES.</header>

...sharp, sweet in smell: the root is useless. It grows more effectively among rocks, and is more warming than the cultivated variety.

### THEOPHRASTUS, *On the History of Plants*, Book 6.
Theophrastus records that Wild Thyme <note>original: *Serpillum*</note> is among the sub-shrubs <note>term: "suffrutices"; plants that are woody at the base but have herbaceous stems</note>, being both woody and used for making garlands. He notes that its whole nature is fragrant in its branches and leaves, just like *Helenium* <note>Elecampane</note> and *Sisyumbrium* <note>likely Water-mint or a similar aquatic herb</note>; according to some, it even smells entirely like Thyme. It clings to the surface of the earth; with a multi-part and parched root, it has a peculiar way of producing shoots. Indeed, it is able to grow to any length if it finds a support or is planted near hedges. It endures rough conditions and requires little water. He even claims it arises from Basil <note>original: *Ocimum*</note> that has degenerated through excessive drought; as he says in the fifth book of *On the Causes of Plants*, it comes from the Basil itself, but with a smaller leaf and a more powerful scent. From this we may gather that Wild Thyme is more fragrant than Basil and has smaller leaves.

### WILD THYME
He says that this same **WILD** Thyme is a variety that people bring down from the mountains, as is commonly done at Sicyon, and also at Athens from Mount Hymettus. Among other peoples, however, such as in Thrace, the mountains and plains all teem with Wild Thyme. This "wild" variety, however, is different from the wild variety described by Dioscorides: for it differs from the garden variety only by cultivation, as Theophrastus himself shows when he mentions it being transplanted from the mountains.

### PLINY
Pliny opposes DIOSCORIDES in Book 20, Chapter 22, where he says the wild variety creeps, but the cultivated variety does not at all. He says: "They think Wild Thyme <note>original: *Serpillum*</note> was named from 'creeping' <note>original: *serpendo*; the Latin *serpere* means to creep</note>, which happens most in the wild variety growing among rocks. The cultivated kind does not creep, but grows to the height of a palm."

### AELIANUS
Aelianus mentions Wild Thyme in Book 9 of his *Various History*, and he seems to count it among the flowers. He says: "Dionysius the Younger, coming into the city of the Locrians, occupied the largest houses of the city and strewed them with Roses, Wild Thyme, and other flowers of that kind." However, Virgil, in the second eclogue of the *Bucolics*, Wild [Thyme]...

<page-num>273</page-num>

<header>ON WILD THYME ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES.</header>

Virgil most clearly testifies that Wild Thyme is a herb with these words:
> Thestylis also crushes garlic and Wild Thyme, fragrant herbs, for the reapers weary from the rapid heat. <note>original: "Thestylis & rapido fessis messoribus æstu / Allia, Serpillumq́;, herbas contundit olenteis." — from Virgil's *Eclogues* 2.10-11.</note>
From this passage, there are those who gather that the common Wild Thyme is the true and legitimate kind, since no other variety grows spontaneously throughout Italy; as if it could only be found in the fields and could not be sown in gardens.
**Wild Thyme** <note>original: *Serpillum*</note> is called *herpyllos* or *herpyllon* <note>original: ἕρπυλλος καὶ ἕρπυλλον</note> by the Greeks. Marcellus, an ancient author, affirms it was called *Gilarum* by the Gauls in antiquity, while Pliny Valerianus says it is called *Laurio* by them.
**Wild Thyme** warms to such an extent, as Galen says, that it promotes both menstruation and urination; it is very sharp to the taste. It is also beneficial, as Dioscorides adds, for griping pains <note>term: "tormina"; severe abdominal or intestinal cramping</note>, ruptures, convulsions, inflammations of the liver, and for snake bites, whether drunk or applied as a poultice. When boiled and mixed with rose oil, it mitigates headaches. When applied with vinegar, it is especially suitable for those suffering from lethargy <note>term: "lethargicis"; a state of pathological drowsiness or coma-like sleep</note> and also for those with frenzy <note>term: "phreneticis"; acute mental disturbance or delirium</note>. Its juice, drunk in the amount of four drachms with vinegar, also settles the vomiting of blood.

### ON BASIL. CAP. LXXXIIII.

There are two common species of **cultivated** Basil <note>original: *Ocimum*</note> that differ in size; both are twiggy and rise to a height of nine inches or a foot, with round branches divided into many "wings" <note>note: side-shoots or axillary branches</note>.
<margin>1</margin>The larger one has broad leaves, slightly notched along the edges, similar in size and shape to Mercury's herb <note>original: *Mercurialis herba*; likely French Mercury (*Mercurialis annua*)</note>.
<margin>2</margin>The smaller one has smaller leaves. Both are shining and pale green; the small flowers are either white or a dark purple in color. The seed is small and blackish; the roots are long and hard, not without clinging fibers. The scent of both—

<header>ON BASIL.</header>

...and a similar, though larger, species may be found, which recalls the scent of the *Citron fruit* <note>original: *Citrium malum*</note>, which they call *Citrate Basil*.

<margin>3</margin>MATTHIOLUS <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a renowned 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> records that besides these two types of Basil, a third very large variety is also found, with leaves larger than those of *Amaranth*, being long, wide, and thick, similar to the leaves of the *Citron tree*.

Basil is SOWN in gardens and in earthenware pots; as THEOPHRASTUS <note>the ancient Greek "Father of Botany"</note> testifies, it grows very quickly. That which is exposed to the Sun, according to the same witness, turns into *Wild Thyme*; this happens because it is dried out more intensely, for the leaf is rendered smaller and the scent becomes more powerful. Basil blooms in June and July, and it does so bit by bit and for a long time, beginning from the bottom of the stem.

The Greeks call it *Okimon* <note>original Greek: Ὤκιμον</note>, and with an 'o' in the first syllable, *Okimon*; some also call it *ozimon* <note>original Greek: ὄζιμον, likely referring to its strong odor</note>: in Latin, *Ocimum*. It differs from the *Ocymum* which we showed in the *History of Grains* to be a type of fodder; to distinguish the two, the fodder is written with a 'y' <note>the Greek letter upsilon</note> in the second syllable; although the fragrant Basil is also found written with a 'y' at times. Modern authors also call it *Basilikon* <note>original Greek: Βασιλικὸν, meaning "Royal"</note>, in Latin *Regium*—that is, "Royal": in the apothecary shops, it is *Basilicum*: the Germans call it *Basilgen*. The Belgians, *Basilicom*: the French, *Basilic*: the Italians, *Basilico*, and the smaller variety, *Basilico gentile* <note>Noble or refined basil</note>: most call this *Basilicum Gariophyllatum* <note>Clove-scented Basil</note>.

Basil, however, according to the authority of GALEN <note>the most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note>, is of the second order of "heating" agents <note>referring to the theory of the Four Humors, where plants were classified by their effect on the body's temperature and moisture</note>, but it has an associated excrementitious moisture. Because of this, he believes it is not suitable to be taken inside the body: but applied externally, it is fit for digesting and ripening <note>term: concocting; used here in the medical sense of maturing an abscess or inflammation</note>.

DIOSCORIDES <note>author of the primary ancient Greek pharmacological text, *De Materia Medica*</note> says that if Basil is eaten in large amounts, it dulls the eyesight; it softens the bowels, causes flatulence, promotes urine, and stimulates the flow of milk, but it is digested with difficulty. However, when applied as a poultice with pearl barley, fine flour, rose oil, and vinegar, it heals inflammations, and

<page-num>276</page-num>

<header>ON BASIL.</header>

[Basil is used for] the wounds of the Greater Weever fish <note>original: *Draconis marini*; a venomous fish known for its painful sting</note> and the Scorpion; [applied] by itself or with Chian wine <note>a famous Greek wine from the island of Chios, often used as a solvent for medicines</note>, it is used for pains of the eyes.

**THE JUICE** clears dimness of the eyes and dries up discharges <note>original: *defluxiones*; the accumulation of fluid or "rheum" in the eyes</note>.

**THE SEED**, when drunk, benefits those gathering black bile <note>original: *atram bilem*; in the medical theory of the time, an excess of this "humor" was thought to cause melancholy and various illnesses</note>, those suffering from shortness of breath <note>original: *suspiriosis*</note>, and those struggling with difficulty in urination. It also causes frequent sneezing if drawn in through the nostrils; the herb itself does the same. However, one ought to close the eyes when a sneeze is imminent.

**THERE ARE THOSE** who avoid Basil and do not eat it, because they believe that when chewed and placed in the sun, it generates small worms. Africans <note>original: *Aphri*</note> add that those who have been stung by a Scorpion and have eaten the herb themselves remain without pain.

**MORE RECENT AUTHORS**, among whom is Simeon Seth <note>original: *Simeon Zethy*, an 11th-century Byzantine Jewish physician and translator</note>, report that Basil benefits the heart and the head through its scent; that the seed aids heart conditions <note>original: *cardiacis affectibus*</note>, dispels sorrow of the mind arising from black bile, and procures cheerfulness and joy.

### ON COMMON WILD BASIL, OR ACINOS. CHAPTER 85.

**THAT WHICH** most recent authors call Wild Basil <note>original: *Ocimum sylvestre*</note> produces several four-sided, weak little stems, reclining toward the ground; the leaves are placed in pairs opposite each other at intervals, being small, somewhat long and wide, and a little hairy and rough, as are the stems. The small flowers emerge from the axils <note>original: *sinubus*, literally "folds" or "pockets"</note> of the leaves, surrounding the stems in a circle, and are white in color, turning somewhat purple. The seed is small; the roots consist of clinging fibers. The scent of this herb is much weaker than that of garden Basil; indeed, it is slight and faint, yet with a certain not unpleasant heaviness approaching that of Calamint <note>original: *Calaminthes*</note> or garden Basil. It grows in many places in Germany and Italy.

# WILD BASIL, ACINOS <note>original: *Ocimum Sylvestre Acinos*; this plant is known today by the scientific name *Clinopodium acinos*, or more commonly as Basil Thyme</note>

S 3 <note>This is a printer's signature mark, used by early modern bookbinders to ensure the pages were assembled in the correct order.</note>

This page is dedicated to a detailed, full-page botanical woodcut of the Wild Basil plant. The illustration provides a comprehensive view of the plant’s anatomy:

The image shows the entire plant from its fibrous root system at the base to its flowering tips. Several branching, upright stems are depicted, which are covered in fine hairs—a detail often used to distinguish this species from the smooth-stemmed garden Basil. The small, serrated leaves grow in opposite pairs along the stems. Small clusters of flowers, arranged in whorls, are situated at the leaf axils (the point where the leaf meets the stem) and at the ends of the branches. 

This visual representation was essential for Renaissance physicians and herbalists to accurately identify the plant in the wild for medicinal use.

### ON COMMON WILD BASIL

[It grows] in locations of its own accord, especially in uncultivated and sandy areas. It blooms throughout the entire summer, and not infrequently even in the month of September.

*Most of the more recent authors call this* **Wild Basil** <note>original: *Ocimum sylvestre*</note>*, as we have said. However, what Pliny calls Wild Basil, others call* **Ocimastrum** *and* **okimoeides** <note>original: ὠκιμοειδὲς; literally "basil-like"</note>*. Yet,* **Ocimoides** *differs greatly from the present little herb. Indeed,* **Ocimoides** *possesses pods similar to the pods of Henbane* <term>Hyoscyamus: a toxic plant in the nightshade family</term>*, with black seeds like those of the teeming Nigella* <term>Melanthium: also known as Love-in-a-mist or Black Cumin</term>*. The seeds of this plant, however, are very small and are contained in altogether tiny vessels that have no similarity to the fruit-cases of Henbane. Thus, it is evident from this that this little herb is not the legitimate Wild Basil or* **Ocimastrum**. *For this reason, it is held by others not to be Wild Basil, but* **Clinopodium** <note>original: *Clinopodium*; likely Basil Thyme or Wild Basil</note>*; but the opinion of these people does not seem worthy of praise either. For if it deserves to be called* **Clinopodium** *simply because of the circling of the flowers along the small stalks, many other plants would have to be called by the same name: such as Calamints, Clarys, Dead-nettles, Dittanies, and certain species of Horehound, Pennyroyal, Sage, Water Germander, Balm-leaf, Ironwort, Galen’s Madwort, the Bugle of the moderns, and Motherwort, and others like them, whose flowers all surround the stalks in a ring; yet none of them is called* **Clinopodium**. 

<margin>Acinos.</margin>
*To us, this herb seems not at all unlike* **Acinos**. *For Dioscorides* <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> *says that* **Acinos** *is a "fine-fruited herb"* <note>original: πόαν λεπτόκαρπον (*poan leptokarpon*)</note> *or a herb of thin twigs, similar to the Basil used for crowns but more hairy and fragrant. Our present plant agrees well enough with this description.*

*In its* **PROPERTIES**, *however, it is somewhat warm and dry, not without a power and potency for binding* <term>astringency: the quality of shrinking or constricting body tissues</term>.

**DIOSCORIDES** *reports that* **ACINOS**, *when drunk, stops the menstrual flow and the bowels; but when applied topically, it cures inflammatory swellings* <note>original: *Phygethla*; these are painful glandular swellings</note> *and erysipelas* <term>a bacterial skin infection characterized by large, red, inflamed patches</term>.

<meta>catchword: DE (On)</meta>

<summary>This page contains a botanical analysis of Wild Basil (Acinos), distinguishing it from other species through seed pod structure and floral arrangement, while detailing its medicinal uses for internal and external ailments.</summary>

<keywords>Wild Basil, Acinos, botanical identification, Dioscorides, Pliny, medicinal properties, astringency, skin conditions, plant nomenclature</keywords>

### ON CLARY SAGE. CHAPTER 86.

<margin>1</margin>
The first species of **CLARY SAGE** <note>original: *Orvala*</note> (*for three or four of them are counted*), which is properly called *Orvala*, produces thick, four-angled stems, two feet long, divided into branches: many leaves grow from the roots, and along the stems at intervals they are placed in pairs opposite each other; they are large, a palm's breadth wide or more, somewhat rough, uneven, and whitish, and like the stems, somewhat hairy. The flowers are in the shape of Sage or Dead-nettle flowers, of a whitish color tinted with dilute blue, after which come oblong, toothed vessels instead of pods, in which there is black seed; the roots are indeed fibrous. The whole herb breathes out a heavy and strong odor that fills the head, and it dies once the seed is ripened, which happens in the second year after sowing. It is sown by gardeners in gardens. It blooms in June and July.

The apothecaries call it **GALLITRICUM**. It is also called *Orvala* or *Orvalla*, *Tota bona* <note>literally "All-good"</note>, *Sclarea*, *Scarlea*, *Centrum Galli* <note>literally "Cock's-spur"</note>, and *Matrisaluia* <note>literally "Mother-sage"</note>: by the French **Orvale**: by the Germans **Scharlach**: by the Belgians **Scharleye**. Those who think it is *Alectorolophon* <note>literally "Cock's-comb"; likely Yellow Rattle</note> are mistaken. Indeed, in the history of Grains, we have shown that *Alectorolophon* differs greatly from Clary Sage. This species of Clary Sage seems to be the Wild *Horminum* <term>Horminum: a Greek name for Clary Sage, derived from the word "to excite," referring to its medicinal properties</term>, which Dioscorides <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> says is more powerful in its properties. For among all species of Clary Sage and *Horminum*, none is more potent than this one in odor or strength.

<margin>2</margin>
**THE SECOND** species of Clary Sage has similarly four-angled stems, but taller, namely two cubits <note>a cubit is an ancient unit of length, approximately 18 inches</note> or higher: the leaves are also rough, yet darker and smaller: the flowers are similar, turning red with a purple color: the roots are like the first one. This one is less prominent in odor, being endowed with a very small one; it is rare. It blooms at the same time as the first. There are some who think it is *Baccharis* <term>Baccharis: an ancient name for a fragrant plant often used in wreaths, though its exact identity was often debated by Renaissance botanists</term>; however, how much their opinion should be approved...

S 4

-># ORVALA.<-

### ON CLARY SAGE. CHAPTER 86.

<margin>1</margin>
The first species of **CLARY SAGE** <note>original: *Orvala*</note> (*for three or four of them are counted*), which is properly called *Clary Sage*, produces thick, four-angled stems, two feet long, divided into branches: many leaves grow from the roots, and along the stems at intervals they are placed in pairs opposite each other; they are large, a palm's breadth wide or more, somewhat rough, uneven, and whitish, and like the stems, somewhat hairy. The flowers are in the shape of Sage or Dead-nettle flowers, of a whitish color tinted with dilute blue, after which come oblong, toothed vessels instead of pods, in which there is black seed; the roots are indeed fibrous. The whole herb breathes out a heavy and strong odor that fills the head, and it dies once the seed is ripened, which happens in the second year after sowing. It is sown by gardeners in gardens. It blooms in June and July.

The apothecaries call it **GALLITRICUM** <note>original: *Gallitricum*</note>. It is also called *Orvala* or *Orvalla*, *All-good* <note>original: *Tota bona*</note>, *Clary* <note>original: *Sclarea* or *Scarlea*</note>, *Cock’s-spur* <note>original: *Centrum Galli*</note>, and *Mother-sage* <note>original: *Matrisaluia*</note>: by the French **Orvale**: by the Germans **Scharlach**: by the Belgians **Scharleye**. Those who think it is *Cock’s-comb* <note>original: *Alectorolophon*</note> are mistaken. Indeed, in the history of Grains, we have shown that *Cock’s-comb* differs greatly from Clary Sage. This species of Clary Sage seems to be the Wild *Horminum* <term>Horminum: a name derived from the Greek *hormao* (to incite or stir up), referring to the plant's reputed aphrodisiac or stimulating properties</term> of Dioscorides.

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 86 on Clary Sage, providing a detailed botanical description of the plant's structure, scent, and growth cycle. It also lists several names for the plant in various European languages and clarifies its identity against other similar species.</summary>

<keywords>Clary Sage, Orvala, Gallitricum, Salvia sclarea, botanical description, plant nomenclature, Horminum, Renaissance botany</keywords>

...the description of *Baccharis* <note>original: *Baccharis*; likely a reference to a plant in the Daisy family, though often confused with others in Renaissance herbals</note> will show, which the next chapter will provide immediately following.

<margin>3</margin>
The stems of the **THIRD** Clary Sage <note>original: *Orvale*</note> are also four-angled and somewhat hairy; the leaves are saw-toothed around the edges, similar to the first Clary Sage, but smaller; the flowers are yellow; the seed is a yellowish-black; the root is fibrous, of a durable nature, and lasts for many years. It is sown in gardens by some among the Belgians; but in the Italian Alps near Trento, it is said to grow wild. It blooms in summer. It is called *Yellow Clary* <note>original: *Horminum luteum*</note> and *Trento Clary* <note>original: *Horminum Tridentinum*</note> by most people.

<margin>4</margin>
To these, a **FOURTH** species can be added, which is depicted by Fuchs <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a founding father of German botany</note> as *Wild Clary* <note>original: *sylvestri Hormino*</note>; its small stems are shorter, but also hairy and four-angled; the leaves are smaller, oblong, and more deeply cut; the flowers are blue; the pods droop downward when mature; the seed is blackish; the roots are similarly black and fibrous. Fuchs says that in Germany it adorns all the meadows and grows in them of its own accord. Its blooming time agrees with the ones above. This is also a species of Clary Sage and *Horminum*, but it seems to be inactive and of no medicinal power.

**BUT** the first one, which we said is properly called Clary Sage <note>original: *Orvala*</note>, is hot and dry in the third degree <term>third degree: a classification in Galenic medicine indicating a very potent effect on the body's humors</term>; taken in any way, it stimulates menstruation; it expels the afterbirth; mixed with beer or wine, it causes drunkenness and fills the head <note>indicates causing a headache or a heavy, congested feeling</note>.

**ANTONIUS MIZALDUS** <note>Antoine Mizauld (1510–1578) was a French physician and astrologer famous for his "secrets" and botanical works</note> reports that the seed is useful for clearing away dimness of the eyes and for removing objects that have fallen into them: for if it is placed into the eye and moved around the eyeball many times, it finally slips out swollen with moisture and laden with thin films and filth <note>the seeds of Clary Sage produce a mucilaginous coating when wet, which was historically used to trap and remove dust from the eye, giving the plant the common name "Clear-eye" or "Clary"</note>.

### ON CLARY SAGE <note>original: *Horminum*</note>. CHAPTER LXXXVII.

CLARY SAGE <note>original: *Horminum*</note> is also a species of Clary <note>original: *Oruala*</note>,
but smaller than the others: it puts forth several stems nine inches <note>original: *dodrantem*</note> or
 a foot high, similarly angled and somewhat hairy: the leaves are broad,
rounded, slightly rough, much smaller than the common Clary <note>original: *Oruala*</note>,
and very similar to Horehound <note>original: *Marrubij*</note>. Of these, the four or six upper leaves
shine with the color of a purplish-black **Violet**, though somewhat paler: the flowers
surround the small stems near the leaves, whitening with a faint purple color:
the seed is tiny, oblong, and blackish, held in oblong receptacles like pods,
which face toward the root once the seed is ripened: the root is long,
thin, and not without fibers.
*This Clary Sage is a stranger to Belgium, and has been brought into gardens from
elsewhere. The production of its flowers occurs in the summer months; meanwhile,
the seed ripens.*

The **Greeks** call it *Horminon* <note>original: Όρμινον</note>: the **Latins** call it *Horminum* and *Geminalis*.

**CLARY SAGE** is **HOT**, however, as **Paul of Aegina** <note>a 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician</note> says,
moderately dry, and cleansing. **Dioscorides** <note>a famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century</note> says the
seed of Clary Sage, when drunk with wine, is thought to excite sexual desire <note>original: *Venerem excitare*</note>: but
with honey, it cleanses white spots <note>original: *argema*</note> and films <note>original: *albugines*</note> of the eyes: 
applied with water, it disperses swellings <note>original: *oedemata*</note> and extracts
thorns. The herb itself does the same when applied. **Galen** in his books
*On the Properties of Foodstuffs*, says of Clary Sage: This too,
he says, they use roasted, then they grind it so finely that
it becomes flour; they also mix honey with it. It has little
nourishment in itself, as its nature is halfway between 
Hedge-mustard <note>original: *Erysimum*</note> and Cumin <note>original: *Cuminum*</note>.

### ON CLARY SAGE <note>original: *Horminum*</note>. CHAPTER LXXXVII.

**CLARY SAGE** <note>original: *Horminum*</note> is also a species of Clary <note>original: *Oruala*</note>, but smaller than the other varieties. It puts forth several stems about nine inches <note>original: *dodrantem*; a measurement based on the span of a hand</note> or a foot high, which are similarly angled and somewhat hairy. The leaves are broad, rounded, and slightly rough; they are much smaller than those of the common Clary Sage <note>original: *Oruala*</note> and look very similar to **Horehound** <note>original: *Marrubij*</note>. Of these, the four or six uppermost leaves shine with the color of a purplish-black **Violet**, though they are somewhat paler in shade. The flowers surround the small stems near the leaves, appearing white with a faint purple tint. The seed is tiny, oblong, and blackish, held in oblong receptacles resembling pods; these face toward the root once the seed has ripened. The root is long, thin, and fibrous.

*This species of Clary Sage is a stranger to the Low Countries <note>original: *Belgium*</note> and has been brought into gardens from other regions. Its flowers bloom during the summer months, while the seed ripens shortly thereafter.*

The **Greeks** call it *Horminon* <note>original: Όρμινον</note>; the **Latins** call it *Horminum* and *Geminalis* <note>likely referring to the paired nature of its leaves or flowers</note>.

**CLARY SAGE** is considered **HOT** in temperament; however, as **Paul of Aegina** <note>a 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician whose work summarized ancient medical knowledge</note> says, it is moderately dry and possesses cleansing properties. **Dioscorides** <note>a famous Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> writes that the seed of Clary Sage, when drunk with wine, is thought to excite sexual desire <note>original: *Venerem excitare*; literally "to stir Venus"</note>. Furthermore, when mixed with honey, it is used to cleanse white spots <note>original: *argema*; small white ulcers or scars on the cornea</note> and films from the eyes.

<summary>This page provides a detailed botanical description of a smaller variety of Clary Sage (Horminum), noting its violet-colored upper leaves and its introduction to European gardens. It concludes with traditional medicinal uses, specifically as an aphrodisiac and a treatment for eye conditions.</summary>

<keywords>Clary Sage, Horminum, botanical illustration, Paul of Aegina, Dioscorides, medicinal herbs, aphrodisiac, eye treatments, garden plants</keywords>

### ON BACCHARIS, ACCORDING TO DIOSCORIDES AND OTHERS. CHAPTER LXXXVIII.

**Baccharis** <note>a plant historically identified with various species, including *Inula* or *Conyza*</note>, according to Dioscorides, is a fragrant and ornamental plant <note>original: *coronaria*; used for making garlands</note> whose leaves are **rough** <note>original: τραχέα (*trachea*)</note>, or otherwise **dense** <note>original: δασεία (*daseia*)</note>. In size, it is intermediate between a **Violet** and **Mullein** <note>original: *Verbascum*</note>. The stem is angular, about eighteen inches <note>original: *cubitalis*; a cubit</note> long, somewhat rough, and possesses **offshoots** <note>original: παραφυάδας (*paraphyadas*)</note> growing alongside it. The flowers are purple, fading toward white, and fragrant. The roots resemble those of **Black Hellebore** <note>original: *Veratrum nigrum*</note> and have the scent of **Cinnamon**. It loves rough and uncultivated places, growing in thin soil that is not damp: *Pliny*.

It is called **Baccharis** <note>original: Βάκχαρις</note> in Greek, or *pakcharis* <note>original: πάκχαρις</note>, or as some prefer, *pangcharis* <note>original: πάγχαρις; literally "all-delightful"</note>. By some, it is called **Wild Nard** <note>original: *Nardum rusticum*</note>, as Pliny testifies in Book 21, Chapter 6. Indeed, Pliny himself calls it **Wild Nard** in Book 12, Chapter 12. But in Book 21, Chapter 19, he reports that it is called so incorrectly—just as he wrote that some people mistakenly call it **Barbaric** <note>original: *Barbaricam*</note>. But their error also, he says, must be corrected—those who have called Baccar "Wild Nard." For there is another plant so named, which the Greeks call **Asaron** <note>original: *Asaron*; likely Asarabacca or Wild Ginger</note>. The same author in the same book, Chapter 21, says that some call Baccharis **Perpensa**. However, if *Perpensa* is actually **Asarum**, as it is found among illegitimate and added names, the error of these people must be refuted no less than the previous ones. For **Baccharis** and **Asarum** differ significantly, as Pliny is a witness and as is evident from a comparison of their descriptions. Consequently, the apothecary shops <note>original: *Officinæ*</note> also incorrectly call *Asarum* by the name *Asarum baccara*, a name composed from *Asarum* and *Baccharis*, as if *Asarum* and *Baccharis* were the same thing. This error seems to have arisen because the word *Baccharis* is even found among the illegitimate names for *Asarum*, or because **Crateuas** <note>an ancient Greek pharmacologist</note> named Baccharis as *Asarum*. But since Crateuas calls it *Asarum*, [this] by no means [agrees with] Dioscorides... <note>the text breaks off here; the catchword "quam" suggests the word *nequaquam*, meaning "by no means"</note>

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of Baccharis and addresses the historical confusion among botanists like Pliny and Crateuas regarding its identification with Asarum or Wild Nard.</summary>
<keywords>Baccharis, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Asarum, Asarabacca, botanical nomenclature, herbal history, Wild Nard, Crateuas</keywords>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical argument from page 284, concluding the comparison between Baccharis and Asarum (Wild Ginger). It resolves the confusion caused by ancient authors who shared names for distinct plants, specifically focusing on the descriptions provided by Crateuas and Dioscorides.</meta>

...than **Asarum** <note>likely *Asarum europaeum* or Wild Ginger</note>, but it is actually **Baccharis**. For the Asarum described by **Dioscorides** <note>a first-century Greek physician and botanist</note> lacks a stem; it is not fragrant except for the roots alone; nor is it used for garlands—if, as **Pliny** says, it was called *Asarum* because it was not added to wreaths <note>the name *Asarum* was etymologically linked to the Greek *a-saron*, meaning "not for a garland"</note>. However, the Asarum of **Crateuas** <note>an early Greek pharmacologist and illustrator</note> does produce stems, and it is both fragrant and suitable for garlands. This is clear from his own description, which reads as follows:

<margin>The Asarum of Crateuas.</margin>
**ASARUM** is a fragrant and garland-making herb; its stems are jointed <note>original: *geniculati*</note>; its leaves are **thick** <note>original: δασέα (*dasea*), meaning dense or hairy</note>; the flowers are purple; the root has a pleasant scent, resembling **Black Hellebore** <note>original: *Veratrum nigrum*</note>, and it smells like **Cinnamon**. It grows in rough and neglected places.

From these words, it is perfectly obvious that the "Asarum" of Crateuas is the same plant as the **Baccharis** of Dioscorides, and is different from the true Asarum described by that same author. It was a mistake to attribute the name Baccharis to Asarum among the "spurious names" <note>original: *nothas voces*; referring to lists of synonyms often appended to ancient herbals</note>. It was an even worse error to add that Asarum is a fragrant garland-herb, a claim which is also found recorded among those same spurious lists.

**Virgil** also mentions Baccharis in the seventh eclogue of his *Bucolics* <note>a collection of pastoral poems</note>, where he shows that the plant is not only used for garlands but is also a protector against the "evil eye" <note>original: *fascini depulsoriam*; a plant used to ward off charms or curses</note>:

->—Bind my brow with **Baccharis**, he says,<-
->Lest an evil tongue harm the poet-to-be.<-

There is also an ointment called **Baccharis** mentioned in Book 15 of **Athenaeus** <note>a Greek rhetorician and grammarian</note>, which may take its name from the fragrant Baccharis herb. For, as Pliny notes, ointments were commonly made from its root, a fact to which **Aristophanes**, the poet of Old Comedy, bears witness among the ancient writers.

Furthermore, as **Paul of Aegina** <note>a 7th-century Byzantine physician</note> summarized in a few words, a decoction of the root opens obstructed passages, promotes urination, and stimulates menstruation. Since the leaves are **astringent** <note>having a drying or tightening effect on tissues</note>, they are helpful in treating fluid discharges <note>original: *fluxionibus*</note>.

<summary>This page distinguishes Baccharis from Asarum by comparing classical descriptions and settles botanical nomenclature through the works of Crateuas, Pliny, and Virgil. It also details the medicinal uses of the plant, such as treating obstructions and acting as an astringent.</summary>
<keywords>Baccharis, Asarum, Crateuas, Virgil, ancient botany, herbal medicine, garlands, fragrant plants, Paul of Aegina, pharmacological history</keywords>

# ON COSTMARY. CHAPTER LXXXIX.
<note>original: *DE BALSAMITA*</note>

**COSTMARY** <note>original: *Balsamita*; likely *Tanacetum balsamita*, also known as Alecost</note> **is not a herb used for garlands, but the scent of its leaves is pleasant, though somewhat heavy. There are two kinds, differing greatly in their leaves: namely, the Greater and the Lesser.**

<margin>1</margin> **THE GREATER** *kind rises with stalks that are round, hard, and a foot and a half high. Its leaves are oblong, wide, and lightly notched along the edges, being very similar to the leaves of* **Dock** <note>original: *Lapathi*</note>, *yet smaller and of a whiter green. The flower-head is golden in color, made of many small blossoms like clusters* <note>original: *corymbis*</note> *clinging together in an umbel* <note>an umbrella-like flower cluster</note> *in the manner of* **Tansy** <note>original: *Tanaceti*</note> *and* **Everlasting** <note>original: *Elichrysi*</note>; *each of these is composed of many tiny yellow filaments packed together. The roots are woody and durable in nature, with many clinging fibers. Among the* **Belgians** <note>referring to the people of the Low Countries</note> *it is a garden plant and is also cultivated everywhere by greengrocers. In the summer months, it puts forth its golden, umbrella-shaped flower-heads.*

**THE GERMANS** *call it* **Ladies' Herb** <note>original: *Frauwenkraut*</note>; *the Belgians call it* **Balleme**; *and their apothecaries name it* **Balsamita**, *or* **Greater Balsamita**. *There is, however, another plant called* **Balsamina**, *which is also called* **Charantia** *and* **Momordica** <note>the Balsam Apple or Bitter Melon</note>; *likewise* **Balsaminum** *in the neuter gender; these herbs are different and unlike this Costmary. **Mattioli** <note>a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> calls it **Greek Mint**; others call it **Roman Sage**; likewise **Lassulata herb**, **Garden Costmary**, **Saracen Mint**, or **Our Lady’s Herb**. There are those who believe it to be **Chiron’s All-heal** <note>original: *Panaces Chironium*</note>. For **Pliny** and the common copies of **Theophrastus** report that **Chiron’s All-heal** has a leaf like **Dock**, and a golden flower, which is the nature of this herb. But **Dioscorides**, and the interpreter of **Nicander** citing **Theophrastus** as a witness, say that its leaf is like **Marjoram** <note>original: *Amaraci*</note>; the leaf of which is not at all similar to Costmary.*

<margin>2</margin> **THE LESSER** **Costmary resembles the greater in color, scent, taste, and in its golden umbrella-like flower-heads. It puts forth numerous stalks that are thin and round. The leaves are small, oblong, saw-toothed, and deeply notched.**

<summary>This page describes the Greater and Lesser varieties of Costmary (Balsamita), detailing its physical characteristics, its cultivation in the Low Countries, and the various names attributed to it by Renaissance and ancient botanists.</summary>

<keywords>Costmary, Balsamita, Tansy, Mattioli, Chiron’s All-heal, botany, medicinal herbs, Renaissance gardening</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical survey of aromatic herbs, moving from the analysis of ancient Greek texts on Baccharis to the practical and descriptive identification of Costmary. It establishes the distinction between common garden herbs and the more exotic "Balsams" of the East.</meta>

# ON COSTMARY. CHAPTER LXXXIX.
<note>original title: *DE BALSAMITA*</note>

**COSTMARY** <note>original: *Balsamita*; identified today as *Tanacetum balsamita*, also known as Alecost due to its historical use in flavoring ale</note> **is not a herb used for garlands, but the scent of its leaves is pleasant, though somewhat heavy. There are two kinds, differing greatly in their leaves: namely, the Greater and the Lesser.**

<margin>1</margin> **THE GREATER** *kind rises with stalks that are round, hard, and a foot and a half high. Its leaves are oblong, wide, and lightly notched along the edges, being very similar to the leaves of* **Dock** <note>original: *Lapathi*; a common field weed of the Rumex genus</note>, *yet smaller and of a whiter green. The flower-head is golden in color, made of many small blossoms like clusters* <note>original: *corymbis*</note> *clinging together in an umbel* <note>an umbrella-like flower cluster where stalks spread from a common center</note> *in the manner of* **Tansy** <note>original: *Tanaceti*</note> *and* **Everlasting** <note>original: *Elichrysi*; a flower known for retaining its color when dried</note>; *each of these is composed of many tiny yellow filaments packed together. The roots are woody and durable in nature, with many clinging fibers. Among the* **Belgians** <note>referring to the inhabitants of the Low Countries or Netherlands in the early modern period</note> *it is a garden plant and is also cultivated everywhere by greengrocers. In the summer months, it puts forth its golden, umbrella-shaped flower-heads.*

**THE GERMANS** *call it* **Ladies' Herb** <note>original: *Frauwenkraut*; likely referencing the Virgin Mary, hence the English name "Costmary" or "Alecost"</note>; *the Belgians call it* **Balleme**; *and their apothecaries* <note>early pharmacists</note> *name it* **Balsamita**, *or* **Greater Balsamita**. *There is, however, another plant called* **Balsamina** <note>original: *Balsamina*</note>, *which is also called* **Charantia** *and* **Momordica** <note>the Balsam Apple or Bitter Melon, a climbing vine unrelated to Costmary</note>; *likewise* **Balsaminum** <note>original: *Balsaminum*</note> *in the neuter gender, which refers to the fragrant resinous oil of the true Balsam tree of the East, a substance far more precious than this garden herb.*

<summary>This page describes the Greater Costmary (Balsamita Maior), detailing its physical appearance, its golden umbrella-shaped flowers, and its common names across Europe. The author also clarifies the botanical distinction between this common herb and the exotic Balsam tree or the Balsam Apple.</summary>

<keywords>Costmary, Balsamita, Alecost, Ladies' Herb, botanical description, 16th-century gardening, Tansy, aromatic herbs</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical entry for Costmary from the previous page, finishing the cultural names for the "Greater" variety before providing the visual and descriptive identification for the "Lesser" variety (Balsamita Minor).</meta>

...hence it is also dedicated to her and called **Saint Mary’s Herb**.

# LESSER COSTMARY.
<note>original: *BALSAMITA MINOR*</note>

[An illustration of the plant is positioned here]
<note>The woodcut displays several upright, leafy stems growing from a dense, fibrous root system. The leaves are spear-shaped with clearly defined, jagged edges, and the stems end in clusters of small, button-like flower heads.</note>

<margin>2</margin> **THE LESSER** *kind of Costmary is smaller in all its parts than the variety previously described, though it shares a similar pleasant and aromatic scent. It rises with slender, upright stalks. The leaves are oblong and narrow, possessing a* **serrated** <term>serrated: having a jagged edge like the teeth of a saw</term> *margin that is more deeply notched than the Greater kind. The roots are composed of many thick, tangled fibers. At the summits of the branches, the flowers appear as small, golden-yellow buttons, gathered together in clusters* <note>original: *corymbis*</note> *that form a flat-topped arrangement* <note>resembling an umbrella-like shape called an umbel</note>*. This plant is likewise found in gardens and is valued for its sweet fragrance, which some compare to a mixture of mint and balsam.*

<summary>This page provides the botanical description and illustration of Lesser Costmary (Balsamita Minor), distinguishing it from the Greater variety by its smaller size and deeply jagged leaf edges.</summary>
<keywords>Lesser Costmary, Balsamita Minor, botanical illustration, serrated leaves, aromatic herbs, garden plants</keywords>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of the Lesser Costmary (Balsamita Minor), specifically focusing on its appearance, the confusion regarding its name among historical authorities, and its medicinal applications. It follows the visual description provided on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>289</page-num>
<header>ABOUT COSTMARY.</header>

notched; it has a golden-colored head <note>original: *comam aurei coloris*</note>, but smaller clusters <note>original: *corymbos*</note>: the roots are like those of the previous kind. This variety is rarer, and is found nowhere else except among those who are students of the knowledge of herbs. It blooms at the same time as the preceding variety. Costmary <note>original: *Balsamita*</note> is, without doubt, the smaller species.

The **ITALIANS** call it *Giula*: Valerius Cordus calls it the **Lesser Cluster-bearing Mint** <note>original: *Mentham Corymbiferam minorem*</note>: but the previous one he calls the **Greater Cluster-bearing Mint**. Most believe it to be the *Eupatorium* of Mesue <note>Mesue the Younger was a mysterious 11th-century author of influential Arabic medical texts</note>; others wish it to be the *Ageraton* of Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*</note>. However, Mesue’s *Eupatorium* is the same as the *Eupatorium* of Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (Avicenna), the famous 11th-century Persian polymath</note>: for the medicinal powers are the same in both; but the description of the form in both authors is very corrupted: as is clearly shown by the copies of Avicenna, whose later versions—based on the correction of Bellunensis <note>Andrea Alpago of Belluno, an Italian physician who re-translated Avicenna from Arabic into Latin in the early 1500s</note>—differ greatly from the earlier ones in this passage. Furthermore, the *Eupatorium* of Avicenna does not differ at all from the *Eupatorium* of Dioscorides; that herb is very well known and is commonly called **Agrimony**, with which the **Lesser Costmary**, or *Giula*, has little or no resemblance. Likewise, it is not likely that this herb is *Ageraton*. For Dioscorides attributes the leaves of **Oregano** <note>original: *Origani*</note> to *Ageraton*: but the leaf of Oregano is somewhat round; while the leaf of this plant is long and **serrated** <term>serrated: having a jagged edge like the teeth of a saw</term>, differing much from the form of Oregano; this shows that *Giula* and *Ageraton* are different plants.

**FURTHERMORE**, both kinds of Costmary are hot and dry in the second degree <note>referring to the Galenic system of grading the intensity of a plant's "humoral" qualities from 1 to 4</note>, with a bitterness and a clearly visible astringent quality. A conserve <note>original: *Conferuam*</note> made of its leaves helps with long-lasting headaches when taken in the morning: it suppresses and dries out discharges <note>original: *defluxiones*; catarrhs or "runny" humors</note> from the head: and it brings raw humors to **concoction** <term>concoction: the "ripening" or processing of bodily fluids into a healthy state</term>. It is likewise beneficial to those suffering from **Cachexia** <term>cachexia: a state of general ill health and wasting of the body</term> or **Leucophlegmatia** <term>leucophlegmatia: a type of dropsy or edema characterized by pale, phlegmatic swelling</term>, especially at the onset; and for all those whose liver is weak and chilled. The seed, not unlike that of **Lavender Cotton** <note>original: *Santolinae*</note>, expels roundworms and intestinal worms.

->T<-

# TO THE READER

## EPILOGUE.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'Q' containing a heraldic shield. The shield features a plant branch with three leaves, surmounted by a cross. Below the shield, the date '1563' is inscribed. The letter and central emblem are surrounded by intricate floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

SINCE it was our purpose, friend Reader, to arrange this history of plants <note>original: *stirpiū* (stirpium)</note> that we have conceived not in alphabetical order <note>original: *elementorum serie*—literally "by the series of elements," meaning alphabetically</note>, but by another method—namely, to organize them by genera—we have striven with all zeal and diligence to distribute the plants themselves into certain very general categories and to divide them by their most common differences. We have arranged these divisions into several books, gathering in each book not only one genus, but several similar genera, each in its own proper order. In one book, for instance, it was our care to join together flowers and garland-herbs <note>herbs used to weave wreaths or crowns</note>, fragrant plants, and also the <term>umbellifers</term> <note>plants with flower clusters that spread from a common point like the ribs of an umbrella, such as carrots or fennel</note>. In another, we gathered roots that are <term>purgative</term> <note>original: *καθαρτικὰς* (kathartikas); used to cleanse the bowels</note> or endowed with the power of purging, as well as those having an extreme quality, such as poisonous and sleep-inducing plants, and several others. In the history of grains <note>original: *frugum*</note>, besides cereals and legumes, we have included the blights of grains, as well as marsh and aquatic plants. The next place was left for garden vegetables <note>original: *oleraceis*</note>, and the latter part for trees and shrubs. A place was also granted to the unorganized multitude of plants which could not conveniently be referred to those primary categories.

This history, having been thus arranged, was formerly published in the native tongue of the people of Brabant <note>referring to Flemish or Dutch, Dodoens’ native language</note>, and also in the French...

<summary>Rembert Dodoens explains his botanical classification system, which organizes plants by their physical characteristics and medicinal uses rather than alphabetically. He details how he grouped flowers, purgatives, grains, and vegetables into distinct "books" within his work.</summary>
<keywords>botanical classification, taxonomy, Rembert Dodoens, medicinal herbs, umbellifers, purgatives, 16th-century botany, Flemish language</keywords>

<header>->TO THE READER.<-</header>
<sig>T 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: other [original: alia]</meta>

...published in the French language <note>original: *Gallico idiomate*</note>, it has long been my intention to provide it written in Latin; but while pressing business delayed my attempts, and it was not permitted to complete the work in its entirety at once, the next best thing was to present it piece by piece during my leisure time; as I wrote more extensively in the preface to the history of grains, legumes, etc.

AND SO now we share for your reading not the whole work, but once again a part of it—namely, the history of flowers, and of garland-making <note>original: *coronariarum*; plants used for weaving wreaths and crowns</note> and some sweet-smelling herbs. In this part, we have gathered nearly all flowers indeed—those accepted into crowns and wreaths, as well as the others which are sown and cultivated in gardens for the sake of the grace with which they please—with only a very few exceptions.

Now, I call "flowers" those plants <note>original: *stirpes*</note> which are commended and welcome on account of their blossoms: just as Theophrastus <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and student of Aristotle, often called the "father of botany" (c. 371 – c. 287 BC)</note> calls "roots" those whose powers are chiefly in their roots, but "herbs" those having them in their leaves.

However, only those flowers have been omitted and passed over for now which seemed to belong to other types of plants: such as what our people call the Menapian Rose <note>Named for the Menapii, a Belgic tribe of the Roman era; this refers to the Guelder-rose or Snowball bush (*Viburnum opulus*)</note>, or the Rose of Guelders; the <term>Rhododendron</term> <note>from the Greek for "rose-tree"</note>, the late-blooming or Winter Rose, and many such which are more appropriately categorized with the similar plants of which they are species.

For the Menapian Rose is a species of Water or Marsh Elder <note>original: *Sambuci aquatici siue palustris*</note>, with multiple flowers gathered into a globe the size of a large apple, from...

<header>EPILOGUE TO THE READER.</header>

...differing only from the other common one <note>original: *alia vulgari*</note> in that it produces flowers in umbels <note>flat-topped or rounded flower clusters where the stalks spring from a single point</note>. <term>Valerius Cordus</term> <note>A famous German botanist (1515–1544) known for his descriptions of plants from nature</note> calls this shrub **Lycostaphylum** <note>original: *Lycostaphylum*, literally "wolf-grape"</note>, and he describes both species elegantly: he calls the one whose flowers are in umbels the "female," and the "male" the one with multiple small flowers which is sterile.

**RHODODENDRON** is a similar shrub, which I have nowhere happened to observe numbered among the flowers <note>The author likely means he has not seen it used in gardens or for wreaths in the same way as other plants in this volume</note>.

**WINTER ROSE**, or rather the **Autumn Rose**, is a cultivated species of **Mallow** <note>original: *Maluae*</note>, which belongs to the category of potherbs <note>original: *oleracea*; plants grown for the kitchen or as vegetables</note>.

<margin>Anemone.</margin>
**ANEMONES** have been passed over for a similar reason; for they are of the genus of "sharp" or acrid herbs, not much unlike **Buttercups** <note>original: *Ranunculis*</note>. Nevertheless, we have added an image of one here—specifically the cultivated or garden variety named by <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD whose 'De Materia Medica' was the primary botanical text for centuries</note>. <term>Matthiolus</term> <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, an influential 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> depicts three or four cultivated types with roots resembling standing olives, and many leaves divided into many parts. But this one of ours has very few leaves, similar to **Coriander** and a certain **Marsh Buttercup**; the flowers, however, are a beautiful purple-red, as if diluted, and most pleasing to the sight. The root of a one-year-old plant is similar to an olive lying on its side and is somewhat jointed; an older root, however, is entirely knotty, appearing as if gathered from many such olives. This plant grows in Germany in several places around the Rhine. It blooms not in early Spring, but later. <term>Theophrastus</term> <note>The "Father of Botany" (c. 371 – c. 287 BC)</note> calls the wild species of it *leimōnian* <note>original Greek: λειμώνιαν</note>, that is, "of the meadow" or "marshy." This variety blooms immediately in early Spring. <term>Fuchsius</term> <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a German physician and one of the three "German Fathers of Botany"</note> once depicted it as a species of **Buttercup**. There are those who [call it] a **Reed-Buttercup**...

# ANEMONE.

<margin>Anemone <note>The name is derived from the Greek word for "wind," hence it is often called the Windflower.</note></margin>

The drawing depicts the anatomy of the plant in various stages of development. At the base, it features thick, knobby tuberous roots <note>original: *radix tuberosa*; a thickened underground part of a stem or rhizome used by the plant for food storage</note> with fine rootlets extending downwards. The basal leaves are shown with deeply divided, palmate <note>shaped like an open palm with lobes spreading from a central point</note> lobes.

Three tall, slender stems rise from the root system: 
* The leftmost stem bears a flower in profile with its petals half-open.
* The central stem supports a large, fully open flower with numerous narrow, pointed petals arranged in a star shape.
* The rightmost stem ends in a closed, conical flower bud with a scaly texture.

### EPILOGUE TO THE READER

...they may call it. Its flowers are either yellow, white, or purple.

While we were preparing the edition of this history, look! Images of two very rare plants pertaining to this part of the work were given to us: namely the *Peruvian Chrysanthemum* <note>original: *Chrysanthemi Peruniani*; this refers to the Sunflower (*Helianthus annuus*), which was then a new arrival from the Americas and often called "Chrysanthemum of Peru" by early botanists</note> and the *Marsh Asphodel*. The image of the *Peruvian Chrysanthemum* was shared with us by the most upright and honorable lady *Christina Bertolf*, sent to her from Spain by her most dear husband, the most famous and distinguished man Mr. *Joachim Hopper* <note>A Dutch jurist and professor who served as a counselor to King Philip II of Spain</note>, counselor to the King. As for the *Marsh Asphodel*, it was sent by the most decorated and noble Mr. *Charles of Saint-Omer, Lord of Dranoutre* <note>A prominent 16th-century patron of botany and a close friend of the botanist Carolus Clusius</note>, a man not only most studious but also most skilled in the history of plants and all animals. We thought both should be added to this book as a finishing touch <note>original: *coronidis vice*; "coronis" refers to the flourish or decorative scroll at the end of a book or section</note>. For it seemed that the image of a flower of such celebrated and admirable size, or of such an exotic *Asphodel*, should by no means be omitted.

On the same occasion, we also joined the images of several other very rare plants not previously published: such as *Cirsium* <note>A type of thistle</note>, the *Azure Bellflower* <note>original: *Campanæ Lazuræ*; likely a species of *Campanula* or Morning Glory</note> as it is called by more recent authors, *Indian Millet* <note>original: *Panici Indici*; possibly Job's Tears or a variety of Sorghum</note>, and a certain species of *Panax* <note>An "All-heal" or medicinal herb</note>. Even though these plants do not belong to this specific history of flowers, it cannot be an inconvenience that they are brought to your attention here. For to increase the knowledge of plants is not only beautiful and pleasant, but also useful and honorable.
