->### PERUVIAN SUNFLOWER<-
<note>original: *Chrysanth[emum] Perunianum*; literally "Peruvian Gold-flower." This is an early botanical identification of the Sunflower (*Helianthus annuus*). In the 16th century, many plants arriving from the Americas were broadly attributed to "Peru" or "Mexico," and the sunflower's resemblance to a giant daisy led to its classification as a *Chrysanthemum* or "gold-flower."</note>

[A detailed botanical woodcut illustration of a sunflower plant, shown in its entirety from the fibrous roots to the flowering head. The plant features a thick, textured, upright stem. Large, heart-shaped leaves with prominent veins and serrated margins are arranged alternatingly along the stalk. At the summit, the large flower head is turned slightly, showcasing the dense central disc of florets surrounded by a vibrant fringe of ray petals. A small bud is tucked behind the primary blossom, indicating the plant's growth habit.]

<summary>This page presents one of the first European botanical illustrations of the Sunflower, labeled here as the "Peruvian Chrysanthemum." It serves as a visual record of a then-exotic species recently introduced to Europe from the Americas.</summary>

# EPILOGUE

<margin>
The Peruvian Sunflower.
</margin>

The <term>Peruvian Sunflower</term> <note>original: *Chrysanthemum Perunianum*; literally "Peruvian Gold-flower." At the time, botanists categorized the sunflower as a type of giant chrysanthemum or daisy.</note> is indeed an annual, but it is an exceptionally tall plant; its flower is beautiful and of an unusual size. It is said to have been found in Peru and in certain other provinces of America. When sown at Madrid in Spain, in the Royal Garden, it grew to a height of 24 feet. It puts forth a straight stem as thick as an arm, with very broad leaves, and a flower somewhat similar in shape to the <term>Chrysanthemum</term> <note>literally "gold-flower," referring to common European daisies like the corn marigold</note>, but much larger. Indeed, its central disk or orb exceeded a foot in width by two or three inches. The individual petals <note>original: *folia*, literally "leaves," often used in this period to refer to flower petals</note> surrounding that orb in a circle are somewhat similar to the flower of the great purple Lily, but larger and of a golden-yellow color. They call it the **Indian Sun**, because it appears to radiate in the manner of the Sun.

We saw this plant in the most delightful garden—filled with various kinds of plants—belonging to the magnificent and most noble Master Jean de Brancion <note>Latin: *Ioannis Brancionis*; a wealthy official in Mechelen and a famous patron of botany who supplied many specimens to early naturalists</note>, a man most devoted to the study of plant varieties. Through his generosity and kindness, many flowers were added to this history that otherwise would not have been included, as it would have been pointless to seek them anywhere else but in his gardens. In his garden, I say, the Peruvian Sunflower plant was seen, but it grew only to 10 or 11 feet. The leaves were somewhat rough and much smaller than those that grew in Spain. Only the beginning of a flower appeared, and that with winter approaching, for which reason it could not reach perfection. 

However, that same plant in Padua, Italy, grew much taller than in Spain; for it grew to 40 feet, or 120 geometric spans <note>A "span" (*palmus*) was a unit of measurement based on the width of a hand. While 40 feet sounds extraordinary for a sunflower, early accounts often emphasized the "gigantism" of New World species.</note>. It did indeed complete its flower, but did not bring its seed to maturity, as [was reported] by...

### TO THE READER

We received [these details] from the most excellent man, **Giacomo Antonio Cortuso**, a nobleman of Padua, who is most practiced and learned in the arena of <term>simples</term> <note>original: *simplicium*; 'simples' refers to medicinal plants used individually, as opposed to compound medicines</note>. It was in his most delightful garden that the plant grew. He also took the greatest care to understand its properties. For he discovered through clear experiment that the tender stalks of the leaves—once the hairs are scraped off, boiled on a small grill, and prepared beforehand with salt and oil—are sweet and very pleasant to eat. He found the same to be true of the calyxes <note>the green, leaf-like base of the flower head</note> in which the petals and the seeds sit. He writes that these are more delicate and sweeter as food than the cones or heads of the **Artichoke** <note>original: *Cinarae*</note>; and not less, but rather more effective, <term>for aphrodisiac purposes</term> <note>original: *πρὸς τὰ ἀφροδίσια* (pros ta aphrodisia); Greek for matters of sexual desire or potency</note>, for which they are very powerful.

In this matter, his industry and diligence cannot be praised or commended enough. He is certainly worthy of being revered and honored by all learned men and those devoted to the study of medicinal plants—both because of the diligence and industry he employs in investigating the properties of rare and exotic simples, and especially because he nobly endeavors to assist the efforts of the many people working in this part of Medicine. Indeed, he frequently assists them as much as possible and greatly advances their studies. This is most well-known, for example, from all the commentaries of **Pietro Andrea Mattioli** <note>a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note>, which took on their greatest growth and no small splendor through the work of Cortuso himself, as Mattioli himself left candidly attested in many places. 

The same could also have been attested by our own history, if it had been permitted to finish and bring them to a conclusion. For we received from him not a few very rare and exotic specimens, which in the remaining parts, by which...

<keywords>Giacomo Antonio Cortuso, sunflower edibility, artichoke comparison, aphrodisiacs, Pietro Andrea Mattioli, botanical history, medicinal plants</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion from page 297, specifically moving from the culinary uses of the Peruvian Sunflower toward its identification in classical texts, followed by a description of the Marsh Asphodel.</meta>

->### EPILOGUE TO THE READER<-

...[if these observations] are preserved, they will contribute no small addition, if at any time there shall be leisure to complete them. But let the discourse, wandering off, return to the **Peruvian Chrysanthemum** <note>The Sunflower (*Helianthus annuus*)</note>. This flower might be thought to be **Pliny’s** *Bellio*, if it were credible that at that time in Rome it was either seen or in any way recognized; for it is yellow around the circumference, and its central disk is surrounded by more than fifty leaves <note>petals</note>. Indeed, **Pliny** seems to suggest that the *Bellio* is such a thing in Book 21, Chapter 8. "The yellow *Bellio*," he says, "is crowned with fifty-five spreading little barbs <note>original: *barbulis*; referring to the ray florets or petals</note>." But it is more likely that the *Bellio* is entirely different and smaller, and perhaps the same as the **Daisy** <note>original: *Bellide*</note>; as not a few learned men suspect, who judge this passage of Pliny to be corrupt.

<margin>
Marsh Asphodel.
</margin>

The **Marsh Asphodel** <note>likely *Narthecium ossifragum*, the Bog Asphodel</note> produces leaves that are a beautiful green, somewhat striated, similar to **Iris** or **Gladiolus**, but narrow and shorter by a <term>span</term> <note>original: *dodrante*; a classical measurement of about nine inches</note>. It has a straight stem a foot long, quite similar to the **Asphodel**; the flowers consist of six small petals, with tiny stamens in the middle, just like the **Asphodel**, yet smaller, with a color fading from yellow to pale. The seed is very small, contained in oblong, pointed vessels. The root is long and jointed; not without fibers, it creeps in the manner of **Couch Grass** <note>original: *Graminis*; referring to the spreading nature of grass rhizomes</note>. This kind of Asphodel is found in damp and marshy places in the <term>heaths</term> <note>original: *Ericetis*; uncultivated open lands</note>, not far from **Bruges in Flanders**. It flowers in the summer months. It is commonly called "the nameless one" <note>original: *Anonymos*</note>. That it is a species of **Asphodel** is shown by the stem and flowers, which are similar to the **Anthericum** <note>a genus of plants in the subfamily Agavoideae</note>; although the leaves are like those of a **Gladiolus** or **Iris**.

<meta>catchword: CIR- (referring to the next page's likely first word, possibly *Circa* or *Circulus*)</meta>

<summary>The author concludes a discussion on the Peruvian Sunflower by refuting its identification with Pliny's "Bellio" and provides a detailed botanical description of the Marsh Asphodel found in Flanders.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific descriptions of marsh plants to an epilogue or supplementary section addressed to the reader. It introduces two plants foreign to the Low Countries: the Plume Thistle (Cirsion) and the Azure Bell (Morning Glory), contrasting their features with those described by other contemporary botanists like Mattioli.</meta>

### EPILOGUE TO THE READER

<margin>Plume Thistle</margin>
The **Plume Thistle** <note>original: *Cirsion*; likely a species of Plume Thistle such as *Cirsium heterophyllum*</note> is of the genus of **Thistles** <note>original: *Carduorum*</note>: it has oblong leaves, somewhat similar to the leaves of **Lettuce** <note>original: *Lactucæ*</note>: but they are pointed, whiter than the common **Bugloss** <note>original: *Buglossi*; an herb often called Oxtongue</note>, prickly along the edges and somewhat notched. It has straight stalks of two **cubits** <note>a cubit is an ancient unit of length, approximately 18 inches, based on the length of a forearm</note> divided into several branches, upon each of which stands a prickly head. After the flower has withered, this head reflects downward and is finally resolved into downy fluff. The flower is made of many stamens, beautiful and a reddish-purple color. The seed is oblong and shining, similar to other thistles, but smaller than many. The root is oblong, divided into two or three parts. It is a stranger to **Belgium** <note>original: *Belgio*; here referring generally to the Low Countries</note> and has been brought into gardens from elsewhere. It is said to grow in some places near the sea. It flowers in the summer time, in the second year after it is sown. **Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist</note> also depicts a certain kind of *Cirsion*, with leaves not much different from this one, but with flowers crowded together in many small heads; quite different from this plant, whose flowers, as we have said, are solitary and emerge from larger heads.

<margin>Azure Bell</margin>
**AZURE BELL** <note>original: *Campana Lazura*; identified today as a species of Morning Glory, likely *Ipomoea nil*</note> is a species of **Bindweed** <note>original: *Conuoluuli*</note>, a stranger to Belgium and many other provinces. Its leaves are broad and angular, larger than the old, angular leaves of **Ivy**, not smooth but somewhat hairy. The little stalks are tender, and by winding themselves around nearby stakes and poles, they climb upward. The flowers are shaped like a basket, almost similar to the **Smooth Bindweed** <note>original: *Smilacis læuis*</note>, but angular along the edges and of a purple-blue color. For this reason, many call it the **Blue Bell**. It appears to be a species of **Privet** <note>original: *Ligustri*</note>—not the shrub, but another kind which some of the ancients mentioned.

<meta>catchword: ...cies.</meta>

This plant, though often grouped with common weeds, is distinguished by its height and the delicate nature of its purple-red flowers which eventually turn to soft down. It remains a rare sight in our local landscapes, found primarily where it has been carefully cultivated in the gardens of the learned.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Azure Bell" (Morning Glory) from the previous page, specifically debating its identity in classical literature before moving on to a description of Indian Millet.</meta>

EPILOGUE TO THE READER.

...[spe]cies. For there are two kinds of this <term>Privet</term><note>original: *Ligustrum*. While modern *Ligustrum* refers to the privet hedge, Renaissance botanists often debated if the classical *Ligustrum* was actually a type of Bindweed or Morning Glory due to its white flowers.</note>: one with a white flower, the other with a black one.

Virgil mentions the white Privet in his second Eclogue, with this verse:

> *The white Privets fall, while the dark Hyacinths are gathered.* <note>original: *Alba Ligustra cadunt, Vaccinia nigra leguntur.*</note>

Likewise, Martial in the first book of his *Epigrams*, writing to Procillus:

> *A certain girl desires me, envious Procillus,*
> *she who is whiter than a whole swan,*
> *than silver, snow, lily, or privet.* <note>original: *Quædam me cupit inuide Procille / Toto candidior puella Cygno, / Argento, niue, Lilio, Ligustro.*</note>

Columella truly made mention of the black variety in book 10, "On Gardens":

> *Bring violets in baskets, and weave balsams*
> *mingled with black privet and cassia.* <note>original: *Fer calathis Violam, & nigro permixta Ligustro / Balsama cum Caſia nectens.*</note>

However, this **BLACK** Privet is thought to be the "Azure" or "Blue Bell" <note>likely the *Ipomoea* or Morning Glory discussed previously</note>. For a blue-purple color is even sometimes called "black," just as with "black violets." The white Privet, on the other hand, is the flower of this kind of smooth **Bindweed** <note>original: *Smilacis læuis*; likely *Convolvulus*</note>; it is of little value and perishes because no one gathers it, which is the reason Virgil writes that it "falls."

<margin>Indian Millet.</margin>
No less, and indeed more exotic, is what is called **Indian Millet** <note>original: *Panicum Indicum*. Based on the height and description, this likely refers to Sorghum or perhaps a variety of Maize, which were often confused in early botanical texts.</note>. It produces thick stalks eight or nine feet high, which are frequently jointed; the leaves from each node are reed-like, large, and wide. It bears thick spikes—or more accurately, panicles—that are densely packed, shorter than legitimate Millet, but nonetheless thicker. These do not only emerge from the top, but sit upon shorter and thinner stalks emerging from the "bosoms" <note>axils</note> of the leaves. The oblong seed is wrapped in hair-like husks; the roots, as in similar plants, are fibrous.

<summary>This page concludes a philological argument identifying classical "Privet" with Bindweed and Morning Glory varieties before describing the physical characteristics of "Indian Millet" (Sorghum or Maize).</summary>
<keywords>Privet, Ligustrum, Bindweed, Smilax, Indian Millet, Panicum Indicum, Virgil, Martial, Columella, botanical identification, Renaissance botany</keywords>

# EPILOGUE TO THE READER
<note>original: *EPILOG. AD LECT.* This header suggests a concluding section, though the text continues botanical descriptions.</note>

...fibrous and firm. Toward the end of summer, even if it has been burning and hot, the seed hardly reaches maturity in Belgium; in warmer regions it fares better. The dense, compact panicle <note>a loose, branching cluster of flowers</note> itself shows it to be a species of Millet.

This species of **All-heal** <note>original: *Panax*. This refers to a group of plants historically believed to have universal healing properties; the name is the root of the word "panacea."</note> produces leaves that are somewhat hairy and rough, and those closest to the ground are large and multiple, and divided into several other parts; however, around the stalks they are more contracted and fewer. The individual parts from which the composition of the whole leaf is made are oblong, somewhat similar to the leaves of **dock** <note>original: *lapathum*, a common weed in the buckwheat family</note>, but yet smaller. The stalk, which is three or four **cubits** <note>a cubit is roughly 18 inches (45 cm), making this plant 4.5 to 6 feet tall</note> high, is divided above into branch-like growths. The flowers, which stand out above in **umbels** <note>umbrella-like flower clusters</note>, are yellow; the seed from these is broad and yellowish. The root is long and white, having joints like knees in certain places. It flowered in the gardens of Belgium in July and August. It seems clear that this is a species of All-heal; for we have learned from the most faithful and diligent apothecary of Antwerp, **Peter Coldenberg**, that the seed from which it first grew here was found in the "tear" of All-heal—which is called **Opoponax** <note>a gum resin used in traditional medicine, harvested as "tears" or sap droplets from the plant</note>.

Moreover, according to **Theophrastus** <note>a Greek philosopher often called the "Father of Botany" (c. 371–287 BCE)</note> in book 9, All-heal is manifold. A certain kind grows in Syria; then there are also three others: the **Chironian**, the **Heraclean**, and the **Asclepian**, which are described only by **Dioscorides** <note>a Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica* (c. 40–90 CE)</note>. Besides these there is also one **broad-leaved** <note>original: *πλατύφυλλον* (platyphyllon)</note> and another **thin-leaved** <note>original: *λεπτόφυλλον* (leptophyllon)</note>; so that in total six species of All-heal are recorded by Theophrastus. Now, the present plant seems to agree with the first of these or with the Chironian, while with the rest...

### SPECIES OF ALL-HEAL
<note>original: *PANACIS SPECIES*</note>

# EPILOGUE TO THE READER
<note>original: *EPILOG. AD LECT.* This heading marks a concluding thought on the previous subject before the botanical description of the next plant begins.</note>

...fibrous and firm. Toward the end of summer, even if it has been burning and hot, the seed hardly reaches maturity in **the Low Countries** <note>original: *Belgio*; at this time, the term referred broadly to the region of modern-day Belgium and the Netherlands</note>; in warmer regions it fares better. The dense, compact **panicle** <term>panicle: a loose, branching cluster of flowers, often seen in grasses</term> itself shows it to be a species of Millet.

This species of **All-heal** <note>original: *Panax*. Derived from the Greek *pan* (all) and *akos* (remedy), this refers to a group of plants—including what we now know as Ginseng or Lovage—historically believed to have universal healing properties; it is the root of our word "panacea."</note> produces leaves that are somewhat hairy and rough, and those closest to the ground are large and multiple, and divided into several other parts; however, around the stalks they are more contracted and fewer. The individual parts from which the composition of the whole leaf is made are oblong, somewhat similar to the leaves of **dock** <note>original: *lapathum*, a common hardy weed in the buckwheat family</note>, but yet smaller. 

The stalk, which is three or four **cubits** <note>a cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18 inches (45 cm), making this plant 4.5 to 6 feet tall</note> high, is divided above into branch-like growths. The flowers, which stand out above in **umbels** <note>flat-topped or rounded umbrella-like flower clusters</note>, are yellow; the seed from these is broad and yellowish. The root is long and white, having joints like knees in certain places. It flowered in the gardens of the Low Countries in July and August. It seems clear that this is a species of All-heal; for we have learned from the most faithful and diligent apothecary of Antwerp, **Peter Coldenberg** <note>a notable 16th-century pharmacist and botanist who maintained a famous botanical garden in Antwerp</note>, that the seed ...

<keywords>All-heal, Panax, Millet, botany, Peter Coldenberg, Antwerp, medicinal plants, 16th-century gardening</keywords>

--- Page 311 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical investigation into the genus *Panax* (All-heal), moving from a physical description of the author's specimen to a scholarly comparison with classical authorities. The author is attempting to identify which specific species of All-heal he is observing by cross-referencing the works of Pliny the Elder, Theophrastus, and Dioscorides.</meta>

<page-num>307</page-num>
<header>EPILOGUE TO THE READER</header>
<sig>V 2</sig>

...but which is it? Certainly not the **Heraclean** <note>original: *Heraclium*; referring to the "All-heal of Hercules," often identified as a species of *Heracleum* or Hogweed</note>, since that has a broad, large leaf approaching that of a fig tree. Nor is it the **Asclepian** <note>original: *Asclepium*; "All-heal of Asclepius," often identified as a type of *Hardy Ferula*</note>, which is like **Thapsia** <term>Thapsia: a genus of plants in the carrot family, sometimes called "deadly carrots" for their toxic properties</term>, or as Dioscorides says, similar to fennel. This plant of ours, however, is neither of this form nor of that, but of an entirely different shape, resembling neither the fig, nor Thapsia, nor fennel. Again, the broad-leaved **All-heal** <note>original: *Panaces*</note> is thought to be **Great Centaury** <note>original: *Centaurium magnum*</note>, and the thin-leaved is not rashly considered to be **Lesser Centaury**. Indeed, according to the testimony of **Pliny** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), a Roman author and naturalist</note>, the All-heal found by **Chiron** <note>In Greek mythology, the centaur Chiron was a master of medicine and herbalism</note> is named Centaury. And **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC), the Greek "Father of Botany"</note> makes no mention of a Greater or Lesser Centaury. From these points it appears that none of these four are this species of All-heal. Which of the remaining two it might be cannot yet be affirmed with certainty, both because of the brevity of the ancient authors and because of their variations. What the **Syrian** type is like, Theophrastus does not describe.
<margin>Pliny’s error</margin>
Pliny, in Book 12, chapter 26, says that the Syrian type has leaves of broad size and round shape; but there is a suspicion that these words were taken from the description of the Heraclean species. For in Book 25, chapter 4, he wrongly substituted **Heraclean Oregano** <note>original: *Heracleoticum Origanum*</note> in its place. The copies of Theophrastus and Pliny report that the **Chironian** species <note>original: *Chironium*</note> has the leaf of **Dock** <note>original: *Lapathum*</note>; but those of Dioscorides and the translator of **Nicander** <note>Nicander of Colophon, a 2nd-century BC Greek poet and physician</note> say it is like **Marjoram** <note>original: *Amaracum*</note>. Consider, Reader, and think on which reading should be more approved. If the Chironian type has the leaf of Dock, then this specimen could be Chironian, since we have written that its individual leaves are similar to Dock. But if it is like Marjoram, it certainly will not be; rather, it follows that it is the Syrian type, which seems most likely. For it pours out a **tear** <term>tear: a drop of sap or resin that hardens on the surface of a plant when it is cut</term> if its stalk is wounded in the summer months when the sun is burning. Pliny clearly writes that the Syrian type does this, and Theophrastus hints at it, whereas nowhere is it recorded that the Chironian yields...

<summary>The author compares a specimen of "All-heal" against descriptions by classical botanists, concluding it is likely the Syrian variety because it exudes resin when cut in the summer heat.</summary>
<keywords>botany, All-heal, Panax, Pliny, Theophrastus, Dioscorides, plant identification, resin, Chiron, Centaury</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page concludes the botanical discussion on identifying species of "All-heal" and transitions into the author's formal epilogue. It marks the completion of the "History of Flowers" and includes a contemporary ecclesiastical censorship approval from Antwerp, dating the work to late 1567.</meta>

<page-num>307</page-num>
### EPILOGUE TO THE READER

...is found written. This **tear** <note>original: *lachryma*; a common botanical term for the resin or sap that beads on the surface of a plant</note> is similar in color to **Opoponax** <term>Opoponax: a medicinal gum resin, often called "sweet myrrh," harvested from plants in the carrot family</term>, but in our climate it has no scent or very little, which occurs because of the coldness of the soil. Indeed, in warm regions all things grow more fragrant; but in cold regions, they are produced with less scent, as **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BC) was a Greek philosopher and the primary successor to Aristotle; his works formed the foundation of botanical science for centuries</note> testifies. And so much for these matters.

But as to what has been achieved by us in this **history of flowers** <note>original: *florum historia*</note>, he who is prepared to offer a fair judgment will easily decide. Just as we use few or no words to criticize the writings of others where they might lean less on the truth, so too we do not think we should dwell on the praise of our own late-night labors. Farewell. The day before the Kalends of November <note>October 31st</note>, in the year 1567.

->THE END.<-

> *This book contains nothing contrary to the faith of the Roman Church, or offensive to the Commonwealth.*
>
> *To which I attest, **Sebastian Baer** of Delft, Parish Priest of the distinguished Collegiate Church of Saint Mary in Antwerp.*

<summary>The author concludes his botanical study by noting how climate affects plant fragrance and offers a humble farewell to the reader. The page includes an official ecclesiastical sanction from a priest in Antwerp, confirming the book's orthodoxy.</summary>

--- Page 322 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a botanical index (concluding with the letter Omega on the previous page) to the formal legal front matter of the book. It introduces the "Imperial Privilege," a 16th-century form of copyright protection granted by the Holy Roman Emperor to the printer Christophe Plantin.</meta>

## IMPERIAL PRIVILEGE

**MAXIMILIAN II**, by the favor of Divine mercy, elected Emperor of the Romans, ever August; King of Germany, Hungary, Bohemia, Dalmatia, Croatia, and Slavonia, etc.; Archduke of Austria; Duke of Burgundy, Styria, Carinthia, Carniola, and Württemberg; Count of Tyrol, etc. We recognize and make known to all by the tenor of this document: among the other strategies of our ancestors, we find it was by no means the least that they judged it contributed greatly to the glory and dignity of the Roman Empire to propagate literature no less than military discipline. They recognized that through the monuments of literature, whatever was bravely and nobly achieved would be brought to light, and, being consecrated to eternal memory, would be transmitted to posterity so that it might serve as an example of virtue to both foreign and domestic peoples. They also learned that the study of the finest arts, and whatever had been devised for the cultivation and utility of human life in every art, is best contained and preserved in writing.

Desiring with the greatest zeal to follow in their praised footsteps, we have judged it worthy above all to provide prudently and accurately so that if anyone has contributed anything to that end by their industry, labors, or expenses, this public benefit should not result in a loss to them.

**SINCE**, therefore, our sincerely beloved Christophe Plantin <note>One of the most influential printers of the Renaissance, based in Antwerp.</note>, citizen and printer of Antwerp, has seen to it that it be humbly explained to us that he has resolved to print several ancient authors—some never seen before and rare, others in corrected editions—and other works of great importance. These include, primarily:
*   The Hebrew Bible <note>original: "Biblia Hebraica"; this likely refers to Plantin's massive "Polyglot Bible" project.</note>
*   The New Testament
*   Eunapius on the *Lives of the Philosophers*
*   *On Illustrious Poets*
*   Nonius Marcellus <note>A 4th-century Roman grammarian and lexicographer.</note>
*   Petronius Arbiter
*   All the ancient poets, either corrected or illustrated with commentaries
*   The *Emblems* of Sambucus <note>original: "Emblemata Sambuci"; Johannes Sambucus was a Hungarian scholar whose book of "emblems" (moralizing pictures and poems) was a bestseller.</note>
*   A Hebrew Dictionary
*   A Greek Dictionary
*   Lucius Julius Florus on the *Deeds of the Romans* with the commentary of Jean Stade <note>Jean Stade, or Stadius, was a Flemish mathematician and historian.</note>

...and any others of this kind, whether previously unpublished or made more correct, richer, expanded, and clearer, in whatever language they may be. 

He fears, however, as has frequently been known to happen, that certain others—who are accustomed to gape at and enjoy the fruits of another’s labor and industry like drones <note>original: "fucorum"; a metaphor comparing literary pirates to male bees that do no work but eat the hive's honey.</note>—might soon imitate and forge for their own profit, with less expense and labor, those things which he has collected and printed at great cost and effort. Thus, all his labor...

<summary>This page contains the opening of a legal "Imperial Privilege" granted by Emperor Maximilian II to the printer Christophe Plantin, protecting his upcoming publications from unauthorized copying.</summary>
<keywords>Maximilian II, Christophe Plantin, Imperial Privilege, copyright, Renaissance printing, Antwerp, Hebrew Bible, Jean Stade, Johannes Sambucus</keywords>

--- Page 323 ---
<meta>This page continues the legal decree from Emperor Maximilian II, completing the formal "Imperial Privilege" granted to the printer Christophe Plantin. The previous page established the Emperor's authority and Plantin’s reputation; this page details the specific legal protections, the duration of the copyright (six years), and the penalties for piracy.</meta>

...labors, costs, and expenses, as well as all profit and reward for his work, and even—as the saying goes—his "oil and toil" <note>original: "oleum et opera"; a Latin idiom referring to time and effort spent on a difficult task, like a lamp burning oil late into the night</note> should perish. For this reason, he has humbly petitioned us that we might deign to protect him with the prerogative of our Privilege against the wicked attempts, fraud, and injuries of certain people greedy for profit.

We, graciously granting these fair and just petitions, and desiring to promote the honorable efforts of the aforementioned Christophe Plantin and to look out for his protection from loss as much as possible, wish it to be cautioned and decreed by this public edict that no one—of whatever status, rank, order, or condition they may be, throughout the extent of the borders of the Holy Roman Empire and our dominion—shall henceforth print any of the aforementioned works or any other works of approved authors within the next six years from the first edition of each work or volume. This applies to works never before printed, or those previously printed by others but now expanded and illustrated with new scholia <note>brief explanatory notes or ancient grammatical comments</note>, annotations, or commentaries, which the said Plantin has been the first to strike from his type.

No one may print these in any way using the same or a different typeface, nor import such books printed by others into our and the Empire's borders for sale, nor distribute them in any way, openly or secretly, under penalty of ten Marks of pure gold <note>a significant fine intended to deter counterfeiters</note>. Half of this fine shall go to our imperial treasury as the punisher of fraud, and the remaining part shall go to the aforementioned Plantin. Furthermore, the offender shall lose the books printed in imitation, which Plantin may claim for himself and take into his own possession wherever he finds them, either through himself or his agents, with the help of the local magistrate or even by his own authority.

In testimony of which, we have signed this present edict with our own hand and ordered our seal to be affixed. Given in our city of Vienna, Austria, on the 21st day of February, in the year of our Lord 1565; of our reigns, the 3rd of the Roman, the 2nd of the Hungarian, and the 17th of the Bohemian.

---

->MAXIMILIAN.<-

->By the personal command of his Sacred Imperial Majesty.<-

<sig>Haller.</sig>

<summary>This page concludes the Imperial Privilege granted by Maximilian II to Christophe Plantin, establishing a six-year copyright on his publications and setting heavy fines for unauthorized reprinting or importing. It reflects the early modern transition toward legal protections for intellectual and commercial investments in the printing industry.</summary>
